Bible Companion: summary approval table

Reviewed all 1,189 English chapter summaries across the Old and New Testaments. This table contains the chapters flagged for wording or essential context; inclusion does not mean every original summary was factually wrong.

All 129 rows approved. All 129 approved chapters implemented in all 13 languages; independent translation review and final preservation checks passed. The Verse of the Day quote fix remains included.

Keep the summary close to what the passage says. Put inferred motives, disputed identities, textual variants, and interpretive conclusions in labeled notes. The references below are the passages that help explain each issue.

S001
Genesis 2Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

"God rests ... then forms Adam" can make the creation of humans occur after the seventh-day rest.

Original app summary

God rests on the seventh day after completing creation, then forms Adam from dust, places him in the Garden of Eden with instructions not to eat from the tree of knowledge of good and evil, and creates a woman from Adam's rib as his helper. Adam names all the animals, names the woman, and they live together naked without shame.

Original translation notes

Rest: To cease; the seventh day is set apart as a gift and marker in time.

Adam and adamah: 2:7 forms ha-adam, the man, out of the dust of ha-adamah, the ground, and the two words are the same root. English cannot show it, so a reader misses that the name is the material. The same pairing returns as the sentence at 3:19, back to the adamah, because out of it you were taken.

The breath of life: Neshamah is breath drawn through the nose, and 2:7 has it breathed into the nostrils. Nephesh chayyah, what the man then becomes, is rendered living soul or living being, and it is the same phrase already used of the animals at 1:20-21 and 1:24. What separates the man in 2:7 is not the phrase at the end but the breathing at the start.

The four rivers: 2:10-14 names four and locates each one. Two are still on the map: the Chiddeqel is the Tigris, which the text places east of Asshur, and the Perath is the Euphrates. The Pishon runs around Havilah, where the text notes gold, bdellium and onyx, and the Gihon around Cush; neither of those two courses can be traced on the ground today. Bdellium is a pale aromatic gum, named again at Numbers 11:7 for the colour of manna.

To work it and keep it: Avad is to work or to serve, and shamar is to guard or keep watch over. The two verbs appear as a pair again at Numbers 3:7-8 and 18:5-6, where they are the Levites' duty toward the tabernacle: serve it and guard it. 2:15 gives the man a job with the same two words before anything has gone wrong.

A helper fit for him: English helper suggests an assistant, and the Hebrew does not carry that. Ezer is used about twenty times in the Old Testament and most of those are of God Himself: my help and my shield (Psalm 33:20), the God of your father who helps you (Genesis 49:25), our help is in the name of the LORD (Psalm 124:8). Kenegdo means corresponding to him, standing opposite. 2:18 and 2:20 pair a word of strength with a word of match.

Ish and ishshah: 2:23 is a sentence built on sound: she shall be called ishshah because she was taken out of ish. The two Hebrew words differ by one ending, so the naming and the reason are audible in a way no English pair reproduces. 2:24 then draws the conclusion about leaving and holding fast, which Jesus quotes at Matthew 19:5 and Paul at Ephesians 5:31.

Naked and not ashamed: The last word of 2:25 and one of the first words of 3:1 are near twins in Hebrew. The couple are arummim, naked, and the serpent is arum, crafty. The chapter break falls between them, but in a scroll they sit side by side, and the pun is the hinge English readers never see.

After describing God's seventh-day rest, the chapter recounts how the LORD God formed the man from dust, placed him in Eden, gave him a command, and made the woman as his corresponding helper. The man and woman live together without shame.

Direct textual basis

Verses 1-3 conclude the creation account; verse 4 introduces the account focused on the garden and humanity.

 Genesis 1:26-31: God creates humanity male and female before the sixth day ends, placing human creation before the seventh-day rest described in Genesis 2.
Matthew 19:4-6: Jesus joins the creation of male and female with the one-flesh statement, reading the creation and marriage passages together.
All implemented reference changes

Genesis 1:26-31: God creates humanity male and female before the sixth day ends, placing human creation before the seventh-day rest described in Genesis 2.
Matthew 19:4-6: Jesus joins the creation of male and female with the one-flesh statement, reading the creation and marriage passages together.

Genesis 1 already places humanity within the completed creation. Genesis 2 first describes the seventh-day rest, then turns to the garden account; its chapter order does not require humans to have been created on an eighth day. Verse 23 calls her woman; the name Eve appears in 3:20.

All implemented note changes

Context and interpretation: Genesis 1 already places humanity within the completed creation. Genesis 2 first describes the seventh-day rest, then turns to the garden account; its chapter order does not require humans to have been created on an eighth day. Verse 23 calls her woman; the name Eve appears in 3:20.

Editorial rationale

The current detailed wordplay notes do not address this chronological wording. Existing cross-references are about creation by the word and miss the immediately relevant Genesis 1 sequence.

S002
Genesis 7Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The summary extends the flood deaths to every creature, including aquatic life.

Original app summary

The flood destroys all living creatures outside the ark; only Noah, his family, and the animals with him survive.

Original translation notes

Clean / Unclean: Categories later defined in the law; here indicating animals acceptable for sacrifice versus others.

Ark: A large box-like vessel; term also used for the basket of baby Moses.

Fifteen cubits: A cubit runs about 18 inches, so fifteen cubits is roughly 22 feet or 6.8 meters. Genesis 6:15 gives the ark a height of 30 cubits, exactly twice that figure. Verse 20 is measuring how far the water stood above the high ground, not the total depth of the flood.

The seventeenth day of the second month: Genesis dates the flood by month and day, and the numbers are internally consistent. The water rises on the second month, day seventeen (7:11) and the ark grounds on the seventh month, day seventeen (8:4). That is five months, and 7:24 and 8:3 both call the same span 150 days, which gives months of exactly 30 days. The year is counted from Noah's birthday, not from a national calendar.

The flood destroys all breathing creatures on dry land outside the ark; only Noah, his family, and the animals with him survive.

Direct textual basis

The deaths are specified as breathing creatures on dry land outside the ark.

 Genesis 6:17: God announces destruction of creatures with the breath of life, supplying the wording that defines the flood warning's scope.
All implemented reference changes

Genesis 6:17: God announces destruction of creatures with the breath of life, supplying the wording that defines the flood warning's scope.

Genesis 7:21-22 describes creatures on dry land with the breath of life. It does not say that every aquatic creature died.

All implemented note changes

Context and interpretation: Genesis 7:21-22 describes creatures on dry land with the breath of life. It does not say that every aquatic creature died.

Editorial rationale

The current ark note does not address the scope of the deaths; Genesis 6:17 supplies closely related wording.

S003
Genesis 10Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

Note-focused: Nimrod as builder follows this translation, but the Hebrew subject is disputed; the city note falsely says every listed city has been excavated.

Original app summary

The clans of Japheth, Ham, and Shem spread out into their lands, languages, and nations after the flood; among them Nimrod, a mighty hunter before the LORD, begins his kingdom in Babylon and builds Nineveh, and from these clans the nations of the earth spread abroad.

Original translation notes

Nations: Peoples or ethnic groups organized by clan, land, and language.

Nimrod: A mighty hunter and kingdom builder, associated with Babel and Nineveh.

Peleg: Means 'division'; in his days the earth was divided.

Mighty: Strong or valiant; used of Nimrod as a notable figure.

Babel, Erech, Akkad, Nineveh, Calah: Genesis 10:10-12 is a list of real cities that have all been dug up. Babel is Babylon on the Euphrates; Erech is Uruk, one of the oldest cities in the world; Akkad gave its name to the Akkadian language that the whole Near East later used for diplomacy. Nineveh and Calah, both on the Tigris, became the capitals of Assyria, and the palace at Nineveh is where Sennacherib's wall reliefs were found. The verse traces the movement from the southern plain of Shinar northward to Assyria.

the Philistines and Caphtor: Genesis 10:14 places the Philistines in the line of the Casluhites and names the Caphtorites in the same breath; Amos 9:7 says the LORD brought the Philistines from Caphtor, and Jeremiah 47:4 calls them the remnant from the coasts of Caphtor. Caphtor is a place across the sea, not another Canaanite hill town, which is why the Philistines in Judges and Samuel look and fight differently from Israel's other neighbors: five confederated cities on the coastal plain, iron working they kept to themselves (1 Samuel 13:19-22), and uncircumcised, which is the standing insult used against them.

The clans of Japheth, Ham, and Shem spread out into their lands, languages, and nations after the flood; among them Nimrod, a mighty hunter before the LORD, begins his kingdom in Babylon and builds Nineveh, and from these clans the nations of the earth spread abroad.

Direct textual basis

The app follows the reading in which Nimrod goes to Assyria. Some translations read Asshur as the subject. Akkad's exact location is uncertain.

 1 Chronicles 1:10: The genealogy identifies Nimrod as Cush's son and a mighty figure, providing the parallel identification of the man discussed in Genesis 10.
Genesis 11:1-9: People build Babel's city and tower before God confuses their language; the passage does not name Nimrod among the builders.
All implemented reference changes

1 Chronicles 1:10: The genealogy identifies Nimrod as Cush's son and a mighty figure, providing the parallel identification of the man discussed in Genesis 10.
Genesis 11:1-9: People build Babel's city and tower before God confuses their language; the passage does not name Nimrod among the builders.

This translation reads Nimrod as the subject who went into Assyria and built Nineveh. Other translations read Asshur as that subject. Babel is Babylon and Erech is Uruk; the exact location of Akkad remains uncertain, so the cities in this list have not all been identified and excavated.

All implemented note changes

Babel, Erech, Akkad, Nineveh, Calah: Genesis 10:10-12 names cities of Mesopotamia; several have been identified and excavated, while the exact location of Akkad remains uncertain. Babel is Babylon on the Euphrates; Erech is Uruk, one of the oldest cities in the world; Akkad gave its name to the Akkadian language that the whole Near East later used for diplomacy. Nineveh and Calah, both on the Tigris, became the capitals of Assyria, and the palace at Nineveh is where Sennacherib's wall reliefs were found. The verse traces the movement from the southern plain of Shinar northward to Assyria.

Context and interpretation: This translation reads Nimrod as the subject who went into Assyria and built Nineveh. Other translations read Asshur as that subject. Babel is Babylon and Erech is Uruk; the exact location of Akkad remains uncertain, so the cities in this list have not all been identified and excavated.

Translation source notes 1 | Translation source notes 2

Editorial rationale

Retain the summary's base-translation reading rather than label it an error. Add the translation distinction and replace the unsupported all-excavated claim in the current city note.

S004
Genesis 14Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

"Captured kings" reverses the identity of the captors.

Original app summary

Abram rescues Lot from captured kings and is blessed by Melchizedek; Abram refuses reward from the king of Sodom.

Original translation notes

Priest: An authorized minister/officiant of worship and blessing.

Tenth (tithe): A tenth portion of goods; here given by Abram to Melchizedek.

Trained men: The word appears only here in the Hebrew Bible and means men trained or tried in service. These 318 are not hired soldiers but retainers born in Abram's own household, which tells you the scale of his establishment: a private force large enough to chase a coalition army more than 150 miles north, from Mamre near Hebron to beyond Damascus, and break it in a night attack.

Tar pits: Natural asphalt seeps, real geology at the southern end of the Dead Sea, where lumps of bitumen still surface after earthquakes. Bitumen was a traded commodity and a waterproof glue. The same substance mortars the bricks at Babel (Genesis 11:3), seals Noah's ark (Genesis 6:14), and coats the basket that carries the infant Moses (Exodus 2:3).

Abram rescues Lot from the kings who captured him and is blessed by Melchizedek; Abram refuses reward from the king of Sodom.

Direct textual basis

The four kings captured Lot; Abram defeats their force and recovers him.

 Hebrews 7:1-2: Melchizedek meets and blesses Abraham after his return from defeating the kings, confirming the battle setting of Lot's rescue.
All implemented reference changes

Hebrews 7:1-2: Melchizedek meets and blesses Abraham after his return from defeating the kings, confirming the battle setting of Lot's rescue.

The kings were Lot's captors. Abram recovered Lot, his possessions, and the other captives after routing their army.

All implemented note changes

Context and interpretation: The kings were Lot's captors. Abram recovered Lot, his possessions, and the other captives after routing their army.

Editorial rationale

Most localized summaries already express the captor relationship correctly; English and Hindi need correction. Existing references focus on Melchizedek.

S005
Genesis 29Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The sequence can imply that Jacob waited fourteen years before marrying Rachel.

Original app summary

Jacob meets Rachel at a well near Harran, works seven years to marry her, but Laban tricks him into marrying Leah first; Jacob then works another seven years for Rachel, and Leah bears him four sons while Rachel remains childless.

Original translation notes

Bride price: The mohar was paid by the groom to the bride's father, the opposite direction from a dowry. Exodus 22:16-17 treats it as a fixed going rate for a virgin, and Saul demanded a hundred Philistine foreskins in place of it (1 Samuel 18:25). Jacob reached Haran with nothing to weigh out, so he paid in labor instead: seven years, then seven more, fourteen years of a herdsman's work as the purchase price of two wives.

This week's celebration: The wedding feast ran seven days. Samson's ran the same seven (Judges 14:12). Laban tells Jacob to finish out Leah's week before Rachel is given, so the second marriage came about seven days after the first, and the second seven years of service were worked afterward rather than in advance.

Maidservant: Zilpah and Bilhah are handed over with the brides (29:24, 29:29) as the father's contribution to his daughter's new household. The maid stayed the wife's own property, which is why each sister could give her own maid to Jacob and reckon the resulting sons to herself (30:3-4, 30:9). Sarai had used the same arrangement with Hagar (16:1-4).

Reuben, Simeon, Levi, Judah: Each name puns on the sentence Leah speaks as she gives it, and English keeps the name but drops the play. Reuven sounds like ra'ah ben, 'look, a son', and she says the LORD has looked on her affliction. Shimon comes from shama, to hear. Levi comes from lavah, to attach or join. Yehudah comes from yadah, to praise.

Jacob meets Rachel at a well near Harran, works seven years to marry her, but Laban tricks him into marrying Leah first; Jacob marries Rachel after Leah's wedding week and then works another seven years for her, and Leah bears him four sons while Rachel remains childless.

Direct textual basis

Jacob marries Rachel after Leah's wedding week, then serves seven additional years.

 Hosea 12:12: Hosea recalls Jacob tending sheep to obtain a wife, supplying a later biblical reference to his marriage-by-service arrangement.
All implemented reference changes

Hosea 12:12: Hosea recalls Jacob tending sheep to obtain a wife, supplying a later biblical reference to his marriage-by-service arrangement.

Jacob served seven years before Leah's wedding. After that wedding week he also married Rachel, then served another seven years; Rachel's wedding did not wait until the end of those years.

All implemented note changes

Context and interpretation: Jacob served seven years before Leah's wedding. After that wedding week he also married Rachel, then served another seven years; Rachel's wedding did not wait until the end of those years.

Editorial rationale

Existing references confirm service for a wife but do not clarify when the second marriage occurred.

S006
Genesis 30Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The summary can present the branches as an effective breeding mechanism while omitting Jacob's later attribution to God.

Original app summary

Rachel gives her servant Bilhah to Jacob to bear children for her, then Leah does the same with her servant Zilpah; eventually Rachel conceives and bears Joseph. After Joseph's birth, Jacob negotiates with Laban to claim spotted and speckled animals as wages, then uses peeled branches at watering troughs to breed animals that match his agreed-upon colors, growing wealthy in the process.

Original translation notes

Remember: To call to mind with action; used for God regarding Rachel.

Mandrakes: Mandragora officinarum, a low Mediterranean plant with a thick forked root and a small yellow fruit that ripens in late spring, which is why Reuben finds them during the wheat harvest (30:14). The fruit carried a long reputation across the ancient world as an aid to love and conception. The Hebrew name shares its consonants with dod, 'love', and the only other occurrence is Song of Songs 7:13, where it stands beside the lovers' door.

That she may bear children for me: Rachel's words in 30:3 read literally 'she shall bear on my knees'. Setting a newborn on someone's knees was the act that made the child theirs. Joseph does the same with Machir's sons at the end of the book (50:23). The maid bore the child, the mistress received it on her knees, and the child counted in the mistress's line.

Joseph: The name carries two puns at once, and the text supplies both. Rachel says God has taken away (asaph) her disgrace, then asks the LORD to add (yasaph) another son (30:23-24). English can carry one of the two, never both.

Rachel and Leah build their households through their servants, and God enables Rachel to bear Joseph. Jacob agrees to receive marked animals as wages and places peeled branches before the breeding flocks. His flocks grow; in the following chapter he credits God with giving him Laban's livestock.

Direct textual basis

Jacob puts peeled branches before the mating flocks; in chapter 31 he credits God with taking the livestock from Laban and giving it to him.

 Genesis 31:8-12: Jacob tells his wives that God gave him Laban's livestock despite changing wage terms, identifying whom he credits for the increase.
All implemented reference changes

Genesis 31:8-12: Jacob tells his wives that God gave him Laban's livestock despite changing wage terms, identifying whom he credits for the increase.

The account records Jacob's use of peeled branches; it does not explain a biological mechanism. In Genesis 31:8-12 Jacob tells his wives that God gave him the livestock despite Laban's changing wage terms.

All implemented note changes

Context and interpretation: The account records Jacob's use of peeled branches; it does not explain a biological mechanism. In Genesis 31:8-12 Jacob tells his wives that God gave him the livestock despite Laban's changing wage terms.

Editorial rationale

The essential Genesis 31 cross-reference is already present and explicitly rejects branches as the source of the transfer. The summary needs the same restraint about causality.

S007
Genesis 37Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

Note-focused: notes for Tamar and Potiphar's wife belong to later chapters; the price note treats sanctuary valuation as a demonstrated market rate.

Original app summary

Joseph's brothers, jealous of their father's favoritism and Joseph's dreams of dominion, strip him of his robe and sell him to Ishmaelites heading to Egypt; they deceive their father Jacob with the bloodied robe, leading him to believe Joseph is dead.

Original translation notes

Sell: To exchange for money; Joseph was sold for twenty shekels of silver.

Prostitute: A woman offering sex for payment; Tamar disguised herself this way.

Lie with: To lie down sexually; Potiphar’s wife demanded Joseph lie with her.

Flee: To escape or run away; Joseph fled when seized by Potiphar’s wife.

sackcloth: A coarse, dark goat-hair fabric worn against bare skin as a universal sign of mourning, repentance, or distress (1 Kings 21:27; Jonah 3:5; Matthew 11:21). Wearing it publicly broadcast grief or contrition; the discomfort itself was part of the symbolism.

myrrh: A fragrant gum resin from the Commiphora tree of Arabia and East Africa. Used as a holy anointing-oil ingredient (Exodus 30:23), as perfume, as an embalming spice (John 19:39), and, mixed with wine, as a pain-dulling drink (Mark 15:23). One of the magi's gifts.

Sheol: The Old Testament realm of the dead; a shadowy underworld where both righteous and wicked descend (Genesis 37:35; Psalm 16:10). Not necessarily a place of torment; more a state of cessation and silence. The Septuagint and New Testament render it with the Greek word hades.

twenty shekels of silver: Genesis 37:28 gives the price paid for Joseph, and Leviticus 27:5 sets exactly twenty shekels as the valuation of a male between five and twenty years old. Joseph was seventeen (37:2). The figure is not arbitrary; it is the going rate for a boy of that age. Exodus 21:32 later prices a slave gored by an ox at thirty shekels, the valuation of an adult woman in Leviticus 27:4, and the same thirty pieces appear in Zechariah 11:12-13 and Matthew 26:15.

the caravan out of Gilead: Genesis 37:25 puts a loaded caravan on the road at Dothan, and that is where the road was. Dothan sits at the north end of the hill country where the route from Gilead crosses toward the coastal plain and turns south for Egypt; the whole eastern trade in aromatic resins moved along it. Balm of Gilead was a medicinal resin famous enough to be used as a figure for healing in Jeremiah 8:22 and 46:11, and myrrh appears again as a burial spice at John 19:39.

Joseph's brothers, jealous of their father's favoritism and Joseph's dreams of dominion, strip him of his robe and sell him to Ishmaelites heading to Egypt; they deceive their father Jacob with the bloodied robe, leading him to believe Joseph is dead.

Direct textual basis

This chapter gives Joseph's price as twenty shekels. Leviticus 27:5 independently gives twenty shekels as a sanctuary valuation for a male aged five to twenty.

 Leviticus 27:5: The law assigns twenty shekels to a male aged five to twenty for a vow valuation, the figure compared with Joseph's sale price.
Genesis 38:14-19: Tamar disguises herself and sleeps with Judah, locating the incident described by the misplaced prostitute note in Genesis 38.
Genesis 39:7-12: Potiphar's wife repeatedly solicits Joseph and seizes his cloak as he flees, locating those incidents in Genesis 39.
All implemented reference changes

Leviticus 27:5: The law assigns twenty shekels to a male aged five to twenty for a vow valuation, the figure compared with Joseph's sale price.
Genesis 38:14-19: Tamar disguises herself and sleeps with Judah, locating the incident described by the misplaced prostitute note in Genesis 38.
Genesis 39:7-12: Potiphar's wife repeatedly solicits Joseph and seizes his cloak as he flees, locating those incidents in Genesis 39.

Joseph was seventeen and was sold for twenty shekels. Leviticus 27:5 gives the same number for the sanctuary valuation of a male aged five to twenty; that is a comparison, not proof of a slave-market price schedule. Tamar appears in Genesis 38, and Potiphar's wife in Genesis 39.

All implemented note changes

twenty shekels of silver: Genesis 37:28 gives the price paid for Joseph, and Leviticus 27:5 sets exactly twenty shekels as the valuation of a male between five and twenty years old. Joseph was seventeen (37:2). The numerical match compares a sale price with a sanctuary valuation; it does not establish a slave-market price schedule. Exodus 21:32 later prices a slave gored by an ox at thirty shekels, the valuation of an adult woman in Leviticus 27:4, and the same thirty pieces appear in Zechariah 11:12-13 and Matthew 26:15.

Context and interpretation: Joseph was seventeen and was sold for twenty shekels. Leviticus 27:5 gives the same number for the sanctuary valuation of a male aged five to twenty; that is a comparison, not proof of a slave-market price schedule. Tamar appears in Genesis 38, and Potiphar's wife in Genesis 39.

Editorial rationale

The current Prostitute, Lie with, and Flee notes describe events absent from chapter 37. The existing valuation cross-reference is useful, but its current note turns numerical similarity into an unsupported market-price explanation.

S008
Exodus 7Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The summary gives Aaron's action to Moses.

Original app summary

The LORD instructs Moses and Aaron to confront Pharaoh; Aaron's staff becomes a snake and swallows the staffs of Pharaoh's magicians, but Pharaoh's heart hardens. Moses then strikes the Nile with his staff and turns all the water to blood throughout Egypt, yet Pharaoh's magicians replicate the act and Pharaoh refuses to listen.

Original translation notes

Harden: Three different Hebrew verbs arrive in English as one word. At 7:3 God says He will make Pharaoh's heart qasheh, hard; at 7:13 it is chazaq, strong or firm; at 7:14 it is kaved, heavy, the same root as the 'heavy' labour of 5:9 and the 'heavy' mouth Moses complained of at 4:10. The text distributes the action as well as the vocabulary: Pharaoh hardens his own heart at 8:15, 8:32 and 9:34, and the LORD is named as the one hardening it from 9:12 on.

magicians: A loanword from Egyptian, describing the lector priests who read the ritual books and were credited with power over nature. They are court professionals, not stage entertainers. 2 Timothy 3:8 names two of them, Jannes and Jambres.

serpent: Exodus 4:3 turns the staff into a nachash, an ordinary snake. Before Pharaoh in Exodus 7:9 the word changes to tannin, the term for the great creatures of Genesis 1:21, and the word Ezekiel 29:3 uses for Pharaoh himself, the monster lying in his own streams. English prints 'snake' and 'serpent' and the change disappears.

the Nile: The Hebrew uses an Egyptian loanword for the river, not the usual word for a stream. Egypt's whole food supply depended on the Nile flood, and the inundation was worshipped as the god Hapi. Exodus 12:12 states that the LORD executed judgment against all the gods of Egypt, and the first blow lands on the river.

eighty years old: Verse 7:7 dates the confrontation precisely: Moses is eighty and Aaron eighty-three when they walk into the throne room. Acts 7:23 and 7:30 divide his life into forty in Egypt and forty in Midian, and Deuteronomy 34:7 puts him at a hundred and twenty when he dies, which makes the wilderness the last forty.

The LORD instructs Moses and Aaron to confront Pharaoh; Aaron's staff becomes a snake and swallows the staffs of Pharaoh's magicians, but Pharaoh's heart hardens. Aaron then strikes the Nile with his staff and turns all the water to blood throughout Egypt, yet Pharaoh's magicians replicate the act and Pharaoh refuses to listen.

Direct textual basis

Aaron raises the staff and strikes the Nile in Pharaoh's presence.

 No additional contextual reference needed.

Moses receives the command to instruct Aaron. Verse 20 identifies Aaron as the one who raises the staff and strikes the Nile.

All implemented note changes

Context and interpretation: Moses receives the command to instruct Aaron. Verse 20 identifies Aaron as the one who raises the staff and strikes the Nile.

Editorial rationale

The Nile note discusses Egypt's river worship but does not clarify the actor. Exodus 4 describes a related sign given to Moses and can contribute to the confusion.

S009
Exodus 9Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

Pharaoh's promise is placed after the hail stops; "all Egyptian livestock" also loses the stated field restriction.

Original app summary

God sends a plague killing all Egyptian livestock while sparing Israel's, then a plague of boils on people and animals; when Pharaoh still refuses to release Israel, God sends a devastating hailstorm that destroys crops and kills anyone caught outside, though it avoids Goshen. Pharaoh temporarily agrees to let Israel go after the hail stops, but immediately hardens his heart again and refuses.

Original translation notes

livestock: The word covers herds and flocks as property and wealth. Egypt kept sacred bulls, Apis at Memphis and Mnevis at Heliopolis, and the cow goddess Hathor was among the most widely worshipped deities in the land. Exodus 9:4 records that the plague drew a line between Israel's animals and Egypt's.

soot from the furnace: Ash from a brick kiln, the very furnace Israel had been forced to work. Thrown into the air it became festering boils on man and beast. The lector priests could not even stand before Moses because the boils were on them too (Exodus 9:11).

flax and barley, wheat and spelt: This is a dated agricultural note dropped into the narrative. Barley ripens about a month ahead of wheat, and flax is pulled while in flower. Exodus 9:31-32 says the barley was already in the ear and the flax in bloom while the wheat and spelt were still green, which places the hail a few weeks before the Exodus in the month of Abib.

a plague on the livestock: Dever is the standard word for an epidemic, used of both animals and people, and it turns up in the covenant curses of Deuteronomy 28:21 and in David's choice at 2 Samuel 24:13-15. Here at 9:3 it falls on horses, donkeys, camels, cattle and flocks, which is to say on Egypt's transport, its ploughing, and its meat and milk at once.

the officials who feared the word: Verses 9:20-21 split Pharaoh's own court in two. Some officials believed the warning and brought their slaves and animals under cover before the hail; others left them in the field and lost them. It is the first sign in the account that the warnings were landing on Egyptians, and 12:38 records that a mixed crowd left the country with Israel.

God sends a plague killing Egyptian livestock in the field while sparing Israel's, then a plague of boils on people and animals; when Pharaoh still refuses to release Israel, God sends a devastating hailstorm that destroys crops and kills anyone caught outside, though it avoids Goshen. During the hail, Pharaoh promises to let Israel go, but after it stops he hardens his heart again and refuses.

Direct textual basis

The livestock warning concerns animals in the field. Pharaoh promises release during the hail, then refuses after it stops.

 Exodus 8:15: Pharaoh hardens his heart after relief from a plague, establishing the earlier pattern repeated after the hail stops.
All implemented reference changes

Exodus 8:15: Pharaoh hardens his heart after relief from a plague, establishing the earlier pattern repeated after the hail stops.

The livestock warning in verse 3 specifies animals in the field. Later animals are brought under shelter before the hail. Pharaoh promises release while asking for the hail to stop, then hardens his heart after it stops.

All implemented note changes

Context and interpretation: The livestock warning in verse 3 specifies animals in the field. Later animals are brought under shelter before the hail. Pharaoh promises release while asking for the hail to stop, then hardens his heart after it stops.

Editorial rationale

The current notes do not explain either qualification. The chapter itself records surviving livestock and the correct order.

S010
Exodus 10Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The summary attributes this chapter's hardening to Pharaoh himself.

Original app summary

Moses warns Pharaoh of a locust plague; when it devastates Egypt, Pharaoh asks forgiveness, but after the locusts are blown away he hardens his heart again. The LORD then brings three days of darkness over Egypt, and when Pharaoh offers to let the Israelites go but retain their livestock, Moses refuses and Pharaoh orders him never to return.

Original translation notes

locusts: The desert locust, which swarms and migrates on the wind; Exodus 10:13 brings them on an east wind and Exodus 10:19 clears them on a west one. The UN Food and Agriculture Organization reckons a swarm covering one square kilometer holds about 40 million insects and eats in a day what 35,000 people would eat. The text says nothing green was left in Egypt.

a palpable darkness: The verb means to grope or to feel with the hand. Egypt's chief deity was the sun god Ra, and Pharaoh held his throne as Ra's son, so the sun going out for three days struck at the crown as well as the temple. Exodus 10:23 notes that Israel had light where they lived.

Egypt lies in ruins: At 10:7 Pharaoh's own officials tell him the country is finished and ask him to let the people go, before the locusts have even arrived. The plagues have already taken the river, the livestock, and the barley and flax harvest (9:31), and the court can read a ledger. It is the second time in the book that Egyptians break with their king, after 9:20.

only the men may go: Pharaoh negotiates three times and each offer keeps a hostage. Sacrifice inside Egypt (8:25); go, but not far (8:28); only the men, leaving wives and children behind (10:11); and finally everyone but the flocks and herds (10:24). Moses refuses each in turn, and answers the last one at 10:26 with 'not a hoof will be left behind,' because the animals are what the sacrifices are made of.

the east wind: The locusts arrive on an east wind (10:13) and leave on a west one (10:19), which is how desert locust swarms actually travel: they ride the prevailing wind rather than steering. The same east wind drives back the sea at 14:21. Hebrew uses one word, ruach, for wind, breath and spirit, so the reader of the original hears the same word in all three places.

Moses warns Pharaoh of a locust plague; when it devastates Egypt, Pharaoh asks forgiveness, but after the locusts are blown away the LORD hardens Pharaoh's heart again. The LORD then brings three days of darkness over Egypt, and when Pharaoh offers to let the Israelites go but retain their livestock, Moses refuses and Pharaoh orders him never to return.

Direct textual basis

Verses 20 and 27 explicitly say that the LORD hardens Pharaoh's heart.

 Exodus 9:34: Pharaoh and his officials harden their hearts after the hail stops, supplying the self-hardening attribution alongside chapter 10's divine attribution.
Romans 9:17-18: Paul cites God's words to Pharaoh and speaks of God showing mercy and hardening, giving the later apostolic discussion of divine agency.
All implemented reference changes

Exodus 9:34: Pharaoh and his officials harden their hearts after the hail stops, supplying the self-hardening attribution alongside chapter 10's divine attribution.
Romans 9:17-18: Paul cites God's words to Pharaoh and speaks of God showing mercy and hardening, giving the later apostolic discussion of divine agency.

This chapter names the LORD as the one who hardens Pharaoh's heart. Other passages describe Pharaoh hardening his own heart; the narrative uses both descriptions.

All implemented note changes

only the men may go: Pharaoh negotiates several times and each offer keeps a hostage. Sacrifice inside Egypt (8:25); go, but not far (8:28); only the men, leaving wives and children behind (10:11); and finally everyone but the flocks and herds (10:24). Moses refuses each in turn, and answers the last one at 10:26 with 'not a hoof will be left behind,' because the animals are what the sacrifices are made of.

Context and interpretation: This chapter names the LORD as the one who hardens Pharaoh's heart. Other passages describe Pharaoh hardening his own heart; the narrative uses both descriptions.

Editorial rationale

The existing darkness note addresses Egyptian religion, not the passage's explicit attribution. This preserves both forms of agency without replacing either one.

S011
Exodus 13Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The consecration summary omits the explicit redemption of human firstborn, a material distinction beside the Jephthah discussion.

Original app summary

God commands Moses to consecrate all firstborn males and establish an annual Feast of Unleavened Bread to commemorate the exodus; he then leads Israel away from Egypt by the desert road toward the Red Sea, guiding them by a pillar of cloud by day and fire by night, while Moses carries Joseph's bones as promised.

Original translation notes

Pillar: An ammud is an upright column, the same word used for the posts holding up the tabernacle curtains (26:32). Verses 13:21-22 say neither pillar ever left its place in front of the people, so the column functions as a marching guide that can be seen from anywhere in a camp of that size, by day as a cloud and by night as fire. Numbers 9:15-23 makes the rule explicit: the camp moved when it lifted and stayed when it settled.

redeem the firstborn: To buy back by paying a set price. Every firstborn male belonged to God, so a firstborn son and a firstborn donkey had to be bought back rather than sacrificed; Numbers 18:16 fixes the price for a son at five shekels of the sanctuary, about 57 grams of silver. Luke 2:22-24 shows the rite still being kept.

a sign on your hand and a reminder on your forehead: The Hebrew totafot appears only in this command and in Deuteronomy 6:8 and 11:18. It is the line behind the leather boxes of scripture, tefillin, called phylacteries in Matthew 23:5, worn on the arm and the head.

the road through the land of the Philistines: The coastal highway from the eastern Delta to Gaza, the shortest way into Canaan at roughly 150 miles, and the route Egypt used for its armies. It was garrisoned with forts and wells at intervals, which is exactly why Exodus 13:17 says God did not lead Israel that way.

Succoth and Etham: Succoth is the first stop out of Egypt (12:37, 13:20) and its name means booths, the temporary branch shelters people threw up when they camped. The Feast of Booths in Leviticus 23:42-43 is named for the same thing and given the same reason: so Israel would remember living in shelters on the way out of Egypt. Etham is described as on the edge of the wilderness, which means the party had reached the point where cultivated Delta land stops.

the bones of Joseph: Joseph made Israel swear to carry his bones out (Genesis 50:25), and 13:19 records Moses collecting on that oath while the country is in chaos behind him. Egyptians embalmed and Genesis 50:26 says Joseph was embalmed and put in a coffin, so there was something to carry. Joshua 24:32 finishes the account: they buried him at Shechem, in the plot of ground Jacob had bought.

God commands Israel to consecrate the firstborn, redeem every firstborn son, and remember the exodus through the Feast of Unleavened Bread. He leads the people by cloud and fire, while Moses carries Joseph's bones as promised.

Direct textual basis

Firstborn sons must be redeemed; the firstborn consecration command does not require sacrificing them.

 Numbers 18:15-16: Firstborn sons must be redeemed for five shekels at one month old, explicitly distinguishing their consecration from sacrifice.
Deuteronomy 12:31: Israel must not imitate the nations' worship that burns sons and daughters, providing the Torah's prohibition of child sacrifice.
Luke 2:22-24: Jesus' parents present Him to the Lord and bring the prescribed birds, showing firstborn consecration within a family's observance of the law.
All implemented reference changes

Numbers 18:15-16: Firstborn sons must be redeemed for five shekels at one month old, explicitly distinguishing their consecration from sacrifice.
Luke 2:22-24: Jesus' parents present Him to the Lord and bring the prescribed birds, showing firstborn consecration within a family's observance of the law.
Deuteronomy 12:31: Israel must not imitate the nations' worship that burns sons and daughters, providing the Torah's prohibition of child sacrifice.

Every firstborn son must be redeemed, not sacrificed. The chapter distinguishes human redemption from the treatment of sacrificial animals, so consecration to the LORD cannot be read as permission for human sacrifice.

All implemented note changes

Context and interpretation: Every firstborn son must be redeemed, not sacrificed. The chapter distinguishes human redemption from the treatment of sacrificial animals, so consecration to the LORD cannot be read as permission for human sacrifice.

Editorial rationale

The current redemption note and Numbers 18 reference already state this correctly. The proposal carries that essential distinction into the summary.

S012
Exodus 22Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The opening makes death sound like a judicial punishment for ordinary property theft.

Original app summary

Laws govern theft and restitution, with penalties ranging from double repayment to death depending on the offense; additional rules address property damage, borrowed animals, seduction, sorcery, idolatry, treatment of foreigners and the poor, lending practices, and offerings to God.

Original translation notes

his cloak as collateral: The outer garment doubled as a blanket, so seizing it overnight left a poor man without bedding; Exodus 22:26-27 orders it returned by sunset. An ostracon from Mesad Hashavyahu near Yavneh-Yam, written in the late seventh century BC, is a field worker's petition complaining that exactly this happened to him.

interest: The word is built on the verb 'to bite.' The law does not regulate a rate; Exodus 22:25 forbids acting as a creditor toward a poor Israelite at all, and Deuteronomy 23:19-20 restates it. The prohibition is about lending inside the covenant community.

set apart for destruction: To devote something irrevocably to God, so that it can never be redeemed, sold, or put to ordinary use. Leviticus 27:28-29 defines the term, and Joshua 6:17-19 applies it to Jericho. In Exodus 22:20 it is the sentence on anyone who sacrifices to another god.

He entices her: This verb is the third of three that English versions blur together. Exodus 22:16-17 covers a man who talks an unbetrothed woman into it: he must pay the bride price and marry her, but 22:17 lets her father refuse the marriage outright, and the man pays the silver anyway. Deuteronomy 22:28-29 handles the parallel case with taphas, to take hold of, and Deuteronomy 22:25-27 handles force with chazaq, where the man is executed and the woman is declared guiltless. In Hebrew Bibles these two verses are numbered 22:15-16.

The bride price: The mohar was paid by the groom to the bride's father, the opposite direction from a dowry, and it is what Exodus 22:16-17 requires whether or not the marriage goes ahead. Shechem offers to pay any mohar Jacob's sons name (Genesis 34:12). Saul asks David for one made of a hundred Philistine foreskins instead of silver, intending David to die collecting it (1 Samuel 18:25). Deuteronomy 22:29 fixes the sum for its case at fifty shekels.

The foreigner, the widow, and the orphan: These three had no clan to stand up for them, which in a society run on family claims meant no one to press a case at the gate. Exodus 22:21-24 forbids wronging any of them and gives the reason for the first: you were foreigners in Egypt. Then it does something unusual for a law code and names the enforcement, saying that if they cry out He will hear the cry Himself. Deuteronomy 24:17-21 attaches the harvest gleanings to the same three.

The sorceress: The word covers paid ritual work: spells and binding formulas, potions, and figures made to act on a person at a distance. Exodus 7:11 uses the masculine form of the same root for the specialists Pharaoh calls in against Moses. Deuteronomy 18:10-11 gives the full list of trades Israel was to have none of, and 2 Kings 9:22 charges Jezebel with these practices by name. Exodus 22:18 puts the practitioner under a death sentence.

The chapter sets restitution for theft and property loss, and distinguishes bloodguilt in the killing of a burglar by night or after sunrise. It also gives laws on sexual conduct, sorcery, idolatry, loans, offerings, and protection of foreigners, widows, orphans, and the poor.

Direct textual basis

Theft requires restitution; verses 2-3 separately address bloodguilt when a householder kills a burglar, distinguishing night from daylight.

 Job 24:16: Job describes thieves digging through houses in darkness, supplying the physical break-in setting relevant to the burglary law.
Leviticus 6:1-5: A person guilty of theft or deception must restore the property and add a fifth, providing a parallel law of restitution.
All implemented reference changes

Leviticus 6:1-5: A person guilty of theft or deception must restore the property and add a fifth, providing a parallel law of restitution.
Job 24:16: Job describes thieves digging through houses in darkness, supplying the physical break-in setting relevant to the burglary law.

Verses 1-4 prescribe restitution for theft. The death in verses 2-3 is a burglar killed during a break-in, with different bloodguilt rules before and after sunrise; it is not a court's death sentence for stealing property.

All implemented note changes

Context and interpretation: Verses 1-4 prescribe restitution for theft. The death in verses 2-3 is a burglar killed during a break-in, with different bloodguilt rules before and after sunrise; it is not a court's death sentence for stealing property.

Editorial rationale

The current notes address related laws but do not separate burglary bloodguilt from theft penalties. Job 24 supplies the same breaking-through-a-house context.

S013
Leviticus 10Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

"Forbids ... to mourn" turns restrictions on priestly mourning rites into a ban on grief itself.

Original app summary

Nadab and Abihu offer unauthorized fire before the LORD, and fire from his presence consumes them; Moses forbids Aaron and his remaining sons to mourn or leave the tent of meeting. The LORD himself commands Aaron not to drink wine or strong drink when entering the tent and to distinguish holy from common; Moses directs the eating of the priests' portions, and when he finds the sin-offering goat burned rather than eaten, Aaron's explanation satisfies him.

Original translation notes

Unauthorized/foreign fire: Zar means foreign or belonging to someone else, the same word used of an outsider who may not eat holy food (22:10). The coals for the incense were to be taken from the altar itself (16:12), so fire brought from anywhere else broke the one rule governing the whole procedure. Leviticus 10:1 says plainly that they offered fire He had not commanded them, and 10:9 follows immediately with a ban on priests drinking wine before entering the tent.

Outside the camp: Place for burning sacrificial remains and carrying out bodies; symbolizes removal of impurity from the holy camp.

anointing oil: Olive oil mixed with specific spices (Exodus 30:22-25), used to consecrate priests, kings, and tabernacle furniture. The Hebrew word for an anointed one is mashiach; transliterated 'Messiah.' The Greek equivalent is christos.

censer: A metal pan for carrying live coals from the altar, with incense laid on top. Numbers 16:6-7 has Korah's company each take one, and Numbers 16:39 has the bronze ones hammered into altar plating afterward. It was a priest's tool, and Nadab and Abihu used theirs with fire the LORD had not commanded.

holy and common, clean and unclean: Two separate pairs, not one sliding scale, and Leviticus 10:10 makes teaching the difference part of the priest's job. Holy and common describe what is set apart to God and what is not. Clean and unclean describe ritual status. A thing can be common and clean at the same time, which is the ordinary condition of most of life.

wine or strong drink: Yayin is grape wine; shekhar covers other fermented drink, including that made from grain or dates. The ban applies to priests on duty in the tent (10:9), and the Nazirite vow in Numbers 6:3 extends it further. Ezekiel 44:21 repeats it for the priests of the restored temple.

Nadab and Abihu die after offering unauthorized fire. Moses forbids Aaron and his remaining sons to perform the stated mourning rites or leave the tent, while Israel may mourn. The chapter gives priestly duties and records Moses accepting Aaron's explanation about the uneaten sin offering.

Direct textual basis

Moses forbids disheveled hair, torn garments, and leaving the tent; the rest of Israel may mourn. Aaron's response later acknowledges his loss.

 Leviticus 21:1-12: Priests have specified family exceptions to corpse defilement, while the high priest faces stricter mourning and sanctuary restrictions, clarifying the ritual limits.
All implemented reference changes

Leviticus 21:1-12: Priests have specified family exceptions to corpse defilement, while the high priest faces stricter mourning and sanctuary restrictions, clarifying the ritual limits.

The restrictions name particular public mourning acts and departure from the tent. They do not say that Aaron felt no grief or was forbidden to grieve inwardly; verses 19-20 record his loss being taken seriously.

All implemented note changes

Context and interpretation: The restrictions name particular public mourning acts and departure from the tent. They do not say that Aaron felt no grief or was forbidden to grieve inwardly; verses 19-20 record his loss being taken seriously.

Editorial rationale

No current mourning note supplies this distinction. The priestly mourning laws in chapter 21 provide direct legal context.

S014
Leviticus 11Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The exhaustive food list omits permitted birds and then declares all other animals unclean.

Original app summary

God instructs Moses and Aaron on which animals the Israelites may eat: land animals with divided hooves that chew cud, water creatures with fins and scales, and certain insects; all others are unclean, and touching their carcasses causes ceremonial uncleanness until evening.

Original translation notes

sackcloth: A coarse, dark goat-hair fabric worn against bare skin as a universal sign of mourning, repentance, or distress (1 Kings 21:27; Jonah 3:5; Matthew 11:21). Wearing it publicly broadcast grief or contrition; the discomfort itself was part of the symbolism.

clean and unclean: These are ritual categories, not verdicts about hygiene, health, or sin. Being unclean is a status that bars a person from the sanctuary until it lapses or is washed off; most cases end at sunset (11:24-25). Ordinary life produced it constantly, and nothing in Leviticus calls an unclean person guilty for being unclean.

the criteria: The text gives observable tests, not reasons. A land animal must have a fully split hoof and bring up the cud, and both are required, which is why the camel and the pig each fail on one count (11:4-7). Water creatures need fins and scales (11:9); insects need jointed legs for hopping (11:21).

detestable: Leviticus 11 uses sheqets, a word reserved for these creatures and what touches them. The stronger term toevah, usually rendered abomination, is the one used for moral offenses in Leviticus 18:22 and 20:13. English blurs two different Hebrew words into one.

the bird list: Leviticus 11:13-19 never gives a rule for birds, only a list of about twenty. Nearly all of them are birds of prey, carrion eaters, or waders, and the bat is counted with them. Several of the Hebrew names cannot now be matched to a species with certainty, which is why English versions differ from one another in this list.

the pig: Leviticus 11:7 gives the pig its own line because it is the near miss: the hoof is split clean through, but it brings up no cud. Pigs were farmed and eaten all around Israel, and Isaiah 65:4 and 66:17 place the eating of pig flesh inside pagan rites, among the graves and in the sacred groves. Refusing pork became the most visible thing that marked a Jew off from his neighbors in the ancient world.

why the clay pot is smashed: Leviticus 11:32-36 treats a vessel by what it is made of. Ancient earthenware is porous and cannot be scoured out, so a clay pot or a clay oven that takes uncleanness is broken and thrown away. Wood, cloth, leather, and sackcloth are only rinsed and are clean at sunset, and a spring or a cistern stays clean because running and stored water is not a container. The rule follows the material, not the price of the object.

God instructs Moses and Aaron on which animals the Israelites may eat: land animals with divided hooves that chew cud, water creatures with fins and scales, permitted birds, and certain insects; all others are unclean, and touching their carcasses causes ceremonial uncleanness until evening.

Direct textual basis

The chapter lists forbidden birds; Deuteronomy explicitly permits clean birds.

 Deuteronomy 14:11-20: The law expressly permits clean birds while listing prohibited birds, confirming that the dietary rules do not exclude every bird.
All implemented reference changes

Genesis 7:2-3: Noah is told to take seven pairs of each kind of clean animal and each kind of bird, but one pair of each kind of unclean animal, before the Sinai laws.
Deuteronomy 14:11-20: The law expressly permits clean birds while listing prohibited birds, confirming that the dietary rules do not exclude every bird.

The bird list names prohibited birds; it does not prohibit every bird. Deuteronomy 14:11 and 14:20 explicitly allow clean birds.

All implemented note changes

Context and interpretation: The bird list names prohibited birds; it does not prohibit every bird. Deuteronomy 14:11 and 14:20 explicitly allow clean birds.

Editorial rationale

The current bird-list note already describes a list of forbidden species, and Deuteronomy 14 is already linked. The summary contradicts that distinction.

S015
Leviticus 15Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

One washing, waiting, and offering rule is applied to every discharge and every contact.

Original app summary

The LORD establishes rules for various bodily discharges; anyone or anything touched by someone with a discharge becomes unclean and requires washing and waiting until evening, with purification offerings required after seven days of cleansing.

Original translation notes

discharge: A general term for an abnormal bodily flow, not a named disease. The chapter distinguishes it from ordinary emissions and from menstruation, and prescribes washing, waiting until evening, and for the chronic case a seven-day count and two birds (15:13-15). Mark 5:25 describes a woman under exactly this chapter's long-term condition.

unclean until evening: The most common ruling in the chapter, and the one that shows the scale of the system: most uncleanness ended at sundown with a wash. The Hebrew day turns at evening, so the status lapsed the same night. Leviticus 22:6-7 applies the same limit to priests.

lest they die by defiling My tabernacle: Leviticus 15:31 gives the reason for the whole chapter: the danger is uncleanness carried into the sanctuary, not uncleanness itself. Numbers 5:1-4 acts on the same principle by moving affected people outside the camp while the tent stands in the middle of it.

the bed and the seat: The rules at 15:4-12 and 15:19-27 work outward by contact: the bed, the seat, the saddle, anyone who touches them, anyone the man spits on, and any clay pot he handles, which is broken. The remedy in almost every case is the same and it is small, wash your clothes, bathe, and be clean at sunset. What the chapter builds is a traceable chain of contact, not a scale of guilt.

a discharge of blood for many days: Verses 15:25-30 cover bleeding outside the monthly cycle or continuing past it, and rule the woman unclean for as long as it lasts, with everything she lies or sits on unclean too. That is the standing of the woman in Matthew 9:20-22, Mark 5:25-34 and Luke 8:43-48, who had bled for twelve years: twelve years in which touching anyone passed the uncleanness on, which is why she comes up behind Him and says nothing.

The LORD gives distinct purification rules for abnormal discharges, emissions of semen, and menstruation. These include washing, periods of uncleanness, and, for specified abnormal discharges, offerings after seven clean days.

Direct textual basis

Abnormal discharges, semen, and menstruation have distinct rules; the seven-day count and bird offerings apply to specified abnormal discharges.

 Mark 5:25-34: Jesus heals a woman who has bled for twelve years, supplying a specific prolonged-bleeding case alongside Leviticus' distinct discharge rules.
All implemented reference changes

Mark 5:25-34: Jesus heals a woman who has bled for twelve years, supplying a specific prolonged-bleeding case alongside Leviticus' distinct discharge rules.

The chapter distinguishes ordinary semen emissions and menstruation from abnormal or prolonged discharges. The offerings after seven clean days belong to the specified abnormal discharges, not every bodily emission.

All implemented note changes

Context and interpretation: The chapter distinguishes ordinary semen emissions and menstruation from abnormal or prolonged discharges. The offerings after seven clean days belong to the specified abnormal discharges, not every bodily emission.

Editorial rationale

The current discharge notes already explain this distinction, and Mark 5 is already linked. The summary needs to agree with those notes.

S016
Leviticus 20Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

Both summary and medium note merge consulting a medium with practicing as one under the same death penalty.

Original app summary

God commands Moses to enforce death penalties for child sacrifice to Molek, consulting mediums, cursing parents, adultery, incest, bestiality, and certain sexual violations; Israel must keep these decrees and distinctions between clean and unclean to remain holy and avoid being rejected by the land.

Original translation notes

Medium / Necromancer: Spiritist or consulter of the dead; strictly forbidden under penalty of death.

Cut off: To be removed from the people, sometimes by death, sometimes by exclusion; the text does not always specify which.

pass through the fire to Molech: Leviticus 20:2-5 sets the death penalty on giving a child to Molech and adds a second penalty for a community that looks the other way. 2 Kings 23:10 has Josiah wrecking the site in the Valley of Ben Hinnom to stop it, and Jeremiah 32:35 names the same place. The practice is treated as importable, not theoretical.

I will set My face against: A judicial formula, the opposite of the blessing in Numbers 6:25 where the LORD makes His face shine on a person. It appears at 17:10, 20:3, 20:5, and 20:6, always where the community itself might fail to act. Ezekiel 14:8 uses the same phrase.

their blood is upon them: A legal declaration that the executed person, not the court, bears responsibility for the death. It runs through Leviticus 20 as the closing formula on capital cases. 2 Samuel 1:16 and Matthew 27:25 both use the same idiom.

stoning: Leviticus 20:2 and 20:27 assign stoning, and Deuteronomy 17:5-7 gives the procedure. The condemned was taken outside the town gate, at least two witnesses were required, and those witnesses threw first, before the rest of the people, so that a false accuser had to kill with his own hands. Jesus points at that rule at John 8:7. Acts 7:58 shows the witnesses laying their cloaks at the feet of a young man named Saul so they could throw freely.

the kings who did it: Leviticus 20:4-5 anticipates a community that hides its eyes rather than act, and Israel's own record shows it happening at the top. Ahaz sacrificed his son in the fire (2 Kings 16:3) and Manasseh did the same and added sorcery, divination, and mediums (2 Kings 21:6). Josiah finally wrecked the site itself, defiling the Topheth in the Valley of Ben Hinnom so no one could use it again (2 Kings 23:10).

If a man lies with a male: Leviticus 20:13 repeats the wording of 18:22 and adds the sentence: both are put to death, and the chapter's closing formula follows, 'their blood is upon them,' which places responsibility for the death on the condemned rather than on the court. Leviticus 20 is the penalty chapter for the practices listed in chapter 18, and it runs the same way through the other cases it takes up. The Greek Old Testament of this verse is the source of the compound Paul uses at 1 Corinthians 6:9 and 1 Timothy 1:10.

Where Paul's word arsenokoitai comes from: Paul's word arsenokoitai, at 1 Corinthians 6:9 and 1 Timothy 1:10, means men who lie with males. Literally it is male-bedders: men who engage in sexual acts with other males. Koite is the ordinary Greek word for a bed and the standard term for sexual intercourse, which is how the New Testament itself uses it at Romans 13:13 and Hebrews 13:4. The word is a rare compound, and its two halves stand side by side in the Greek Old Testament of these two verses: Leviticus 18:22 reads meta arsenos ou koimethese koiten gynaikos, and Leviticus 20:13 reads hos an koimethe meta arsenos koiten gynaikos, where arsenos and koiten are adjacent.

The chapter assigns penalties for Molech worship and specified sexual and family offenses, and calls Israel to holiness. It distinguishes those who consult mediums, whom God will cut off, from mediums and spiritists themselves, who receive the stated death sentence.

Direct textual basis

Verse 6 says God will cut off the consulter; verse 27 gives a judicial death sentence for the medium or spiritist.

 Deuteronomy 18:9-14: The law forbids consulting mediums, spiritists, and the dead, providing the broader prohibition while Leviticus distinguishes the stated penalties.
1 Samuel 28:7-14: Saul consults a medium who fears being killed under his ban, and the narrative distinguishes the consulting king from the practitioner.
All implemented reference changes

Deuteronomy 18:9-14: The law forbids consulting mediums, spiritists, and the dead, providing the broader prohibition while Leviticus distinguishes the stated penalties.
1 Samuel 28:7-14: Saul consults a medium who fears being killed under his ban, and the narrative distinguishes the consulting king from the practitioner.

Verse 6 addresses the person who turns to mediums and says God will cut that person off. Verse 27 addresses the medium or spiritist and commands stoning. The chapter distinguishes the offenses and their stated penalties.

All implemented note changes

Medium / Necromancer: A medium or spiritist practices consultation of spirits or the dead. Verse 6 says God will cut off the consulter, while verse 27 assigns the stated death penalty to the medium or spiritist.

Context and interpretation: Verse 6 addresses the person who turns to mediums and says God will cut that person off. Verse 27 addresses the medium or spiritist and commands stoning. The chapter distinguishes the offenses and their stated penalties.

Editorial rationale

The current Medium / Necromancer note calls a consulter subject to death and repeats the summary's conflation. Both relevant contextual passages are already linked.

S017
Leviticus 22Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

"All animals" without defect omits an explicit exception in the same chapter.

Original app summary

Moses is told to instruct Aaron and his sons that priests must be ceremonially clean to eat sacred offerings, and that only certain family members may consume them. God also specifies that all animals presented as offerings must be without defect, and gives rules about timing for young animals and thank offerings.

Original translation notes

Profane: To treat something holy as common or ordinary.

Blemish: Leviticus 22:20-25 lists what rules an animal out of an offering: blind, injured, maimed, with a wart, a festering sore or a running sore, and anything with bruised, crushed, torn or cut parts. The same word bars a priest from serving at the altar in 21:17-23, though 21:22 lets him eat the holy food. Malachi 1:8 charges Israel with breaking exactly this rule by bringing the blind and lame animals to the altar.

Sanctify: To set apart as holy; God sanctifies priests and offerings.

when the sun has set: A priest made unclean bathes and waits for sundown, and then may eat the holy food again (22:6-7). The Hebrew day begins at evening, so sunset both ends the uncleanness and starts the new day. This is the rhythm behind Mark 1:32, where the crowds come to Jesus after sunset on the Sabbath.

vow and freewill offering: A neder is a promise already made and now owed; a nedavah is given with no obligation behind it. Leviticus 22:23 lets a slightly deformed animal serve as a freewill offering but not in payment of a vow, because a vow names what will be brought. Deuteronomy 23:21-23 warns against making one lightly.

not on the same day as its young: Leviticus 22:28 forbids slaughtering a parent animal and its offspring on one day, and 22:27 requires a newborn to stay with its mother seven days first. Deuteronomy 22:6-7 gives the matching rule for a bird's nest, releasing the mother before taking the young.

Moses is told to instruct Aaron and his sons that priests must be ceremonially clean to eat sacred offerings, and that only certain family members may consume them. God also specifies that sacrificial animals must be without defect, with a stated exception for certain freewill offerings, and gives rules about timing for young animals and thank offerings.

Direct textual basis

A deformed or stunted limb is permitted for a freewill offering but not fulfillment of a vow.

 Deuteronomy 23:21-23: A freely made vow must be fulfilled, while refraining from a vow incurs no guilt, clarifying the obligation behind a vow offering.
All implemented reference changes

S018
Leviticus 27Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

Crop tithes are incorrectly declared irredeemable; ordinary vow valuations and cherem also need separation for Judges 11 context.

Original app summary

God instructs Moses on how to value dedicated persons, animals, houses, and land if someone makes a vow to the LORD, with specific redemption rules and prohibitions on substitution. Tithes of land produce and livestock are declared holy to the LORD and cannot be redeemed or exchanged.

Original translation notes

Devoted thing (ḥerem): Something irrevocably given over to God, often destroyed; cannot be redeemed.

Tithe: A tenth part dedicated to God, from crops or herds.

gerah: The smallest Hebrew weight unit; twenty gerahs equal one shekel (Exodus 30:13). Approximately half a gram in modern measure.

jubilee: Every 50th year, after seven sabbatical-year cycles (Leviticus 25). Land returned to original families, Hebrew slaves were freed, and debts cancelled; a structural reset of social and economic inequality. The 'year of the Lord's favor' Jesus proclaims in Luke 4:19 draws directly on jubilee language from Isaiah 61.

shekel: Both a weight (about 0.4 oz / 11 g) and a currency unit. In Old Testament economy a common laborer earned roughly half a shekel per month, so the 20 shekels paid for Joseph (Genesis 37:28) was about three years of wages. The temple tax was half a shekel (Exodus 30:13); in New Testament reckoning that equaled two denarii / two days' wages.

the valuations: A fixed price list for redeeming a person vowed to the LORD: fifty shekels for a man aged twenty to sixty, thirty for a woman, twenty and ten for those aged five to twenty, five and three for infants, fifteen and ten for the elderly (27:3-7). At about 11.4 grams a shekel, fifty shekels is roughly 570 grams of silver. Verse 8 lets the priest set a lower figure for anyone who cannot pay.

homer: The largest dry measure, ten ephahs, about 220 liters or a little over six bushels; the word shares its consonants with chamor, a donkey, and a homer is reckoned as a donkey's load. Ezekiel 45:11 fixes the ephah and the bath each at a tenth of it. Leviticus 27:16 prices land by the seed it takes to sow, fifty shekels of silver per homer of barley.

passes under the shepherd's rod: The flock was counted out through a narrow gate while the shepherd touched every tenth animal with his staff to mark it for the tithe (27:32). The owner is forbidden to look them over and pick, or to swap a marked animal for a worse one. Jeremiah 33:13 and Ezekiel 20:37 both use the same picture.

God instructs Moses on how to value dedicated persons, animals, houses, and land if someone makes a vow to the LORD, with specific redemption rules and prohibitions on substitution. Things irrevocably devoted to the LORD (cherem) follow separate rules and cannot be redeemed. Crop tithes may be redeemed by adding a fifth to their value; livestock tithes may not be redeemed or exchanged.

Direct textual basis

Crop tithes can be redeemed with a fifth added. Livestock tithes cannot. Valuation vows and irrevocably devoted things are separate categories.

 Leviticus 5:4-6: A person who realizes guilt from a rash oath must confess and bring an offering for atonement, providing a remedy for rashly sworn words.
Deuteronomy 12:31: Israel must not imitate the nations' worship that burns sons and daughters, providing the Torah's prohibition of child sacrifice.
Judges 11:30-39: Jephthah vows a burnt offering, his only daughter greets him, and the account reports her mourning virginity and his fulfilling the vow.
All implemented reference changes

Judges 11:30-39: Jephthah vows a burnt offering, his only daughter greets him, and the account reports her mourning virginity and his fulfilling the vow.

Verses 2-8 assign monetary valuations for vows involving persons. Verses 28-29 treat cherem, irrevocable devotion, separately; they do not give a father permission to sentence an innocent child to death by a personal vow. Current references already link vows, Jephthah, Hannah, and tithes, but the only note defines a tenth. The proposed note supplies the missing legal distinctions without treating every vow as cherem.

S019
Numbers 17Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

Returning the staff "to the ark" can be read as placing it inside, unlike the placement specified here.

Original app summary

God instructs Moses to collect twelve staffs from the tribal leaders, place them in the tent of meeting, and declares that the staff of his chosen leader will sprout; the next day Aaron's staff has budded, blossomed, and produced almonds. Moses returns Aaron's staff to the ark as a sign to silence the Israelites' complaints, though they respond with fear that they will die for approaching the tabernacle.

Original translation notes

Staff: The same Hebrew word means a staff and a tribe, and the whole chapter turns on that. Twelve matteh are collected, one per matteh, with each leader's name written on it. English has to choose one word and the pun disappears completely. A leader's staff was his badge of office, cut and carried personally, which is what makes each one identifiable in verse 9.

Almonds: The almond is the first tree in the land to flower, blossoming in late January or February while everything else is bare, and its name puns with shoqed, watching or being awake. Jeremiah 1:11-12 uses that pun outright. A staff that goes from bare wood to bud, blossom and ripe fruit overnight compresses a whole season of the earliest-waking tree into one night.

Kept as a sign: The staff is put back in front of the Testimony and stored permanently, in the same place as the jar of manna (Exodus 16:33-34). Hebrews 9:4 lists all three items together: the jar of manna, Aaron's budded staff and the tablets. These were the physical archive of the disputes God had already settled, kept where the disputes had been settled.

We are all lost: The people's reaction in verses 12 and 13 is not repentance but panic about proximity: anyone who comes near the tabernacle dies, so are we all going to die? Chapter 18 opens as the direct answer to that question, assigning the Levites as the buffer who bear the risk on everyone else's behalf. The chapter division cuts a single exchange in half.

Moses returns Aaron's staff to its place before the ark as a sign to silence the Israelites' complaints, though they respond with fear that they will die for approaching the tabernacle.

Direct textual basis

 Hebrews 9:4: Hebrews lists Aaron's budded staff inside the ark, making its wording important alongside Numbers' description of placement before the Testimony.
