S020
Numbers 26Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The surviving-generation claim can be read as applying to every Israelite rather than the men in the first military census.

Original app summary

Moses and Eleazar conduct a census of Israel on the plains of Moab, recording 601,730 men twenty years old or more across the twelve tribes; the LORD instructs that land inheritance be distributed by lot according to population size. The Levites are counted separately at 23,000 and receive no land inheritance; notably, none of the original generation counted in the wilderness remain except Caleb and Joshua.

Original translation notes

601,730: The second count comes to 601,730 against the first count's 603,550, a drop of 1,820 across thirty-eight years, and it counts an entirely different set of men: every one from the first census except Caleb and Joshua has died (26:64-65). Simeon falls hardest, from 59,300 to 22,200, and Manasseh rises most, from 32,200 to 52,700. The purpose is stated in verses 52 to 56: this roll, not the old one, is what the land will be divided by.

Divided by lot: The allotment worked in two steps. The size of each tribe's share was fixed by its number in this census, larger tribes getting more land, and then the lot decided which piece of country each tribe got (26:54-56). Lots were physical objects, stones or marked pieces, drawn or shaken out of a container; Proverbs 16:33 says the lot is cast into the lap, but its every decision is from the LORD.

The line of Korah did not die out: Verse 11 is a single sentence with long consequences. Korah's sons were not swallowed up with him, and their descendants became temple singers and gatekeepers: Heman the singer is traced back through Korah in 1 Chronicles 6:33-37, and eleven psalms carry the heading of the sons of Korah, among them Psalms 42, 44 to 49 and 84.

Clans: The chapter is not a list of individuals but of clans, each named for a founding ancestor, and the counts are given by clan. This was the working unit of Israelite society between the household and the tribe: it held land together, supplied the kinsman-redeemer, and provided the men who answered a muster. Achan is identified through exactly this ladder, tribe then clan then household then man (Joshua 7:16-18).

Moses and Eleazar count 601,730 men of military age and assign land by lot according to tribal size. The Levites are counted separately. Of the men counted in the first military census, only Caleb and Joshua remain.

Direct textual basis

The two censuses count men of military age; Levites are counted separately, beginning at one month old.

 Numbers 1:2-3: The first census registers men twenty or older who can serve in the army, defining the group compared with the later census.
Numbers 14:29-31: The counted adults are sentenced to die in the wilderness except Caleb and Joshua, while their children are promised entry into the land.
All implemented reference changes

Numbers 14:29-32: The LORD declares that the grumbling generation counted from age twenty upward will fall in the wilderness, while expressly excepting Caleb and Joshua from exclusion from the land.
Numbers 1:2-3: The first census registers men twenty or older who can serve in the army, defining the group compared with the later census.
Numbers 14:29-31: The counted adults are sentenced to die in the wilderness except Caleb and Joshua, while their children are promised entry into the land.

The comparison concerns the men registered for military service, not every person alive at the exodus. The Levites have a separate count, and the children of the wilderness generation are the people now preparing to enter the land.

All implemented note changes

Context and interpretation: The comparison concerns the men registered for military service, not every person alive at the exodus. The Levites have a separate count, and the children of the wilderness generation are the people now preparing to enter the land.

Editorial rationale

The current Numbers 1 and 14 references already support the narrower scope. The summary's generation wording needs that explicit limit.

S021
Numbers 30Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

"Always stand" can make a personal vow appear to override prohibitions such as child sacrifice.

Original app summary

Moses conveys laws about vows; a man's vow is binding on him, but a father can void his unmarried daughter's vows and a husband can void his wife's vows if he forbids them when he hears of them, while a widow's or divorced woman's vows always stand.

Original translation notes

Vow and pledge: Two different undertakings. A neder is a positive promise to give or do something, usually an offering. An issar is a binding restriction a person puts on themselves, going without something, which is why verse 13 links it to denying oneself. The Nazirite vow of chapter 6 is both at once. Both were legally enforceable, which is why this chapter exists.

He must not break his word: The Hebrew verb is the one used for profaning something holy. A man's spoken undertaking is treated as a sacred object he can desecrate, which is a stronger idea than breaking a promise. In a world with almost no written contracts, an oath was the enforcement mechanism, and that is why Scripture takes false swearing so seriously.

On the day he hears of it: The window for a father or husband to cancel a vow is a single day, the day he learns of it (30:5,8,12). Silence past that day confirms it permanently. And if he cancels it late, after having already let it stand, verse 15 puts the liability on him: he bears her guilt. The rule protects the woman from being bound to something the household cannot honour, and it stops a man from using the power casually.

A widow or divorced woman: Verse 9 states the exception without qualification: whatever a widow or a divorced woman binds herself to stands. She is answerable to no father and no husband and her word is her own. Anna in Luke 2:36-37 is a widow living permanently at the temple in fasting and prayer, which is the kind of standing commitment this verse leaves open to her.

Moses gives laws on binding vows and the circumstances in which a father or husband may annul a woman's vow. The chapter also states that the vows of widows and divorced women are binding.

Direct textual basis

The chapter makes vows binding and defines when a father or husband may annul them. Other Torah laws provide atonement for rash oaths and forbid child sacrifice.

 Leviticus 5:4-6: A person who realizes guilt from a rash oath must confess and bring an offering for atonement, providing a remedy for rashly sworn words.
Deuteronomy 12:31: Israel must not imitate the nations' worship that burns sons and daughters, providing the Torah's prohibition of child sacrifice.
Judges 11:30-39: Jephthah vows a burnt offering, his only daughter greets him, and the account reports her mourning virginity and his fulfilling the vow.
All implemented reference changes

Judges 11:30-39: Jephthah vows a burnt offering, his only daughter greets him, and the account reports her mourning virginity and his fulfilling the vow.
Leviticus 5:4-6: A person who realizes guilt from a rash oath must confess and bring an offering for atonement, providing a remedy for rashly sworn words.
Deuteronomy 12:31: Israel must not imitate the nations' worship that burns sons and daughters, providing the Torah's prohibition of child sacrifice.

These rules address whether a vow is binding or can be annulled within a household. They do not turn a forbidden act into a lawful one. Leviticus 5:4-6 provides confession and atonement for rash oaths, and Deuteronomy 12:31 condemns child sacrifice.

All implemented note changes

A widow or divorced woman: Verse 9 states that the vows of a widow or divorced woman are binding, without the father-or-husband annulment described elsewhere in the chapter. She is answerable to no father and no husband and her word is her own. Anna in Luke 2:36-37 is a widow living permanently at the temple in fasting and prayer, which is the kind of standing commitment this verse leaves open to her.

Context and interpretation: These rules address whether a vow is binding or can be annulled within a household. They do not turn a forbidden act into a lawful one. Leviticus 5:4-6 provides confession and atonement for rash oaths, and Deuteronomy 12:31 condemns child sacrifice.

Editorial rationale

The current references stress fulfillment and include Jephthah, but omit the rash-oath remedy and child-sacrifice prohibition that prevent this chapter from being read as requiring an unlawful act.

S022
Deuteronomy 10Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The summary places the tablets in the ark before God writes on them on the mountain.

Original app summary

Moses makes two new stone tablets, places them in an acacia ark, and spends forty days on the mountain where God rewrites the Ten Commandments; he then recounts the Levites' assignment to carry the ark, rehearses God's demand for obedience and love, and reminds Israel of their growth from seventy ancestors to a nation as numerous as the stars.

Original translation notes

An ark of acacia wood: An aron is a chest or box, the same word used for Joseph's coffin in Genesis 50:26. Exodus 25:10-16 gives its size as two and a half cubits by one and a half by one and a half, roughly three and three quarter feet long by two and a quarter high and deep, made of acacia, overlaid inside and out with gold, and carried on poles that stayed in its rings. Acacia is the hard desert timber available in Sinai.

Levi has no portion or inheritance: Every other tribe received a block of land. Levi received none, and lived instead on the tithe of the other tribes (Numbers 18:21-24) and in forty-eight towns with their surrounding pasture, scattered through everyone else's territory (Numbers 35:1-8; Joshua 21). Verse 9 states the trade plainly: the LORD is his inheritance.

Circumcise your hearts: Literally the foreskin of your heart. Circumcision on the eighth day was the physical sign of the covenant cut with Abraham, and an uncircumcised male was to be cut off from his people (Genesis 17:10-14). Verse 16 applies the same operation to the inner man, as Deuteronomy 30:6 and Jeremiah 4:4 and 9:25-26 do.

The foreigner: A ger is a resident alien, someone living permanently in Israel who holds no clan land and therefore has no family to fall back on, which is why the law names him alongside the widow and the fatherless. He gleans the corners and the fallen fruit (Leviticus 19:9-10), rests on the sabbath (Exodus 20:10), stands under one law with the native-born (Leviticus 24:22), and may eat the Passover if circumcised (Exodus 12:48).

Seventy in all: The figure comes from the household list in Genesis 46:8-27, repeated in Exodus 1:5. The Greek Septuagint reads seventy-five in both places, and that is the number Stephen gives when he retells the story in Acts 7:14.

Moses makes two new stone tablets, and God rewrites the Ten Commandments on the mountain; Moses then places the tablets in an acacia ark; he then recounts the Levites' assignment to carry the ark, rehearses God's demand for obedience and love, and reminds Israel of their growth from seventy ancestors to a nation as numerous as the stars.

Direct textual basis

Moses carries the new tablets up the mountain and puts them in the ark after returning.

 Exodus 34:1-4: Moses carries the two newly cut tablets up Mount Sinai for God to write on them, confirming that their placement in the ark comes later.
All implemented reference changes

Exodus 34:1-4: Moses carries the two newly cut tablets up Mount Sinai for God to write on them, confirming that their placement in the ark comes later.

All implemented note changes

Context and interpretation: Moses took the two new tablets up the mountain. After the LORD wrote the commandments and gave them back, Moses came down and put them in the ark.

Editorial rationale

The ark note describes its construction. The existing Exodus 34 reference already supports the correct sequence.

S023
Deuteronomy 13Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

"Towns ... burned as an offering" blurs execution by sword with burning the city and its plunder.

Original app summary

Moses instructs Israel to execute any prophet or dreamer who performs signs but urges worship of other gods, and to put to death anyone - including family members - who secretly entices them to worship unknown gods; entire towns that turn to idolatry must be destroyed completely and burned as an offering to the LORD.

Original translation notes

Put to death (capital punishment): The chapter attaches this sentence to one crime, persuading Israel to serve other gods, and it applies it three ways: to a prophet whose sign comes true (13:5), to a family member who does it in private (13:9-10), and to a whole town (13:15). The procedure is not a lynching. Verse 13:14 requires that you inquire, investigate and interrogate thoroughly, and 17:6 fixes the evidence standard at two or three witnesses with never a conviction on one.

Purge the evil from your midst: A fixed closing formula on a completed case, used at 13:5, 17:7, 17:12, 19:19, 21:21, 22:21 and 24:7. The verb baar means to burn away or sweep out. It always follows a verdict and a stated procedure rather than standing on its own as a command to act, and Paul quotes the Greek of it at 1 Corinthians 5:13 when he tells the Corinthians to remove a man from the congregation.

a sign that comes true: Verses 13:1-3 set a test that runs the other way from the one at 18:21-22. There, a prediction that fails marks a false prophet. Here the sign does come about, and the man is still to be rejected, because of what he says next: let us follow other gods. The chapter states the reason plainly at 13:3, that the LORD is testing whether Israel loves Him with all their heart.

the condemned city: Verses 13:12-18 handle a whole town that has turned to other gods. The plunder is gathered in the public square and burned with the city as a whole burnt offering, nothing is taken, and the site is left a permanent ruin never to be rebuilt (13:16). Joshua 6:17-19 applies the same rule to Jericho, and Joshua 7 records Achan taking from the ban there and what followed.

Moses warns against prophets, relatives, or towns that entice Israel to worship other gods. A town's guilt must be thoroughly established; its inhabitants are put to the sword, and the city and its plunder are burned and left in ruins.

Direct textual basis

After thorough investigation, inhabitants are put to the sword; the city and plunder are then burned.

 Deuteronomy 12:31: Israel must not imitate the nations' worship that burns sons and daughters, providing the Torah's prohibition of child sacrifice.
Exodus 22:20: Sacrificing to another god brings a sentence of devotion to destruction, giving the judicial idolatry context alongside Deuteronomy 13.
All implemented reference changes

Exodus 22:20: Sacrificing to another god brings a sentence of devotion to destruction, giving the judicial idolatry context alongside Deuteronomy 13.
Deuteronomy 12:31: Israel must not imitate the nations' worship that burns sons and daughters, providing the Torah's prohibition of child sacrifice.

Verses 14-16 require investigation and distinguish putting inhabitants to the sword from burning the city and its plunder. This is a specified judicial command about an idolatrous town, not permission for an individual to promise a child as a burnt offering.

All implemented note changes

Context and interpretation: Verses 14-16 require investigation and distinguish putting inhabitants to the sword from burning the city and its plunder. This is a specified judicial command about an idolatrous town, not permission for an individual to promise a child as a burnt offering.

S024
Deuteronomy 23Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The summary narrows an uncertain birth-status term to "forbidden marriages" and leaves assembly membership undefined.

Original app summary

Moses lists who cannot enter the LORD's assembly - including the emasculated, those born of forbidden marriages, Ammonites, and Moabites - while permitting Edomites and Egyptians after certain generations. He then instructs the people on camp purity, treatment of refugees, prohibitions on prostitution and interest-charging, proper vow-keeping, and gleaning rights in neighbors' fields.

Original translation notes

Do not hand back a runaway slave: Deuteronomy 23:15-16 forbids returning an escaped slave, orders that he be allowed to settle in whatever town he chooses, and adds 'do not oppress him.' Every other surviving law code of the region runs the opposite way. The laws of Hammurabi of Babylon, carved about 1750 BC and now in the Louvre, make it a capital offence to bring a slave out through the city gates (law 15) or to shelter a runaway and refuse to surrender him when the officer demands it (law 16), and law 17 pays a two-shekel reward to anyone who catches one and brings him back. The Torah offers no reward and orders no return.

The assembly of the LORD: The qahal is Israel gathered as a congregation, not the population at large and not the land. Exclusion at 23:1-8 removed a man from that standing; it did not put him outside Israel. The same passage admits Edomites and Egyptians in the third generation and gives the reasons, Edom is your brother and you lived as a foreigner in Egypt (23:7-8). Isaiah 56:3-5 takes up the eunuch of 23:1 by name and promises him a memorial in God's house better than sons and daughters.

Ammon and Moab: Both nations descend from Lot by his daughters after Sodom, and Genesis 19:37-38 records the names given to the two sons. They held the plateau and the deep valleys east of the Dead Sea, on Israel's approach route from the wilderness. Deuteronomy 23:4 states the charge against them: they met Israel with no food or water, and they hired Balaam of Pethor to curse them (Numbers 22:1-6). Ruth 4:13-17 then has a Moabite woman become great-grandmother to David.

Shrine prostitute: Both words are built on the root for holy, so these were people formally attached to a sanctuary rather than street traders, and 23:17 forbids Israel to supply either. Genesis 38:21-22 uses qedeshah of the woman Judah's friend goes back to look for. 1 Kings 14:24 reports the qadesh present in the land under Rehoboam, and 15:12 has Asa driving them out. Hosea 4:14 charges the men of Israel with sacrificing alongside such women.

The price of a dog: Where English versions of 23:18 read 'male prostitute,' the Hebrew word is dog. The verse refuses both payments as an offering in the house of the LORD for any vow, and calls both detestable to Him. Micah 1:7 uses etnan the same way, of harlot's wages gathered into an idol and burned back out of it.

Interest: The noun for interest is built on the verb to bite, and 23:19 uses both, closing with 'anything that may be lent for interest' in the verb form. The ban covers money, food, and anything else, and it runs toward a brother Israelite; 23:20 permits interest toward a foreigner. Exodus 22:25 and Leviticus 25:36-37 state the same rule, and Leviticus pairs it with a second word, tarbit, increase.

Eating from a neighbor's field: Deuteronomy 23:24-25 lets any passer-by eat his fill of a neighbor's grapes or pull heads of grain by hand, and draws the line at the container and the tool: no basket, no sickle. That marks off a traveler's meal from a harvest. It is the law the disciples are keeping at Matthew 12:1 when they pluck heads of grain crossing the fields, which is why the objection raised against them is about the sabbath and not about theft.

Moses gives restrictions on entry to the LORD's assembly, including people of illegitimate birth, Ammonites, and Moabites, and specifies when Edomite and Egyptian descendants may enter. He also gives laws on camp purity, refugees, lending, vows, and neighboring fields.

Direct textual basis

The app translates the term as illegitimate birth and sets restrictions on entering the LORD's assembly; it does not define a general category of guilt for children.

 Ruth 4:13-17: Ruth marries Boaz and bears Obed, David's grandfather, supplying a family-history passage to read alongside the descent restrictions.
Isaiah 56:3-7: The LORD promises covenant-keeping eunuchs and foreigners a place in His house and accepted worship, supplying context for assembly restrictions.
Acts 8:26-39: Philip explains the good news about Jesus to an Ethiopian eunuch and baptizes him, providing a later account of a eunuch's reception.
All implemented reference changes

Ruth 4:13-17: Ruth marries Boaz and bears Obed, David's grandfather, supplying a family-history passage to read alongside the descent restrictions.
Isaiah 56:3-7: The LORD promises covenant-keeping eunuchs and foreigners a place in His house and accepted worship, supplying context for assembly restrictions.
Acts 8:26-39: Philip explains the good news about Jesus to an Ethiopian eunuch and baptizes him, providing a later account of a eunuch's reception.

The Hebrew birth-status term is rendered differently in English translations, including illegitimate birth or birth from an unlawful union. This legal restriction is not a statement that a child personally committed the parents' sin. Ruth and Isaiah 56 are important context for the chapter's language about descent and belonging.

All implemented note changes

Context and interpretation: The Hebrew birth-status term is rendered differently in English translations, including illegitimate birth or birth from an unlawful union. This legal restriction is not a statement that a child personally committed the parents' sin. Ruth and Isaiah 56 are important context for the chapter's language about descent and belonging.

Translation source notes

Editorial rationale

The current Ruth and Isaiah references are useful and already present, but no note explains the disputed term or the scope of assembly membership. Keep the proposed summary tied to the base translation rather than silently choosing a narrower definition.

S025
Joshua 11Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

"Destroys the Anakites" omits the explicit surviving populations and can imply their total extinction.

Original app summary

Jabin of Hazor rallies multiple northern kings and their armies against Israel; Joshua defeats them at the Waters of Merom, then destroys Hazor and captures all the royal cities, eliminating their kings and populations as the Lord commanded. Joshua conquers the entire region from Mount Halak to Baal Gad, destroys the Anakites, and distributes the land to Israel's tribes.

Original translation notes

The waters of Merom: Verses 5 and 7 put the whole northern coalition in one camp at the waters of Merom, in the uplands of Galilee north-west of the Sea of Galilee. It is one of the few places in that broken country with room to assemble horses and chariots in numbers. Verse 7 says Joshua and his whole army came on them there suddenly.

Hazor: Verse 10 calls Hazor the head of all those kingdoms, and the site bears it out: Tel Hazor in upper Galilee is the largest ancient mound in Israel, roughly two hundred acres with an upper citadel and a walled lower city, sitting on the trade road from Egypt to Mesopotamia. Yigael Yadin excavated it from 1955 to 1958 and found a violent destruction layer with burned mudbrick and smashed statuary. Verse 11 says Joshua burned Hazor with fire, and only Hazor.

Hamstrung their horses: Verses 6 and 9 have Israel cripple the captured horses by cutting the tendon at the back of the hock and burn the chariots rather than keep them. Chariots were the dominant weapon of the coalition and the one thing Israel could have taken over. The order removes the technology from the field instead, which is the same policy Deuteronomy 17:16 sets for Israel's future kings.

Herem (*ban*): Verses 11 to 15 show that the ban was not one blanket rule applied the same way everywhere. Hazor alone is burned. The other towns still standing on their mounds are left standing (verse 13), and verse 14 says Israel took the livestock and the goods of those cities as plunder for themselves. The account insists Joshua left nothing undone of what Moses commanded, and what Moses commanded was this specific about what burns and what is kept.

The Anakim: Verses 21 and 22 clear the Anakim out of Hebron, Debir and Anab in the hill country. These are the tall clans the twelve reported on at Numbers 13:28 and 33, and Deuteronomy 9:2 quotes the proverb about them: who can stand before the sons of Anak? Verse 22 records where the survivors went, to Gaza, Gath and Ashdod, which is the town Goliath of Gath comes from in 1 Samuel 17.

Joshua defeats the northern alliance at Merom, destroys Hazor, and captures its allied cities. He removes the Anakim from Israel's hill country, while some remain in Gaza, Gath, and Ashdod. The chapter closes with the land allotted to Israel and rest from war.

Direct textual basis

Joshua removes the Anakim from Israel's hill country; some remain in Gaza, Gath, and Ashdod.

 Numbers 13:28-33: The spies report fortified cities and descendants of Anak, identifying the people discussed in Joshua's later conquest account.
1 Samuel 17:4: Goliath comes from Gath, one of the cities named in Joshua 11's qualification about surviving Anakim.
Joshua 13:1-6: The LORD tells the aged Joshua that much territory remains unpossessed, including Philistine cities, qualifying an unrestricted reading of conquest summaries.
All implemented reference changes

Verse 22 explicitly leaves Anakim in Gaza, Gath, and Ashdod. The conquest summary must be read alongside that limit and the later account of territory still remaining, rather than as the extinction of every Anakite.

The current Anakim note already names the surviving cities, but the summary drops the qualification. Joshua 13 also prevents an unqualified reading of the whole-land formula.

S026
Joshua 19Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The remaining tribes are all called northern, although Simeon is inside southern Judah.

Original app summary

The remaining northern tribes receive their allotted territories: Simeon within Judah's borders, followed by Zebulun, Issachar, Asher, Naphtali, and Dan, with specified town lists and boundaries for each. Joshua is then given Timnath Serah in the hill country of Ephraim, completing the land division overseen by Eleazar, Joshua, and the tribal leaders at Shiloh.

Original translation notes

Simeon inside Judah: Verses 1 and 9 give Simeon no block of its own. Its towns sit inside Judah's southern territory because Judah's share had been drawn too large, and the tribe is steadily absorbed; by the time of the kings Simeon has almost stopped appearing as a separate people. Jacob had said of Simeon and Levi that he would divide them in Jacob and scatter them in Israel (Genesis 49:7), and both tribes end up living inside other men's land.

Gath-hepher: Verse 13 puts this village on Zebulun's border in the hills of lower Galilee, a few miles north-east of Nazareth. It is the home town of the prophet Jonah son of Amittai, named as such at 2 Kings 14:25, where he foretells Jeroboam II restoring Israel's northern border. The man sent to Nineveh came from a hill village in Galilee.

Mount Tabor: Verse 22 sets Tabor on Issachar's border, at the point where Issachar, Zebulun and Naphtali meet. It is a bare dome of a hill rising about 1,886 feet straight out of the flat Jezreel plain, steep on every side and visible from a long way off. Barak musters ten thousand men on it and comes down off it into Sisera's chariots (Judges 4:6-14), which is the whole of the tactic: chariots cannot climb it and cannot fight on the slope.

Dan moves north to Leshem: Verses 40 to 46 give Dan a small allotment on the coastal plain, squeezed between Judah, Ephraim and the Philistine towns of Ekron and Timnah, and verse 47 records that the tribe lost it and went north instead. They took Leshem by the sword and renamed it Dan after their ancestor. Judges 18 tells the same migration at length and calls the town Laish; it sits at the far north of the country over the largest spring feeding the Jordan, and from then on the phrase from Dan to Beersheba means the whole land.

Timnath-serah: Verses 49 and 50 give Joshua his portion last, after every tribe has been served, and he asks for one particular town in the hill country of Ephraim instead of drawing it by lot. Verse 50 says he had to rebuild it before he could live in it. He is buried there at the end of the book (24:30).

The remaining tribes receive their allotted territories: Simeon within Judah's borders, followed by Zebulun, Issachar, Asher, Naphtali, and Dan, with specified town lists and boundaries for each. Joshua is then given Timnath Serah in the hill country of Ephraim, completing the land division overseen by Eleazar, Joshua, and the tribal leaders at Shiloh.

Direct textual basis

Simeon's towns lie within Judah's allotment; the chapter also assigns the other remaining tribes.

 Joshua 15:20-32: Judah's southernmost towns are listed in the Negev, establishing the southern geography of the territory within which Simeon receives towns.
All implemented reference changes

S027
Judges 2Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The Judge note categorically says a shofet was not a courtroom official, even though the book expressly describes Deborah receiving Israel for judgment.

Original app summary

The angel of the LORD rebukes Israel at Bokim for breaking covenant and disobeying his commands; after Joshua's death, a new generation abandons the LORD for foreign gods, prompting him to give them over to raiders and establish a cycle where judges save them temporarily but they return to idolatry after each judge dies.

Original translation notes

Baal / Ashtaroth: Baal means lord or master, and he was the Canaanite storm god, held to send the rain that every harvest depended on; the plural in verse 11, the Baals, reflects the fact that each locality had its own. His worship ran on the farming year, and his prophets on Carmel cut themselves with swords and lances until the blood ran (1 Kings 18:28). Ashtoreth, plural Ashtaroth, was the goddess set beside him, named at 1 Kings 11:5 as the goddess of the Sidonians and served with sexual rites; the Philistines put Saul's armour in her temple (1 Samuel 31:10). Verse 13 says Israel forsook the LORD and served both.

Angel of the LORD: Malakh means one who is sent, a messenger, and the word is used of human couriers as readily as of heavenly ones; the Greek angelos behind the English angel means the same thing. In verse 1 the messenger comes up from Gilgal to Bochim and speaks in the first person as God, saying I brought you up out of Egypt and I will never break My covenant with you. The same figure appears to Gideon (6:11-22) and to Manoah and his wife (13:3-21), and in each case the person addressed reacts as though he has met God Himself.

Bochim: The place gets its name in verse 5 from what happened there, which is how a great many places in Scripture are named. Israel hears the charge, weeps, and sacrifices, and the site carries the word for weeping from then on. The same pattern names Bethel, Beersheba, Massah, Meribah, and the Valley of Achor.

Judge: A shofet was not a courtroom official. The word covers ruling and setting things right, and in this book it names a deliverer raised up for a particular crisis who then led that part of Israel for the rest of his life. None of them was elected, none inherited the office, and none ruled the whole nation. The book covers roughly three centuries between Joshua and Samuel.

Played the harlot after other gods: Verse 17 does not say Israel experimented with other religions; it uses zanah, the ordinary verb for prostitution, and applies it to the nation. The image runs right through the prophets, most of all in Hosea, because the covenant at Sinai was framed as a marriage bond with exclusive claims. The word is chosen to say that this is betrayal of a person, not a change of opinion.

The angel of the LORD rebukes Israel at Bokim for breaking covenant and disobeying his commands; after Joshua's death, a new generation abandons the LORD for foreign gods, prompting him to give them over to raiders and establish a cycle where judges save them temporarily but they return to idolatry after each judge dies.

Direct textual basis

Judges 2 presents judges as deliverers; Judges 4:4-5 gives Deborah judicial authority and says Israelites came to her for judgment. The role includes more than one function.

Judges 2:16-19: The LORD raises judges to deliver Israel from its enemies, but the people return to other gods after each judge dies, establishing the book's recurring pattern.

 Judges 4:4-5: Deborah is both a prophet and a judge, and Israelites come to her for judgment, showing that the judges' role includes judicial decisions.
Deuteronomy 16:18-20: Israel must appoint judges who judge fairly, reject bribes, and pursue justice, establishing the judicial function associated with the title.
All implemented reference changes

Judges 4:4-5: Deborah is both a prophet and a judge, and Israelites come to her for judgment, showing that the judges' role includes judicial decisions.
Deuteronomy 16:18-20: Israel must appoint judges who judge fairly, reject bribes, and pursue justice, establishing the judicial function associated with the title.

The Hebrew shofet can describe a ruler or someone who gives judgment. In Judges, God raises deliverers against Israel's oppressors, but their role is not limited to warfare. Deborah is both a prophet and a judge; Israelites come to her for judgment (4:4-5). The title therefore includes judicial authority as well as leadership and deliverance.

All implemented note changes

Judge: The Hebrew shofet can describe a ruler or someone who gives judgment. In Judges, God raises deliverers against Israel's oppressors, but their role is not limited to warfare. Deborah is both a prophet and a judge; Israelites come to her for judgment (4:4-5). The title therefore includes judicial authority as well as leadership and deliverance.

Editorial rationale

The takeaway can remain. Replace the note's exclusion with: 'In Judges the title can include deliverance, leadership, and judicial decisions; Deborah is both prophet and judge.' Do not turn the military role of some judges into a denial of Deborah's judicial authority.

S028
Judges 8Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The summary blurs Gideon with the object he made. The Ephod note also identifies Gideon's artifact too precisely by importing the high-priestly garment's form and the entire captured-gold weight.

Original app summary

Gideon appeases angry Ephraimites, pursues and defeats the Midianite kings Zebah and Zalmunna, and punishes Sukkoth and Peniel for refusing aid; he refuses kingship but collects gold that becomes an idol in Ophrah. After Gideon dies, Israel abandons the LORD and forgets his deliverance.

Original translation notes

The gleaning of Ephraim: Gideon answers an armed complaint with a vine-grower's proverb in verse 2. The olelot are the small clusters left on the vines after the main picking, which the law reserved for the poor and the foreigner and forbade the owner to go back for (Leviticus 19:9-10, Deuteronomy 24:21). He is saying that Ephraim's leftovers beat his own whole harvest, and verse 3 says the flattery worked.

Succoth and Penuel: Both towns stand on the Jabbok in the Jordan valley east of the river, and Jacob named both of them: Peniel where he wrestled through the night and said he had seen God face to face, and Succoth where he built shelters for his livestock (Genesis 32:30, 33:17). They refuse bread to three hundred exhausted men because they expect Gideon to lose and do not want Midianite reprisals afterwards. Verses 16 and 17 record what he does to them on the way back.

Crescent ornaments: Verses 21 and 26 have Gideon strip crescent-shaped pendants from the necks of the kings' camels, along with chains and pendants. Saharon means little moon, and the crescent was one of the commonest ornaments and amulets in that world, worn by people and hung on valuable animals. Isaiah 3:18 lists the same item among the finery stripped from the women of Jerusalem.

Seventeen hundred shekels of gold: Verse 26 weighs the earrings alone at 1,700 shekels. A shekel is a weight, not a coin, about two-fifths of an ounce, so this is roughly forty-three pounds of gold, and it does not count the crescents, pendants, purple garments and camel chains listed with it. The purple is worth noting on its own: that dye came from a sea snail harvested on the Phoenician coast and was among the most costly substances anyone traded in.

Ephod: The ephod of the law is a garment, not an image: a sleeveless vestment of gold, blue, purple and scarlet worn by the high priest over his robe, with two shoulder pieces and a woven band, and the breastpiece with the Urim and Thummim fastened to the front of it (Exodus 28:6-30). Because the means of enquiring of God hung on it, bring the ephod came to mean ask God a question (1 Samuel 23:9-12). Verse 27 has Gideon make one out of forty-three pounds of captured gold and set it up in his home town, and Israel prostitutes itself to it there.

Gideon appeases Ephraim, defeats the Midianite kings Zebah and Zalmunna, and punishes Succoth and Penuel for refusing aid. He refuses kingship but makes a gold ephod and places it in Ophrah; Israel worships the ephod, which becomes a snare to Gideon and his household. After his death, Israel turns to the Baals and forgets the LORD's deliverance.

Direct textual basis

Judges 8:26 records 1,700 shekels of earrings; 8:27 says Gideon made an ephod, set it in Ophrah, and Israel went astray after it. The ephod became a snare to Gideon and his household.

Judges 8:23-27: Gideon refuses rule, collects gold earrings, and makes an ephod that Israel worships; the object becomes a snare to Gideon and his household.

 Exodus 28:6-30: The prescribed priestly ephod is a woven garment associated with Aaron's breastpiece of judgment, providing the priestly background for the object named in Gideon's story.
Judges 17:5: Micah makes an ephod and household idols for his private shrine and appoints his son as priest, another use of an ephod within the book's accounts of unauthorized worship.
Judges 18:17-20: The Danites take Micah's ephod along with his images, and his priest gladly joins them, continuing the story of religious objects diverted into private and tribal worship.
All implemented reference changes

Exodus 28:6-30: The prescribed priestly ephod is a woven garment associated with Aaron's breastpiece of judgment, providing the priestly background for the object named in Gideon's story.
Judges 9:1-6: Abimelech massacres his brothers, Jotham escapes, and the men of Shechem make Abimelech king, beginning the aftermath of Gideon's household.

Judges 8:27 uses the same word for the object Gideon made and placed in Ophrah, but does not describe its exact shape or say whether it was wearable. Verse 26 weighs the collected earrings at 1,700 shekels; the account does not explain how that gold was incorporated. Israel's idolatrous attachment is to the ephod. All implemented note changes

Ephod: Exodus 28 describes the high priest's ephod as a garment associated with the breastpiece of judgment. Judges 8:27 uses the same word for the object Gideon made and placed in Ophrah, but does not describe its exact shape or say whether it was wearable. Verse 26 weighs the collected earrings at 1,700 shekels; the account does not explain how that gold was incorporated. Israel's idolatrous attachment is to the ephod. It becomes a snare to Gideon and his household; Gideon himself is not described as becoming an idol. The prescribed garment uses gold, blue, purple, and scarlet, with shoulder pieces and a woven band; the breastpiece containing the Urim and Thummim is fastened to it (Exodus 28:6-30). In 1 Samuel 23:9-12, David calls for the ephod when inquiring of the LORD.

Editorial rationale

Distinguish the priestly garment prescribed in Exodus from Gideon's artifact. Judges names it an ephod and describes its idolatrous use, but does not explain its exact shape, whether it was wearable, or how all the gold was incorporated. Do not make Gideon himself the idol.

S029
Judges 9Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The summary attributes Abimelech's death only to the woman's millstone. The note 'every rival claimant killed' also loses Jotham's explicit escape.

Original app summary

Abimelek murders his seventy brothers to become king of Shechem; Jotham, the sole survivor, pronounces a curse on both Abimelek and the city. After three years, conflict erupts between Abimelek and Shechem; Abimelek destroys the city and its tower, then dies when a woman drops a millstone on his head at Thebez.

Original translation notes

Seventy brothers on one stone: Verse 5 is a dynastic massacre: every rival claimant killed together, on one stone, in one act. Abimelech's own name means my father is king, which is what Gideon had refused to be (8:23), and his claim rests on his mother's family at Shechem rather than his father's house at Ophrah. Jehu does the same thing to Ahab's seventy sons at Samaria, and has their heads sent to him in baskets (2 Kings 10:1-8).

Baal-berith: The god of Shechem, named at 8:33 and here in verses 4 and 46, where the same shrine is called the temple of El-berith. Two details show what such a temple was for besides worship: verse 4 has Abimelech drawn seventy shekels of silver out of its treasury to hire men, so it held the town's money, and verse 46 has the townspeople take refuge in its stronghold, so it was also the strongest building they had. Shechem is the town where Joshua had made Israel swear its covenant to the LORD (Joshua 24:25).

Jotham's trees: The fable in verses 8 to 15 is built out of the four plants everyone listening farmed. The olive, the fig and the vine were the crops a hill farm lived on, slow to grow, worth years of care, and all three are made to say they will not leave their own work to sway over the trees. The atad is a scrubby thorn that gives no fruit and no shade and burns fiercely once alight, which is why the last line of the fable is about fire coming out of it.

Mount Gerizim: Shechem sits in the pass between two mountains: Gerizim on the south, about 2,890 feet, and Ebal on the north, about 3,084 feet. Moses assigned the blessings to Gerizim and the curses to Ebal, and Joshua read the whole law between them (Deuteronomy 11:29, Joshua 8:30-35). Jotham climbs the blessing mountain in verse 7 and shouts a curse down into the pass.

An upper millstone: Household grain was ground between two stones, a fixed lower one and a shaped upper one that a woman worked back and forth with both hands. It is a solid block of basalt, and dropped from a tower it is a killing weight. Verse 53 is remembered well enough that Joab quotes it as a caution about approaching walls generations later (2 Samuel 11:21).

Abimelech murders his brothers to become king of Shechem; Jotham escapes and pronounces a curse on Abimelech and the city. After three years, conflict erupts and Abimelech destroys Shechem and its tower. At Thebez a woman crushes his skull with a millstone; at his request, his armor-bearer then kills him with a sword.

Direct textual basis

The woman crushes Abimelech's skull, and his armor-bearer kills him with a sword at his request (9:53-54). Jotham escapes the massacre in 9:5.

Judges 9:5: Abimelech murders his brothers at Ophrah, but Jotham survives by hiding, identifying an explicit survivor of the massacre.
Judges 9:53-57: A woman crushes Abimelech's skull, and his armor-bearer then kills him at his request; the narrator presents the outcome as repayment for wickedness and fulfillment of Jotham's curse.

 2 Samuel 11:21: Abimelech's death at Thebez from a woman's upper millstone is recalled as a warning about approaching a wall, preserving the memory of the woman's decisive blow.
All implemented reference changes

2 Samuel 11:21: Abimelech's death at Thebez from a woman's upper millstone is recalled as a warning about approaching a wall, preserving the memory of the woman's decisive blow.

Jotham escapes the massacre of Gideon's sons (9:5). At Thebez, a woman's millstone crushes Abimelech's skull, and his armor-bearer then kills him with a sword at his request (9:53-54). Both the woman's blow and the armor-bearer's action belong to the account of his death.

All implemented note changes

Seventy brothers on one stone: Verse 5 describes a dynastic massacre of Abimelech's brothers on one stone, but explicitly says that Jotham, the youngest, escaped. Abimelech's own name means my father is king, which is what Gideon had refused to be (8:23), and his claim rests on his mother's family at Shechem rather than his father's house at Ophrah. Jehu does the same thing to Ahab's seventy sons at Samaria, and has their heads sent to him in baskets (2 Kings 10:1-8).

Context and interpretation: Jotham escapes the massacre of Gideon's sons (9:5). At Thebez, a woman's millstone crushes Abimelech's skull, and his armor-bearer then kills him with a sword at his request (9:53-54). Both the woman's blow and the armor-bearer's action belong to the account of his death.

Editorial rationale

Keep both actions in the death sequence. Preserve Jotham as the surviving brother rather than treating every potential rival as dead.

S030
Judges 10Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The summary rounds two stated reigns to two decades and makes eighteen years sound like the combined duration of all oppression. The first Ammonites note reverses the Gilead/oppression/river-crossing sequence. An existing note also equated Milcom and Molech without labeling that identification; it now states what 1 Kings 11:5,7 and 2 Kings 23:10 actually name.

Original app summary

Tola and Jair each judge Israel for two decades before the people turn to foreign gods, prompting God to let the Philistines and Ammonites oppress them for eighteen years; after Israel repents and removes idols, the Ammonites gather for war and the Gileadites assemble at Mizpah, promising leadership to whoever attacks first.

Original translation notes

The Ammonites: Ammon descended from Lot through his younger daughter, which is what Genesis 19:38 records, so Israel and Ammon were reckoned distant kin. Their country lay east of the Jordan on the desert edge, with its capital at Rabbah, the site of modern Amman, and their god was Milcom, also called Molech, whose worship took children in fire (1 Kings 11:5-7, 2 Kings 23:10). Verses 7 to 9 have them cross the river and press Gilead for eighteen years.

Mizpah of Gilead: Verse 17 gathers Israel at Mizpah, and the name means lookout or watchpost, from the verb to keep watch. This is the Gileadite Mizpah east of the Jordan, where Jephthah is made head in 11:11 and where he comes home in 11:34, and it is not the Mizpah in Benjamin where Samuel later gathers the nation (1 Samuel 7:5-6). Genesis 31:49 attaches the same name to the heap of stones Jacob and Laban set up as a boundary, for the reason that the LORD would watch between them.

The gods of seven nations: Verse 6 lists them one by one: the Baals and the Ashtaroth of Canaan, the gods of Aram, of Sidon, of Moab, of Ammon, and of the Philistines. Each name is a real cult with its own rites. Baal was the storm god who was believed to give rain, Ashtoreth the goddess served with sexual rites, Chemosh of Moab and Milcom of Ammon both took children in fire, and Dagon of the Philistines was a god of grain. Israel is not described as vaguely drifting; it is described taking up all of them.

Gilead: The highland east of the Jordan between the Yarmuk and the Arnon, wooded, well watered, and good for flocks; it was known for balm (Jeremiah 8:22). Reuben, Gad, and half of Manasseh settled there, which left them outside the Jordan and first in line when Ammon pushed west. Verse 8 has the Ammonite pressure crush Gilead for eighteen years before it crosses the river.

Tola judges Israel for twenty-three years and Jair for twenty-two. Israel again serves foreign gods, and the LORD gives them over to Philistine and Ammonite oppression; the Ammonites oppress the Israelites in Gilead for eighteen years. Israel repents and removes its idols, and Gilead's leaders promise leadership to whoever begins the fight against Ammon.

Direct textual basis

Tola judges twenty-three years and Jair twenty-two (10:2-3). Verse 8 locates eighteen years of Ammonite oppression among Israelites east of the Jordan in Gilead; verse 9 then describes attacks across the Jordan.

Judges 10:2-9: Tola judges for twenty-three years and Jair for twenty-two; the account locates eighteen years of oppression in Gilead and also reports Ammonite attacks across the Jordan.
Judges 10:16-18: Israel removes its foreign gods, and Gilead's leaders promise leadership to whoever begins the attack against Ammon, setting the immediate background for Jephthah's appointment.

 No additional contextual reference needed.

The chapter gives Tola twenty-three years and Jair twenty-two (10:2-3). It names both Philistine and Ammonite oppressors, but the eighteen years in verse 8 refer to the oppression of Israelites east of the Jordan in Gilead. Verse 9 then reports the Ammonites crossing the Jordan to attack Judah, Benjamin, and Ephraim.

All implemented note changes

The Ammonites: Ammon descended from Lot through his younger daughter, which is what Genesis 19:38 records, so Israel and Ammon were reckoned distant kin. Their country lay east of the Jordan on the desert edge, with its capital at Rabbah, the site of modern Amman. 1 Kings 11:5 names Milcom as the Ammonite god; 11:7 and 2 Kings 23:10 name Molech, with the latter passage condemning the burning of children as offerings. Verse 8 describes eighteen years of oppression in Gilead east of the Jordan; verse 9 then reports the Ammonites crossing the river to attack Judah, Benjamin, and Ephraim.

The gods listed in verse 6: Verse 6 lists the Baals and the Ashtaroth, then the gods of Aram, Sidon, Moab, Ammon, and the Philistines. It reports Israel serving these gods and forsaking the LORD. It does not name each nation's god in this verse or describe the rites practiced. Names and practices found in other passages need to be identified from those passages, rather than supplied as details stated here.

Context and interpretation: The chapter gives Tola twenty-three years and Jair twenty-two (10:2-3). It names both Philistine and Ammonite oppressors, but the eighteen years in verse 8 refer to the oppression of Israelites east of the Jordan in Gilead. Verse 9 then reports the Ammonites crossing the Jordan to attack Judah, Benjamin, and Ephraim.

Editorial rationale

Keep the Philistine and Ammonite enemies distinct and limit the stated eighteen years to the oppression specified in verse 8. Revise the first Ammonites note so it agrees with the Gilead note and the chapter's sequence.

S031
Judges 11Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The vow note calls spoken vows absolutely binding, which can imply that a vow requires an unlawful act. The revision should preserve the vow's burnt-offering language and distinguish the narrative's report from divine approval, without giving the dedication reading equal textual weight.

Original app summary

Gilead's elders recall Jephthah, the outcast son of a prostitute, to command them against Ammon, and he answers the Ammonite king's land claim by recounting how the LORD gave Israel the land of Sihon. He vows to offer up whatever first comes out of his house if the LORD grants victory; the LORD delivers Ammon and his only daughter comes out to meet him, and after two months mourning her virginity he does to her as he vowed, so Israel's daughters go out each year to lament her.

Original translation notes

Vow: A neder is a promise made to God, usually offering something in return for help, and the law treats it as absolutely binding once spoken: Numbers 30:2 says a man shall not break his word, Deuteronomy 23:21-23 says it is no sin not to vow but delay in paying is charged against you, and Ecclesiastes 5:4-5 repeats it. The law also provided a way out that Jephthah does not take. Leviticus 27:1-8 sets a fixed scale of silver by which a person vowed to the LORD is redeemed instead of given: fifty shekels for a man between twenty and sixty, thirty for a woman, ten for a girl between five and twenty, with reduced rates for the very young and the old.

Driven out with no inheritance: Verses 1 to 3 turn on inheritance law. Jephthah is Gilead's son by a prostitute, and his half-brothers by the wife expel him with the words you shall have no inheritance in our father's house. Land passed to the sons of the household, and a son with no standing in it had nothing to hold and nowhere to go, so he leaves for Tob, north-east of Gilead, and worthless men gather to him. The same elders who let that happen come looking for him to command their army in verse 6.

Chemosh: Verse 24 has Jephthah name Chemosh in his message about disputed land. Chemosh was the national god of Moab; Numbers 21:29 calls the Moabites the people of Chemosh, Solomon built him a high place east of Jerusalem (1 Kings 11:7), and 2 Kings 3:27 records a king of Moab burning his own heir on the wall. King Mesha's inscription, found at Dhiban in 1868, credits Chemosh with his victories over Israel in his own words.

Four days each year: Verses 39 and 40 close with a yearly practice: the daughters of Israel went out four days every year on account of Jephthah's daughter. Israel kept the memory of what a vow cost by setting aside a fixed period for it, the same way the nation kept fasts and feasts. The book records the custom without excusing the vow that caused it.

Gilead makes Jephthah its leader, and he disputes Ammon's claim to Israel's land. Before fighting, he vows to offer as a burnt offering whatever comes out of his house to meet him if the LORD grants victory. He defeats Ammon, but his only child, his daughter, comes out to greet him. After two months mourning her virginity, she returns, and he does to her as he vowed. The narrator adds that she had never known a man. Israel's daughters lament her for four days each year.

Direct textual basis

Judges 11:31 pledges an olah, a burnt offering. Verse 39 says Jephthah did to his daughter as he had vowed. Verse 34 identifies her as his only child; verses 37-39 emphasize her virginity. The Spirit comes upon Jephthah before the vow (11:29-30). The Torah condemns child sacrifice.

Judges 11:30-40: Jephthah promises a burnt offering for victory; his only child greets him, and after two months mourning her virginity she returns and he fulfills the vow, followed by Israel's annual lament.
Judges 11:29-30: The Spirit of the LORD comes upon Jephthah before he makes his vow, placing the divine empowerment before the sworn promise rather than presenting it as a response to that promise.

 Deuteronomy 12:31: Israel must not worship the LORD through practices such as burning sons and daughters as sacrifices, explicitly prohibiting the kind of act a personal vow might be invoked to excuse.
Deuteronomy 18:10: No one in Israel may sacrifice a son or daughter in the fire, establishing a prohibition that Jephthah's vow cannot override.
Leviticus 18:21: Giving children as sacrifices to Molech is forbidden as profaning God's name, another explicit Torah prohibition of child sacrifice.
Leviticus 5:4-6: Guilt from a thoughtless oath calls for confession and a sin offering through which the priest makes atonement, providing the Torah's response to sinful rash sworn speech.
Leviticus 27:1-8: Special vows involving persons carry monetary valuations, and a priest adjusts the amount for someone too poor to pay, distinguishing these rules from instructions to sacrifice a person.
Leviticus 27:28-29: Devoted things cannot be sold or redeemed, and a person devoted to destruction cannot be ransomed but must be put to death; this concerns cherem, a distinct category from the vow recorded in Judges 11.
Genesis 22:1-14: Abraham is stopped before killing Isaac and offers a ram in his place, contrasting God's intervention here with the vow and its reported fulfillment in Judges 11.
Leviticus 1:3-9: A burnt offering from the herd is slaughtered, cut into pieces, and burned on the altar, establishing the sacrificial meaning of the offering named in Jephthah's vow.
All implemented reference changes

Olah in verse 31 means a burnt offering. Verse 39 reports that Jephthah did to his daughter as he had vowed; together, these statements strongly support literal sacrifice, although the killing is not separately described. Her virginity is consistent with death before marriage and motherhood; she was his only child. The lifelong-dedication reading is a less direct alternative: some interpreters read the two clauses in verse 31 as dedication or sacrifice, but that distinction is disputed, and verse 39 does not state a subsequent life of celibacy. The Torah condemns child sacrifice (Deuteronomy 12:31; 18:10; Leviticus 18:21). A vow cannot authorize it, and recording an act does not establish God's approval. The Spirit comes upon Jephthah before his vow (11:29-30). Leviticus 5:4-6 provides confession and atonement for sinful rash oaths. Leviticus 27:1-8 concerns monetary valuations; cherem in 27:28-29 is distinct and does not authorize a father to kill an innocent daughter through a personal vow.

Editorial rationale

Retain the burnt-offering vow and its reported fulfillment in the summary. Explain in the note that literal sacrifice is the stronger reading; do not imply equal textual support for dedication or infer obedience from the Torah's prohibition. The virginity clause does not establish continued celibate service. Separate Jephthah's act from divine authorization, and keep ordinary vow valuations distinct from cherem.

S032
Judges 12Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The summary attributes all 42,000 deaths to the pronunciation test. The Shibboleth note repeats the same overstatement and must be revised with the summary.

Original app summary

Ephraim confronts Jephthah over not being called to fight the Ammonites; Jephthah defeats them at the Jordan, using the word "Shibboleth" to identify and kill forty-two thousand Ephraimites. Jephthah leads Israel six years before dying, followed by judges Ibzan, Elon, and Abdon.

Original translation notes

Shibboleth: The word itself means an ear of grain, or in other passages a flowing stream, and its meaning is beside the point; it was chosen because it starts with the sh sound that the Ephraimite dialect across the river did not use. A man could claim any tribe he liked, but he could not change how his mouth had been trained to speak since childhood. Verse 6 records the toll: forty-two thousand Ephraimites killed at the crossings, on the evidence of one syllable.

The fords of the Jordan: The Jordan runs in a deep, thicketed trench and can only be crossed at a handful of shallow places. Whoever holds the fords controls who gets across, which is why they are seized three separate times in this book (Judges 3:28, 7:24, and here in verse 5). An army cut off from the fords is trapped on the wrong side of the river with nowhere to go.

Thirty daughters married outside the clan: Verse 9 says Ibzan sent thirty daughters out of his clan in marriage and brought thirty wives in from outside for his sons. Marriage was a treaty between households, and daughters going out and wives coming in built a network of obligation across a whole region. Sixty such matches is a record of a man who ruled by alliance, which is why the writer counts them the way he counts years in office.

Riding on donkeys: Verse 14 counts Abdon's forty sons and thirty grandsons riding seventy donkeys, and 10:4 does the same for Jair's thirty sons. This is a note about standing, not transport: before horses were common in Israel the donkey was the mount of a man of rank, and a household that could put seventy men on their own animals was wealthy and settled. Zechariah 9:9 draws on the same picture of a king coming on a donkey.

Ephraim confronts Jephthah over the Ammonite war, and Gilead defeats Ephraim. At the Jordan fords, Gileadite guards use 'Shibboleth' to identify and kill fleeing Ephraimites; the chapter reports forty-two thousand Ephraimites killed at that time. Jephthah judges Israel for six years, followed by Ibzan, Elon, and Abdon.

Direct textual basis

Judges 12:4 describes the battle; 12:5-6 describes the ford killings and then gives the total killed 'at that time'. The text does not isolate 42,000 as a separate count from the pronunciation test alone.

Judges 12:4-6: After describing the battle and the killings at the Jordan fords, the chapter reports forty-two thousand Ephraimites killed at that time, without separate battle and ford totals.

 Judges 8:1-3: An earlier quarrel over exclusion from battle ends when Gideon's reply calms Ephraim, contrasting with the fighting between Ephraim and Gilead in Judges 12.
Proverbs 15:1: A gentle answer turns away anger, while harsh speech stirs it up, providing a wisdom parallel to the contrasting responses to Ephraim in Judges 8 and 12.
All implemented reference changes

Judges 8:1-3: An earlier quarrel over exclusion from battle ends when Gideon's reply calms Ephraim, contrasting with the fighting between Ephraim and Gilead in Judges 12.
Proverbs 15:1: A gentle answer turns away anger, while harsh speech stirs it up, providing a wisdom parallel to the contrasting responses to Ephraim in Judges 8 and 12.

The guards use Shibboleth to identify fleeing Ephraimites at the Jordan crossings. The chapter reports forty-two thousand Ephraimites killed 'at that time' after describing both the battle and the ford killings (12:4-6). It gives no separate battle and ford totals. The summary should retain the reported count without assigning every death to the pronunciation test.

Editorial rationale

Retain the reported total and the sequence of battle and ford killings without claiming a separately stated count for either stage.

S033
Judges 13Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The summary makes Samson the one who will deliver Israel, obscuring 'begin' in the announcement and the unfinished conflict.

Original app summary

An angel of the LORD appears to Manoah's barren wife, promising her a son who will be a Nazirite dedicated to delivering Israel from the Philistines; when Manoah offers a sacrifice, the angel ascends in the flame and the woman gives birth to Samson.

Original translation notes

A Nazirite to God from the womb: From nazar, to separate. Numbers 6:1-21 sets out the vow in full: nothing at all from the grapevine, not wine, not vinegar, not fresh or dried grapes, not even the skins or the seeds; no razor on the head for the whole term; and no going near a dead body, not even a father's or mother's. It was normally taken for a set period and ended at the sanctuary with offerings and the shaving of the head, the hair burned under the fellowship sacrifice. Samson's is imposed for life and begins before he is born.

Zorah, Eshtaol, and Mahaneh-dan: Zorah and Eshtaol are villages in the Sorek valley on the western edge of the hill country, looking straight out onto Philistine territory, which is why this family lives under Philistine rule. Mahaneh-dan means camp of Dan and lies between the two. Dan never took the coastal allotment it was given, because the Amorites forced the tribe back up into the hills (Judges 1:34), and Judges 18:11-12 has six hundred armed Danites camping at this same spot on their way north to take Laish instead.

The angel of the LORD: Malakh means one who is sent, a messenger, and the word is used of human couriers as readily as of heavenly ones. Manoah's wife first reports him as a man of God, the ordinary term for a prophet, and Manoah only draws his conclusion after the figure goes up in the altar flame. His reaction in verse 22, that they will die because they have seen God, rests on the rule stated in Exodus 33:20 and on Jacob's surprise in Genesis 32:30 at surviving the encounter.

Why do you ask my name: The word rendered beyond comprehension is peli, from pala, to be wonderful or out of reach. The chapter then uses the same root twice more: verse 19 says the LORD acted wondrously, maphli laasot, while Manoah and his wife watched. The same root supplies the first title in Isaiah 9:6, pele yoets, Wonderful Counsellor. The answer and the sign are the same word.

A burnt offering and a grain offering on a rock: An olah is the whole burnt offering, the one sacrifice burned entirely with no portion eaten by priest or worshipper; the name means that which goes up. A minchah is a grain offering of flour and oil. Manoah uses a bare rock rather than a built altar, exactly as Gideon did when the angel touched the meat and bread with his staff and fire came up out of the rock (Judges 6:19-21).

The angel of the LORD promises Manoah's childless wife a son, a Nazirite to God from the womb, who will begin to deliver Israel from the Philistines. Manoah offers a sacrifice to the LORD, and the angel ascends in its flame. The woman later gives birth to Samson, whom the LORD blesses.

Direct textual basis

Judges 13:5 says Samson will begin to deliver Israel. It also identifies him as dedicated from the womb, before a personal voluntary vow could have been made.

Judges 13:3-5: The promised child will be a Nazirite from the womb, with no razor touching his head, and will begin Israel's deliverance from the Philistines.
Judges 13:19-25: Manoah offers to the LORD, the angel ascends in the flame, and Samson is later born and blessed as the Spirit begins to stir him, tracing the promise's fulfillment.

 Numbers 6:1-21: Nazirite separation includes uncut hair, restrictions on grape products and corpse contact, and offerings and shaving when the period ends, supplying the legal background for Samson's dedication.
1 Samuel 7:7-14: The LORD answers Samuel against the Philistines, Israel recovers territory, and Philistine attacks cease during Samuel's days, showing a later stage of the deliverance Samson was to begin.
All implemented reference changes

Numbers 6:1-21: Nazirite separation includes uncut hair, restrictions on grape products and corpse contact, and offerings and shaving when the period ends, supplying the legal background for Samson's dedication.
1 Samuel 7:7-14: The LORD answers Samuel against the Philistines, Israel recovers territory, and Philistine attacks cease during Samuel's days, showing a later stage of the deliverance Samson was to begin.

The announcement says Samson will begin to deliver Israel from the Philistines (13:5); it does not promise that he will complete their liberation. His dedication begins in the womb and lasts for life. Numbers 6 also describes voluntary Nazirite vows that end after a stated period with offerings and the shaving of the head. Samson's lifelong calling is therefore distinct from that ordinary pattern.

S034
Judges 14Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The summary says Samson's wife coerces him but omits the Philistines' threat to burn her and her father's household. It also omits the narrator's explanation in verse 4.

Original app summary

Samson sees a Philistine woman in Timnah, insists on marrying her despite his parents' objections, and tears apart a lion with his bare hands on the way there. At his wedding feast he poses a riddle to thirty Philistine men, but when his wife coerces him into revealing the answer, the Spirit of the LORD empowers him to kill thirty men in Ashkelon and give their clothes to the riddle solvers; his wife is then given to another man.

Original translation notes

Riddle: A chidah is a saying deliberately built to be hard, and posing them was a contest of wit, not a children's game; the queen of Sheba comes to test Solomon with them (1 Kings 10:1), and Proverbs 1:6 lists them among the things wisdom teaches a man to read. Samson's, in verse 14, cannot be solved by thinking, because it describes a private incident nobody else saw. The stake in verses 12 and 13 is thirty linen wraps and thirty changes of clothing, a serious wager for thirty village men.

The lion's carcass and the Nazirite rule: Verses 8 and 9 have Samson turn aside to the dead lion, scoop honey out of it with his hands, eat it as he walks, and give some to his parents without telling them where it came from. Numbers 6:6-7 forbids a Nazirite to go near any dead body at all, even a father's or mother's, and touching a carcass makes any Israelite unclean (Leviticus 11:24-28). The chapter reports his silence about it, and expects the reader to know what has been broken.

Timnah: Verse 1 has Samson go down to Timnah, a Philistine town in the Sorek valley a few miles down from his home at Zorah; down is literal, since it is a drop from the hill country toward the coastal plain. It had been allotted to Judah and then to Dan (Joshua 15:10, 19:43) and neither held it. Deuteronomy 7:3 forbids marriage with the peoples of the land, which is exactly what his parents say to him in verse 3.

The seven-day feast: A wedding was not a ceremony but a week of eating and drinking at the bride's town, with the groom's side hosting and thirty local men attached to him as companions (verse 11). Jacob's week for Leah works the same way (Genesis 29:27). The riddle and its wager in verses 12 and 13 are entertainment for the feast, thirty linen garments and thirty changes of clothes being a substantial stake.

Samson insists on marrying a Philistine woman, while the narrator says the LORD is seeking an occasion against Philistine rule. At the wedding feast, the Philistine companions threaten to burn his wife and her father's household unless she obtains the riddle's answer. Under that threat she presses Samson, and he tells her. After the answer is disclosed, the Spirit of the LORD empowers Samson; he kills thirty men in Ashkelon and takes their clothes to pay the wager. His wife is then given to his companion.

Direct textual basis

Judges 14:4 says the LORD was seeking an occasion against Philistine rule. Verse 15 records the death threat against Samson's wife and her family; verses 16-17 then report her pressure on Samson.

Judges 14:3-4: Samson's parents object to his Philistine marriage, while the narrator says the LORD is seeking an occasion against Philistine rule, distinguishing their knowledge from the larger purpose.
Judges 14:12-20: The companions threaten Samson's wife and her father's household to obtain the riddle's answer; she presses him under that threat, and the disclosure leads to Samson's violent payment of the wager.

 Judges 15:6: The Philistines burn Samson's wife and her father to death, showing that the threatened violence in the wedding story later reaches both of them.
All implemented reference changes

Verse 4 separately explains the LORD's purpose in creating an occasion against Philistine rule; that larger purpose does not make every character's conduct a command from God. Leviticus 11:24-28 addresses animal-carcass uncleanness. Numbers 6:6-7 addresses corpse contact for Nazirites and specifically mentions parents and siblings; applying that passage directly to the lion requires interpretation.

Separately, distinguish the lion-carcass uncleanness rule in Leviticus 11 from the human-corpse language and close-relative examples of Numbers 6:6-7 rather than stating their scope as identical.

S035
Judges 15Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The summary says the Philistines kill Samson's wife but omits her father, and it can flatten the escalation into a simple righteous-retaliation chain.

Original app summary

Samson's wife is given to another man, prompting him to burn the Philistines' crops with foxes; when they retaliate by killing his wife, he slaughters many of them and hides in a cave. Judah binds him and hands him over to the Philistines, but the Spirit of the LORD empowers him to break free and kill a thousand men with a donkey's jawbone; God provides water when he nearly dies of thirst.

Original translation notes

Three hundred foxes: One Hebrew word covers both the fox and the jackal, and translators have to choose. Jackals run in packs and scavenge around towns and ruins, which makes taking them in numbers plausible in a way that trapping three hundred solitary foxes is not. Scripture uses the same word for the animals that haunt a ruined city (Lamentations 5:18) and that feed on the bodies of the fallen (Psalm 63:10). Tied tail to tail in pairs, three hundred animals carry a hundred and fifty torches.

The standing grain, the vineyards and olive groves: This is the destruction of an entire year in one night, at the worst possible moment. Wheat was cut with a hand sickle, bound, and stacked in the field before threshing, so verse 5 names both the cut stacks and the grain still standing. The vines and olives are worse: they are not an annual crop but decades of capital, and a young olive tree takes years to come into full bearing.

Lehi, Ramath-lehi, En-hakkore: All three names are made in the chapter itself. Lehi means jawbone, Ramath-lehi means the height of the jawbone, and En-hakkore means the spring of the one who called. The pattern of naming a place after what happened there runs through the whole Old Testament, and it is how a landscape kept its history when almost nobody could read.

The ropes became like burnt flax: Cord was spun from flax fibre or palm fibre and laid up into rope, and the text stresses that these two were new, at full strength. Flax that has been through a fire keeps its shape until it is touched and then falls apart. The verb used of the Spirit here and in Judges 14:6 and 14:19 is tsalach, to rush or break in on someone, not a gentle arrival.

The uncircumcised: Circumcision on the eighth day was the physical sign of the covenant made with Abraham (Genesis 17:10-14). It was not unique to Israel; Jeremiah 9:25-26 lists Egypt, Edom, Ammon, Moab, and the desert peoples alongside Judah as circumcised in the flesh. The Philistines, who had come to the coast from across the sea, did not practise it, which is why the word becomes the standing insult for them specifically, in Judges 14:3 and in David's question at the Valley of Elah in 1 Samuel 17:26.

Samson finds that his wife has been given to his companion and burns the Philistines' crops. The Philistines burn his wife and her father, and Samson retaliates with a great slaughter. Judah binds him and hands him over, but the Spirit of the LORD empowers him to break free and kill a thousand men with a donkey's jawbone. When he cries out in thirst, God provides water.

Direct textual basis

Judges 15:6 says the Philistines burn both the woman and her father. Verses 3, 7, and 11 put competing justifications for revenge in the characters' own words; verses 14 and 19 separately report divine empowerment and provision.

Judges 15:1-8: Samson burns the Philistines' crops after finding his wife given to another man; they burn both her and her father, and he answers with further slaughter.
Judges 15:11-19: Judah hands Samson over, the Spirit empowers him against the Philistines, and God supplies water after his plea, distinguishing the reported divine help from the characters' claims about retaliation.

 Deuteronomy 32:35: The LORD claims vengeance and repayment as His own, providing a point of comparison for Samson's personal declarations of retaliation.
Exodus 22:6: The person whose fire consumes stacked or standing grain or a field must make restitution, supplying legal background for the destruction of the Philistines' crops.
All implemented reference changes

Deuteronomy 32:35: The LORD claims vengeance and repayment as His own, providing a point of comparison for Samson's personal declarations of retaliation.
Exodus 22:6: The person whose fire consumes stacked or standing grain or a field must make restitution, supplying legal background for the destruction of the Philistines' crops.

The Philistines burn both Samson's wife and her father (15:6). Samson and the men of Judah give their own explanations for the cycle of retaliation, while the narrator separately reports the Spirit's empowerment and God's provision of water. The place-name explanations also differ: Lehi is already named in verse 9; Ramath-lehi and En-hakkore receive explicit naming explanations later in the chapter.

All implemented note changes

Context and interpretation: The Philistines burn both Samson's wife and her father (15:6).
