Editorial rationale

Keep the death of the father as well as the wife. Attribute the retaliatory claims to the speakers; divine empowerment in the battle does not turn every preceding act into a divine instruction. The place-name note should also avoid saying all three names originate in this chapter: Lehi is already used in verse 9 before Ramath-lehi and En-hakkore receive their explicit naming explanations.

S036
Judges 16Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The summary makes uncut hair the source of Samson's strength. The Gaza note also turns the Hebron reference into a definite forty-mile journey and exact elevation gain.

Original app summary

Samson visits a prostitute in Gaza and tears out the city gates; later, Delilah deceives him into revealing that his strength comes from his uncut hair, and after the Philistines shave his head and blind him, he prays for strength one final time and pulls down the temple, killing himself and thousands of Philistines.

Original translation notes

Gaza and its gate: Gaza was the southernmost of the five Philistine cities, on the coast road running down to Egypt. The gate was the single heaviest and most valuable piece of a town's defence: two leaves of timber plated or braced, hung on posts sunk into the ground, with a bar dropped across at dusk. Verse 3 has Samson pull out the doors, both posts and the bar together and carry them to a hill facing Hebron, which is roughly forty miles away and three thousand feet higher. The town wakes up with no gate.

Dagon: Verse 23 names the god the Philistines credit with handing Samson over. Dagon had temples at Gaza and at Ashdod, and his name matches the Hebrew word for grain, dagan; two Israelite towns are called Beth-dagon, house of Dagon (Joshua 15:41, 19:27), which shows how far his worship reached. 1 Samuel 5:2-5 records what happened to his image at Ashdod when the captured ark was set beside it: it fell face down twice, and the second time its head and both hands were broken off on the threshold.

The two pillars of the house: Verse 27 puts about three thousand men and women on the roof watching, and verses 26 and 29 have Samson set his hands on the two central pillars the building rested on. Philistine temples of this kind have been dug up: the one excavated at Tell Qasile, on the northern edge of modern Tel Aviv, has two stone bases for wooden roof pillars set close enough together for one man to reach both at once. Pull those and the roof comes down on everyone standing on it.

Grinding at the mill: Verse 21 has the blinded Samson set to grind in the prison. Daily flour was made by hand between two stones, hours of work every morning, and it was the job of women and slaves; Exodus 11:5 uses the slave girl at the mill as the picture of the very bottom of a household. Putting a captured champion on the mill was a deliberate public humiliation, not just forced labour.

Eleven hundred pieces of silver: Verse 5 has each of the five Philistine lords offer Delilah 1,100 pieces of silver, so 5,500 in all. Judges 17:10 hires a Levite for ten shekels of silver a year with food and clothing, which puts the offer at several lifetimes of wages. By weight, at about 0.4 ounces a shekel, it is roughly 140 pounds of silver.

Samson carries away Gaza's gates and later tells Delilah that his uncut hair marks his dedication to God as a Nazirite. After his hair is shaved, his strength leaves him; the narrator says the LORD has departed from him. The Philistines blind and imprison him. He later prays for strength and brings down their temple, dying with thousands of Philistines.

Direct textual basis

Judges 16:17 links uncut hair to Nazirite dedication. Verse 20 explicitly says the LORD had departed from Samson. Verse 3 identifies a hill in relation to Hebron but gives no mileage or elevation.

Judges 16:3: Samson carries Gaza's gates, posts, and bar to a mountain overlooking Hebron; the verse gives the landmark but no mileage or elevation gain.
Judges 16:17-22: Samson connects his hair with Nazirite dedication; after it is shaved, the narrator says the LORD has departed, and the Philistines blind and imprison him.
Judges 16:28-30: Samson asks God to strengthen him before pushing the temple's supporting pillars and dying with the Philistines, placing his plea for strength immediately before the final collapse.

 Judges 13:5: Samson is designated a Nazirite to God from the womb, and no razor is to touch his head, connecting his uncut hair with his consecration.
Numbers 6:5: Uncut hair belongs to a Nazirite's vowed separation to the LORD, supplying the legal background for Samson's explanation of his hair.
All implemented reference changes

Samson identifies his uncut hair with his dedication to God as a Nazirite (16:17). After the shaving, the narrator explains that the LORD had departed from him (16:20); the account does not present hair as an independent source of power. All implemented note changes

Gaza and its gate: Gaza was the southernmost of the five Philistine cities, on the coast road running down to Egypt. The gate was the single heaviest and most valuable piece of a town's defence: two leaves of timber plated or braced, hung on posts sunk into the ground, with a bar dropped across at dusk. Verse 3 has Samson pull out the doors, both posts and the bar together and carry them to a hill facing Hebron; the verse gives no travel distance or elevation gain. The town wakes up with no gate.

Context and interpretation: Samson identifies his uncut hair with his dedication to God as a Nazirite (16:17). After the shaving, the narrator explains that the LORD had departed from him (16:20); the account does not present hair as an independent source of power. Verse 3 places the hill in relation to Hebron but gives neither a travel distance nor an elevation gain. Those numerical estimates depend on identifying the hill geographically.

Editorial rationale

Present the hair as the sign of dedication, with the narrator's explicit statement that the LORD departed. Label the hill's identification and travel distance as geographical interpretation, or remove the precise forty-mile/three-thousand-foot claim; the verse does not supply those figures.

S037
Judges 17Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

Two notes assert that the divinely arranged Levite support system had stopped working, presenting an inferred motive as narrated fact and softening the private priesthood's illegality.

Original app summary

Micah restores stolen silver to his mother, who uses part of it to make a silver idol for his shrine; he then hires a young Levite from Bethlehem as his priest, believing this will bring God's blessing.

Original translation notes

Carved image and molten idol: Two different manufacturing methods, named separately because the law names both. A pesel is carved, from the verb to hew, usually in wood or stone; a massekah is cast, from the verb to pour, in molten metal over a core. Deuteronomy 27:15 puts a public curse on the man who makes either one, and Exodus 20:4 forbids them outright. Verse 3 is the sting of this chapter: the silver is dedicated to the LORD, by name, and then spent on exactly what He forbade.

Household gods: Verse 5 has Micah make an ephod and teraphim for his private shrine. Teraphim are household images, kept in the home rather than at a sanctuary, and Scripture shows them used for divination (Ezekiel 21:21) and treated as valuable enough to steal: Rachel takes Laban's and hides them in a camel saddle (Genesis 31:19, 34). At least some were man-sized, since Michal lays one in David's bed to pass for a sleeping man (1 Samuel 19:13). Josiah swept them out of Judah along with the mediums and the idols (2 Kings 23:24).

Filled his hand: The word translated ordained in verses 5 and 12 is literally filled his hand, and it is the fixed technical phrase for installing a priest, used of Aaron and his sons at Exodus 28:41 and 29:9. It carries the sense of putting the office, and the income, into a man's hands. Micah performs the rite on his own son, and then on a passing Levite, in a private house, with no authority to do either.

Levite: The tribe of Levi got no territory. It was given forty-eight towns scattered through the other tribes, and it lived on the tithes and the offerings brought to the sanctuary (Numbers 18:21-24, Joshua 21:41). Deuteronomy 18:6-8 gives a Levite the right to leave his town, come to the place the LORD chooses, and serve there on equal terms. Verses 7 to 13 show what happens when that system stops working: a young Levite from Bethlehem is on the road looking for somewhere to live, and takes a job as a private household priest for wages.

Ten shekels a year: Verse 10 sets the Levite's terms: ten shekels of silver a year, a suit of clothes, and his food. That is about four ounces of silver annually, and it is one of the few plain wage figures in the Old Testament, useful for reading every other price in Scripture. The Levite takes a private household job because the arrangement God set up for his tribe had stopped working.

Micah returns stolen silver to his mother, who dedicates it to the LORD but uses part to make an idol. Micah makes a private shrine and hires a wandering Levite as his priest, then assumes the LORD will prosper him. The narrator frames the period as one in which everyone did what was right in his own eyes.

Direct textual basis

Judges 17:7-12 describes a wandering Levite accepting Micah's offer of wages, clothing, and food. Verse 13 gives Micah's own expectation of the LORD's favor. Numbers 3:10 distinguishes the Aaronic priesthood from general Levite status.

Judges 17:3-6: Micah's mother dedicates silver to the LORD yet has images made, and Micah establishes a shrine and appoints his son as priest amid the refrain about doing right in one's own eyes.
Judges 17:7-13: The wandering Levite accepts Micah's wages, clothing, and food to become his priest, and Micah voices his own confidence that this will secure the LORD's favor.

 Numbers 3:5-10: The Levites are assigned to assist Aaron and his sons, while Aaron and his sons receive the priestly duties, distinguishing Levite status from appointment to the priesthood.
Deuteronomy 18:1-8: Levites receive designated support and may come to minister at the place the LORD chooses, supplying the lawful setting for their service alongside Micah's private arrangement.
Exodus 20:4-6: Making and worshiping images is forbidden, so dedicating Micah's silver to the LORD does not authorize the images made from it.
All implemented reference changes

Exodus 20:4-6: Making and worshiping images is forbidden, so dedicating Micah's silver to the LORD does not authorize the images made from it.
Numbers 3:5-10: The Levites are assigned to assist Aaron and his sons, while Aaron and his sons receive the priestly duties, distinguishing Levite status from appointment to the priesthood.
Deuteronomy 18:1-8: Levites receive designated support and may come to minister at the place the LORD chooses, supplying the lawful setting for their service alongside Micah's private arrangement.

Micah's mother dedicates the silver to the LORD while using it for an image; Micah assumes that hiring a Levite guarantees the LORD's favor (17:3-5,13). The chapter reports these actions and Micah's belief without endorsing them. Levite status alone does not confer Aaronic priesthood (Numbers 3:5-10). The chapter reports the Levite accepting wages, clothing, and food; it does not identify a collapse of the national tithe system as the reason for his choice.

Editorial rationale

S038
Judges 18Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The summary says the Danites take Micah's priest without conveying that he willingly accepts their offer. A genealogy note treats 'Moses' and an exact grandson relationship as undisputed across editions.

Original app summary

Five Danite spies scout Laish, approve it as a settlement, and the tribe sends six hundred armed men who seize Micah's priest and religious objects, intimidate Micah into silence, then conquer Laish and rename it Dan.

Original translation notes

Six hundred armed men: Verses 11 and 16 count the fighting men who move north as six hundred, with the families and livestock behind them and the children in front (verse 21). This is a whole clan on the move under an armed screen, not a raiding party. Six hundred is the standing size of a body of men in this period: David's band settles at six hundred (1 Samuel 23:13), and six hundred Benjaminites survive at the rock of Rimmon (20:47).

Priest to a tribe rather than to one house: Verse 19 offers the Levite a promotion and he takes it: be priest to a tribe and a clan in Israel instead of to one man's household. The offer is put to him with a hand over his mouth and six hundred armed men at the gate, and verse 20 says his heart was glad. Priesthood here has become a post with a salary and a career, which is the point the chapter is making about the period.

Jonathan son of Gershom: The book waits until verse 30 to name the priest, and the name lands hard: Jonathan son of Gershom, the son of Moses. Gershom was Moses' firstborn, born in Midian (Exodus 2:22, 18:3). So the shrine at Dan, built around stolen idols, is served by Moses' own grandson and by his sons after him, and the verse says the line held the post until the captivity of the land.

Laish, later Dan: The town lay at the northern springs that feed the Jordan, under Mount Hermon, isolated from Sidon and with no defence treaty (verses 7 and 28). Renamed Dan, it becomes the northern marker of the whole land in the phrase from Dan to Beersheba, and centuries later Jeroboam puts one of his golden calves there (1 Kings 12:29). An Aramaic victory inscription naming the house of David was found at the site in 1993.

Danite spies scout Laish, and six hundred armed Danites march there. They steal Micah's religious objects, and his priest gladly joins them after being offered a position with the tribe. They threaten Micah, destroy unsuspecting Laish, rebuild it as Dan, and establish idol worship there.

Direct textual basis

Judges 18:19-20 describes an offer to serve a tribe and the priest's glad acceptance. Verse 30 names Jonathan son/descendant of Gershom, with Moses/Manasseh readings differing among textual witnesses and editions.

Judges 18:17-20: The Danites take Micah's religious objects and offer his priest a position with the tribe; he gladly accepts and takes the objects with him.
Judges 18:27-31: The Danites destroy unsuspecting Laish, rebuild it as Dan, and establish image worship; Jonathan is identified as a son of Gershom, son of Moses, in the account of the shrine's priesthood.

 Deuteronomy 12:5-7: Israel is to bring sacrifices and vow offerings to the place the LORD chooses, contrasting with the Danites' establishment of worship around Micah's stolen images.
Exodus 2:22: Moses names his son Gershom, supplying the family background for the Moses reading in the genealogy of the Danite priest.
All implemented reference changes

Deuteronomy 12:5-7: Israel is to bring sacrifices and vow offerings to the place the LORD chooses, contrasting with the Danites' establishment of worship around Micah's stolen images.
Exodus 2:22: Moses names his son Gershom, supplying the family background for the Moses reading in the genealogy of the Danite priest.

The Danites steal Micah's religious objects, but the priest gladly accepts their offer to serve the tribe rather than one household (18:19-20). Verse 30 has a textual difference: some witnesses read Moses, while many Hebrew manuscripts indicate Manasseh with a raised letter nun. Editions differ accordingly. The Hebrew word translated son can also express descent, as reflected in translations using descendant. The genealogy connects the shrine's priesthood with an Israelite family; the textual and generational details require care.

All implemented note changes

Six hundred armed men: Verses 11 and 16 count six hundred armed men. Verse 21 says the Danites put their small children, livestock, and possessions in front of them as they leave. The group includes dependents and possessions along with its fighters. Other passages also mention groups of six hundred men: David's band in 1 Samuel 23:13 and the Benjaminites who survive at the rock of Rimmon in Judges 20:47.

Priest to a tribe rather than to one house: Verse 19 offers the Levite a wider position: priest for a tribe and clan instead of one man's household. The Danites tell him to be quiet, put his hand over his mouth, and come with them; the verse reports that command rather than describing someone physically gagging him. Six hundred armed men stand at the entrance in verse 16. Verse 20 says the priest is glad and takes the ephod, household idols, and carved image as he goes with them.

Jonathan son of Gershom: The Danites steal Micah's religious objects, but the priest gladly accepts their offer to serve the tribe rather than one household (18:19-20). Verse 30 has a textual difference: some witnesses read Moses, while many Hebrew manuscripts indicate Manasseh with a raised letter nun. Editions differ accordingly. The Hebrew word translated son can also express descent, as reflected in translations using descendant. The genealogy connects the shrine's priesthood with an Israelite family; the textual and generational details require care. Exodus 2:22 and 18:3 name Moses' son Gershom. Judges 18:30 names Jonathan and says his descendants served as priests for the Danites until the captivity of the land.

Translation source notes

Editorial rationale

Keep coercion of Micah separate from the priest's willing career move. Explain the Moses/Manasseh textual variant where the genealogy is discussed; follow the selected edition without treating a cross-edition rendering or exact generational interval as settled.

S039
Judges 19Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The summary leads with the woman's alleged unfaithfulness, where the textual tradition differs, but omits that she is handed to the mob. It can also imply a death sequence the narrator does not explicitly establish.

Original app summary

A Levite retrieves his unfaithful concubine from Bethlehem, and after lingering with her father, they travel to Gibeah where wicked men of the city gang-rape her through the night; the Levite cuts her body into twelve pieces and sends them throughout Israel.

Original translation notes

Concubine: A pilegesh was a secondary wife: a real and recognised marriage, but without the standing, the settlement or the family backing of a full wife, and her children ranked below the wife's. Verses 3 to 9 show the relationship for what it legally was, with the Levite going to her father's house and being kept there five days as a son-in-law. The law protects such a woman in Exodus 21:7-11, which requires that her food, clothing and marital rights not be reduced, and lets her go free if they are.

Gibeah: A Benjamite town on the central ridge road about three miles north of Jerusalem, which is why the Levite passes over Jebusite Jerusalem to reach it (verses 11 and 12). He chooses the Israelite town over the foreign one on purpose. Gibeah is later Saul's home and seat (1 Samuel 10:26, 11:4), and Hosea twice uses its name as a byword for how low Israel went.

No one took them into his house: Verse 15 has the travellers sit in the open square of the town because nobody offers them shelter, and that is the first thing wrong in the chapter. In a country with no inns, taking in a traveller before dark was a duty every household understood; Abraham runs to do it in Genesis 18. The old man who finally takes them in is not from Gibeah at all, but from Ephraim (verse 16).

Cut into twelve pieces: Verse 29 uses the vocabulary of butchering a sacrifice, the word for the joints an animal is cut into. What the Levite sends round the tribes is a summons that cannot be filed away or ignored, and it works: chapter 20 opens with the whole nation assembled. Saul does the same thing with a yoke of oxen when he calls out the militia for Jabesh-gilead and says this is what will be done to the oxen of any man who does not come (1 Samuel 11:7).

A Levite brings his concubine back from her father's house in Bethlehem. At Gibeah, a mob demands to abuse him. The host offers the women instead, and the Levite seizes his concubine and puts her outside. The men rape and abuse her through the night. In the morning she lies unresponsive at the doorway; the Levite takes her home, cuts her into twelve pieces, and sends them throughout Israel.

Direct textual basis

Judges 19:2 is rendered 'unfaithful' in the selected English text; other ancient witnesses and translations read anger. Verse 25 reports the man seizing the concubine and putting her outside; she is abused all night. Verses 26-28 describe her collapsed at the doorway and unresponsive. Verse 29 describes the dismemberment; the Levite reports her death in 20:5.

Judges 19:2-3: The woman leaves for her father's house, and the Levite goes to speak kindly and bring her back, introducing the separation whose wording differs across translations.
Judges 19:22-30: The host offers women to the mob, and the Levite sends his concubine outside; after the night's abuse she is found unresponsive, and he later cuts her into twelve pieces and sends them through Israel.

 Judges 20:4-6: In his report to the assembly, the Levite says the attackers meant to kill him and that his concubine died, providing his own account to compare with chapter 19's narration.
Genesis 19:4-11: Sodom's mob demands Lot's guests, and Lot offers his daughters instead before the visitors intervene, paralleling the threat and proposed substitution at Gibeah.
Deuteronomy 22:25-27: The law condemns the man who overpowers a betrothed woman and expressly clears her of guilt, providing a legal contrast to blaming a woman for being attacked.
All implemented reference changes

Deuteronomy 22:25-27: The law condemns the man who overpowers a betrothed woman and expressly clears her of guilt, providing a legal contrast to blaming a woman for being attacked.
Judges 20:4-6: In his report to the assembly, the Levite says the attackers meant to kill him and that his concubine died, providing his own account to compare with chapter 19's narration.

In verse 25, the Levite seizes her and puts her outside to the mob. The narrator then describes the night's abuse, her collapse at the doorway, and her failure to respond in the morning. The passage does not explicitly state the exact moment of her death. The Levite later says that she died in his account to the assembly (20:5).

Editorial rationale

Explain 'unfaithful' versus 'angry' as a textual/lexical difference rather than silently making either reading universal. Retain the Levite's action in giving her over. Do not assert that she was killed specifically by dismemberment or at a precise point overnight; distinguish the chapter 19 narrator from the Levite's account in chapter 20.

S040
Judges 20Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The summary can read the Levite's self-report as the narrator's complete account of the prior atrocity, although his report omits his own part and describes the mob's intent differently.

Original app summary

Israel assembles at Mizpah, hears the Levite's account of his concubine's rape and death in Gibeah, and demands Benjamin turn over the guilty men; when Benjamin refuses, Israel fights three battles against them, using an ambush on the third day to defeat Benjamin and destroy their towns, leaving only six hundred Benjamite survivors who flee to the rock of Rimmon.

Original translation notes

From Dan to Beersheba: Verse 1 measures the muster by the two ends of the country, Dan in the far north under Hermon and Beersheba on the edge of the Negev, and adds Gilead across the Jordan. There is no king and no standing army; the tribes assemble themselves at Mizpah, a hilltop in Benjamin about eight miles north of Jerusalem, and act as one body. Samuel gathers the nation at the same place in 1 Samuel 7:5-6 and 10:17.

Seven hundred left-handed slingers: A sling is two cords on a leather pouch, whirled and released by letting one cord go. It was not a toy: armies fielded slingers as a standing arm, and the Assyrian relief panels of the siege of Lachish, cut for Sennacherib's palace and now in the British Museum, show them working the line alongside the archers. A stone thrown that way kills outright, which is what happens at 1 Samuel 17:49. Verse 16 says these seven hundred could sling at a hair and not miss.

Phinehas at Bethel: Verses 27 and 28 mention almost in passing that the ark of the covenant was at Bethel and that Phinehas son of Eleazar, the son of Aaron, was ministering before it. That dates the whole episode. Phinehas is the man who stopped the plague at Peor in Moses' own lifetime (Numbers 25:7-8), so this atrocity belongs near the start of the period of the judges, not at its end, even though the book places it last.

The rock of Rimmon: Verse 47 has six hundred Benjaminites hold out four months on a rock in the wilderness east of the hill country. Sela means a crag or cliff, not a boulder, and country like that is full of limestone spurs with caves and one approach, which is why fugitives head for them all through Scripture; David does the same at Adullam and En-gedi. Those six hundred men are the entire tribe of Benjamin that survives chapter 20, which is the problem chapter 21 exists to solve.

Israel assembles at Mizpah and hears the Levite's account of the atrocity at Gibeah. Benjamin refuses to surrender the guilty men. After two costly defeats, Israel defeats Benjamin using an ambush and burns its towns. Six hundred Benjaminite men escape to the rock of Rimmon.

Direct textual basis

Judges 20:4-6 is the Levite's speech to the assembly. Compare 19:22-25: the mob demands sexual abuse, the host offers women, and the man gives the concubine to the mob. The ensuing civil war also includes violence against Benjamin's towns, animals, and those found there (20:48).

Judges 20:4-13: The assembly hears the Levite's account and demands that Benjamin surrender Gibeah's guilty men, but Benjamin refuses, leading into the civil war.
Judges 20:18-28: Israel consults the LORD yet suffers two costly defeats before receiving a promise of victory the next day, preserving the losses that precede Benjamin's defeat.
Judges 20:35-48: An ambush leads to Benjamin's defeat, six hundred men escape to Rimmon, and Israel burns Benjamin's towns, showing destruction beyond the battlefield alone.

 Judges 19:22-30: The narrator describes a demand to abuse the male guest and the Levite putting the woman outside, details that differ from or disappear in his later speech to the assembly.
Judges 21:16: The elders say Benjamin's women have been destroyed, explaining why the surviving Benjaminites face a separate crisis over wives.
All implemented reference changes

Judges 19:22-30: The narrator describes a demand to abuse the male guest and the Levite putting the woman outside, details that differ from or disappear in his later speech to the assembly.
Judges 21:16: The elders say Benjamin's women have been destroyed, explaining why the surviving Benjaminites face a separate crisis over wives.

Verses 4-6 record the Levite's own speech. His account omits his role in putting the woman outside and presents the mob's intent differently from 19:22-25. The assembly acts on that testimony, while the reader has also heard the preceding narrator. The war's final devastation extends to Benjamin's towns and those found there (20:48). The six hundred who escape to Rimmon are explicitly men; chapter 21 then addresses the loss of Benjamin's women.

All implemented note changes

The rock of Rimmon: Verse 47 has six hundred Benjaminites hold out four months on a rock in the wilderness east of the hill country. Sela means a crag or cliff, not a boulder, and country like that is full of limestone spurs with caves and one approach, which is why fugitives head for them all through Scripture; David does the same at Adullam and En-gedi. These are the six hundred male survivors named at the chapter's close; chapter 21 addresses the separate crisis over wives after the destruction of Benjamin's women.

Context and interpretation: Verses 4-6 record the Levite's own speech. His account omits his role in putting the woman outside and presents the mob's intent differently from 19:22-25. The assembly acts on that testimony, while the reader has also heard the preceding narrator. The war's final devastation extends to Benjamin's towns and those found there (20:48). The six hundred who escape to Rimmon are explicitly men; chapter 21 then addresses the loss of Benjamin's women.

Editorial rationale

Signal that the assembly hears the Levite's account and allow readers to compare it with the preceding narration. The survival count names men, not an ungendered list of all people. Preserve the devastation of the towns rather than describing only the battlefield outcome.

S041
Judges 21Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The summary calls the first action 'taking four hundred virgins' without reporting the massacre that produced those captives; 'provide wives' can make two acts of coercion sound like an ordinary solution.

Original app summary

Israel grieves over Benjamin's near destruction and devises two plans to provide wives for the surviving Benjamites: first taking four hundred virgins from Jabesh Gilead, then instructing the Benjamites to seize young women dancing at the festival in Shiloh.

Original translation notes

The oath at Mizpah: This chapter runs on two oaths sworn in the heat of the assembly, and Israel treats both as unbreakable. Verse 1 swears that no one will give a daughter to a Benjaminite; verse 5 swears death on any town that failed to turn up. A shevuah is a sworn oath, stronger than a vow, and Numbers 30:2 binds a man to everything that comes out of his mouth. The tribes will not simply repent of the oath, so they spend the rest of the chapter finding ways round it.

Jabesh-gilead: Verses 8 to 12 destroy an Israelite town under the ban for not sending men to the assembly, and spare four hundred unmarried women out of it. The town sits east of the Jordan in Gilead. Two later episodes turn on this: Saul's first act as king is to march overnight and break the Ammonite siege of Jabesh-gilead (1 Samuel 11:1-11), and when he dies it is the men of Jabesh-gilead who travel all night to take his body off the wall of Beth-shan and bury him (1 Samuel 31:11-13).

Seize wives: Verses 20 to 23 solve the oath by arranging an abduction: the surviving Benjaminites lie in the vineyards and each carries off a woman from the dance, and the elders undertake to square it with the fathers and brothers afterwards. The law they are working around is the one they swore themselves, since the fathers cannot be said to have given their daughters if the daughters were taken. Verse 25 closes the book on the sentence it has repeated four times: in those days there was no king in Israel, and everyone did what was right in his own eyes.

The dance at Shiloh: Verses 19 to 21 describe a yearly feast of the LORD at Shiloh where the young women came out to dance in the vineyards, and the chapter gives directions to the place: north of Bethel, east of the road from Bethel to Shechem, south of Lebonah. Vineyard dances at a harvest feast were a normal part of the year. The chapter uses that ordinary festival as cover for carrying women off, and reports it without approving it.

Israel grieves for Benjamin but seeks to work around its oaths through further violence. The assembly destroys Jabesh-gilead and gives four hundred spared young women to the surviving Benjaminites. When that is insufficient, the elders arrange for Benjaminites to abduct women dancing at Shiloh. The book closes with everyone doing what was right in his own eyes.

Direct textual basis

Judges 21:10-12 orders killing the inhabitants of Jabesh-gilead while sparing four hundred unmarried young women; verses 20-23 arrange the seizure of women dancing at Shiloh. Verse 25 ends with everyone doing what was right in his own eyes.

Judges 21:1-12: Israel's oaths lead the assembly to attack Jabesh-gilead and kill its inhabitants while sparing four hundred young women who had not had relations with a man, identifying the violence behind their capture.
Judges 21:16-25: The elders arrange the seizure of women dancing at Shiloh while claiming to avoid their marriage oath; the book ends with everyone doing what was right in his own eyes.

 Leviticus 5:4-6: Guilt from rash sworn speech calls for confession and a sin offering for atonement, relevant when Israel's own oaths become the reason given for further violence.
1 Samuel 14:24-45: Saul demands Jonathan's death after Jonathan unknowingly violates his food oath, but the people rescue Jonathan, another account of a leader's oath placing a child under threat.
All implemented reference changes

Leviticus 5:4-6: Guilt from rash sworn speech calls for confession and a sin offering for atonement, relevant when Israel's own oaths become the reason given for further violence.
1 Samuel 14:24-45: Saul demands Jonathan's death after Jonathan unknowingly violates his food oath, but the people rescue Jonathan, another account of a leader's oath placing a child under threat.

The women from Jabesh-gilead are captives spared from an attack that kills the town's other inhabitants (21:10-12). The women at Shiloh are abducted under a plan arranged by Israel's elders (21:20-23). These are the assembly's decisions, not commands from God. Numbers 30:2 requires truthful fulfillment of vows and oaths, while Leviticus 5:4-6 provides confession and atonement for sinful rash speech. An oath does not authorize wrongdoing. The book's closing statement about everyone doing what was right in his own eyes frames the outcome.

All implemented note changes

The oath at Mizpah: The women from Jabesh-gilead are captives spared from an attack that kills the town's other inhabitants (21:10-12). The women at Shiloh are abducted under a plan arranged by Israel's elders (21:20-23). These are the assembly's decisions, not commands from God. Numbers 30:2 requires truthful fulfillment of vows and oaths, while Leviticus 5:4-6 provides confession and atonement for sinful rash speech. An oath does not authorize wrongdoing. The book's closing statement about everyone doing what was right in his own eyes frames the outcome. The assembly had sworn not to give daughters to Benjaminites (21:1) and to put to death anyone who failed to attend the gathering (21:5).

Seize wives: Verses 20 to 23 solve the oath by arranging an abduction: the surviving Benjaminites lie in the vineyards and each carries off a woman from the dance, and the elders undertake to square it with the fathers and brothers afterwards. The law they are working around is the one they swore themselves, since the fathers cannot be said to have given their daughters if the daughters were taken. Verse 25 closes the book on its repeated refrain: in those days there was no king in Israel, and everyone did what was right in his own eyes.

Editorial rationale

The assembly's oaths and plans are reported as human decisions; they are not divine authorization for massacre or abduction. The oath note should not imply that invoking Numbers 30 makes an evil oath morally binding or that an oath is categorically stronger than every vow. Connect this case to the rash-oath correction proposed for Judges 11.

S042
1 Samuel 12Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The summary makes obedience to the king a second condition beside obedience to God. The final sentence also made 12:22's assurance conditional on service, replacing the reason the verse actually gives.

Original app summary

Samuel vindicated his leadership before all Israel, then recounted God's faithfulness through the judges before warning that blessing depends on obedience to the LORD and the new king. When the people fear the thunder and rain Samuel called down, he reassures them that God will not reject his people if they serve faithfully.

Original translation notes

Whose ox have I taken: Verses 3 to 5 are a formal audit, not a burst of feeling. A judge leaving office stands in front of the people and invites any claim against him: whose ox or donkey have I taken, whom have I defrauded, from whose hand have I taken a bribe. The people are then made to answer on the record, and verse 5 seals it with the LORD as witness and the newly made king as witness, and their reply, he is witness. Samuel hands over with his accounts formally closed.

*fear of the LORD*: This chapter uses the phrase at both ends of its range within a few verses. In verse 18 the people greatly feared the LORD and Samuel when thunder came out of a clear harvest sky, which is fright. In verse 24 Samuel tells them to fear the LORD and serve Him faithfully with all their heart, which is a settled way of living. Deuteronomy 10:12 puts the same word first in the list of what God requires, and Proverbs 1:7 makes it the beginning of knowledge.

The deliverers Samuel names: Verses 9 to 11 run through the pattern of the book of Judges in three sentences: Israel forgot the LORD, He sold them into the hand of Sisera, of the Philistines and of the king of Moab, they cried out, and He sent deliverers. The men listed are Jerubbaal, who is Gideon, then Barak, then Jephthah, then Samuel himself. He puts his own name last in the list, which is how he closes the era before handing the country to a king.

Rain in the wheat harvest: Israel's rain falls from about October to April, and the wheat harvest in May and June is reliably dry; that is when threshing floors are in use and a downpour ruins the crop on the ground. Samuel calls for thunder and rain at exactly the season when nobody had ever seen it, which is why verse 18 says the people greatly feared. Proverbs 26:1 uses the same impossibility as a byword.

Samuel vindicated his leadership before all Israel, then recounted God's faithfulness through the judges before warning that blessing depends on both the people and the new king obeying the LORD. When the people fear the thunder and rain Samuel called down, he reassures them that the LORD will not abandon His people for the sake of His great name, and calls them to serve Him faithfully.

Direct textual basis

Both the people and their king must follow the LORD.

 Deuteronomy 17:18-20: Israel's king must copy, read, and obey God's law, confirming that the king is also subject to the LORD's commands.
All implemented reference changes

Deuteronomy 17:18-20: Israel's king must copy, read, and obey God's law, confirming that the king is also subject to the LORD's commands.

The command applies to the people and their king together. Verse 14 requires both to follow the LORD; it does not put the king beside God as a separate object of this obedience. In verse 22, the stated reason that the LORD will not abandon His people is His great name and His choice to make them His own. Verses 24-25 call for faithful service and warn against persisting in wickedness; they should not be substituted for the reason given in verse 22.

All implemented note changes

Context and interpretation: The command applies to the people and their king together. Verse 14 requires both to follow the LORD; it does not put the king beside God as a separate object of this obedience. In verse 22, the stated reason that the LORD will not abandon His people is His great name and His choice to make them His own. Verses 24-25 call for faithful service and warn against persisting in wickedness; they should not be substituted for the reason given in verse 22.

Editorial rationale

Korean and Portuguese already express this relationship correctly. Existing references do not include the law binding the king to God's instruction.

S043
1 Samuel 13Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

"Without waiting" can imply Saul did not wait at all, although the chapter explicitly records seven days.

Original app summary

Jonathan strikes a Philistine outpost, prompting Saul to gather troops, but the Philistines assemble a vastly superior force that terrifies the Israelites into hiding. Saul offers a burnt offering without waiting for Samuel, who arrives to condemn him for disobeying the LORD's command and announces that his kingdom will not endure.

Original translation notes

Burnt offering: The olah is the offering burned entire, with nothing kept back for the priest or the worshipper to eat, and the name means the thing that goes up in smoke. Before a battle it was normal to offer one, which is what Saul is waiting seven days at Gilgal to have Samuel do (10:8). Verses 9 to 12 have him call for it and offer it himself as the army melts away, and Samuel arrives as he finishes. The Greek word behind it gives English holocaust.

A man after His own heart: Samuel's sentence in verse 14 says the LORD has sought out for Himself a man according to His own heart and appointed him ruler over His people. In Hebrew the heart is where a person decides, not where he feels; it is closer to the mind and the will than to the emotions. The phrase describes a man whose choices line up with God's, and the chapter has just shown Saul making the opposite kind. Paul quotes the line at Acts 13:22.

No blacksmith in Israel: Verses 19 to 22 report that the Philistines allowed no smith in Israel, so farmers had to go down to them to sharpen a ploughshare, mattock, axe, or sickle, and only Saul and Jonathan had a sword or spear. Controlling ironworking was how the coastal cities kept the hill country disarmed; Israel could farm but could not arm. Judges 1:19 gives the other half of the picture, iron chariots the highlanders could not face.

A pim: Verse 21 gives the Philistine sharpening charge as a pim. The word appears nowhere else in Scripture and nobody knew what it meant until inscribed stone weights marked pym were dug up in Judah in the early twentieth century. A pim weighs about 7.8 grams, roughly two thirds of a shekel, so the fee for putting an edge on a farm tool was two thirds of the standard silver unit, charged per tool.

Michmash: A town about seven miles northeast of Jerusalem, on the north lip of the Wadi Suweinit, a steep gorge that cuts the road running east from the central ridge. Michmash sits on one side and Geba on the other, with the ravine between them, so an army holding Michmash sealed the pass. That gorge is the ground of Jonathan's climb in the next chapter.

Jonathan's attack brings a Philistine mobilization that terrifies Israel. Saul waits seven days at Gilgal but offers the burnt offering before Samuel arrives as his army disperses. Samuel rebukes his disobedience and says his dynasty will not endure.

Direct textual basis

Saul waits seven days, sees his army scattering, and offers before Samuel arrives.

 1 Samuel 10:8: Samuel tells Saul to wait seven days at Gilgal until he comes, supplying the instruction behind the later rebuke.
All implemented reference changes

1 Samuel 10:8: Samuel tells Saul to wait seven days at Gilgal until he comes, supplying the instruction behind the later rebuke.

Saul did wait seven days. The disputed action comes when he offers before Samuel arrives, explaining that the army was scattering and the Philistines threatened him. Samuel calls it disobedience to the LORD's command.

All implemented note changes

Context and interpretation: Saul did wait seven days. The disputed action comes when he offers before Samuel arrives, explaining that the army was scattering and the Philistines threatened him. Samuel calls it disobedience to the LORD's command.

Editorial rationale

The current burnt-offering note and 1 Samuel 10 reference already preserve the seven-day wait; the summary's compression should not erase it.

S044
1 Samuel 18Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

"Impossible bride-price" contradicts its fulfillment and obscures Saul's expressly stated murder plot.

Original app summary

Jonathan swears a covenant with David and gives him his robes and weapons; David's military success makes Saul jealous and fearful, so Saul tries to kill him with a spear, then manipulates him into marrying his daughter Michal by setting an impossible bride-price of Philistine foreskins, which David fulfills.

Original translation notes

Covenant: A berit is a binding agreement sworn in public, with obligations on both sides and a penalty behind it, and Hebrew does not make one, it cuts one, from the practice of cutting animals in half and passing between the pieces (Genesis 15:9-18). Verse 3 has Jonathan cut one with David, and the covenant is renewed twice more (20:16, 23:18). Its terms are spelled out at 20:14-15, and they run past both their lifetimes: David is not to cut off Jonathan's household when he comes to power, which is the obligation he honours in 2 Samuel 9.

The robe and the weapons: Verse 4 has Jonathan strip off the robe he was wearing and hand it over with his tunic, sword, bow and belt. Clothing and weapons stood for the man who wore them, and handing them across was how an agreement was made visible; that is why cutting the hem off Saul's robe is such a serious act at 24:4-5, and why Samuel reads a torn robe as a kingdom torn away at 15:27-28. What Jonathan gives away is the outfit of the king's son and heir, in front of witnesses.

The women singing: Verses 6 and 7 describe a fixed custom: women coming out of the towns to meet a returning army with drums, dancing and a sung refrain. Miriam does it at the sea (Exodus 15:20-21) and Jephthah's daughter does it at Mizpah (Judges 11:34). The couplet in verse 7 is built the ordinary way for Hebrew verse, with the second line raising the number of the first, so thousands and tens of thousands is how the form works. Verse 8 has Saul refuse to hear it that way.

Bride price: A marriage was arranged between households, and the groom paid the bride's father a mohar in silver, livestock, or service; Jacob worked seven years twice over for it (Genesis 29:18-27). Saul names a price of a hundred Philistines in verse 25 precisely because David is poor and the errand should kill him. David brings back two hundred.

Jonathan makes a covenant with David, whose success makes Saul jealous. Saul tries to kill him and sets a dangerous bride-price for Michal, hoping the Philistines will kill him. David exceeds the required price and marries Michal.

Direct textual basis

Saul asks for one hundred Philistine foreskins hoping David will die; David returns with two hundred.

 2 Samuel 9:1-7: David seeks Jonathan's surviving son and promises restored land and a place at his table, showing the later expression of their covenant loyalty.
All implemented reference changes

2 Samuel 9:1-7: David seeks Jonathan's surviving son and promises restored land and a place at his table, showing the later expression of their covenant loyalty.

The price was a trap, not an impossible condition: Saul hoped David would die while collecting one hundred Philistine foreskins. David returned with two hundred. The chapter also says Michal loved David.

All implemented note changes

Context and interpretation: The price was a trap, not an impossible condition: Saul hoped David would die while collecting one hundred Philistine foreskins. David returned with two hundred. The chapter also says Michal loved David.

Editorial rationale

The current bride-price note already explains both the motive and the numbers. The summary needs to agree with that concrete account.

S045
2 Samuel 9Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The summary and Lo-debar note turn residence with Machir into a stated hiding operation.

Original app summary

David seeks out anyone remaining from Saul's house to show kindness for Jonathan's sake and brings Mephibosheth, Jonathan's lame son, from hiding to restore his family's land and grant him a permanent place at the royal table.

Original translation notes

Kindness: Chesed is loyalty owed under a standing obligation and kept after the obligation could safely be dropped. Verse 1 asks whether anyone of Saul's house is left, so that David may show chesed for Jonathan's sake, and verse 3 calls it the chesed of God. This is not a spontaneous kindness; it is a clause being honoured. Jonathan had made David swear it, for himself and for his descendants, at 1 Samuel 20:14-15, at a time when the odds ran the other way.

Lame: 4:4 explains the injury: Mephibosheth was five years old when the news came from Jezreel, his nurse picked him up and ran, and he fell and was crippled in both feet. In a world with no wheelchairs, a man lame in both feet could not farm, could not fight and could not travel, so he depended entirely on a household that would keep him. Verse 13 notes that he lived in Jerusalem and ate at the king's table, and repeats that he was lame in both feet, which is why that arrangement was the whole of his security.

Lo-debar: Verse 4 finds the last heir of Saul's house living at Lo-debar in Gilead, east of the Jordan, in the household of Machir son of Ammiel. Across the river was the safest place to keep him: outside the reach of the new king, in the region that had backed Ish-bosheth. Machir turns up once more, sending food and bedding to David at Mahanaim when Absalom drives him out (17:27-29), so the man sheltering Saul's grandson later supplies David's army.

Eating at the king's table: Verses 7, 10, 11, and 13 repeat that Mephibosheth eats at the king's table. A place at the table was a formal grant of maintenance for life at royal expense, recorded the same way for the released Jehoiachin in Babylon (2 Kings 25:29). It also kept the last heir of the previous dynasty where the king could see him every day.

David seeks a survivor of Saul's house to show kindness for Jonathan's sake. He brings Mephibosheth from Machir's house at Lo-debar, restores Saul's land to him, and gives him a permanent place at the royal table.

Direct textual basis

Ziba identifies Mephibosheth as living in Machir's house at Lo-debar; the narrator does not state why he lives there.

S046
2 Samuel 12Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The final sentence makes Joab and David each capture Rabbah without distinguishing the stages. Edition comparison also requires identifying the forced-labor versus execution rendering rather than silently importing English wording. A retained repentance note also invented a fortnight; the unsupported duration has been removed.

Original app summary

Nathan confronts David with a parable about a rich man stealing a poor man's lamb, then reveals that David himself is that man for taking Uriah's wife and arranging Uriah's death; David confesses his sin, the child born to him and Bathsheba dies despite his fasting and prayer, and later David and Bathsheba conceive Solomon, whom the LORD loves. Joab captures Rabbah of the Ammonites, and David takes the city, plunders it, and enslaves its people.

Original translation notes

*sin*: Chata is the plain verb for missing a target; Judges 20:16 uses it of slingers who do not miss a hair. David's answer in verse 13 is three words in Hebrew, chatati la-YHWH, I have sinned against the LORD, and Nathan's reply comes back in the same breath. The heading attached to Psalm 51 names this occasion, and that psalm reaches for three different words in its first two verses: transgression, iniquity, and this one.

*repent*: The Hebrew word behind repent is not about feeling; it is the ordinary verb for turning round and going back, used of a traveller or an army. What the prophets ask for is a change of direction that can be seen from outside. This chapter shows the difference between remorse and repentance in one man across a fortnight: verses 16 to 20 have David fasting and lying on the ground for seven days over the child, then washing, anointing himself and going in to worship the moment the child dies.

Fourfold restitution: David's own sentence in verse 6, that the man must repay the lamb four times over, is a quotation of the statute. Exodus 22:1 requires four sheep for one sheep stolen and slaughtered, and five oxen for an ox. He is not inventing a penalty in anger; he is applying the law he knows, correctly, to a case he has not yet realised is his own. Nathan then answers in verse 7 with four words in Hebrew, you are the man.

Rabbah and the water supply: Rabbah was the Ammonite capital, on the upper reaches of the Jabbok where modern Amman stands, and the siege that opens at 11:1 is still running here. Verse 27 explains how a siege was actually won: Joab reports that he has taken the water supply, the lower town over the springs, which means the citadel above cannot hold out and is now only waiting. He then sends for the king so the credit for the capture goes where it belongs.

talent (money): The largest unit of weight and money in the ancient world. A talent of silver weighed about 75 lbs (34 kg) and equaled roughly 6,000 denarii; about 20 years of a laborer's wages. A talent of gold was worth far more. When Jesus tells the Parable of the Talents (Matthew 25:14-30), each talent represents a staggering trust.

sackcloth: A coarse, dark goat-hair fabric worn against bare skin as a universal sign of mourning, repentance, or distress (1 Kings 21:27; Jonah 3:5; Matthew 11:21). Wearing it publicly broadcast grief or contrition; the discomfort itself was part of the symbolism.

Lay on the ground and fasted: Verses 16 to 20 set out the standard signs of mourning and pleading: fasting, sleeping on the bare ground, refusing to wash, anoint, or change clothes. Anointing with olive oil was daily grooming in a hot, dry country, so going unanointed was visible to everyone. David's servants are frightened in verse 18 because he does all of it before the death and none of it afterward.

Nathan confronts David over Bathsheba and Uriah. David confesses, the child dies despite his prayer, and Solomon is later born. At Rabbah, Joab captures the water supply and calls David to complete the conquest; David takes the crown and plunder and puts the people to work.

Direct textual basis

Joab captures its water supply and summons David, who completes the capture.

 1 Chronicles 20:1-3: The parallel account assigns Rabbah's siege to Joab and the crown, plunder, and labor arrangements to David, distinguishing their actions.
All implemented reference changes

1 Chronicles 20:1-3: The parallel account assigns Rabbah's siege to Joab and the crown, plunder, and labor arrangements to David, distinguishing their actions.

Joab reports taking the water supply and asks David to bring the remaining troops and take the city. David then captures it. The account distinguishes Joab's siege success from David's final capture. The English Berean Standard Bible renders 12:31 as forced labor with tools and brickmaking; other editions describe killing the captives with tools. The wording is interpreted differently, so this treatment needs to be distinguished from the account of who captured the city. 1 Chronicles 20:3 is the parallel account.

S047
2 Samuel 18Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The summary substitutes hair for head and compresses the killing into Joab's three blows.

Original app summary

David organizes his forces into three divisions under Joab, Abishai, and Ittai, then positions himself at the city gate while his troops march out; Absalom is caught by his hair in an oak tree during the battle, and Joab kills him with three javelins despite David's command to spare him. Runners bring word to David that the battle is won, but when he learns Absalom is dead, he grieves deeply in the gateway chamber, lamenting that he wishes he had died instead of his son.

Original translation notes

The king kept out of the battle: The army refuses to let David take the field in verse 3, and their reasoning is cold arithmetic: if they run, nobody cares, and if half of them die, nobody cares, but he is worth ten thousand of them. A king in the line is the one target whose loss ends a war on the spot, which is why the enemy hunts him and why his own men would rather he sat in the gate at Mahanaim with a runner posted on the wall. His absence is also why the order about Absalom is given publicly to three commanders and then ignored.

A thousand shekels of silver: The soldier in verse 12 turns down a hypothetical bounty of a thousand shekels, and the figure is meant to sound enormous: a shekel weighs about two-fifths of an ounce, so this is roughly twenty-seven pounds of silver, when a Levite could be hired for ten shekels a year with board and clothing (Judges 17:10). His reason is not money but the order everyone heard the king give to Joab, Abishai and Ittai in the hearing of the whole army. Joab's answer is to take three spears and do it himself.

A heap of stones over the pit: Verse 17 gives the king's son the burial of a condemned criminal: thrown into a large pit in the forest with a great mound of stones piled on top. The same cairn goes over Achan in the Valley of Achor (Joshua 7:26) and over the king of Ai (Joshua 8:29). It is a public marker meant to be seen from the road, and it stands directly against verse 18, where Absalom had raised a pillar in the King's Valley in his own lifetime so that his name would be remembered.

His head caught fast in the oak: Verse 9 says his head caught in the tree, not his hair; the hair is the reader's addition. He is riding a mule under a low, thick-limbed terebinth in broken forest country, and he is left hanging between the tree and the sky when the animal keeps going. Verse 8 has already noted that the forest killed more men that day than the sword.

Two runners: Verses 19 to 27 turn on the two routes out of the forest of Ephraim: the Cushite goes straight over the hills, and Ahimaaz takes the longer flat road through the Jordan plain and still arrives first. News travelled at the pace of a man on foot, and a watchman on the gate tower identified runners by their gait long before they arrived (verse 27). Being first with the news was itself a position at court.

David organizes his forces into three divisions under Joab, Abishai, and Ittai, then positions himself at the city gate while his troops march out; Absalom's head is caught in an oak tree during the battle; despite David's command to spare him, Joab thrusts three spears into him and ten armor-bearers kill him. Runners bring word to David that the battle is won, but when he learns Absalom is dead, he grieves deeply in the gateway chamber, lamenting that he wishes he had died instead of his son.

Direct textual basis

Absalom's head catches in the tree. Joab pierces him and ten armor-bearers strike and kill him.

 2 Samuel 14:25-26: Absalom's appearance and unusually heavy hair are described, supplying the earlier detail often connected with the later tree episode.
All implemented reference changes

Verse 9 says Absalom's head was caught in the tree; it does not identify his hair as the cause. Joab thrust three spears into him, and ten armor-bearers then struck and killed him.

All implemented note changes

S048
2 Samuel 21Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The summary makes David the executioner and can obscure who demanded and carried out the killings.

Original app summary

A three-year famine leads David to execute seven descendants of Saul to satisfy the Gibeonites, whom Saul had tried to destroy; after proper burial of the remains, God ends the famine and David's forces defeat several Philistine giants in subsequent battles.

Original translation notes

The Gibeonites and the broken oath: Verses 1 and 2 reach back nearly four centuries. The Gibeonites had obtained a treaty from Joshua by deception, and Israel swore to them by the LORD and kept it even after the fraud came out (Joshua 9:15-20). Saul broke that oath and tried to wipe them out, and the famine is laid at that. Verse 4 is the detail a reader should not skip: they refuse silver or gold, which is the ransom Numbers 35:31 forbids being taken for a life, and ask instead for men of the house that shed the blood.

Atonement: David's question in verse 3 is how he shall kaphar, which English renders as make atonement or make amends. The verb means to cover over or wipe away so that a debt no longer stands, and it is the technical word for what the sacrifices do (Leviticus 16:30). Behind this chapter lies the rule that bloodshed pollutes the land itself and that nothing cleanses it except the blood of the man who shed it (Numbers 35:33), which is why the famine is read as a matter still outstanding on Saul's account.

The sons of the giant: Verses 15 to 22 close with four Philistine champions, each said to be descended from Rapha, the term behind the Rephaim, the tall peoples the Bible places in the land before Israel (Deuteronomy 2:10-11, 3:11). The details are physical and specific: Ishbi-benob's bronze spear weighed three hundred shekels, about seven and a half pounds, against the fifteen pounds of Goliath's iron spearhead at 1 Samuel 17:7, and one man at Gath had six fingers on each hand and six toes on each foot. Joshua 11:22 had already noted that the Anakim survived in Gaza, Gath and Ashdod.

sackcloth: A coarse, dark goat-hair fabric worn against bare skin as a universal sign of mourning, repentance, or distress (1 Kings 21:27; Jonah 3:5; Matthew 11:21). Wearing it publicly broadcast grief or contrition; the discomfort itself was part of the symbolism.

Rizpah on the rock: Verses 9 and 10 set the exposure at the start of barley harvest, which is April, and say Rizpah kept the birds and the wild animals off the bodies until rain fell. The first rains come in October. She sat out on sackcloth beside her sons for something close to six months, in the open, and the law's requirement that a body be buried the same day (Deuteronomy 21:23) is what makes her vigil an accusation the king finally answers in verse 13.

A three-year famine is linked to Saul's attack on the Gibeonites. David hands seven of Saul's descendants over to the Gibeonites, who execute them; after the remains are buried, God answers prayer for the land. David's forces also defeat several Philistine giants.

Direct textual basis

David gives the seven descendants to the Gibeonites, who execute them.

 Joshua 9:15-20: Israel's leaders preserve their sworn treaty with the Gibeonites after discovering the deception, establishing the oath behind the later bloodguilt dispute.
Deuteronomy 24:16: Parents and children must not be executed for one another's sins, supplying the law relevant to the execution of Saul's descendants.
All implemented reference changes

The Gibeonites and the broken oath: Verses 1 and 2 reach back nearly four centuries. The Gibeonites had obtained a treaty from Joshua by deception, and Israel swore to them by the LORD and kept it even after the fraud came out (Joshua 9:15-20). Saul broke that oath and tried to wipe them out, and the famine is laid at that. Verse 4 records their refusal of silver or gold. They ask for seven descendants of Saul, whom David hands over for execution; the chapter does not identify each victim's personal involvement in Saul's attack. Numbers 35:31 forbids accepting a ransom for a murderer who is guilty and deserves death.

Editorial rationale

The existing oath note explains the broken treaty but implies more about the victims' guilt than the text supplies. A proposal must avoid a dangling "whom" that attaches Saul's attack to his descendants instead of the Gibeonites.

S049
2 Samuel 23Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

"He then catalogs" attributes the narrator's warrior list to David's final oracle.

Original app summary

David delivers his final words affirming God's covenant with him and promising judgment for evil men. He then catalogs his mighty warriors, listing their individual exploits and naming all thirty-seven of his chief fighters.

Original translation notes

The last words of David: Verse 1 introduces the poem with neum, the formal word for a prophetic utterance, the same one used in the phrase declares the LORD throughout the prophets, and Balaam's oracles at Numbers 24:3. It then gives David four titles in a row: son of Jesse, the man raised on high, the anointed of the God of Jacob, and the sweet singer of Israel. Verse 2 states the claim directly, that the Spirit of the LORD spoke by him and His word was on his tongue.

The Three and the Thirty: Verses 8 to 39 are a formal roll of honour with two ranks in it, the Three at the top and the Thirty below, each with named captains, and it works the way a decorations list works: a man is admitted for a specific recorded action, so each entry gives the deed. It closes at verse 39 with the total, thirty-seven in all, and with one last name, Uriah the Hittite. The writer sets it there and says nothing about it.

Benaiah and the lion in the pit: Verse 20 credits Benaiah with three things, and the middle one needs unpacking. The Hebrew says he struck down two ariels of Moab, a word built from lion and God; English versions render it champions, and what the title actually designated is not known. Then he went down into a pit on a snowy day and killed a lion, which tells you both that cisterns were deep rock-cut shafts a man had to climb into, and that snow falls in the Judean hills in winter. Verse 23 makes him commander of David's bodyguard, and 1 Kings 2:35 makes him commander of the whole army under Solomon.

Poured it out to the LORD: Three men break through a Philistine garrison to fetch water from the well at Bethlehem, and David will not drink it (verses 15 to 17). Pouring a liquid out on the ground before God was the drink offering that went with the daily sacrifices (Numbers 15:5-10). He treats water bought at that risk as something only God has a right to.

The chapter presents David's final oracle about just rule, God's covenant, and judgment on evil. It then lists his mighty warriors and their exploits, ending with thirty-seven men in all.

Direct textual basis

David's oracle occupies verses 1-7. Verse 8 begins the list of mighty men in the narrative voice.

 1 Chronicles 11:10-47: Chronicles separately lists David's mighty men and their exploits, providing the parallel catalog to the list following his last oracle.
All implemented reference changes

1 Chronicles 11:10-47: Chronicles separately lists David's mighty men and their exploits, providing the parallel catalog to the list following his last oracle.
Psalm 72:1-7: The prayer asks for a king who judges justly, defends the poor, and brings flourishing like rain on freshly cut grass.

Verses 1-7 present David's oracle. The list of his mighty men begins at verse 8; the chapter does not say David compiled or recited that list as part of the oracle.

All implemented note changes

Context and interpretation: Verses 1-7 present David's oracle. The list of his mighty men begins at verse 8; the chapter does not say David compiled or recited that list as part of the oracle.

Editorial rationale

The current last-words note explains the oracle but does not separate it from the following catalog. The existing Chronicles parallel supports treating the catalog as a distinct narrative section.

S050
2 Samuel 24Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

Note-focused: the summary's divine-incitement wording is textual, but the note treats an unreported ransom failure and kingly motive as the established explanation.

Original app summary

The LORD incites David to take a census; after Joab completes the count of Israel's fighting men, David repents and chooses plague as his punishment, which kills seventy thousand people before stopping at Jerusalem. David buys Araunah's threshing floor, builds an altar, and offers sacrifices, and the plague ceases.

Original translation notes

The ransom for a census: Nothing in this chapter makes sense without Exodus 30:11-16, and no modern reader has it to hand. That law says when a census of Israel is taken, every man counted must pay half a shekel as a ransom for his life to the LORD, rich and poor alike, so that there be no plague among them when they are numbered. A count of fighting men treats the nation as an asset belonging to the king, and the half-shekel is the standing reminder that it belongs to God. Joab's objection in verse 3 is made without argument, and he is right.

Repent/Conscience struck: The Hebrew in verse 10 says David's heart struck him, using nakah, the ordinary verb for hitting or striking down. The same expression is used once before in his life, at 1 Samuel 24:5, after he cut the corner off Saul's robe in the cave. Both times it comes after the act and before anyone accuses him, and both times what follows is a confession in plain words: I have sinned greatly in what I have done.

Threshing floor altar: A threshing floor is a flat, hard, open circle of levelled rock, sited high and clear so the wind can carry the chaff off, and there was one on the rise just north of the old city, still owned by a Jebusite. Verse 24 has David refuse to take it as a gift, saying he will not offer to the LORD what costs him nothing, and pay for the floor and the oxen. 2 Chronicles 3:1 records what was built on that ground afterwards: Solomon's temple, on Mount Moriah, on the threshing floor of Araunah the Jebusite.

Fifty shekels of silver: Verse 24 records the price as fifty shekels of silver, about a pound and a quarter of it, and says exactly what it bought: the threshing floor and the oxen, that is, the working surface and the animals for the sacrifice. 1 Chronicles 21:25 records a second and much larger payment, six hundred shekels of gold, and says what that bought: the site. One is the price of a floor and a pair of oxen; the other is the price of the ground the temple would stand on.

From Dan to Beersheba: The census route in verses 2 to 8 runs from Dan at the northern springs under Mount Hermon to Beersheba in the dry south, about 150 miles apart, and it took Joab nine months and twenty days. The phrase is Israel's standard way of saying the whole country, from one end to the other. Counting fighting men over that whole span is what makes this a military census.

In anger against Israel, the LORD incites David to order a census. David later confesses sin, and a plague kills seventy thousand before the LORD stops it. David buys Araunah's threshing floor and offers sacrifices, and God answers prayer for the land.

Direct textual basis

Samuel names the LORD's anger and incitement; Chronicles names Satan. David confesses sin, but this chapter does not explicitly identify omission of the ransom as the offense.

 1 Chronicles 21:1-8: The parallel account names Satan as the census inciter and records David's confession, preserving its different attribution from Samuel.
Exodus 30:11-16: A census requires a half-shekel ransom from each counted man to prevent plague, supplying the relevant law without proving what David omitted.
All implemented reference changes

Exodus 30:11-16: A census requires a half-shekel ransom from each counted man to prevent plague, supplying the relevant law without proving what David omitted.
1 Chronicles 21:1-8: The parallel account names Satan as the census inciter and records David's confession, preserving its different attribution from Samuel.

Samuel opens with the LORD's anger against Israel and says He incited David; Chronicles names Satan as the inciter. David confesses that the census was sinful. Exodus 30 connects a census ransom with protection from plague, but neither account explicitly says David omitted that payment or gives that as the sole explanation.

All implemented note changes

The ransom for a census: Samuel opens with the LORD's anger against Israel and says He incited David; Chronicles names Satan as the inciter. David confesses that the census was sinful. Exodus 30 connects a census ransom with protection from plague, but neither account explicitly says David omitted that payment or gives that as the sole explanation. Exodus 30:11-16 sets the census ransom at half a shekel for each counted man, rich and poor alike, to support the Tent of Meeting.

Repent/Conscience struck: The Hebrew expression behind David's conscience being stricken also appears after he cuts Saul's robe in 1 Samuel 24:5. In the census account, David immediately confesses, I have sinned greatly in what I have done (2 Samuel 24:10). In the robe episode, he tells his men he must not raise his hand against the LORD's anointed (1 Samuel 24:6). The passages share the conscience-stricken expression but record different responses.

Fifty shekels of silver: Second Samuel 24:24 records fifty shekels of silver for the threshing floor and oxen; 1 Chronicles 21:25 records six hundred shekels of gold for the site. Some interpreters reconcile these figures by proposing a smaller purchase followed by a larger acquisition of the temple site. Neither account explicitly narrates two transactions or states that explanation, so it is a proposed harmonization rather than an established sequence of events.

Editorial rationale

Both essential references are already present. The current note's claim that a military census treats people as the king's asset is an interpretation and should not be presented as the narrator's stated motive.

S051
1 Kings 7Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

"Solomon himself makes" turns royal agency into a claim that he personally crafts the gold objects.

Original app summary

Solomon's palace construction takes thirteen years; he also commissions Huram from Tyre to cast bronze furnishings for the temple, including two pillars, a large basin called the Sea, ten movable stands, and various vessels, while Solomon himself makes the interior gold furnishings.

Original translation notes

Cubits: A cubit was roughly elbow to fingertip, about 18 inches (45 cm).

Cherubim: Heavenly throne guardians, depicted with wings outstretched over the ark.

Glory: Literally 'weight'; the manifest presence of God filling the temple.

Jachin and Boaz: Names of the two bronze pillars; meanings: 'He will establish' and 'In Him is strength.'

cubit: The distance from elbow to fingertip; about 18 inches (45 cm). The standard biblical measure for buildings (Noah's ark, the tabernacle, Solomon's temple) and bodies. Goliath was 'six cubits and a span' tall, about 9 feet 9 in. A 'long cubit' (Ezekiel 40:5) added a handbreadth.

handbreadth: The width of four fingers, about 3 inches (7.5 cm). Used for small dimensions on temple furniture and as a metaphor for the brevity of life (Psalm 39:5).

House of the Forest of Lebanon: The largest building in Solomon's palace complex, a hundred cubits by fifty by thirty, roughly 150 by 75 by 45 feet, named for the four rows of cedar columns inside it that stood like a forest. It served as an armoury and treasury: 1 Kings 10:17 stores the three hundred gold shields there, and Isaiah 22:8 calls it the House of the Forest when Judah looks to its weapons.

Thirteen years: Verse 1 notes Solomon spent seven years on the temple (1 Kings 6:38) and thirteen on his own house. The comparison is left to stand without comment. The palace complex was larger, taking in the throne hall, the hall of pillars, his own residence, and a house for Pharaoh's daughter.

Solomon builds his palace over thirteen years and commissions Huram of Tyre to make the temple's bronze furnishings. The chapter also records the gold sanctuary furnishings made for Solomon's temple.

Direct textual basis

The chapter credits Solomon with the furnishings but specifically identifies Huram as the bronze craftsman; it does not describe Solomon doing metalwork personally.

 2 Chronicles 4:11-22: Huram's bronze craftsmanship and furnishings credited to Solomon appear together, providing the parallel account of the temple work.
All implemented reference changes

2 Chronicles 4:1-22: The parallel account distinguishes the bronze furnishings cast in clay molds in the Jordan plain from the gold furnishings of the sanctuary.
2 Chronicles 4:11-22: Huram's bronze craftsmanship and furnishings credited to Solomon appear together, providing the parallel account of the temple work.

The chapter names Huram as the skilled bronze craftsman. Its statement that Solomon made the gold furnishings credits the king with the work; it does not describe him personally shaping the metal.

All implemented note changes

Context and interpretation: The chapter names Huram as the skilled bronze craftsman. Its statement that Solomon made the gold furnishings credits the king with the work; it does not describe him personally shaping the metal.

Editorial rationale

No current note explains royal agency. Removing "himself" preserves the text's attribution without adding a manual-craft claim.

S052
1 Kings 21Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The summary gives Jezebel's predicted fate to Ahab.

Original app summary

Ahab covets Naboth's vineyard; when Naboth refuses, Jezebel orchestrates his execution through false testimony and has him stoned to death. Elijah confronts Ahab with God's judgment - his line will be destroyed and dogs will eat his body - but when Ahab humbles himself in sackcloth, God delays the disaster until his son's time.

Original translation notes

Elijah: Means 'My God is Yahweh.'

Baal: Canaanite storm/fertility deity.

Asherah: Canaanite goddess; associated with poles/groves for worship.

sackcloth: A coarse, dark goat-hair fabric worn against bare skin as a universal sign of mourning, repentance, or distress (1 Kings 21:27; Jonah 3:5; Matthew 11:21). Wearing it publicly broadcast grief or contrition; the discomfort itself was part of the symbolism.

Samaria: The central highland region between Judea and Galilee; capital of the northern kingdom of Israel from 880 BC until Assyrian deportation in 722 BC. The mixed-population descendants who remained ('Samaritans') were despised by Judeans as ethnically and religiously impure. Jesus' choice to travel through Samaria (John 4) and to make a Samaritan the hero of His parable (Luke 10) deliberately upends that prejudice.

Inheritance: Naboth's answer in verse 3 is not stubbornness about a price. Under the law each family's plot was permanently theirs, could not be sold outright, and returned to them in the jubilee year even if leased (Leviticus 25:23-28). Selling the vineyard would have cut his family out of the land God assigned to them, and Ahab, as king, knew the law he was asking Naboth to break.

Proclaim a fast: Jezebel's letters in verses 9 and 10 call a public fast and seat Naboth at the head of the people. A town called a fast when it believed some hidden sin had brought trouble on it, and the assembly then looked for the guilty party. She sets the stage so that the two paid witnesses have a court and a crowd ready, and the sentence follows the law's own procedure exactly.

Ahab covets Naboth's vineyard; when Naboth refuses, Jezebel orchestrates his execution through false testimony and has him stoned to death. Elijah confronts Ahab with God's judgment - his line will be destroyed and dogs will lick his blood - but when Ahab humbles himself in sackcloth, God delays the disaster until his son's time.

Direct textual basis
