S053
2 Kings 2Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The familiar fiery-chariot retelling replaces the stated means of ascent.

Original app summary

Elijah is taken up to heaven in a chariot of fire after crossing the Jordan; Elisha inherits his cloak and spirit, heals bad water in Jericho with salt, and curses youths who mock him, resulting in bears mauling forty-two of them.

Original translation notes

Mantle: A heavy outer cloak, the garment a man slept under and could not be kept from him overnight if taken in pledge (Exodus 22:26-27). Elijah's is the object he throws over Elisha to call him from the plough (1 Kings 19:19), rolls up to strike the Jordan in verse 8, and leaves behind in verse 13. Picking it up is the visible transfer of the office.

Double portion: Elisha's request in verse 9 uses a legal term. The double portion is the firstborn's share of an estate, two parts where every other son gets one, guaranteed at Deuteronomy 21:17 even when the father prefers another son. He is not asking for twice Elijah's spirit; he is asking to be treated as the heir of the man's office.

The sons of the prophets: Companies of prophets living together at Bethel, Jericho, and Gilgal, appearing throughout this chapter and again at 4:38 and 6:1-2. Son of in Hebrew often marks membership in a class rather than descent, so the phrase names a guild under a master. Fifty of them stand off at a distance at the Jordan in verse 7, and one of them is sent to anoint Jehu in chapter 9.

Small boys: The Hebrew word naar covers anyone from a young child to a man of military age; Joseph is called a naar at seventeen and Solomon uses it of himself as king. Forty-two of them come out of Bethel, the town holding one of Jeroboam's calves, to jeer the prophet out of their territory. The number and the place mark this as a mob, not a handful of children.

Bears: The Syrian brown bear lived in the wooded hills of Israel and the Lebanon range into modern times, standing over five feet and weighing several hundred pounds. Scripture treats it as a known danger alongside the lion: David killed both (1 Samuel 17:34-36), and Amos 5:19 pictures a man escaping a lion only to meet a bear.

Elijah is taken up to heaven in a whirlwind as a chariot of fire separates him from Elisha after crossing the Jordan; Elisha inherits his cloak and spirit, heals bad water in Jericho with salt, and curses youths who mock him, resulting in bears mauling forty-two of them.

Direct textual basis

The fiery chariot separates Elijah and Elisha; Elijah ascends in the whirlwind.

 No additional contextual reference needed.

The chariot and horses of fire separate the two prophets. Verse 11 says that Elijah went up to heaven in a whirlwind; it does not say he rode the chariot.

All implemented note changes

Context and interpretation: The chariot and horses of fire separate the two prophets. Verse 11 says that Elijah went up to heaven in a whirlwind; it does not say he rode the chariot.

Editorial rationale

The current references do not supply this distinction. Several translations repeat the chariot-as-vehicle assumption.

S054
2 Kings 3Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The summary ends in an unqualified allied victory and omits the chapter's disturbing final reversal.

Original app summary

Joram of Israel, with Judah and Edom's help, campaigns against rebellious Moab; Elisha promises water and victory, the armies prevail, and the Moabites are defeated and their land ravaged.

Original translation notes

Debt slavery: Children could be seized as slaves to pay debts. The oil miracle prevented this fate.

Leprosy: Not modern Hansen’s disease but a range of skin conditions causing ritual uncleanness.

Jordan: River of Israel central to baptismal symbolism and repeated miracles.

Samaria: The central highland region between Judea and Galilee; capital of the northern kingdom of Israel from 880 BC until Assyrian deportation in 722 BC. The mixed-population descendants who remained ('Samaritans') were despised by Judeans as ethnically and religiously impure. Jesus' choice to travel through Samaria (John 4) and to make a Samaritan the hero of His parable (Luke 10) deliberately upends that prejudice.

Chemosh and the king's firstborn: Chemosh was the national god of Moab; Numbers 21:29 calls the Moabites the people of Chemosh, and Solomon built him a high place east of Jerusalem (1 Kings 11:7). Verse 27 reports Mesha taking his oldest son, the heir to his throne, and burning him as an offering on the city wall in full view of the besieging armies. This was what Moab did when a siege went badly, and the text states it without comment.

Mesha of Moab: Mesha left his own account of this war. A black basalt stone found at Dhiban in Jordan in 1868, now in the Louvre, carries thirty-four lines in Moabite in which King Mesha names Omri king of Israel, describes throwing off Israel's rule, and credits Chemosh with the victory. It is the same conflict this chapter reports, told from the other side.

A hundred thousand lambs: Verse 4 sets Moab's annual tribute at 100,000 lambs and the wool of 100,000 rams. Moab's plateau east of the Dead Sea is high, dry grazing country, so the payment came in what the land produced. Withholding tribute was how a vassal declared independence, which is what verse 5 records him doing the moment Ahab died.

Joram of Israel, with Judah and Edom's help, campaigns against rebellious Moab; Elisha promises water and victory, the armies prevail, and the Moabites are defeated and their land ravaged. Moab's king then sacrifices his firstborn son on the wall; great fury comes against Israel, and the allies withdraw.

Direct textual basis

After Mesha sacrifices his firstborn, great fury comes against Israel and the allies withdraw.

S055
2 Kings 5Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

Naaman's personal search for healing becomes a mission on his king's behalf, and the last clause assigns the affliction directly to Elisha.

Original app summary

Naaman, an Aramean military commander with leprosy, travels to Israel on his king's behalf; after initially resisting Elisha's instruction to wash in the Jordan, he obeys and is healed, then offers gifts that Elisha refuses. Elisha's servant Gehazi lies to obtain silver and clothing from Naaman, and Elisha strikes him with Naaman's leprosy as punishment.

Original translation notes

Debt slavery: Children could be seized as slaves to pay debts. The oil miracle prevented this fate.

Leprosy: Not modern Hansen’s disease but a range of skin conditions causing ritual uncleanness.

Jordan: River of Israel central to baptismal symbolism and repeated miracles.

talent (money): The largest unit of weight and money in the ancient world. A talent of silver weighed about 75 lbs (34 kg) and equaled roughly 6,000 denarii; about 20 years of a laborer's wages. A talent of gold was worth far more. When Jesus tells the Parable of the Talents (Matthew 25:14-30), each talent represents a staggering trust.

talent (weight): About 75 lbs (34 kg) of metal. A 'talent of gold' in the Old Testament (e.g. 2 Samuel 12:30) refers to physical mass rather than coined currency. The bronze for the Tabernacle (Exodus 38:29) was 70 talents and 2,400 shekels.

Samaria: The central highland region between Judea and Galilee; capital of the northern kingdom of Israel from 880 BC until Assyrian deportation in 722 BC. The mixed-population descendants who remained ('Samaritans') were despised by Judeans as ethnically and religiously impure. Jesus' choice to travel through Samaria (John 4) and to make a Samaritan the hero of His parable (Luke 10) deliberately upends that prejudice.

Two mule loads of earth: Naaman asks in verse 17 to carry Israelite soil home to Damascus. Ancient religion generally tied a god to his territory, so a foreigner who wanted to sacrifice to the God of Israel took ground from Israel to build his altar on. The request shows what he had grasped and what he had not, and the text records it without correcting him.

Rimmon: The temple in Damascus in verse 18 belonged to the Aramean storm god Hadad, called Rammanu, the thunderer, in Akkadian, which is where the name Rimmon comes from. Zechariah 12:11 preserves the fuller form Hadad-rimmon. As the king's chief officer, Naaman had to attend the king in that temple and support him when he bowed.

Ten talents and six thousand shekels: Verse 5 has Naaman set out with ten talents of silver, six thousand shekels of gold, and ten changes of clothing. A talent is about 75 pounds, so that is roughly 750 pounds of silver and, at about 0.4 ounces to the shekel, some 150 pounds of gold. It is a king's ransom for a cure, and Elisha will not take any of it.

Naaman travels to Israel seeking healing with his king's letter. He finally obeys Elisha's command to wash in the Jordan and is cleansed; Elisha refuses payment. Gehazi obtains gifts by lying, and Elisha declares that Naaman's disease will cling to him and his descendants.

Direct textual basis

Naaman travels with royal permission and a letter asking for his cure. Elisha announces that Naaman's disease will cling to Gehazi after his deception.

 Luke 4:27: Jesus names Naaman the Syrian as the one cleansed in Elisha's time, identifying the healing associated with Naaman's journey.
Leviticus 14:1-9: Priests examine a healed skin condition and administer cleansing rites, supplying the ritual context for the disease terminology used of Naaman.
All implemented reference changes

Luke 4:27: Jesus names Naaman the Syrian as the one cleansed in Elisha's time, identifying the healing associated with Naaman's journey.
Leviticus 14:1-9: Priests examine a healed skin condition and administer cleansing rites, supplying the ritual context for the disease terminology used of Naaman.

The king sends a letter on Naaman's behalf so that Naaman may be healed; Naaman is not seeking a cure for the king. Elisha announces Gehazi's judgment, and the narrative then says Gehazi leaves with the skin disease. The Hebrew disease category is broader than modern Hansen's disease.

S056
2 Kings 9Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The summary makes Jehu personally throw Jezebel out of the window.

Original app summary

Elisha sends a prophet to anoint Jehu as king of Israel with orders to destroy the house of Ahab; Jehu's army officers proclaim him king, he kills King Joram and chases down King Ahaziah, then throws Jezebel from a window in Jezreel where dogs consume her body.

Original translation notes

Anoint: Olive oil poured from a flask over the head, the act that installed a man in office and made the appointment public and irreversible. Kings, priests, and on occasion prophets were anointed, and the passive participle of this verb, mashiach, is the word behind messiah. Verses 3 and 6 have it done indoors and in a hurry by a young member of a prophetic guild, because this anointing is a coup against a reigning king.

Eunuchs: The men who throw Jezebel from the window in verses 32 and 33 are the officers of her own household. The word covers senior court servants of a royal establishment, and in the surrounding empires men in such posts, especially those with access to the women's quarters, were commonly castrated so they could found no line of their own. Isaiah 56:3-5 addresses the standing of such men in Israel directly.

Jezreel: A royal residence of the northern kings at the eastern end of the great valley that carries its name, the one flat corridor across northern Israel. Ahab had a palace there next to Naboth's vineyard, seized after Jezebel had the man killed by false witnesses (1 Kings 21:1-16). Verses 25 and 26 have Jehu throw Joram's body onto that same plot of ground and say why.

She painted her eyes: Verse 30 says Jezebel set her eyes in pukh and dressed her head. The paint was ground galena or antimony, black, applied around the eye with a small stick from a pot; jars and applicators turn up in graves all over the region. Doing it before appearing at an upper window is the act of a queen presenting herself in state, not a woman hiding.

Zimri: Jezebel's greeting in verse 31, 'Have you come in peace, O Zimri, murderer of your master,' names a specific man. Zimri commanded half the chariots, killed King Elah while he was drinking, wiped out the royal house, and held the throne seven days before the army made Omri king and Zimri burned the palace down over himself (1 Kings 16:9-20). She is telling Jehu how long she expects his coup to last.

Elisha sends a prophet to anoint Jehu as king of Israel with orders to destroy the house of Ahab; Jehu's army officers proclaim him king, he kills King Joram and chases down King Ahaziah, then orders eunuchs to throw Jezebel from a window in Jezreel where dogs consume her body.

Direct textual basis

At Jehu's command, eunuchs throw her down.

 No additional contextual reference needed.
All implemented reference changes

1 Kings 21:1-16: Jezebel arranges for two scoundrels to accuse Naboth falsely of cursing God and the king; he is stoned, and Ahab takes possession of his vineyard.

Jehu calls to the people at the window and orders them to throw Jezebel down. The eunuchs carry out the order.

All implemented note changes

Context and interpretation: Jehu calls to the people at the window and orders them to throw Jezebel down. The eunuchs carry out the order.

Editorial rationale

French, Italian, and Russian already distinguish command from execution; the other affected versions need the same precision.

S057
1 Chronicles 10Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The head displayed in Dagon's temple is conflated with the bodies recovered by Jabesh-gilead.

Original app summary

The Philistines rout Israel on Mount Gilboa, kill Saul's sons, and wound Saul, who falls on his own sword; they hang his head in the temple of Dagon until the men of Jabesh-gilead retrieve and bury the bones. Saul dies for his unfaithfulness, because he did not keep the word of the LORD and consulted a medium, so the LORD turns the kingdom over to David son of Jesse.

Original translation notes

Medium: Spirit-consultant/necromancer; expressly forbidden.

Unfaithfulness: Breach of trust against covenant obligations.

Mount Gilboa: A ridge rising to about 1,700 feet on the southeast rim of the Jezreel Valley, roughly fifty miles north of Jerusalem. The valley below is the one open corridor across northern Israel, wide and flat, which is exactly the ground Philistine chariots could use and Israel could not. Saul had to fight there, and the archers on the slope broke his line (verse 3).

Dagon: The chief god of the Philistine cities, whose temples stood at Ashdod, Gaza, and Beth-shan. The name is tied to dagan, grain, and his worship was a fertility cult of the harvest. Verse 10 has Saul's head fastened up in Dagon's temple, which reverses 1 Samuel 5:1-5, where Dagon's own image fell on its face before the captured ark and broke its head and hands off on the threshold.

Jabesh-gilead: A town east of the Jordan in Gilead, about twenty miles south of the Sea of Galilee, and a night's hard march from Beth-shan where the Philistines displayed the bodies. The men of Jabesh went because Saul's first act as king had been to march all night and break Nahash the Ammonite's siege of their town (1 Samuel 11:1-11). They buried his bones under the oak and fasted seven days.

The Philistines rout Israel on Mount Gilboa, kill Saul's sons, and wound Saul, who falls on his own sword; they hang his head in the temple of Dagon. The men of Jabesh-gilead recover the bodies of Saul and his sons and bury their bones. Saul dies for his unfaithfulness, because he did not keep the word of the LORD and consulted a medium, so the LORD turns the kingdom over to David son of Jesse.

Direct textual basis

The Philistines display Saul's head; Jabesh-gilead recovers the bodies of Saul and his sons and buries their bones.

All implemented note changes

Context and interpretation: The displayed head and the recovered bodies are separate details. The men of Jabesh-gilead retrieve Saul's body and his sons' bodies, then bury their bones; the parallel in 1 Samuel places the bodies at Beth-shan.

S058
1 Chronicles 20Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The summary can assign David's crown, plunder, and forced labor to Joab. The crown note invents its physical handling. Edition comparison also requires identifying the forced-labor versus execution rendering rather than silently importing English wording.

Original app summary

Joab besieges and destroys Rabbah while David remains in Jerusalem, takes the Ammonite king's crown and plunder, and puts the conquered people to forced labor. David's forces then fight several battles against the Philistines, with his men killing several giants including Lahmi the brother of Goliath.

Original translation notes

talent (money): The largest unit of weight and money in the ancient world. A talent of silver weighed about 75 lbs (34 kg) and equaled roughly 6,000 denarii; about 20 years of a laborer's wages. A talent of gold was worth far more. When Jesus tells the Parable of the Talents (Matthew 25:14-30), each talent represents a staggering trust.

Rabbah: The Ammonite capital, about twenty-three miles east of the Jordan on the King's Highway trade route; the site is modern Amman. It sat on a high citadel above a spring, which is why a siege rather than an assault was needed and why taking the water supply broke it (2 Samuel 12:27). Verse 1 has Joab do the work while David stays home.

Descendants of the giants: Verses 4, 6, and 8 call Sippai, Lahmi, and the six-fingered man at Gath descendants of the Rapha. Israel had met unusually large men before: the spies reported them at Numbers 13:32-33, and Og king of Bashan slept in an iron bed thirteen and a half feet long (Deuteronomy 3:11). They survived in the Philistine cities of the coast after Joshua cleared them from the hill country (Joshua 11:22).

A talent of gold: The crown in verse 2 weighs a talent, about 75 pounds. No head wears that, so it was lifted from the idol or the throne and held above David rather than worn; the text says its weight was found to be a talent and that a precious stone from it went into David's crown. As bullion that is a fortune: gold at any period is priced by the ounce, and a talent is about 1,200 troy ounces.

Joab besieges and destroys Rabbah while David remains in Jerusalem. David then takes the Ammonite king's crown and plunder and puts the conquered people to forced labor. David's forces then fight several battles against the Philistines, with his men killing several giants including Lahmi the brother of Goliath.

Direct textual basis

Joab conducts the siege; David takes the crown and plunder and imposes labor. The verse says the crown was placed on David's head.

 2 Samuel 12:26-31: Joab takes Rabbah's water supply and summons David, who captures the city and takes its crown and plunder, distinguishing the stages of conquest.
All implemented reference changes

2 Samuel 12:26-31: Joab takes Rabbah's water supply and summons David, who captures the city and takes its crown and plunder, distinguishing the stages of conquest.

Joab besieges Rabbah; David takes the crown and plunder and puts the people to work. The crown is said to weigh a talent of gold and to be placed on David's head. The passage does not explain how it was supported or say it was held above him. The English Berean Standard Bible renders 20:3 as forced labor with tools; other editions describe cutting the captives with those tools. This translation difference also affects the parallel in 2 Samuel 12:31.

All implemented note changes

The current talent note asserts that no head could wear the crown, that it came from an idol or throne, and that only a stone went into David's crown. Those are reconstructions, not the wording of the app's verse. The parallel is already linked.

S059
2 Chronicles 4Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

Gold lampstands and tables are wrongly included among the bronze furnishings.

Original app summary

Hiram completes the bronze furnishings for the temple - a large altar, the Sea with twelve bulls underneath, ten basins, ten lampstands, and ten tables - and Solomon adds gold furnishings including the altar, lampstands, and doors. The total bronze work is so extensive that its weight cannot be calculated.

Original translation notes

Wisdom: Practical discernment in governance and justice; not mere knowledge but skillful application.

Glory: The manifest presence of God, filling the tabernacle and later the temple.

Temple: Palace/house of God; in Chronicles, the center of worship and covenant relationship.

cubit: The distance from elbow to fingertip; about 18 inches (45 cm). The standard biblical measure for buildings (Noah's ark, the tabernacle, Solomon's temple) and bodies. Goliath was 'six cubits and a span' tall, about 9 feet 9 in. A 'long cubit' (Ezekiel 40:5) added a handbreadth.

handbreadth: The width of four fingers, about 3 inches (7.5 cm). Used for small dimensions on temple furniture and as a metaphor for the brevity of life (Psalm 39:5).

The bronze sea: A single cast bronze basin, ten cubits across and five deep, about fifteen feet wide and seven and a half feet from brim to floor, standing on twelve bronze oxen in four groups of three facing out. Verse 6 says it was for the priests to wash in, the temple's replacement for the tabernacle's small bronze basin. At 2,000 baths, the figure given in 1 Kings 7:26, and about 5.8 gallons to a bath, that is over eleven thousand gallons of water weighing more than forty tons.

Cast in the clay ground: Verse 17 says the king cast the bronze work in the plain of the Jordan, in the clay ground between Succoth and Zeredah. Bronze on this scale needed a place with the right casting clay for moulds, fuel, and room for furnaces, and it was done thirty-five miles from the building site, then hauled up. 1 Kings 7:47 says the bronze was never weighed because there was so much of it.

Hiram completes the bronze furnishings for the temple - a large altar, the Sea with twelve bulls underneath, ten basins - and Solomon adds gold furnishings including the altar, lampstands, and doors. The total bronze work is so extensive that its weight cannot be calculated.

Direct textual basis

The chapter distinguishes bronze work from gold sanctuary furnishings.

 1 Kings 7:48-50: The temple's altar, table, lampstands, and other sanctuary furnishings are described as gold, confirming their distinction from the bronze inventory.
All implemented reference changes

1 Kings 7:23-39: The bronze Sea stands on twelve oxen, and ten bronze stands support ten basins; the passage describes their dimensions, construction, and placement.
1 Kings 7:48-50: The temple's altar, table, lampstands, and other sanctuary furnishings are described as gold, confirming their distinction from the bronze inventory.

The ten lampstands are gold, and the sanctuary tables belong with the gold furnishings. They are not part of the bronze inventory.

All implemented note changes

The bronze sea: A single cast bronze basin, ten cubits across and five deep, about fifteen feet wide and seven and a half feet from brim to floor, standing on twelve bronze oxen in four groups of three facing out. Verse 6 says it was for the priests to wash in, the temple's replacement for the tabernacle's small bronze basin. This chapter gives its capacity as 3,000 baths (4:5), while 1 Kings 7:26 gives 2,000 baths. Using the Kings figure and an estimate of about 5.8 gallons per bath gives over eleven thousand gallons of water weighing more than forty tons; the two passages' stated figures remain distinct.

Context and interpretation: The ten lampstands are gold, and the sanctuary tables belong with the gold furnishings. They are not part of the bronze inventory.

Editorial rationale

The existing bronze-sea note does not clarify the separate materials. Its 2,000-bath capacity is from 1 Kings 7:26; this chapter gives 3,000 baths at 4:5, so those figures also need an explicit distinction rather than silent substitution.

S060
2 Chronicles 15Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

Peace is extended to the rest of Asa's reign despite the next chapter's war.

Original app summary

Azariah prophesies to Asa that God will be with him if he seeks God, spurring Asa to remove idols and lead Judah and Benjamin in a covenant to seek the LORD with all their heart and soul. Asa deposes his grandmother Maakah for idolatry, brings dedicated treasures into the temple, and enjoys peace for the rest of his reign.

Original translation notes

Covenant: Verses 12-15 record a sworn national agreement, not a private resolution: the people bind themselves by oath, with trumpets and rams' horns sounding, to seek the LORD, and verse 13 attaches the death penalty to refusal. A berit was cut, sworn aloud, and witnessed, and it carried stated penalties like any treaty of the period. The scene deliberately repeats the covenant ceremonies at Sinai and Shechem (Exodus 24:3-8, Joshua 24:22-25).

Asherah: A Canaanite goddess and also the wooden object that stood for her, a pole or carved tree set beside an altar at a high place. Verse 16 says Asa's grandmother Maacah made a repulsive image for her, and Asa cut it down, crushed it, and burned it in the Kidron. Deuteronomy 16:21 forbids planting one beside the altar of the LORD, and inscriptions found at Kuntillet Ajrud in the Sinai show Israelites pairing her name with the LORD's, which is exactly the mixture the reforming kings are tearing out.

A priest to instruct: Verse 3 describes Israel as having gone long stretches without the true God, without a teaching priest, and without the law. Teaching was part of the priest's job description, not an extra: Deuteronomy 33:10 has Levi teach Jacob God's rules, Leviticus 10:11 makes the priests responsible for instructing Israel in the statutes, and Malachi 2:7 says the people should seek instruction from the priest's mouth.

Defectors from the north: Verse 9 has people from Ephraim, Manasseh, and Simeon settling in Judah in large numbers. Movement ran both ways after the split, and Chronicles keeps recording it: priests and Levites came south under Rehoboam (11:13-14), and Hezekiah later sends couriers north to invite what was left of those tribes to Passover (30:10-11). The border between the two kingdoms was political, and families crossed it.

Queen mother: Verse 16 removes Maacah from being gevirah, a formal office at the court of Judah and not just a family relation. The king's mother held rank, sat at his right (1 Kings 2:19), and is named in the accession formula of almost every king of Judah. Stripping Maacah of the title was a public act of state, which is why it is recorded alongside the burning of her image.

Azariah prophesies to Asa that God will be with him if he seeks God, spurring Asa to remove idols and lead Judah and Benjamin in a covenant to seek the LORD with all their heart and soul. Asa deposes his grandmother Maakah for idolatry, brings dedicated treasures into the temple, and enjoys peace until the thirty-fifth year of his reign.

Direct textual basis

This chapter says there was no war until his thirty-fifth year; chapter 16 records later conflict.

 2 Chronicles 16:1-9: Baasha threatens Judah and Hanani warns Asa of future wars, showing that the earlier period of peace did not last through his reign.
All implemented reference changes

2 Chronicles 16:1-9: Baasha threatens Judah and Hanani warns Asa of future wars, showing that the earlier period of peace did not last through his reign.

Verse 19 says there was no war until the thirty-fifth year of Asa's reign. The next chapter records conflict with Baasha and a warning that Asa would have wars; the peace did not last for the rest of his reign.

All implemented note changes

Context and interpretation: Verse 19 says there was no war until the thirty-fifth year of Asa's reign. The next chapter records conflict with Baasha and a warning that Asa would have wars; the peace did not last for the rest of his reign.

Editorial rationale

No current note explains the limit. This proposal follows the chapter's stated year without resolving the separate chronology question raised by Baasha's reign in Kings.

S061
2 Chronicles 34Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The eighteenth regnal year becomes age eighteen; some versions make Josiah read instead of hear. "Die in peace" also obscures the qualified promise.

Original app summary

Josiah becomes king at eight, spends his youth destroying idols and high places throughout Judah and Israel, and at eighteen sends officials to repair the temple; during repairs Hilkiah discovers the Book of the Law, which Josiah reads and tears his robes in distress. The prophet Huldah tells Josiah disaster will come on Judah but he will die in peace for his repentant heart, so Josiah gathers all the people and renews the covenant to follow the LORD.

Original translation notes

Book of the Law: A book at this period is a scroll of sheets sewn end to end, written on leather or papyrus and rolled, not a bound volume. Verse 15 has Hilkiah find one in the temple during the repairs and hand it to the royal secretary, who reads it aloud to the king; there was no expectation that the king read it himself. Deuteronomy 31:24-26 has Moses order the book of the law placed beside the ark, and 17:18-19 requires each king to have a copy made and read it all his life.

Tore his clothes: Verse 19 records the king's reaction to hearing the book read. Tearing the garment from the neck down was the standard public sign of grief or horror in Israel, used at news of a death (Genesis 37:34), at defeat, and at blasphemy heard in court (Matthew 26:65). It was a visible act done in front of witnesses, which is why the text records who saw it.

Burning bones on the altars: Verse 5 has Josiah burn the bones of the pagan priests on their own altars. Contact with a human corpse or grave made a person or place unclean for seven days (Numbers 19:16), so scattering the dust of the idols over the graves of their worshippers and burning bones on the altars put those sites permanently out of use. 2 Kings 23:15-18 records him doing the same at Bethel.

The king's secretary: Shaphan in verses 8-18 is not a copyist. The sofer was a cabinet officer of the crown, listed with the army commander and the recorder among the king's chief men (2 Samuel 8:16-17), handling correspondence, records, and revenue; 2 Kings 12:10 has the king's secretary count the temple money alongside the high priest. Verse 8 sends him out with the governor of the city and the recorder, mazkir, another standing office of state.

Huldah: Verse 22 sends the high priest, the king's secretary, and three other senior officials to a woman living in the Second Quarter of Jerusalem to authenticate the book. Jeremiah and Zephaniah were both prophesying at the time, and the delegation went to Huldah. She is one of a small number of women named as prophets in Scripture, with Miriam, Deborah, and Noadiah (Exodus 15:20, Judges 4:4, Nehemiah 6:14).

Josiah becomes king at eight, spends his youth destroying idols and high places throughout Judah and Israel, and in the eighteenth year of his reign sends officials to repair the temple; during repairs Hilkiah discovers the Book of the Law, and Josiah tears his robes in distress when he hears it read. The prophet Huldah tells Josiah disaster will come on Judah but because of his repentant heart he will not see the coming disaster, so Josiah gathers all the people and renews the covenant to follow the LORD.

Direct textual basis

Repairs begin in the eighteenth year of the king who began at eight. Shaphan reads to Josiah. Huldah promises he will not see the coming disaster.

 2 Kings 22:3: The parallel account dates Josiah's commission to Shaphan to his eighteenth regnal year, confirming that the number describes his reign rather than his age.
2 Chronicles 35:20-24: Josiah is wounded in battle against Neco and dies in Jerusalem, supplying the later event to read beside Huldah's promise.
All implemented reference changes

Deuteronomy 31:24-26: After finishing the Book of the Law, Moses commands the Levites who carry the ark to place it beside the ark as a witness against Israel.
2 Kings 22:3: The parallel account dates Josiah's commission to Shaphan to his eighteenth regnal year, confirming that the number describes his reign rather than his age.
2 Chronicles 35:20-24: Josiah is wounded in battle against Neco and dies in Jerusalem, supplying the later event to read beside Huldah's promise.

The repairs begin in Josiah's eighteenth regnal year, about age twenty-six, and Shaphan reads the book to him. Huldah promises that he will be gathered to his grave in peace without seeing the coming disaster. The later account reports his death from battle wounds; the promise is not a description of an unwounded death.

All implemented note changes

S062
Ezra 4Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The summary turns a topical collection of later opposition letters into a chronological cause of the earlier temple halt.

Original app summary

Enemies of Judah and Benjamin offer to help build the temple and are refused; they discourage the builders, hire counselors against them, and write letters to Persian kings accusing Jerusalem of rebellion, until Artaxerxes orders the work stopped by force and construction ceases until the second year of Darius.

Original translation notes

The book changes language here: From 4:8 to 6:18, and again at 7:12-26, Ezra is written in Aramaic instead of Hebrew. Aramaic was the working language of Persian government from Egypt to India, so the letters, decrees and court records are quoted in the language they were actually filed in, while the narrative around them stays in Hebrew. The other Aramaic in Scripture is Daniel 2:4b-7:28, Jeremiah 10:11, and two words in Genesis 31:47.

Beyond the River: The Persian satrapy west of the Euphrates, taking in Syria, Phoenicia, Cyprus and the whole land bridge down to the Egyptian frontier. The name is written from the imperial capital looking west, so 'beyond the river' means beyond the Euphrates. Judah, called Yehud, was one small province inside it, and every letter in this chapter travels up and down that chain of command.

Rehum and Shimshai: These are job titles, not honorifics. Be'el te'em is the provincial officer responsible for reports and decrees, and the safra is the secretary who drafted and filed the correspondence. The complaint against Jerusalem goes forward through the ordinary provincial paperwork, correctly formatted and correctly addressed.

Tribute, custom and toll: Three separate Persian revenue streams, named again at 4:20 and 7:24. Mindah is the assessed tribute of a province, belo is a levy in kind or produce, halakh is a toll on goods in transit. The accusation in 4:13 is not religious; it is that a walled Jerusalem will stop paying all three.

The kings in this chapter are out of order: Verse 6 names Ahasuerus (Xerxes, 486-465 BC) and verses 7-23 name Artaxerxes (465-424 BC), both of whom reigned after Darius, and then 4:24 picks the story back up in the reign of Darius (522-486 BC). The chapter gathers the whole file of opposition letters in one place before returning to the sequence of events. Read it as a dossier dropped into the narrative, not as a calendar.

Samaria: The hill country north of Judah and the capital of the northern kingdom from about 880 BC until Assyria took it in 722 BC and deported the population. The men who offer to help build in 4:2 are descended from the settlers Assyria moved in afterwards, which is why they can say they have sacrificed to the God of Israel since Esarhaddon brought them up. The refusal in 4:3 is the beginning of the split that hardens into the Samaritan quarrel of the Gospels.

Enemies oppose the temple rebuilding from Cyrus to Darius. The chapter also recounts later accusations under Xerxes and Artaxerxes, including an order to halt rebuilding Jerusalem; verse 24 returns to the temple work halted until Darius's second year.

Direct textual basis

Verses 6-23 describe later city opposition under Xerxes and Artaxerxes; verse 24 resumes the temple account under Darius.

 Ezra 6:15: The temple is completed in Darius's sixth year, establishing its completion before the later Artaxerxes narrative.
Ezra 7:1: The narrative explicitly moves many years later to Artaxerxes' reign, distinguishing that setting from the temple's completion under Darius.
Nehemiah 2:1-8: Artaxerxes authorizes Nehemiah to rebuild Jerusalem and obtain timber for its gates and wall, supplying the later city-rebuilding setting.
All implemented reference changes

Ezra 6:15: The temple is completed in Darius's sixth year, establishing its completion before the later Artaxerxes narrative.
Ezra 7:1: The narrative explicitly moves many years later to Artaxerxes' reign, distinguishing that setting from the temple's completion under Darius.
Nehemiah 2:1-8: Artaxerxes authorizes Nehemiah to rebuild Jerusalem and obtain timber for its gates and wall, supplying the later city-rebuilding setting.

The chapter groups opposition from different reigns. The letters under Xerxes and Artaxerxes concern Jerusalem and its rebuilding; verse 24 returns to the earlier temple interruption under Darius. Artaxerxes' order is not the cause of an interruption that ended before his reign.

All implemented note changes

Be'el te'em is the provincial officer responsible for reports and decrees, and the safra is the secretary who drafted and filed the correspondence. The complaint against Jerusalem goes forward through the ordinary provincial paperwork, correctly formatted and correctly addressed.

Context and interpretation: The chapter groups opposition from different reigns. S063
Job 4Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The summary turns the reported spirit's comparative question into a categorical denial of human righteousness.

Original app summary

Eliphaz responds by reminding Job of his past wisdom and suggesting that the innocent do not perish, implying Job must have done wrong; he then recounts a frightening nighttime vision in which a spirit told him that mortals cannot be righteous before God and are fragile creatures who perish without understanding.

Original translation notes

Teman: A district of Edom, south and east of the Dead Sea, named for a grandson of Esau (Genesis 36:11,15). It had a standing reputation for wisdom, which is why Jeremiah taunts it: is there no longer any wisdom in Teman (Jeremiah 49:7)? Eliphaz is introduced by that address the way a modern speaker might be introduced by a university. Inscriptions found at Kuntillet Ajrud in the Sinai, written about the eighth century BC, refer to the LORD of Teman.

Deep sleep: Not ordinary sleep. This is the heavy trance that falls on a man when God is about to act or speak: on Adam before the woman is made (Genesis 2:21), on Abram at the covenant between the pieces (Genesis 15:12), on Saul's camp when David walks through it (1 Samuel 26:12). Eliphaz uses the word to claim his night experience was a genuine revelation and not a bad dream.

Houses of clay: An ordinary house was mud brick on a stone footing, and mud brick dissolves. A wall left unmaintained through two or three wet winters slumps back into the ground it came from, which is why ancient towns rebuilt on their own debris. Eliphaz means the human body, and the picture would land instantly on anyone who had watched an abandoned house melt.

Tent cords pulled up: A black goat-hair tent stands only by the tension between its poles and its guy ropes, staked into the ground. Pull the cords and the whole thing collapses at once. It was the standard picture for a life ending suddenly (Isaiah 38:12), and it fits Job, whose household lived in exactly such tents in verse 19 of chapter 1.

The lion: Two verses use five different Hebrew words for lion, and English has only the one, so the effect vanishes in translation. Lions lived in the Jordan thickets and the Syrian steppe into historical memory and were hunted by kings from Assyria to Persia; the last lions in the region were shot out during the nineteenth century. Eliphaz is piling up names for the strongest predator anyone knew, to say that even it starves.

Who is speaking from here on: This chapter opens the friends' speeches, which fill most of the book. At the end the LORD names Eliphaz the Temanite, the speaker here, and says 'My wrath is kindled against you and your two friends. For you have not spoken about Me accurately, as My servant Job has' (Job 42:7). He says it a second time in the next verse and orders seven bulls and seven rams brought so that Job can pray for them (42:8). Lines quoted out of chapters 4 through 25 are the friends talking, and the book records that verdict on them.

Eliphaz implies that Job's suffering exposes wrongdoing, then describes a frightening night encounter with a spirit. In the account he reports, the spirit asks whether a mortal can be more righteous than God and stresses human frailty.

Direct textual basis

In the app's text the question is whether a mortal can be more righteous than God or more pure than his Maker. Eliphaz is the speaker reporting the encounter.

 Job 1:1: The narrator introduces Job as blameless and upright, establishing the characterization against which Eliphaz's accusations must be read.
Job 42:7-8: The LORD rebukes Eliphaz and his two friends for speaking inaccurately about Him and directs them to seek Job's intercession.
All implemented reference changes

Job 42:7-8: The LORD rebukes Eliphaz and his two friends for speaking inaccurately about Him and directs them to seek Job's intercession.

Here the image conveys how suddenly a human life can end, with a related tent image in Isaiah 38:12. Job 1:18-19 places Job's children in their eldest brother's house when it collapses; it does not say they were living in tents.

S064
Job 24Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The takeaway makes the chapter sound like a settled explanation of prompt moral retribution while omitting its opening protest about delayed justice.

Original app summary

Job catalogs the injustices perpetrated by the wicked - stealing, exploiting the poor and vulnerable, committing murder and adultery under cover of darkness - and argues that despite their temporary success, God ultimately brings them down to death and obscurity.

Original translation notes

Boundary stones: Fields had no fences. The line between one man's land and the next was marked by stones, and moving them by a pace at a time quietly stole a crop and could not easily be proved. The law names it three times and puts a public curse on it (Deuteronomy 19:14; 27:17; Proverbs 22:28). In Babylonia the same crime was blocked with carved boundary stones that recorded the grant and called down curses on anyone who shifted them.

Taken in pledge: Seizing a widow's ox or the donkey of an orphan as loan security took away the animal they ploughed and carried with, so the security destroyed the ability to repay. The law forbids taking a millstone in pledge for the same reason: it would be taking a man's livelihood as security (Deuteronomy 24:6). Job is listing lending practices that were legal in form and ruinous in fact.

Glean the vineyards of the wicked: Gleaning was a legal right, not charity: the harvester was forbidden to reap the field's corners, go back for a forgotten sheaf, or strip a vine or olive tree twice, and what was left belonged to the poor, the widow, the orphan and the foreigner (Leviticus 19:9-10; Deuteronomy 24:19-21). Ruth lives on it (Ruth 2). Job's protest in verses 6 to 11 is that the poor now glean for the men who are robbing them.

Tread the winepresses but go thirsty: A winepress was two rock-cut basins, an upper treading floor where grapes were crushed underfoot and a lower vat the juice ran into through a channel. Hundreds are still cut into the bedrock across the hill country. Treading was hot, communal work done at harvest and traditionally accompanied by singing, and the treaders drank. Job's line describes men doing that work and not being allowed a mouthful.

Dig through houses: House walls were mud brick, so a thief did not pick a lock, he cut a hole through the wall. The law recognises the crime by that name and rules on a householder who kills a burglar found breaking in at night (Exodus 22:2-3). Jesus uses the same picture of the thief digging through (Matthew 6:19-20; 24:43). Verse 16 is a technical description of a real method.

Job asks why the Almighty's judgment is not visible while the vulnerable are robbed, exploited, and killed. He describes wicked people acting under darkness, then their insecurity and death, challenging anyone to refute his account.

Direct textual basis

Job asks why judgment is not visible, catalogs oppression, and later describes the wicked's insecurity and death.

 Job 21:7-16: Job protests that wicked people can enjoy long lives, secure homes, and prosperity, challenging a simple equation between present success and righteousness.
Job 42:7-8: The LORD rebukes Eliphaz and his two friends for speaking inaccurately about Him and directs them to seek Job's intercession.
All implemented reference changes

Job 21:7-16: Job protests that wicked people can enjoy long lives, secure homes, and prosperity, challenging a simple equation between present success and righteousness.
Job 42:7-8: The LORD rebukes Eliphaz and his two friends for speaking inaccurately about Him and directs them to seek Job's intercession.

This remains Job's speech in a debate. His opening question and the statement that God charges no one with wrongdoing describe the apparent delay of justice. The later description of the wicked's end does not erase that protest or prove that every sufferer is being punished for personal wrongdoing.

All implemented note changes

Context and interpretation: This remains Job's speech in a debate. His opening question and the statement that God charges no one with wrongdoing describe the apparent delay of justice. The later description of the wicked's end does not erase that protest or prove that every sufferer is being punished for personal wrongdoing.

Editorial rationale

The current notes explain the forms of oppression but do not preserve the opening question. This is a context proposal, not a claim that the closing verses are absent.

S065
Proverbs 11Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The summary can be read as a blanket promise of wealth and physical safety for every righteous person.

Original app summary

God favors honesty, humility, and righteousness while detesting dishonesty, pride, and wickedness; the righteous find safety and prosperity while the wicked face ruin through their own actions.

Original translation notes

Dishonest scales: Literally balances of deceit. There were no coins in Israel in Solomon's day; silver was weighed out on a balance against stone weights carried in a pouch, so a trader who kept a light set for selling and a heavy set for buying stole from every customer. The law bans exactly that in Leviticus 19:35-36 and Deuteronomy 25:13-16. Inscribed stone weights have been dug up in Judah, including the pym, the fee the Philistines charged for sharpening tools in 1 Samuel 13:21.

Gold ring in a pig's snout: The nezem is a nose ring, ordinary jewelry for a woman of means; Abraham's servant puts one on Rebekah's nose in Genesis 24:22 and 24:47. The pig was an unclean animal that no Israelite household kept (Leviticus 11:7; Deuteronomy 14:8). The proverb fastens the finest ornament a woman owned to the filthiest animal a listener could picture.

Puts up security for a stranger: To go surety is to make yourself liable for another man's debt, and the deal was closed by striking hands, taqa kaf (Proverbs 6:1-5; 17:18). If the debtor defaulted the creditor came after the guarantor. Proverbs 20:16 and 27:13 tell the lender to take the cloak of any man foolish enough to do this for a stranger, and 22:26-27 warns that the bed can be dragged out from under him.

The hoarder of grain: Grain was stored in sealed pits and jars and drawn down through the year, so in a lean season whoever held stock set the price of bread. The mashbir is the one who breaks into his store and sells; the monea bar holds it back until the price climbs. Amos 8:4-6 shows the same trade from the inside, dealers waiting for the new moon and the sabbath to be over so they can sell with a short measure and a heavy weight.

Wins souls: Literally takes lives. Nephesh is not an immaterial part of a person; it is the whole breathing creature, which is why Genesis 2:7 calls the man himself a living nephesh. The same two words joined together elsewhere mean to take a life away, as in Psalm 31:13, where enemies plot to take the psalmist's life.

These wisdom sayings commend honesty, humility, generosity, and righteousness, contrasting their life-giving effects with the ruin caused by deceit and greed. They warn against trusting riches and praise those who refresh others.

Direct textual basis

The chapter teaches the value and outcomes of integrity, generosity, and righteous conduct through wisdom sayings, not a narrated guarantee that the righteous never suffer.

 Job 21:7-16: Job protests that wicked people can enjoy long lives, secure homes, and prosperity, challenging a simple equation between present success and righteousness.
Ecclesiastes 7:15: The speaker observes a righteous person dying and a wicked person living long, supplying an explicit counterexample to guaranteed earthly safety.
All implemented reference changes

Job 21:7-16: Job protests that wicked people can enjoy long lives, secure homes, and prosperity, challenging a simple equation between present success and righteousness.
Ecclesiastes 7:15: The speaker observes a righteous person dying and a wicked person living long, supplying an explicit counterexample to guaranteed earthly safety.

These are wisdom sayings about righteous and wicked ways. They are not a promise that every faithful person will become wealthy or avoid suffering. Job and Ecclesiastes explicitly describe exceptions to a simple equation between present prosperity and righteousness.

All implemented note changes

Context and interpretation: These are wisdom sayings about righteous and wicked ways. They are not a promise that every faithful person will become wealthy or avoid suffering. Job and Ecclesiastes explicitly describe exceptions to a simple equation between present prosperity and righteousness.

Editorial rationale

The current notes explain trade and wealth but do not identify this genre limit. The proposal removes an especially broad prosperity formulation rather than rewriting every proverb as a conditional statement.

S066
Song of Songs 8Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

"Stronger than death" changes the comparison, and the last sentence assigns the family's little-sister speech to the woman.

Original app summary

The woman wishes her lover were her brother so she could show him affection publicly, declares that love is stronger than death and cannot be extinguished by any force, and urges him to come to her swiftly. She also reflects on protecting her younger sister and claims her vineyard as her own possession.

Original translation notes

If only you were my brother: 8:1 is not a wish to be related to him. A woman could greet a brother with a kiss in the street and nobody would think anything of it; showing affection to a man who was not kin in public brought contempt. She is saying she wishes she could touch him outside without paying for it, and 8:2 then takes him indoors to her mother's house.

Set me as a seal: A seal was a man's legal identity, not an ornament. It was cut from stone as a cylinder worn on a cord at the throat, or set as a stamp in a ring worn on the hand, and pressing it into clay signed a document and marked property as his. Judah hands over his seal and cord as a pledge (Genesis 38:18), Jezebel seals letters with Ahab's seal so they carry the king's authority (1 Kings 21:8), and Haggai 2:23 has the LORD make Zerubbabel like His signet ring. The heart and the arm named in 8:6 are the two places a seal was actually worn.

Fierce as Sheol: Sheol is the Old Testament word for the grave and the realm of the dead, where all descend (Genesis 37:35, Psalm 16:10), and the Septuagint renders it with the Greek hades. What 8:6 draws on is one property of it: Sheol does not let go and never gives anything back. Qin'ah is rendered jealousy or ardour and is the same word used for God's own zeal at Exodus 20:5.

The flame of the LORD: One Hebrew word at the end of 8:6, built from shalhevet, a flame, with the ending yah, the short form of the divine name that also closes hallelujah. It is the only place in the Song where any form of the name of God appears at all. Versions render it a most vehement flame or the very flame of the LORD, depending on whether the ending is read as a superlative or as the name.

All the wealth of his house: Marriage in Israel was contracted with a mohar, a bride price weighed out by the groom's family to the bride's (Genesis 34:12, Exodus 22:16-17). 8:7 puts that transaction on the table and says a man offering the entire estate of his house for love would be utterly despised, which is the one thing in the book priced and then refused a price.

We have a little sister: 8:8-9 is the brothers holding a family council. A girl's marriage was negotiated by the men of her household, and their question is what they will do when the offer comes. A wall gets battlements of silver and a door gets barred with cedar planks; either way they are deciding in advance what they will build around her. 8:10 is her answer, that she is a wall already.

The vineyard at Baal-hamon: A place named nowhere else in Scripture; the name reads as owner of a multitude. 8:11 has Solomon leasing the vineyard out to tenants, which is how a large holding was worked: the tenant farms it and pays the owner a fixed sum out of the crop. 8:12 then sets the arrangement aside, my own vineyard is before me.

A thousand shekels of silver: Rent on a leased vineyard, paid in weighed silver rather than coin. A shekel ran about four tenths of an ounce, so a thousand shekels is roughly twenty-five pounds of metal. Isaiah 7:23 prices planted vines at a thousand silver shekels for a thousand vines, one shekel a vine, which puts a vineyard of that rent at about that size. The two hundred held back at 8:12 for the men who tend the fruit is a fifth of the total.

The woman longs for open affection and declares love as strong as death, unquenchable and beyond purchase. The poem also asks what will be done for a little sister and records the woman's reply that she is a wall. The lovers close with an invitation to come swiftly.

Direct textual basis

Love is as strong as death. Verses 8-9 use a plural family voice, followed by the woman's reply in verse 10.

 Genesis 29:18-20: Jacob's love for Rachel makes seven years of service seem like a few days, providing a narrative parallel for the strength of love.
All implemented reference changes

Genesis 29:18-20: Jacob's love for Rachel makes seven years of service seem like a few days, providing a narrative parallel for the strength of love.

Verse 6 says love is as strong as death, not stronger. The speakers are not named in the Hebrew poem; verses 8-9 are commonly read as the brothers' words, with the woman replying in verse 10. They are not safely summarized as her plan to protect a younger sister.

All implemented note changes

We have a little sister: Verse 6 says love is as strong as death, not stronger. The speakers are not named in the Hebrew poem; verses 8-9 are commonly read as the brothers' words, with the woman replying in verse 10. They are not safely summarized as her plan to protect a younger sister.

Editorial rationale

The current little-sister note explicitly identifies the brothers' speech and her answer. The summary contradicts it.

S067
Isaiah 7Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

Note-focused: the summary follows the base translation, but the virgin/young-woman translation distinction and the two contextual horizons are not explained.

Original app summary

Rezin of Aram and Pekah son of Remaliah march against Jerusalem, and the LORD sends Isaiah to tell the trembling Ahaz that their plot will not stand; when Ahaz refuses to ask for a sign, the Lord gives one anyway, the virgin conceiving and bearing a son called Immanuel, and before the boy knows to reject evil the land of the two dreaded kings will be laid waste, yet the LORD will also bring the king of Assyria on Judah, a hired razor that leaves the land to curds and honey, briers and thorns.

Original translation notes

Immanuel: Name announced as the sign in 7:14 and echoed in 8:8-10.

Young woman: Word used in (7:14); Hebrew denotes a young woman of marriageable age; Greek renders with ‘virgin.’

Shear-jashub: Isaiah’s son accompanying him in 7:3.

Samaria: The central highland region between Judea and Galilee; capital of the northern kingdom of Israel from 880 BC until Assyrian deportation in 722 BC. The mixed-population descendants who remained ('Samaritans') were despised by Judeans as ethnically and religiously impure. Jesus' choice to travel through Samaria (John 4) and to make a Samaritan the hero of His parable (Luke 10) deliberately upends that prejudice.

Sheol: The Old Testament realm of the dead; a shadowy underworld where both righteous and wicked descend (Genesis 37:35; Psalm 16:10). Not necessarily a place of torment; more a state of cessation and silence. The Septuagint and New Testament render it with the Greek word hades.

Rezin and the son of Remaliah: Isaiah 7 sits inside a specific crisis. Rezin, king of Aram at Damascus, and Pekah son of Remaliah, king of the northern kingdom of Israel, joined against Assyria and marched on Jerusalem to force Judah into their alliance and put their own man, the son of Tabeal, on David's throne (7:1-6). Ahaz instead bought Assyrian help; 2 Kings 16:7-9 records the payment and the result, with Tiglath-Pileser taking Damascus and killing Rezin. This is the war behind everything Isaiah says to Ahaz here.

the road to the Launderer's Field: A fuller cleaned and finished newly woven wool by treading it in tubs, using stale urine for its ammonia and alkaline earth as a scouring agent, then bleaching and stretching it out to dry. The work needed running water and a large open drying field, and it stank, so the fuller worked outside the city walls. This spot by the upper pool's channel was the obvious place to meet a king inspecting his water supply before a siege, and Isaiah 36:2 has the Assyrian envoy stand on the same ground a generation later.

Rezin of Aram and Pekah son of Remaliah march against Jerusalem, and the LORD sends Isaiah to tell the trembling Ahaz that their plot will not stand; when Ahaz refuses to ask for a sign, the Lord gives one anyway, the virgin conceiving and bearing a son called Immanuel, and before the boy knows to reject evil the land of the two dreaded kings will be laid waste, yet the LORD will also bring the king of Assyria on Judah, a hired razor that leaves the land to curds and honey, briers and thorns.

Direct textual basis

The sign addresses Ahaz's crisis before the two threatening kings fall. Matthew later applies the Greek wording to Jesus' birth.

 2 Kings 16:5-9: Ahaz faces Rezin and Pekah's siege and appeals to Assyria for help, establishing the political crisis addressed by Isaiah's sign.
Matthew 1:22-23: Matthew presents Jesus' birth as fulfillment of the Immanuel prophecy, supplying the New Testament application alongside Isaiah's setting under Ahaz.
All implemented reference changes

2 Kings 16:5-9: Ahaz faces Rezin and Pekah's siege and appeals to Assyria for help, establishing the political crisis addressed by Isaiah's sign.
Matthew 1:22-23: Matthew presents Jesus' birth as fulfillment of the Immanuel prophecy, supplying the New Testament application alongside Isaiah's setting under Ahaz.
Deuteronomy 6:16: Israel is commanded not to test the LORD as at Massah, the command Ahaz invokes when he refuses to ask for a sign in Isaiah 7:12.

The Hebrew word almah is translated virgin here and young woman in some other versions. Isaiah 7:16 connects the sign with the crisis facing Ahaz; Matthew 1:22-23 applies the Greek wording to Jesus' virgin birth. The immediate setting and Matthew's application are both part of the biblical context.

All implemented note changes

Young woman: The Hebrew word almah is translated virgin here and young woman in some other versions. Isaiah 7:16 connects the sign with the crisis facing Ahaz; Matthew 1:22-23 applies the Greek wording to Jesus' virgin birth. The immediate setting and Matthew's application are both part of the biblical context.

Editorial rationale

Both contextual references are already linked. The current Immanuel note defines the name but omits the translation issue. Preserve the app's chosen verse translation; the proposal is an added context note, not a declaration that one reading is fraudulent.

S068
Isaiah 15Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The summary makes Isaiah personally the bringer of the final judgment.

Original app summary

Isaiah prophesies the sudden destruction of Moab's cities, describing widespread mourning, shaved heads, sackcloth, and weeping across the land as people flee and their resources are stripped away. The waters dry up, vegetation withers, and Isaiah declares he will bring additional judgment - a lion - upon Moab's fugitives and those remaining.

Original translation notes

Moab and Chemosh: Moab held the high plateau east of the Dead Sea, good sheep country, and it was a relative: Genesis 19:37 traces the nation to Lot. Its god was Chemosh, which is why Numbers 21:29 calls Moabites the people of Chemosh and 1 Kings 11:7 has Solomon building him a high place outside Jerusalem. What that worship could involve is recorded at 2 Kings 3:27, where the king of Moab, losing a siege, offered his own eldest son as a burnt offering on the city wall. The Mesha Stele, a Moabite victory inscription of the ninth century BC found at Dhiban in 1868, has that same king credit Chemosh with his wars against Israel.

Dimon / Dibon: Variant forms in (15:9) and (15:2) place associated with bloodshed imagery.

sackcloth: A coarse, dark goat-hair fabric worn against bare skin as a universal sign of mourning, repentance, or distress (1 Kings 21:27; Jonah 3:5; Matthew 11:21). Wearing it publicly broadcast grief or contrition; the discomfort itself was part of the symbolism.

The towns of the Moab oracle: 15:1-9 names about a dozen real towns on the plateau east of the Dead Sea and expects the reader to know the map. Several of them appear on a stone found in 1868 at Dhiban, the biblical Dibon: an inscription set up by Mesha king of Moab in the ninth century BC, which names Chemosh over and over, names Omri king of Israel, and names Nebo and Medeba as towns Mesha took back. The names in this chapter are places that were still standing when it was written.

Shaved heads and cut beards: 15:2-3 has Moab mourning with every head shaved, every beard cut, sackcloth in the streets and weeping on the housetops. Roofs were flat and were living space, so grief went on in public view. Israel was forbidden this particular form of it: Leviticus 19:27-28 and Deuteronomy 14:1 bar shaving the head and gashing the body for the dead, which is why the description reads as foreign practice.

Isaiah announces Moab's devastation, mourning, fleeing populations, and ruined water and vegetation. The oracle ends with a threat of further judgment, pictured as a lion against Moab's fugitives and survivors.

Direct textual basis

The prophetic oracle announces added judgment in the first person; Isaiah is its messenger, not the one personally inflicting it.

 Jeremiah 48:1-5: The LORD announces Moab's devastation and the mourning of its towns, providing a parallel divine oracle rather than an action personally inflicted by Isaiah.
All implemented reference changes

Jeremiah 48:1-5: The LORD announces Moab's devastation and the mourning of its towns, providing a parallel divine oracle rather than an action personally inflicted by Isaiah.

The first-person threat belongs to the prophetic announcement of judgment. The chapter does not depict Isaiah personally attacking Moab or bringing a lion against its refugees.

All implemented note changes

Context and interpretation: The first-person threat belongs to the prophetic announcement of judgment. The chapter does not depict Isaiah personally attacking Moab or bringing a lion against its refugees.

Editorial rationale

The current Moab notes give history and geography but do not distinguish the prophet's delivery of the oracle from the threatened action.

S069
Isaiah 16Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The summary gives the sheltering request to Moab as its duty, although the app's wording asks for refuge for Moab.

Original app summary

Moab is urged to send tribute and shelter fugitives, but Isaiah laments Moab's pride and her coming destruction - her vineyards will wither, her joy will cease, and within three years her splendor and people will be greatly diminished. A future ruler from David's house will establish a throne of justice and faithfulness.

Original translation notes

Sela: Location in 16:1 on the route mentioned.

As a hired worker counts: Precise time reckoning for three years (16:14).

Steadfast love: Term in 16:5 describing the established throne; listed without interpretation.

Send the lamb to the ruler: 16:1 is tribute, paid the way a herding country pays it. 2 Kings 3:4 gives the figures for the same arrangement: Mesha king of Moab was a sheep breeder and delivered a hundred thousand lambs and the wool of a hundred thousand rams to the king of Israel every year, and stopped when Ahab died. Sending the lamb from Sela to Zion is Moab asking for shelter with the payment in front.

The vines of Sibmah and the shout in the presses: 16:8-10 is Moab's wine country going silent. Grapes were trodden barefoot in a rock-cut vat with the juice running through a channel into a lower basin, and the treaders sang and called in rhythm to keep time; hedad is the word for that shout. 16:10 says the treader treads out no wine and the shout has been stopped, and Jeremiah 48:32-33 repeats the same lines about the same vineyards.

The oracle calls for tribute to Zion and voices an appeal for shelter for Moab's fugitives. It anticipates a faithful ruler in David's house, then laments Moab's pride and ruined vineyards and announces that its glory will fade within three years.

Direct textual basis

All implemented note changes

S070
Isaiah 19Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The summary turns the chapter's singular altar into multiple altars built by the cities.

Original app summary

Isaiah pronounces judgment on Egypt: the Lord will cause internal strife, dry up the Nile, devastate crops and livelihoods, and confound Egypt's leaders; yet he also promises that future Egyptian cities will speak Canaan's language, build altars to the Lord, and worship alongside Assyria and Israel.

Original translation notes

Zoan / Noph: Egyptian cities named in counsel and judgment contexts (19:11-13).

Nile / canals / reeds: River system and vegetation imagery for Egypt’s economy (19:5-7).

Altar… pillar: Sign and witness to the LORD in Egypt (19:19-20).

Highway: Road linking Egypt and Assyria in 19:23.

The flax workers and the weavers: 19:9 names the trade Egypt was known for across the ancient world. Flax was grown on the flooded fields, retted in water, combed out and spun into linen so fine that Egyptian linen was a luxury export everywhere, and it was the cloth Egyptian priests were required to wear. The whole industry depended on the Nile rising, which is why 19:5-10 runs from the water failing to the fishermen mourning to the weavers ruined, in that order.

Isaiah announces judgment on Egypt's society, river, livelihoods, and leaders. He also pictures five cities turning to the LORD, an altar and boundary pillar as witnesses, and Egypt, Assyria, and Israel worshiping under God's blessing.

Direct textual basis

Five cities speak the language of Canaan; an altar stands in Egypt's center and a pillar near its border.

All implemented note changes
