S071
Isaiah 50Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The summary denies the very selling and sending-away that the second half of verse 1 affirms, and the current legal-document note repeats the denial.

Original app summary

God denies divorcing Israel or selling them into bondage, asserting his power to rescue; a servant describes receiving instruction from God, enduring beatings and mockery, and trusting God's vindication. Those who fear the LORD should trust him in darkness, while those who rely on their own fires will lie down in torment.

Original translation notes

Set my face like flint: Fixed resolve amid abuse (50:7).

sackcloth: A coarse, dark goat-hair fabric worn against bare skin as a universal sign of mourning, repentance, or distress (1 Kings 21:27; Jonah 3:5; Matthew 11:21). Wearing it publicly broadcast grief or contrition; the discomfort itself was part of the symbolism.

Your mother's certificate of divorce: 50:1 names two legal instruments and asks to see the paperwork for either. A husband who divorced his wife had to put it in writing and hand it to her, which is the requirement at Deuteronomy 24:1-3, and the document was her protection. A man in debt could sell his children into service to clear it, which 2 Kings 4:1 and Nehemiah 5:5 show happening. The verse asks where the writ is and which creditor was paid, and answers that there was neither.

The beard and the spitting: The three things listed at 50:6 are not random violence, they are calculated humiliations. Beards were a mark of standing, so pulling one out was a public unmaking; when Hanun shaved off half the beards of David's envoys at 2 Samuel 10:4-5 it was an act of war and the men were kept away until they grew back. Spitting in the face carried disgrace in law as well, at Numbers 12:14 and Deuteronomy 25:9.

Morning by morning he awakens my ear: 50:4 uses the vocabulary of a pupil, not a master. Limmudim are those under instruction, the same word for disciples at 8:16. The verse has the ear woken each morning to listen like one being taught, and the purpose given is to know how to sustain with a word the one who is weary. 50:5 then says the ear was opened and he was not rebellious.

The LORD says Israel's sins, not a creditor's claim or lack of divine power, explain its estrangement, and He declares His power to redeem. The servant listens, endures abuse, and trusts God's vindication; those who fear the LORD are called to trust Him in darkness.

Direct textual basis

God asks for a divorce document or creditor, then says the people were sold for their iniquities and their mother sent away for transgressions. He remains able to redeem.

 Isaiah 54:7-8: The LORD acknowledges briefly forsaking His people and promises compassionate return, supplying restoration context for Isaiah 50's separation language.
Jeremiah 3:8: The LORD describes giving faithless Israel a divorce certificate and sending her away, confirming that prophetic divorce imagery cannot simply be denied.
Deuteronomy 24:1-4: The law describes divorce certificates and prohibits a first husband reclaiming his former wife after another marriage, supplying the legal background to divorce language.
All implemented reference changes

Deuteronomy 24:1-4: The law describes divorce certificates and prohibits a first husband reclaiming his former wife after another marriage, supplying the legal background to divorce language.
Isaiah 54:7-8: The LORD acknowledges briefly forsaking His people and promises compassionate return, supplying restoration context for Isaiah 50's separation language.
Jeremiah 3:8: The LORD describes giving faithless Israel a divorce certificate and sending her away, confirming that prophetic divorce imagery cannot simply be denied.

Verse 1 does not simply deny that a separation occurred: it explicitly says the people were sold for their iniquities and their mother sent away for their transgressions. The questions about a certificate and creditors challenge the grounds and finality of the estrangement, while verse 2 asserts God's power to redeem.

All implemented note changes

Your mother's certificate of divorce: Verse 1 does not simply deny that a separation occurred: it explicitly says the people were sold for their iniquities and their mother sent away for their transgressions. The questions about a certificate and creditors challenge the grounds and finality of the estrangement, while verse 2 asserts God's power to redeem.

Editorial rationale

The current note ends by saying there was neither legal instrument and can erase the verse's explanation. Jeremiah 3 also uses divorce imagery, so a blanket claim that God never speaks of sending Israel away would be misleading.

S072
Isaiah 66Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

"Rather than ritual sacrifices" can make the chapter abolish all sacrificial worship instead of condemning rebellious worship.

Original app summary

The LORD declares that true worship requires humility and obedience rather than ritual sacrifices, and promises that those who tremble at his word will see Jerusalem restored and all nations gathered to his holy mountain. He will judge the rebellious with fire while the faithful enjoy his comfort and peace.

Original translation notes

Contrite / tremble at My word: God’s chosen worship posture (66:2-5).

Worm… fire (end warning): Unending sign of judgment against persistent rebellion (66:24).

Worship: To bow down or prostrate oneself in humility. Used for reverence toward God (Genesis 22:5; Exodus 4:31; Psalm 95:6; Isaiah 66:23) but also for kings or angels. Very physical and posture-centered.

sabbath: The seventh day, set apart by God's command (Exodus 20:8-11). All work ceased from sunset Friday to sunset Saturday. By Jesus' day, rabbinical tradition had elaborated 39 categories of forbidden labor; many of Jesus' healings deliberately challenged those additional rules without violating the Torah command itself.

frankincense: A milky-white resin from the Boswellia tree of southern Arabia. Burned as incense in the temple (Exodus 30:34); offered with every grain offering. The magi's second gift, traditionally read as signaling priesthood.

Four offerings and four abominations: 66:3 pairs each lawful offering with something unthinkable: the ox with killing a man, the lamb with breaking a dog's neck, the grain offering with pig's blood, the incense with blessing an idol. Dogs were unclean scavengers, not pets, and breaking an animal's neck was how a firstborn donkey was destroyed if nobody redeemed it (Exodus 13:13). The verse does not say the offerings stopped; it says what they had become.

Tarshish, Pul, Lud, Tubal and Javan: 66:19 sends survivors to named nations at the far edge of the known world. Javan is Greece, the name used for the Greeks at Genesis 10:2 and Daniel 8:21. Tubal lay in Anatolia, Lud in Asia Minor, and Tarshish was the far western port the long-haul ships sailed for, the direction Jonah ran. The point of the list is distance: these are the places that have never heard the report or seen the glory.

The LORD esteems those who are humble and tremble at His word, and rejects worship joined to rebellion. He promises Jerusalem's restoration and the gathering of nations, while announcing judgment on the rebellious and comfort for His people.

Direct textual basis

The LORD esteems humility and condemns offerings from people choosing abominations; the same chapter still describes offerings and worship.

 Isaiah 1:11-17: The LORD rejects offerings joined to bloodshed and demands justice, identifying the rebellious conduct behind the condemnation of worship.
Malachi 1:6-14: Priests are rebuked for defective offerings while pure offerings are anticipated, distinguishing corrupt worship from a rejection of every offering.
All implemented reference changes

Malachi 1:6-14: Priests are rebuked for defective offerings while pure offerings are anticipated, distinguishing corrupt worship from a rejection of every offering.
Isaiah 1:11-17: The LORD rejects offerings joined to bloodshed and demands justice, identifying the rebellious conduct behind the condemnation of worship.

The contrast is with worshipers who choose their own ways and delight in abominations. The chapter later still describes offerings, priests, and worship, so it does not simply say that every ritual offering is rejected.

All implemented note changes

Context and interpretation: The contrast is with worshipers who choose their own ways and delight in abominations. The chapter later still describes offerings, priests, and worship, so it does not simply say that every ritual offering is rejected.

Editorial rationale

The current contrition note identifies the desired posture but does not address this overstatement. Malachi's flawed-offering rebuke supplies the closest existing context.

S073
Jeremiah 17Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

"Sin is permanent" turns deeply engraved guilt into a claim that repentance or healing is impossible.

Original app summary

Jeremiah declares Judah's sin is permanent and warns that those who trust in flesh rather than the LORD will wither like desert shrubs, while those who trust in him will flourish like trees by water. He then receives instruction to stand at Jerusalem's gates and tell the people to keep the Sabbath holy, promising that obedience will bring prosperity and continued rulership, while disobedience will result in fire consuming the city's fortresses.

Original translation notes

Iron pen with a diamond point: Two tools from stone-cutting. An iron stylus scratched soft surfaces; a harder tip, shamir, was needed to cut stone or gemstone, and the same word is used in Ezekiel 3:9 and Zechariah 7:12 for the hardest thing there is. Verse 1 says the sin is engraved this way on the tablet of the heart and on the horns of the altar, meaning it is cut in, not written on.

Horns of the altar: Four projections rising from the corners of the altar. Blood from a sin offering was smeared on them, as in Leviticus 4:7, and a man in danger of his life could seize them for sanctuary, as Adonijah and Joab do in 1 Kings 1:50 and 2:28. Verse 1 says the guilt is carved into the very place where guilt was supposed to be dealt with.

A shrub in the desert: Verses 5-8 set two plants side by side. One is a scrub bush in the salt flats and the parched wilderness, which sees no rain and cannot tell when good comes. The other is a tree with roots run out to a watercourse; it does not notice the heat and keeps bearing in a drought year. In a country where the difference between the two is a matter of where the roots reach, the picture needs no explanation.

A partridge hatching eggs it did not lay: Verse 11 draws on a piece of country lore about the partridge sitting on another bird's clutch and losing the brood when they hatch and go. It is applied to a man who gets rich by injustice and finds it gone by mid-life.

Bearing a burden through the gates: The Sabbath sermon in 17:19-27 targets one specific act: carrying goods in and out of the city gates on the seventh day. Gates were where markets were held and where produce entered, so a load coming through the gate on the Sabbath means business was open. Nehemiah 13:15-22 later shuts the gates from dusk on the sixth day for exactly this reason.

Judah's sin is pictured as engraved on its heart and altars. Jeremiah contrasts trust in people with trust in the LORD, prays for healing, and delivers a Sabbath warning: obedience brings continued life in the city, while refusal brings consuming fire.

Direct textual basis

Sin is pictured as engraved on hearts and altars. The chapter also asks for healing and gives a conditional call to obey.

 Jeremiah 31:31-34: The LORD promises a new covenant with law written on hearts and sins forgiven, providing context against reading engraved guilt as beyond forgiveness.
Ezekiel 36:26-27: The LORD promises a new heart and His Spirit enabling obedience, supplying the restoration counterpart to a deeply corrupted heart.
All implemented reference changes

Jeremiah 31:31-34: The LORD promises a new covenant with law written on hearts and sins forgiven, providing context against reading engraved guilt as beyond forgiveness.
Ezekiel 36:26-27: The LORD promises a new heart and His Spirit enabling obedience, supplying the restoration counterpart to a deeply corrupted heart.

The engraved writing pictures guilt deeply embedded in the people and their worship. It does not state that God cannot forgive or change them: verse 14 asks for healing, and Jeremiah 31 promises God's law written on the heart and sins forgiven.

All implemented note changes

Context and interpretation: The engraved writing pictures guilt deeply embedded in the people and their worship. It does not state that God cannot forgive or change them: verse 14 asks for healing, and Jeremiah 31 promises God's law written on the heart and sins forgiven.

Editorial rationale

The current altar-horns note explains the location of the inscription but not the summary's stronger permanence claim. The later heart-writing promise is essential context.

S074
Jeremiah 22Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

"None ... will rule Judah again" can erase the text's Davidic-throne scope and imply that no descendant can ever hold any office.

Original app summary

Jeremiah delivers judgment against the Judean royal palace and successive kings, warning that disobedience will bring ruin, exile, and disgrace, with specific condemnation of Jehoiakim's oppression and Jehoiachin's removal from power. The palace will become a wasteland, the kings will lose their thrones, and none of Jehoiachin's descendants will rule Judah again.

Original translation notes

Three names for one king: Judah's last kings each carry more than one name, which makes the chapter hard to follow. Shallum in 22:11 is Jehoahaz, Josiah's son, deposed after three months. Coniah in 22:24 is Jehoiachin, also called Jeconiah in Matthew 1:11. Throne names were assigned at accession, sometimes by the foreign king doing the appointing, as 2 Kings 23:34 and 24:17 both record.

Cedar and vermilion: Cedar came by sea from Lebanon and was the most expensive building timber in the region, used for Solomon's temple and palace. Vermilion is a red pigment from mercury ore, used to paint panelling. Verse 14 lists them as the marks of a palace built with unpaid labour, which is why the next verse asks whether cedar makes a king.

The burial of a donkey: Verse 19 says Jehoiakim will be dragged out and thrown beyond the gates of Jerusalem. A dead pack animal was hauled outside the walls and left. For a king of Judah, who expected a tomb among his fathers in the city of David, this is the announcement that there will be no tomb and no mourning.

Signet ring: A carved seal, worn on the hand or on a cord, pressed into wet clay to seal documents and jars. It was the owner's legal signature and never left him. Verse 24 says that even if Coniah were the signet on God's right hand, He would pull it off. Haggai 2:23 later says the opposite of Zerubbabel, Jehoiachin's grandson.

Write this man down as childless: Verse 30 explains its own terms in the same breath: none of his offspring will prosper, sitting on David's throne and ruling in Judah. Jehoiachin had sons, listed in 1 Chronicles 3:17-18, and 52:31-34 has him alive and eating at the Babylonian king's table. The sentence is about the throne, not about descendants.

Jeremiah denounces royal injustice and announces disgrace and exile for Judah's kings. Jehoiachin is judged as a failed royal line: none of his offspring will prosper on David's throne in Judah.

Direct textual basis

The judgment says none of Coniah's offspring will prosper sitting on David's throne and ruling in Judah; later Zerubbabel is a governor and receives signet language.

 Haggai 1:1: Zerubbabel is identified as governor of Judah under Darius, establishing his office in the later setting alongside the judgment on Jehoiachin's royal line.
Haggai 2:23: The LORD calls Zerubbabel His chosen servant and likens him to a signet ring, supplying the later signet image alongside Jeremiah's rejection of Coniah.
Matthew 1:11-12: Matthew includes Jeconiah, Shealtiel, and Zerubbabel in the genealogy, showing descendants continuing beyond the exile.
All implemented reference changes

Matthew 1:11-12: Matthew includes Jeconiah, Shealtiel, and Zerubbabel in the genealogy, showing descendants continuing beyond the exile.
Haggai 1:1: Zerubbabel is identified as governor of Judah under Darius, establishing his office in the later setting alongside the judgment on Jehoiachin's royal line.
Haggai 2:23: The LORD calls Zerubbabel His chosen servant and likens him to a signet ring, supplying the later signet image alongside Jeremiah's rejection of Coniah.

The judgment concerns prosperity on David's throne in Judah. Zerubbabel, a later descendant, appears as governor rather than king, and Haggai calls him God's chosen signet. Matthew still includes Jeconiah in the genealogy; the passage cannot be reduced to a claim that he had no biological children.

All implemented note changes

Zerubbabel, a later descendant, appears as governor rather than king, and Haggai calls him God's chosen signet. S075
Jeremiah 31Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

"All people know him directly" loses the covenant-membership scope and can sound like an unconditional statement about everyone.

Original app summary

The LORD promises to restore Israel from exile, gathering them from distant lands and establishing a new covenant where his law is written on their hearts and all people know him directly; Jerusalem will be rebuilt and made holy.

Original translation notes

Rachel weeping for her children: Jacob's wife, mother of Joseph and Benjamin, buried on the road near Bethlehem according to Genesis 35:19. Ramah in 31:15 is a town in Benjamin about five miles (8 km) north of Jerusalem, and 40:1 records it as the staging camp where prisoners were chained before the march to Babylon. The verse puts the mother of the northern tribes at a deportation depot, hearing her descendants pass.

New covenant: Verses 31-34 are the only place in the Old Testament where the phrase new covenant appears. Four things are named: God's instruction written on the heart rather than on stone, the relationship formula I will be their God and they will be My people, everyone knowing Him without needing to be taught, and sins forgiven and not remembered. Hebrews 8:8-12 quotes the whole passage.

Set up road markers: Verse 21 tells the exiles going out to plant markers and guideposts along the way, and to note the highway they are travelling. Caravan routes were marked with cairns and pillars so a traveller could find the road back across country with no landmarks. The instruction assumes the return trip before the outward journey is finished.

The sour grapes proverb: Verse 29 quotes a saying doing the rounds in Judah: the fathers have eaten sour grapes and the children's teeth are set on edge. It is a complaint about inherited punishment. Verse 30 cancels it, and Ezekiel 18:1-4 takes the same saying and answers it at chapter length.

The fixed order of sun, moon and stars: Verses 35-37 stake the promise on two things nobody can move: the daily and nightly order of the lights, and the unmeasured size of the heavens and the foundations of the earth. The argument is a wager. If the first can be broken, the second falls too.

The valley of the dead bodies and the ashes: Verse 40 names the Hinnom ravine and the ash dumps south of Jerusalem, the ground where children had been burned at Topheth in 7:31 and 19:5. The chapter ends by declaring that ground holy to the LORD and inside the rebuilt city.

The LORD promises to gather and restore His people and make a new covenant with Israel and Judah, writing His law on their hearts and forgiving their sins. All His covenant people will know Him, and Jerusalem will be rebuilt and made holy.

Direct textual basis

The new covenant is named with Israel and Judah; all within that covenant know the LORD.

 Hebrews 8:8-12: Hebrews quotes the covenant promise to Israel and Judah, including shared knowledge of the Lord and forgiveness, preserving the promise's covenant setting.
Hebrews 10:16-17: Hebrews repeats the promises of law on hearts and sins remembered no more, linking the heart-writing language with covenant forgiveness.
All implemented reference changes

Hebrews 8:8-12: Hebrews quotes the covenant promise to Israel and Judah, including shared knowledge of the Lord and forgiveness, preserving the promise's covenant setting.
Hebrews 10:16-17: Hebrews repeats the promises of law on hearts and sins remembered no more, linking the heart-writing language with covenant forgiveness.
Genesis 35:16-20: Rachel dies giving birth to Benjamin and is buried on the way to Bethlehem. This supplies family background for the image of Rachel mourning her children in Jeremiah 31:15, where the voice is heard in Ramah.

The promise that all will know the LORD belongs to the new covenant announced with Israel and Judah. It is not phrased as a statement that every person already knows God regardless of that covenant. Hebrews quotes this promise in its account of Christ's covenant.

All implemented note changes

New covenant: The promise that all will know the LORD belongs to the new covenant announced with Israel and Judah. It is not phrased as a statement that every person already knows God regardless of that covenant. Hebrews quotes this promise in its account of Christ's covenant. Verses 31-34 are the only place in the Old Testament where the phrase new covenant appears.

Editorial rationale

The current new-covenant note names everyone knowing Him but does not restate the antecedent. Both Hebrews references are already present.

S076
Jeremiah 34Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

"Die peacefully" can imply escape or a comfortable end, although the oracle first promises captivity and the later narrative records imprisonment.

Original app summary

Jeremiah tells Zedekiah that Babylon will capture Jerusalem and burn it, though Zedekiah himself will die peacefully; later, God condemns the people for freeing their Hebrew slaves and then re-enslaving them, promising them death by sword, plague, and famine instead.

Original translation notes

Cut a covenant: Hebrew does not make a covenant, it cuts one, and 34:18 shows why. The parties cut an animal in two and walked between the halves. The unspoken sentence was let this be done to me if I break my word, which is why God says the men who passed between the calf's pieces and then broke the oath will be handed over and become food for birds.

The seventh-year release: Exodus 21:2 and Deuteronomy 15:12 require a Hebrew bought as a slave to be freed after six years of service. Verse 14 says the fathers did not listen and the law had been ignored for generations. Under siege the owners suddenly kept it, and 34:11 records that as soon as the Babylonian army withdrew they took their freed people back. The word deror is the same one used for the Jubilee proclamation in Leviticus 25:10.

Lachish and Azekah: Verse 7 says these two were the last fortified cities of Judah still holding out besides Jerusalem. Lachish guarded the approach from the coastal plain about twenty-five miles (40 km) southwest of Jerusalem; Azekah sat between them in the Elah valley. A group of inscribed potsherds found in the gate of Lachish, written during this war, includes a soldier reporting that he is watching for the fire signals of Lachish because he can no longer see Azekah.

You will die in peace: Verse 5 promises Zedekiah death in peace with the customary funeral fire and the lament Alas, lord. It is not a promise of escape. 39:7 and 52:11 record that he was blinded and taken to Babylon in bronze fetters, and 52:11 adds that he stayed in prison there until the day of his death, dying a captive rather than by the sword.

Spices burned at a royal funeral: The burning promised in 34:5 is not cremation, which Israel did not practise. It is a fire of aromatics kindled in the king's honour, recorded for Asa in 2 Chronicles 16:14 and denied to Jehoram in 2 Chronicles 21:19. Withholding it was an insult; granting it, as here, is the promise of a proper burial.

Jeremiah tells Zedekiah that Jerusalem will burn and he will be taken to Babylon, though he will not die by the sword. God then condemns the people for freeing Hebrew slaves and taking them back, announcing judgment for the broken covenant.

Direct textual basis

Zedekiah will be captured and taken to Babylon but will not die by the sword; later he is blinded and imprisoned until death.

 Jeremiah 39:6-7: Zedekiah sees his sons killed, is blinded, and is taken in chains toward Babylon, showing that the peace promise did not mean escape from capture.
Jeremiah 52:11: Zedekiah is taken to Babylon and kept in custody until death, specifying his captive end alongside the promise that he would not die by the sword.
All implemented reference changes

Jeremiah 39:6-7: Zedekiah sees his sons killed, is blinded, and is taken in chains toward Babylon, showing that the peace promise did not mean escape from capture.
Jeremiah 52:11: Zedekiah is taken to Babylon and kept in custody until death, specifying his captive end alongside the promise that he would not die by the sword.

Death in peace is set against death by the sword, not against captivity. The oracle says Zedekiah will be taken to Babylon; the later account says he was blinded and remained imprisoned until death.

All implemented note changes

S077
Ezekiel 4Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

Commands for a symbolic action are narrated as a fully documented uninterrupted performance with an explained historical timetable.

Original app summary

Ezekiel draws Jerusalem on clay and acts out its siege with an iron pan as a wall; he then lies on his left side for 390 days bearing Israel's sin, then on his right side for 40 days bearing Judah's sin, eating rationed bread baked over cow dung to symbolize how Jerusalem will face famine and defilement.

Original translation notes

The brick: A sun-dried mud brick, the standard building block of Mesopotamia and also its writing surface: scribes drew and wrote on damp clay. Verse 1 has Ezekiel scratch a plan of Jerusalem onto one, which is a Babylonian mapmaker's act. Clay tablets carrying city plans have been recovered from Mesopotamia, so the sign uses the local medium.

The siege works: Verse 2 names the whole kit in order. The siege wall is a wall of circumvallation thrown up around the city so nothing gets in or out. The ramp is a packed earth and rubble slope built up against the wall to bring men and machines level with the top; the Assyrian assault ramp at Lachish still stands. The camps are the troop enclosures, and the battering rams are timber frames on wheels housing an iron-headed beam.

The iron plate: A flat iron griddle, ordinary kitchen equipment used for baking flatbread over a fire; the same word appears in Leviticus 2:5. Verse 3 has him stand it on edge between himself and the model city as an iron wall. A cooking plate becomes a barrier, in the chapter that is about to ration his food.

Twenty shekels of food a day: A shekel weighed about two fifths of an ounce (11.4 grams), so twenty shekels is roughly eight ounces (230 grams) of bread for a whole day, weighed out and eaten at set times. That is a starvation ration, and it is measured rather than served because under siege food stopped being a meal and became an allowance.

A sixth of a hin of water: A hin is about a gallon (3.8 litres), so a sixth of a hin is roughly two thirds of a quart, about 21 fluid ounces (0.6 litre), for a day. A working adult needs several times that in a hot climate. The measured water in 4:11 is the same message as the weighed bread: the city's cisterns are being counted out cup by cup.

Baking over dung: Dried animal dung was the ordinary cooking fuel in a land short of firewood, and still is in parts of the region. What God first commands in 4:12 is human excrement, which Deuteronomy 23:12-14 requires to be buried outside the camp. Ezekiel protests in 4:14 that he has never eaten anything unclean, and the fuel is changed to cow dung. The horror is the point: under siege even the fire is defiled.

390 days and 40 days: Verses 4-6 assign a day for each year, the same reckoning Numbers 14:34 uses. He lies 390 days on his left side for the northern kingdom and 40 on his right for Judah, 430 in all, which is the number of years Exodus 12:40 gives for Israel in Egypt. The bread of 4:9 is a famine loaf of six mixed grains and pulses, wheat, barley, beans, lentils, millet and spelt, thrown together because no one ingredient is left in quantity.

The LORD commands Ezekiel to portray Jerusalem's siege, lie on his sides for days assigned to Israel's and Judah's guilt, and eat measured food and water. After Ezekiel objects to human excrement as fuel, God permits cow dung; the sign depicts siege scarcity and exile defilement.

Direct textual basis

God commands the enacted siege and assigns days for years. The chapter records the command and Ezekiel's fuel objection, not a diary of every day's performance.

 Numbers 14:34: Forty days of spying correspond to forty years of bearing guilt, supplying another explicit prophetic day-for-year reckoning.
Exodus 12:40: The text gives 430 years for Israel's stay in Egypt, supplying the numerical comparison made with Ezekiel's assigned days.
2 Kings 25:1-4: Jerusalem endures a Babylonian siege and severe famine before its defenses are breached, supplying the historical siege context for Ezekiel's sign.
All implemented reference changes

2 Kings 25:1-4: Jerusalem endures a Babylonian siege and severe famine before its defenses are breached, supplying the historical siege context for Ezekiel's sign.
Numbers 14:34: Forty days of spying correspond to forty years of bearing guilt, supplying another explicit prophetic day-for-year reckoning.
Exodus 12:40: The text gives 430 years for Israel's stay in Egypt, supplying the numerical comparison made with Ezekiel's assigned days.

The 390 and 40 days are explained as days for years, but the chapter does not identify a calendar start and end for those years. It gives prophetic instructions and the exchange about fuel; further reconstructions of how the daily sign was performed remain interpretations.

All implemented note changes

390 days and 40 days: The 390 and 40 days are explained as days for years, but the chapter does not identify a calendar start and end for those years. It gives prophetic instructions and the exchange about fuel; further reconstructions of how the daily sign was performed remain interpretations.

Editorial rationale

The current days note supplies the numerical comparison with Egypt but states the performance directly. The proposal separates the text's command from an unrecorded daily reconstruction without denying that Ezekiel obeyed.

S078
Ezekiel 10Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The summary and coal note narrate the actual scattering as observed, though the chapter records the order, receipt of coals, and departure.

Original app summary

A man in linen takes burning coals from among the cherubim and scatters them over the city as the LORD's glory rises from above the cherubim and moves toward the temple threshold. The cherubim, each with four faces and accompanied by four wheels covered in eyes, rise upward with the LORD's glory and move toward the east gate of the temple.

Original translation notes

Cherubim: Not infants with wings. They are the guardian figures of God's throne: stationed at Eden's gate with a flaming sword in Genesis 3:24, worked in gold over the ark in Exodus 25:18-22, carved into the temple walls and doors in 1 Kings 6:29-35, and here bearing the throne itself. Composite winged guardian creatures flanking thrones and doorways are standard in the art of the whole region.

The glory leaves in stages: The departure is deliberately slow and is tracked step by step: from above the cherubim to the threshold in 10:4, back over the cherubim in 10:18, out to the east gate in 10:19, up from the middle of the city in 11:23, and finally to the hill east of Jerusalem. Nothing is destroyed until it has gone. The same route runs in reverse in 43:1-5.

Coals scattered over the city: Verse 2 sends the man in linen in among the wheels to take fire from between the cherubim and throw it over Jerusalem. The fire comes from the throne itself. Verse 7 has a cherub hand it to him, so the burning of the city in 2 Kings 25:9 is shown here as beginning with fire carried out of the sanctuary.

Full of eyes: Verse 12 says the whole bodies of the cherubim, their backs, hands, wings and the wheels, were covered with eyes all around. It follows chapter 8, where the elders in the hidden room say the LORD does not see us. The vision answers that in the plainest way available.

The face of a cherub: In 1:10 the four faces are man, lion, ox and eagle. In 10:14 the ox has been replaced by the face of a cherub, and 10:20 states that Ezekiel now recognises the creatures from the Kebar as cherubim. The book records the identification changing as he understands what he saw the first time.

The LORD orders the man in linen to scatter burning coals over Jerusalem; he receives the coals and goes out. The glory of the LORD moves from the temple threshold to the cherubim and the east gate as Ezekiel sees their faces and wheels.

Direct textual basis

The man is commanded to scatter coals, receives them, and goes out. The scattering itself is not separately narrated.

 2 Kings 25:9: The Babylonian destruction burns the temple, palace, and Jerusalem's major buildings, supplying the later historical fire associated with Ezekiel's judgment vision.
Ezekiel 43:1-5: The LORD's glory returns through the east gate and fills the temple, reversing the departure described in Ezekiel 10.
All implemented reference changes

Ezekiel 43:1-5: The LORD's glory returns through the east gate and fills the temple, reversing the departure described in Ezekiel 10.
2 Kings 25:9: The Babylonian destruction burns the temple, palace, and Jerusalem's major buildings, supplying the later historical fire associated with Ezekiel's judgment vision.

Verse 2 commands scattering coals over the city, and verse 7 records the man receiving them and going out. The chapter does not separately describe the scattering. The vision signifies judgment as the LORD's glory departs; the later burning of Jerusalem is a related event, not another detail Ezekiel reports seeing here.

All implemented note changes

S079
Ezekiel 42Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

Note-focused: five hundred cubits follows this translation, but readers comparing the Hebrew or other versions encounter reeds/rods and a materially different scale.

Original app summary

The man shows Ezekiel priests' rooms on the north and south sides of the temple courtyard where holy offerings will be stored and eaten; priests must change out of their ministry garments before leaving the holy area. He then measures the entire temple complex and finds it forms a perfect square, five hundred cubits on each side, with a wall separating the holy from the common.

Original translation notes

Chambers for the priests: Rooms built into the temple courts, used as offices, stores and dining rooms. Verse 13 says the priests eat the most holy offerings there and lay up the grain, sin and guilt offerings. Under Leviticus 6:16 and Numbers 18:9-10 the priests' portion had to be eaten inside the sanctuary precinct, so the building is a purpose-made refectory and strongroom.

Changing out of the holy garments: Verse 14 makes it a rule of movement: a priest who has been serving may not walk out into the outer court until he has taken off the clothes he ministered in, because those garments are holy. 44:19 repeats it and adds the reason, so that he does not transmit holiness to the people by touching them with the cloth. Holiness in these chapters behaves like something that can be carried on fabric.

Five hundred by five hundred: Verses 15-20 close the survey by measuring the outer boundary on all four sides: five hundred cubits each way, a perfect square, with a wall around it. Verse 20 states the wall's whole purpose in one line, to separate the holy from the common. In long cubits that is roughly 875 feet (267 m) on a side, about seventeen acres.

A walkway ten cubits wide: Verse 4 puts a passage in front of the chamber blocks, ten cubits wide and a hundred long, with the doors opening onto it from the north. The vision keeps describing circulation, where a person walks and which way a door faces, because the whole point of the layout is controlling who goes where.

The man shows Ezekiel priests' rooms on the north and south sides of the temple courtyard where holy offerings will be stored and eaten; priests must change out of their ministry garments before leaving the holy area. He then measures the entire temple complex and finds it forms a perfect square, five hundred cubits on each side, with a wall separating the holy from the common.

Direct textual basis

The app prints five hundred cubits. The Hebrew and ancient-version handling of the measurement has produced differing English renderings.

 Ezekiel 40:5: The measuring rod is defined as six long cubits, establishing why a measurement in reeds or rods differs in scale from cubits.
Ezekiel 45:2: The sanctuary's section is described as five hundred cubits square with surrounding open land, providing a related dimension for comparison.
All implemented reference changes

Ezekiel 40:5: The measuring rod is defined as six long cubits, establishing why a measurement in reeds or rods differs in scale from cubits.
Ezekiel 45:2: The sanctuary's section is described as five hundred cubits square with surrounding open land, providing a related dimension for comparison.

This translation gives the enclosure as five hundred cubits on each side. Other versions render the measurement in reeds or rods, reflecting a textual and unit question. Ezekiel 40:5 defines a measuring reed as six long cubits, so the two renderings do not represent the same physical size.

All implemented note changes

S080
Ezekiel 48Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

"The city with the LORD's sanctuary" can place the sanctuary inside the city instead of its separate priestly district.

Original app summary

The twelve tribes receive their territorial allotments arranged north to south; a sacred square in the center contains the priest's portion, Levite lands, and the city with the LORD's sanctuary, and the city's gates are named after the tribes of Israel.

Original translation notes

Strips from east to west: The old allotments in Joshua 13 to 19 followed terrain and were unequal, and Judah's portion dwarfed some others. This map ignores geography: thirteen parallel bands running east to west across the whole country, seven tribes north of the sacred district and five south, each the same width. Nobody gets the better hills, and the tribes descended from the slave-born sons, Dan, Asher, Naphtali and Gad, are no worse placed than the rest.

The sacred square: Verses 8-20 set aside a block 25,000 cubits on each side in the middle of the country: 25,000 by 10,000 for the priests with the sanctuary in it, the same for the Levites, and 25,000 by 5,000 for the city and its farmland. At the long cubit of 40:5 that square runs roughly eight miles (13 km) a side. The prince gets the land on either side of it, so he is next to the sanctuary and not in it.

Twelve gates for twelve tribes: Verses 30-34 give the city three gates on each of its four sides, each named for a tribe. Levi gets a gate, and Joseph is one gate instead of two, so Ephraim and Manasseh are counted together and the twelve comes out even. Revelation 21:12-13 describes the same arrangement, twelve gates with the names of the twelve tribes, three to a side.

The LORD Is There: The last line of the book, 48:35, gives the city a new name meaning The LORD Is There. Everything from chapter 8 onward has turned on presence: the glory leaving the temple, the exiles told in 11:16 that God had been a sanctuary to them in small measure, and the glory returning in 43:1-5. The book closes by naming the city after the one thing it had lost.

Eighteen thousand cubits around: Verse 35 gives the city's perimeter as 18,000 cubits, four sides of 4,500 each, which at the long cubit is roughly one and a half miles (2.4 km) a side. That is a planned square, not the ridge-and-valley shape of the real Jerusalem. The name in the same verse, and not the dimensions, is what the chapter ends on.

The tribes receive their allotments from north to south. The central district has separate priestly land containing the sanctuary, Levitical land, and city land, with the prince's land beside it. The city's gates bear the tribes' names, and its name is The LORD Is There.

Direct textual basis

The holy district contains separate priestly and Levitical allocations; the sanctuary is in the priestly portion and the city occupies the remaining strip.

 Ezekiel 45:1-6: The sanctuary stands within the priests' portion, with adjacent Levitical and city allocations, confirming the separation of sanctuary land and city land.
Revelation 21:12-13: The city has twelve gates named for Israel's tribes, three on each side, providing the later parallel to Ezekiel's tribal gates.
All implemented reference changes

Revelation 21:12-13: The city has twelve gates named for Israel's tribes, three on each side, providing the later parallel to Ezekiel's tribal gates.
Ezekiel 45:1-6: The sanctuary stands within the priests' portion, with adjacent Levitical and city allocations, confirming the separation of sanctuary land and city land.

The sanctuary stands in the priestly allotment. The city and its common-use land form a separate strip within the larger district; the sanctuary is not placed inside the city. The chapter ends with the city's name, The LORD Is There.

All implemented note changes

Context and interpretation: The sanctuary stands in the priestly allotment. The city and its common-use land form a separate strip within the larger district; the sanctuary is not placed inside the city. The chapter ends with the city's name, The LORD Is There.

Editorial rationale

The current sacred-square note already distinguishes the three strips. The summary should preserve that spatial distinction and the chapter's concluding name.

S081
Hosea 3Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The summary states a buy-back of Gomer as certain, while the woman's identification depends on translation; a current cross-reference also calls thirty shekels twice the total price despite the barley.

Original app summary

God commands Hosea to buy back his unfaithful wife for silver and barley, requiring her to live with him in separation; the passage compares this to how Israel will eventually return to seek the LORD after a long period without king, sacrifice, or idols.

Original translation notes

Buy-back price: 3:2 pays fifteen shekels of silver plus a homer and a lethech of barley. The verb is karah, to purchase or acquire, and it is not the ordinary word for buying goods. This is a woman already his wife being paid for a second time, out of whatever hand held her.

What the buy-back price was worth: Half the payment at 3:2 is metal and half is grain. Silver was weighed out, not counted; a shekel ran about four tenths of an ounce, roughly eleven grams, so fifteen shekels is close to six ounces of silver. Thirty shekels was the fixed compensation for a slave killed by an ox (Exodus 21:32) and the sanctuary valuation of a woman (Leviticus 27:4), so this is exactly half the standard figure. A homer is ten ephahs, a little over six bushels, and a lethech is half a homer, which puts the barley at roughly nine bushels.

Barley, not wheat: Barley was the cheap grain, poor man's bread and animal feed, harvested weeks before the wheat. The law prices a barley field accordingly (Leviticus 27:16), and the jealousy offering at Numbers 5:15 is barley flour with no oil and no incense on it, the plainest offering in the system. Paying half of 3:2 in barley says what kind of transaction this was.

Raisin cakes: Pressed cakes of dried grapes, festival food handed out at public celebrations (2 Samuel 6:19). At 3:1 they are what Israel loves while turning to other gods, because the harvest feasts at the Baal shrines ran on the same food and drink. Jeremiah 7:18 shows the same pattern in Judah, whole households baking cakes for the queen of heaven.

Pillar, ephod, teraphim: 3:4 lists what Israel will go without, and every item is a physical object. A matsevah is a standing stone erected at a shrine, which Deuteronomy 16:22 forbids outright. The ephod is the priestly vestment used to seek an answer from God (1 Samuel 23:9-12). Teraphim are household gods, the figures Rachel stole from her father (Genesis 31:19) and the one Michal put in a bed to fake a sleeping man (1 Samuel 19:13), and Ezekiel 21:21 shows them used for divination.

Without king or prince: 3:4 strips out two systems at once: the state, king and officer, and the whole apparatus of worship, sacrifice and pillar and ephod and household gods. What is left is many days of neither. 3:5 then says afterward they will seek the LORD and David their king, so the sentence is bounded and the text says so.

At the LORD's command, Hosea shows love again to an adulterous woman, paying silver and barley and requiring a period of sexual restraint. The sign depicts Israel's time without its institutions and its later return to the LORD and David its king.

Direct textual basis

Hosea pays fifteen shekels plus barley for the woman. The app reads "your wife again"; the Hebrew wording is rendered differently in other versions and does not repeat Gomer's name.

 Hosea 1:2-3: Hosea marries the specifically named Gomer after the LORD's command, supplying the earlier identification considered when reading the unnamed woman in chapter 3.
Exodus 21:32: Thirty shekels compensate the master of a servant gored by an ox, providing a legal comparison rather than a stated total value for Hosea's silver and barley.
All implemented reference changes

Exodus 21:32: Thirty shekels compensate the master of a servant gored by an ox, providing a legal comparison rather than a stated total value for Hosea's silver and barley.
Hosea 1:2-3: Hosea marries the specifically named Gomer after the LORD's command, supplying the earlier identification considered when reading the unnamed woman in chapter 3.

This translation identifies the woman as Hosea's wife again, commonly understood as Gomer, but the chapter does not repeat her name and other versions say a woman. Verse 2 gives fifteen shekels plus barley; comparing only the silver with the thirty-shekel payment in Exodus 21:32 does not establish the total value of the purchase.

All implemented note changes

Buy-back price: This translation identifies the woman as Hosea's wife again, commonly understood as Gomer, but the chapter does not repeat her name and other versions say a woman. Verse 2 gives fifteen shekels plus barley; comparing only the silver with the thirty-shekel payment in Exodus 21:32 does not establish the total value of the purchase.

What the buy-back price was worth: Verse 3:2 names fifteen shekels of silver plus a homer and a lethech of barley. Silver was weighed rather than counted; at roughly eleven grams per shekel, the silver alone is about six ounces. A homer is ten ephahs and a lethech is half a homer, so the grain amounts to one and a half homers. The text does not assign that barley a silver value, so the total cannot be identified as exactly half the thirty-shekel compensation in Exodus 21:32.

Barley, not wheat: Barley was the cheap grain, poor man's bread and animal feed, harvested weeks before the wheat. The law prices a barley field accordingly (Leviticus 27:16), and the jealousy offering at Numbers 5:15 is barley flour with no oil and no incense on it, the plainest offering in the system. Barley forms part of the payment in 3:2, alongside fifteen shekels of silver.

Translation source notes

Editorial rationale

The current Exodus reference says thirty shekels is twice what Hosea pays, ignoring the barley. The proposed wording distinguishes the chosen translation and common identification from what is named explicitly.

S082
Obadiah 1Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The summary can make the individual Esau attack the individual Jacob during Jerusalem's fall.

Original app summary

Edom's pride in its rock-cleft heights will not save it: allies will betray it and its treasures will be pillaged, because Esau did violence to his brother Jacob, gloating, looting, and cutting off fugitives on the day strangers cast lots for Jerusalem. The Day of the LORD is near for all nations, but on Mount Zion there will be deliverance; the house of Jacob will be a blazing fire and the house of Esau stubble, and the kingdom will belong to the LORD.

Original translation notes

Edom / Esau / Seir: People/land descended from Esau dwelling in Mount Seir (the rugged highlands south of Judah).

Messenger: The report/mission that calls nations against Edom (1:1).

Clefts of the rock: High rock dwellings (cf. Sela/Petra) Edom trusted for safety (1:3).

Eagle: Image of height/speed; even there God says He will bring Edom down (1:4).

Samaria: The central highland region between Judea and Galilee; capital of the northern kingdom of Israel from 880 BC until Assyrian deportation in 722 BC. The mixed-population descendants who remained ('Samaritans') were despised by Judeans as ethnically and religiously impure. Jesus' choice to travel through Samaria (John 4) and to make a Samaritan the hero of His parable (Luke 10) deliberately upends that prejudice.

Edom's pride and defenses will fail because Esau's descendants betrayed their kin, the people of Judah, during Jerusalem's disaster. They gloated, looted, and intercepted fugitives. The Day of the LORD brings judgment, deliverance on Zion, and the LORD's kingdom.

Direct textual basis

The oracle addresses Edom and Judah through their ancestral names; it concerns nations descended from the brothers.

 Genesis 36:1-8: Esau is identified with Edom and settles his household in Seir, supplying the ancestral basis for the national names in Obadiah.
Numbers 20:14-21: Israel addresses Edom as a brother, but Edom refuses passage and threatens force, supplying an earlier example of conflict between the related peoples.
Psalm 137:7: The psalm recalls Edom urging Jerusalem's destruction, paralleling Obadiah's charge against the later nation rather than the patriarch personally.
All implemented reference changes

Genesis 25:29-34: Esau exchanges his birthright for Jacob's red stew and is associated with the name Edom, supplying the ancestral background for Obadiah's national names.
Genesis 36:1-8: Esau is identified with Edom and settles his household in Seir, supplying the ancestral basis for the national names in Obadiah.
Numbers 20:14-21: Israel addresses Edom as a brother, but Edom refuses passage and threatens force, supplying an earlier example of conflict between the related peoples.
Psalm 137:7: The psalm recalls Edom urging Jerusalem's destruction, paralleling Obadiah's charge against the later nation rather than the patriarch personally.

Esau and Jacob function here as ancestral names for Edom and Israel or Judah. The oracle is about later peoples and Jerusalem's disaster, not an account of the two patriarchs personally taking part in that event.

All implemented note changes

Edom / Esau / Seir: Esau and Jacob function here as ancestral names for Edom and Israel or Judah. The oracle is about later peoples and Jerusalem's disaster, not an account of the two patriarchs personally taking part in that event.

Editorial rationale

The current Edom/Esau note defines the relationship, but the summary switches from Edom to an unqualified personal Esau. Existing references already include the descendants and earlier national hostility.

S083
Micah 1Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

"Plague of her wickedness" turns the wound of judgment into wickedness spreading from Samaria.

Original app summary

Micah declares God's judgment against Samaria and Jerusalem for their idolatry and sin; God will destroy Samaria and the plague of her wickedness will spread to Judah, causing mourning across the towns of Judah as the people go into exile.

Original translation notes

Harlot's wages (shrine earnings): 'Etnan is the specific word for a prostitute's fee, and it is used here for the income of Samaria's idol shrines (1:7). Deuteronomy 23:18 bars that money from ever being brought into the house of the LORD. Micah says the silver Samaria collected as a harlot's hire will be stripped off her images and end up paying harlots again.

Samaria: The capital of the northern kingdom, built by Omri around 880 BC on a hill he bought for two talents of silver (1 Kings 16:24). It stood alone above the valley, which is why 1:6 pictures its stones poured down the slope and its foundations laid bare. Assyria besieged it three years and deported its people in 722 BC (2 Kings 17:5-6), inside Micah's own lifetime.

Lachish: Judah's second city, about 30 miles southwest of Jerusalem, guarding the road up from the coastal plain, and a chariot base, which is why 1:13 tells it to harness the team. Sennacherib stormed it in 701 BC and covered a room of his palace at Nineveh with carved reliefs of the siege, now in the British Museum. The earthen assault ramp his army raised against the southwest corner is still on the site.

Moresheth-gath: Micah's own town (1:1), in the Shephelah, the low chalk hills between the coastal plain and the Judean highlands, near Gath and roughly 25 miles southwest of Jerusalem. Its neighbours in this same list, Lachish, Mareshah and Adullam, all sit in that corridor, which is the ground an army coming up from the coast has to cross. Micah is naming the towns of his own district as the invasion route.

The town names of 1:10-15: Every town in this list is punned on its own name, and English can carry none of it. Beth-leaphrah means house of dust, so roll in the dust. Shaphir means fair, and its people go by stripped. Tsa'anan sounds like the verb to go out, and its people do not come out. Maroth sounds like bitter, and it waits for good. Lachish sounds like rekhesh, the word for the chariot team it is told to harness. Achzib sounds like akhzav, a wadi that runs dry when you need it. Mareshah sounds like yoresh, the conqueror who takes possession.

Bald as the eagle: Nesher covers both the eagle and the griffon vulture, and the vulture is the bird in view at 1:16 because its head and neck are bare of feathers. Shaving the head was a mourning rite (Job 1:20, Amos 8:10). Walking barefoot and stripped at 1:8 is the same language of grief, and Isaiah 20:2-4 has a prophet doing it as a public sign.

Micah announces judgment on Samaria and Jerusalem for idolatry. Samaria's devastation is pictured as an incurable wound reaching Judah and Jerusalem, and Micah laments the towns facing disaster and exile.

Direct textual basis

Micah mourns Samaria's incurable wound reaching Judah and Jerusalem in the context of coming destruction.

 2 Kings 17:5-6: Assyria captures Samaria after a three-year siege and deports Israelites, supplying the destruction setting behind Micah's wound imagery.
2 Kings 18:13-17: Assyria captures Judah's fortified cities and sends a force to Jerusalem, paralleling Micah's image of devastation reaching Judah's gate.
All implemented reference changes

2 Kings 17:5-6: Assyria captures Samaria after a three-year siege and deports Israelites, supplying the destruction setting behind Micah's wound imagery.
2 Kings 18:13-17: Assyria captures Judah's fortified cities and sends a force to Jerusalem, paralleling Micah's image of devastation reaching Judah's gate.

The wound reaching Judah is part of the oracle's picture of devastation and judgment. The verse does not call it a contagious spread of Samaria's wickedness into Judah.

All implemented note changes

Context and interpretation: The wound reaching Judah is part of the oracle's picture of devastation and judgment. The verse does not call it a contagious spread of Samaria's wickedness into Judah.

Editorial rationale

The current Assyrian conquest references already provide the relevant historical setting, but no note explains the wound image.

S084
Micah 3Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The summary repeats butchery imagery as a bare historical action without identifying the metaphor for exploitation.

Original app summary

Leaders who hate good and love evil tear the skin and flesh of the people, and prophets proclaim peace for whoever feeds them, so God will hide his face and leave the seers without answer; Micah, filled with power by the Spirit of the LORD, declares that because rulers judge for bribes, priests teach for a price, and prophets divine for money while leaning on the LORD's presence, Zion will be plowed like a field and Jerusalem become a heap of rubble.

Original translation notes

Divination: Forbidden practice tied to paid oracles (3:11). Micah contrasts this with true word from God.

Bribe: A payment to a judge to bend a verdict. The law bans it in the same words twice, that a bribe blinds those who see and twists the words of the righteous (Exodus 23:8; Deuteronomy 16:19), and Deuteronomy 27:25 puts a public curse on anyone who takes one to kill an innocent man. Micah 3:11 says Jerusalem's judges took bribes openly and still said the LORD was among them.

Priests who teach for a price: Teaching the law was part of a priest's job, not a service for sale (Leviticus 10:11; Deuteronomy 33:10). Priests were given no tribal land and lived on the tithes and assigned portions of the offerings instead (Numbers 18:20-21), so they were already provided for. Charging for a ruling meant the ruling went to whoever paid.

Zion plowed like a field: This sentence was still being quoted roughly a century later. In Jeremiah 26:17-19 the elders of Judah name Micah the Moreshite, repeat this verse word for word, and point out that Hezekiah did not put him to death for saying it. The quotation is what kept Jeremiah from being executed.

Micah depicts corrupt rulers as butchers devouring the people and condemns prophets who promise peace for payment. Filled with the LORD's Spirit, he exposes injustice and announces that Zion and Jerusalem will be ruined despite the leaders' false confidence.

Direct textual basis

Micah describes predatory rulers through skinning, chopping, and cooking imagery, then explicitly condemns bribery and paid prophecy.

 Ezekiel 22:27-29: Officials are likened to wolves while extortion and oppression are explicitly named, supplying a parallel for Micah's predatory imagery.
Jeremiah 26:18-19: Judah's elders quote Micah's warning about Jerusalem and recall Hezekiah seeking God, preserving the prophecy's later reception.
All implemented reference changes

Jeremiah 26:18-19: Judah's elders quote Micah's warning about Jerusalem and recall Hezekiah seeking God, preserving the prophecy's later reception.
Ezekiel 22:27-29: Officials are likened to wolves while extortion and oppression are explicitly named, supplying a parallel for Micah's predatory imagery.

The skinning and cooking language portrays the rulers' predatory treatment of the people. The chapter identifies their concrete corruption as bribery, paid teaching, and paid prophecy; the imagery is not a separate historical report that those rulers literally ate their subjects.

All implemented note changes

Context and interpretation: The skinning and cooking language portrays the rulers' predatory treatment of the people. The chapter identifies their concrete corruption as bribery, paid teaching, and paid prophecy; the imagery is not a separate historical report that those rulers literally ate their subjects.

Editorial rationale

The current references already include Ezekiel's parallel devouring imagery, but there is no explanatory note. The proposal makes the poetic image visible without softening the abuse.

S085
Micah 6Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The summary makes the LORD ask what offering would satisfy Him; the offering questions are spoken by a worshiper facing the LORD.

Original app summary

The LORD calls the mountains to witness his accusation against Israel, reminding them of his past acts and asking what offering they think will satisfy him; he tells them he requires justice, mercy, and humility, then pronounces judgment on their dishonest dealings and empty harvests because they have followed the evil ways of Omri and Ahab.

Original translation notes

Balak and Balaam, Shittim to Gilgal: At 6:5 the LORD's case points to two things the reader is expected already to know. Balak king of Moab hired Balaam to curse Israel and the curse kept coming out as blessing (Numbers 22-24). Shittim was the last camp east of the Jordan (Numbers 25:1) and Gilgal the first camp west of it (Joshua 4:19), so the two names are the start and the end of the crossing into the land.

Shall I give my firstborn?: 6:7 climbs a ladder of offerings, calves, then thousands of rams, then ten thousand rivers of oil, and ends at burning a child. That was a live practice in the region and not a figure of speech: 2 Kings 3:27 has the king of Moab offer his eldest son on the wall, and 2 Kings 16:3 and 21:6 record kings of Judah passing their own sons through the fire. The law bans it outright at Leviticus 18:21 and 20:2-5.

Ten thousands of rivers of oil: Revavah is the Hebrew for ten thousand and stands for any uncountable amount. Olive oil was pressed by the jar and stored by the jar, never by the streambed, so 6:7 is offering a physical impossibility on purpose. Each rung of the ladder costs more, and the answer at 6:8 costs nothing and is the one thing not offered.

Do justice, love loyal love, walk humbly: Mishpat is the court's verdict, what a judge is supposed to hand down. Chesed is loyalty inside a covenant, the faithfulness owed to someone you are bound to, which is why both mercy and steadfast love render it. Hatsnea lekhet is to walk close in, without display. The three answer the sacrifices piled up in 6:6-7.

Short ephah: The ephah was the standard dry measure for grain, about 22 quarts, the amount Ruth carried home after a day of gleaning (Ruth 2:17). A short ephah is a scoop cut down so the seller hands over less than he charges for (6:10). Leviticus 19:35-36 and Deuteronomy 25:14-15 require a full measure by name, and Amos 8:5 catches merchants at the same trick.

Dishonest scales and deceitful weights: Weights were cut stones carried in a pouch, and 6:11 pictures a bag of them. The fraud was simple: keep two sets, a heavy stone for buying and a light one for selling. Deuteronomy 25:13 forbids exactly that, two kinds of weight in your bag, a large and a small, and Proverbs 20:23 calls a false balance no good thing.

Statutes of Omri, works of the house of Ahab: Omri founded Samaria, and his son Ahab married Jezebel of Sidon and built a temple of Baal in it (1 Kings 16:23-33). Their policy is the standard 6:16 measures Judah against, a century later and in the southern kingdom, which is the sting. Omri was known outside Israel too; Assyrian records went on calling the northern kingdom the house of Omri for generations after his line ended.

The LORD calls creation to hear His case and recalls His saving acts. A worshiper asks what offering could answer the charge, even imagining a firstborn; the answer is justice, mercy, and humble walking with God. The chapter then condemns fraudulent trade and the ways of Omri and Ahab.

Direct textual basis

The LORD recounts His faithfulness, then a human voice asks about offerings, including a firstborn; verse 8 answers with justice, covenant love, and humble walking.

 Deuteronomy 12:31: Israel must not imitate the nations' worship that burns sons and daughters, providing the Torah's prohibition of child sacrifice.
1 Samuel 15:22: Samuel declares obedience better than sacrifice, supplying a parallel to Micah's answer about what the LORD requires.
Hosea 6:6: The LORD calls for mercy and knowledge of God above sacrifice, paralleling Micah's correction of an escalating focus on offerings.
All implemented reference changes

Deuteronomy 12:31: Israel must not imitate the nations' worship that burns sons and daughters, providing the Torah's prohibition of child sacrifice.
1 Samuel 15:22: Samuel declares obedience better than sacrifice, supplying a parallel to Micah's answer about what the LORD requires.
Hosea 6:6: The LORD calls for mercy and knowledge of God above sacrifice, paralleling Micah's correction of an escalating focus on offerings.

The questions in verses 6-7 come from the one asking how to approach the LORD, not from God requesting ever larger sacrifices. The firstborn question is part of that escalating rhetoric; it is not a command to sacrifice a child. Verse 8 states the answer.

All implemented note changes

Shall I give my firstborn?: The questions in verses 6-7 come from the one asking how to approach the LORD, not from God requesting ever larger sacrifices. The firstborn question is part of that escalating rhetoric; it is not a command to sacrifice a child. Verse 8 states the answer.

Editorial rationale

The current Ahaz reference mentions child sacrifice, but neither the cross-references nor a note explicitly separates the human question from divine instruction. This is especially relevant to the Jephthah discussion.

S086
Malachi 2Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The summary makes one difficult rendering of 2:16 appear uncontested, and the current references largely miss the marriage context.

Original app summary

Priests who refuse to honor God's name fall under a curse, for the covenant with Levi called for reverence and true instruction, but their teaching makes many stumble; Judah profanes the sanctuary by marrying the daughter of a foreign god, men break faith with the wife of their youth though God hates divorce and seeks godly offspring, and the people weary the LORD by asking, 'Where is the God of justice?'

Original translation notes

Defective offerings: Blind, lame, or sick animals were forbidden as sacrifices (1:8).

Covenant with Levi: A ministry covenant marked by fear of God, true teaching, and turning many from sin (2:4-8).

The dung of your feasts: The offal of a sin offering, the hide, the entrails and the dung, was carried outside the camp and burned on the ash heap; it could not stay near the altar (Exodus 29:14; Leviticus 4:11-12; Leviticus 16:27). Malachi 2:3 takes that waste and puts it on the faces of the priests. The one thing that had to be removed from the sanctuary is smeared on the men who serve in it.

Covers his garment with violence: Spreading the edge of your garment over a woman was the gesture that claimed her as wife. Ruth asks Boaz for exactly that in Ruth 3:9, and Ezekiel 16:8 uses it of the LORD covering Israel and entering a covenant with her. Malachi says the same garment comes back covered in violence. Deuteronomy 24:1-4 is the law he is speaking against, which allowed a man to write a certificate of divorce and send his wife away.

Malachi condemns priests who corrupt their calling and men who betray their covenant wives. He calls the people to faithfulness in worship and marriage, warns against violence, and rebukes their claim that God approves evildoers or neglects justice.

Direct textual basis

The passage condemns betrayal of a covenant wife. English translations differ over the subject and wording of verse 16.

 Deuteronomy 24:1-4: The law describes divorce certificates and prohibits a first husband reclaiming his former wife after another marriage, supplying the legal background to divorce language.
Matthew 19:3-9: Jesus answers a divorce dispute by citing creation, explaining Moses' concession, and stating an exception for sexual immorality, supplying further marriage context.
1 Corinthians 7:10-15: Paul discusses separation, reconciliation, mixed-belief marriages, and an unbelieving spouse's departure, supplying the distinct circumstances addressed in later divorce teaching.
All implemented reference changes

Deuteronomy 24:1-4: The law describes divorce certificates and prohibits a first husband reclaiming his former wife after another marriage, supplying the legal background to divorce language.
Matthew 19:3-9: Jesus answers a divorce dispute by citing creation, explaining Moses' concession, and stating an exception for sexual immorality, supplying further marriage context.
1 Corinthians 7:10-15: Paul discusses separation, reconciliation, mixed-belief marriages, and an unbelieving spouse's departure, supplying the distinct circumstances addressed in later divorce teaching.

The Hebrew of verse 16 is difficult. This translation reads God as saying He hates divorce; others refer to the man who hates and divorces his wife. Both readings occur in a passage condemning marital betrayal and violence. Other biblical discussions of divorce belong alongside this verse rather than being replaced by a slogan.

All implemented note changes

The Hebrew of verse 16 is difficult. This translation reads God as saying He hates divorce; others refer to the man who hates and divorces his wife. Both readings occur in a passage condemning marital betrayal and violence. Other biblical discussions of divorce belong alongside this verse rather than being replaced by a slogan.

S087
Matthew 7Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The condition by a measure one cannot bear is not what Jesus says; it can replace the warning against hypocritical judgment with an invented test of personal tolerance.

Original app summary

Jesus forbids judging by a measure one cannot bear, promises that everyone who asks receives, and sums up the Law and the Prophets in doing to others as you would have them do to you; he sets the narrow gate against the broad way, exposes false prophets by their fruit and empty 'Lord, Lord' claims, and ends with the houses built on rock and on sand.

Judge: Command warns against condemning others while ignoring one’s own faults (7:1-5).

Holy / pearls… dogs / pigs: Proverb about guarding sacred value from those who will trample or tear (7:6).

Law and the Prophets: Golden Rule presented as their ethical summary (7:12).

S088
Matthew 17Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The takeaway and stater note present finding the coin and paying the tax as completed events. The chapter ends with Jesus' instruction.

Original app summary

On a high mountain Jesus is transfigured, Moses and Elijah appear, and the Father's voice commands the disciples to listen to him; coming down, Jesus identifies Elijah's coming with John the Baptist, frees a boy his little-faith disciples could not, saying mustard-seed faith moves mountains, again foretells his death and rising, and pays the temple tax with the coin from a fish's mouth.

Original translation notes

Transfigured: Changed in appearance/glory before the three.

Beloved Son… listen to him: Heavenly affirmation combining royal/prophetic motifs.

drachma: A Greek silver coin roughly equivalent to a Roman denarius; one day's wage. The lost coin of Luke 15:8 is a drachma; a woman's dowry might be ten drachmas worn as a headpiece, which is why losing one is so distressing.

stater: A silver coin worth four drachmas; exactly the temple tax for two people. The coin Peter finds in the fish's mouth (Matthew 17:27) is a stater, paying the tax for both Jesus and Peter.

mustard seed: The seed of the black mustard plant (Sinapis nigra); proverbially the smallest seed a Galilean farmer planted (about 1 mm across), growing into a 10-foot shrub. Jesus uses it as His image for faith (Matthew 17:20) and for the kingdom of heaven (Matthew 13:31-32).

didrachma: Two drachmas; the annual half-shekel temple-tax amount (Matthew 17:24). Each adult Jewish male was assessed this amount.

On a high mountain Jesus is transfigured, Moses and Elijah appear, and the Father's voice commands the disciples to listen to him; coming down, Jesus identifies Elijah's coming with John the Baptist, frees a boy his little-faith disciples could not, saying mustard-seed faith moves mountains, again foretells his death and rising, and tells Peter to obtain a coin from a fish's mouth and use it to pay the temple tax for them both.

Direct textual basis

Matthew 17:24-27 records the tax question and Jesus telling Peter to catch a fish, find the coin, and pay for both of them; it does not narrate Peter carrying out those steps.

 Exodus 30:13-16: The LORD requires a half-shekel offering from those counted, rich and poor alike, for sanctuary service, providing the Torah background to the temple-tax question.
All implemented reference changes

Exodus 30:13-16: The LORD requires a half-shekel offering from those counted, rich and poor alike, for sanctuary service, providing the Torah background to the temple-tax question.

The stater is the four-drachma coin specified in Jesus' instruction to Peter, enough for two two-drachma payments (17:24-27). Matthew records the instruction and promise; he ends the scene without narrating the catch or payment.

All implemented note changes

stater: The stater is the four-drachma coin specified in Jesus' instruction to Peter, enough for two two-drachma payments (17:24-27). Matthew records the instruction and promise; he ends the scene without narrating the catch or payment.

Editorial rationale

Revise the existing stater note's the coin Peter finds claim as well as the summary.

S089
Matthew 19Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

Permits divorce only for sexual immorality turns a specific saying about divorce and remarriage into a comprehensive permission rule without preserving the sentence's scope.

Original translation notes

Certificate of divorce: Document mentioned in Deut 24:1 (19:7).

Sexual immorality: General term for illicit sexual relations (19:9).

Eunuchs: Includes those physically unable to marry and those choosing celibacy for the kingdom (19:12).

Pharisee: A lay reform movement emphasizing strict observance of the written Torah PLUS an oral tradition (the 'tradition of the elders'). Believed in bodily resurrection, angels, and a coming Messiah. Jesus grounds marriage in the Creator making them male and female, one flesh not to be separated, says that a man who divorces his wife, except for sexual immorality, and marries another commits adultery, and blesses the little children; a rich young man goes away grieving rather than sell all and follow, so Jesus tells the astonished disciples that a camel passes a needle's eye easier than a rich man enters the kingdom, yet with God all things are possible, and promises thrones and a hundredfold to those who left everything.

1 Corinthians 7:10-16: Paul tells spouses not to separate and addresses a further situation: a believer should remain with a willing unbelieving spouse, but let an unbeliever who leaves depart.

All implemented note changes

Editorial rationale
