S090
Matthew 20Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The takeaway begins by narrating a teaching story as an event, without identifying it as Jesus' parable.

Original app summary

A landowner pays the eleventh-hour workers the same denarius as the first, for the last will be first; going up to Jerusalem, Jesus foretells his condemnation, crucifixion, and third-day rising, answers the request of Zebedee's sons by making service the way to greatness, since the Son of Man came to give his life as a ransom for many, and opens the eyes of two blind men near Jericho.

Original translation notes

Denarius: Standard day’s wage in the period (20:2-10).

Eleventh hour: About one hour before the workday ends (20:6-9).

Evil eye: Idiomatic for envious/resentful outlook (20:15).

denarius / denarii: A Roman silver coin equal to one day's wage for a laborer (Matthew 20:2). 100 denarii were 3 to 4 months of wages. Bore the image of the emperor (Mark 12:16), making it the focus of the 'render to Caesar' tax question.

third / sixth / ninth hour: The Jewish day was divided into twelve daylight hours starting at sunrise (~6 a.m.). The third hour is about 9 a.m., the sixth is noon, the ninth is 3 p.m. Jesus is crucified at the third hour (Mark 15:25); darkness comes at the sixth, He dies at the ninth.

In Jesus' parable, a landowner pays the eleventh-hour workers the same denarius as the first, for the last will be first; going up to Jerusalem, Jesus foretells his condemnation, crucifixion, and third-day rising, answers the request of Zebedee's sons by making service the way to greatness, since the Son of Man came to give his life as a ransom for many, and opens the eyes of two blind men near Jericho.

Direct textual basis

Matthew 20:1-16 explicitly presents a kingdom comparison spoken by Jesus, not a new historical incident in the travel narrative.

 Luke 15:11-32: In Jesus' parable, a father welcomes his returning younger son and answers the older son's anger at the celebration, a comparison with resentment of the landowner's generosity.
All implemented reference changes

Luke 15:11-32: In Jesus' parable, a father welcomes his returning younger son and answers the older son's anger at the celebration, a comparison with resentment of the landowner's generosity.

Jesus introduces the vineyard workers as a comparison for the kingdom of heaven (20:1). The landowner and workers belong to that parable, whose closing saying concerns the last and the first (20:16).

All implemented note changes

Context and interpretation: Jesus introduces the vineyard workers as a comparison for the kingdom of heaven (20:1). The landowner and workers belong to that parable, whose closing saying concerns the last and the first (20:16).

Editorial rationale

Make the genre explicit while retaining the existing interpretation and events within the parable.

S091
Matthew 22Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The takeaway begins by narrating a teaching story as an event, without identifying it as Jesus' parable.

Original app summary

A king's wedding banquet fills with guests from the crossroads after the invited refuse, and a man without wedding clothes is cast out, for many are called but few are chosen; Jesus then answers his testers, giving to Caesar what is Caesar's and to God what is God's, declares God the God of the living at the resurrection, names love of God and neighbor as the commandments carrying the Law, and silences the Pharisees by asking how David's son can be David's Lord.

Original translation notes

Wedding garment: Formal festive clothing expected for a royal wedding (22:11-12).

Outer darkness… weeping and gnashing of teeth: Recurring judgment imagery in Matthew (22:13).

denarius / denarii: A Roman silver coin equal to one day's wage for a laborer (Matthew 20:2). 100 denarii were 3 to 4 months of wages. Bore the image of the emperor (Mark 12:16), making it the focus of the 'render to Caesar' tax question.

Pharisee: A lay reform movement emphasizing strict observance of the written Torah PLUS an oral tradition (the 'tradition of the elders'). Believed in bodily resurrection, angels, and a coming Messiah. Paul was a Pharisee (Philippians 3:5); Jesus' sharpest disputes were typically with this group, though He shared many of their core beliefs.

Sadducee: The priestly-aristocratic party. Accepted only the written Torah, rejecting oral tradition. Denied resurrection, angels, and spirits (Acts 23:8). Controlled the temple and collaborated extensively with Rome to maintain that power. Disappeared as a movement after AD 70 when the temple they centered on was destroyed.

In Jesus' parable, a king's wedding banquet fills with guests from the crossroads after the invited refuse, and a man without wedding clothes is cast out, for many are called but few are chosen; Jesus then answers his testers, giving to Caesar what is Caesar's and to God what is God's, declares God the God of the living at the resurrection, names love of God and neighbor as the commandments carrying the Law, and silences the Pharisees by asking how David's son can be David's Lord.

Luke 14:16-24: Jesus tells a banquet parable in which invited guests make excuses and the host brings in poor and disabled people, then others from the roads, paralleling Matthew's refused invitations.

All implemented note changes

Context and interpretation: Matthew explicitly introduces the wedding banquet as a parable about the kingdom of heaven (22:1-2). The king, the invited guests, and the man without wedding clothes are figures within that teaching story.

Editorial rationale

Make the genre explicit while retaining the existing interpretation and events within the parable.

S092
Matthew 25Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The ten virgins and entrusted talents are narrated without a parable marker, before the summary shifts to Jesus' description of judgment.

Original app summary

Ten virgins wait for the bridegroom and the unprepared return to a shut door; a master settles accounts over entrusted talents, rewarding the faithful and casting the worthless servant into outer darkness; and the Son of Man in glory separates sheep from goats by what each did for the least of his brothers, the cursed going to eternal punishment and the righteous to eternal life.

Original translation notes

Virgins: Bridal party attendants (25:1).

I do not know you: Statement of non-recognition (25:12).

talent (money): The largest unit of weight and money in the ancient world. A talent of silver weighed about 75 lbs (34 kg) and equaled roughly 6,000 denarii; about 20 years of a laborer's wages. A talent of gold was worth far more. When Jesus tells the Parable of the Talents (Matthew 25:14-30), each talent represents a staggering trust.

Jesus tells parables of ten virgins awaiting a bridegroom and servants entrusted with talents, calling for readiness and faithfulness. He then describes the Son of Man gathering the nations and separating people like sheep and goats according to their treatment of "the least of these brothers of Mine," ending with eternal punishment and eternal life.

Direct textual basis

Matthew 25:1 and 25:14 introduce kingdom comparisons; 25:31 shifts to the Son of Man coming in glory and gathering the nations.

 Matthew 24:42-44: Jesus tells his disciples to stay ready because the Son of Man will come at an unexpected hour, the warning developed in the bridegroom parable.
Matthew 10:40-42: Receiving Jesus' messengers is linked with receiving him.
All implemented reference changes

Matthew 24:42-44: Jesus tells his disciples to stay ready because the Son of Man will come at an unexpected hour, the warning developed in the bridegroom parable.
Matthew 10:40-42: Receiving Jesus' messengers is linked with receiving him.

The ten virgins and talents are teaching comparisons introduced in 25:1 and 25:14. In 25:31 Jesus turns to the coming of the Son of Man. Verse 40 calls those whose treatment matters 'these brothers of Mine'; the wording is preserved here without replacing that phrase with a narrower or broader identification.

All implemented note changes

Context and interpretation: The ten virgins and talents are teaching comparisons introduced in 25:1 and 25:14. In 25:31 Jesus turns to the coming of the Son of Man. Verse 40 calls those whose treatment matters 'these brothers of Mine'; the wording is preserved here without replacing that phrase with a narrower or broader identification.

Editorial rationale

Clarify the genre change; the existing talent note already correctly identifies its passage as a parable.

S093

First day of the week: Sunday morning timing (16:2).

He has been raised: Passive/Divine passive: God has raised him (16:6).

sabbath: The seventh day, set apart by God's command (Exodus 20:8-11). All work ceased from sunset Friday to sunset Saturday. By Jesus' day, rabbinical tradition had elaborated 39 categories of forbidden labor; many of Jesus' healings deliberately challenged those additional rules without violating the Torah command itself.

Women find the stone rolled away and hear that the crucified Jesus has risen, with word for his disciples and Peter, and they flee trembling; the risen Lord then appears to Mary Magdalene, to two walkers, and to the Eleven, rebukes their unbelief, sends them into all the world to preach the gospel to every creature with signs following, and is taken up into heaven to sit at the right hand of God.

Matthew 28:16-20: The risen Jesus sends the Eleven to make disciples of all nations, baptize, and teach obedience, giving another Gospel's commission account alongside Mark's longer ending.
Luke 24:50-53: Jesus blesses his disciples near Bethany and is carried into heaven, giving Luke's account of the ascension also reported in Mark's longer ending.

Verses 9-20 are commonly called Mark's longer ending. Some early manuscripts end after verse 8, and others contain a shorter ending, as the Berean Standard Bible footnote records. All implemented note changes

Context and interpretation: Verses 9-20 are commonly called Mark's longer ending. S094
Luke 3Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The imprisonment clause immediately before Jesus' baptism can imply that John was already imprisoned when Jesus was baptized.

Original app summary

The word of God comes to John, who preaches repentance, answers crowds, tax collectors, and soldiers, and points to One mightier who will baptize with the Holy Spirit and fire, until Herod locks him up; at Jesus' baptism the Spirit descends like a dove as heaven calls him beloved Son, and his line is traced from Joseph back through David and Abraham to Adam, the son of God.

Original translation notes

Baptism / baptize: Immersion/washing rite associated with repentance (3:3).

Repentance: Change of mind/turning expressed by deeds (3:3-8).

Winnowing fork / threshing floor: Harvest imagery for sorting wheat and chaff (3:17).

threshing floor: A flat, hard, outdoor surface, usually a leveled rock or beaten-earth circle at the edge of town, where harvested grain was separated from chaff. Oxen pulled a wooden sledge over the stalks; the mixed kernels and chaff were then winnowed in the wind. Threshing floors doubled as public civic spaces (Ruth 3; 2 Samuel 24:18; Solomon's temple site).

tetrarch: Literally 'ruler of a fourth'; a Roman client-ruler over a portion of a former kingdom. Herod the Great's territory was divided after his death among three of his sons; Herod Antipas, tetrarch of Galilee and Perea, is the Herod who executes John the Baptist (Matthew 14:1) and mocks Jesus before His crucifixion (Luke 23:7-11).

John preaches repentance, answers the crowds, tax collectors, and soldiers, and points to one mightier who will baptize with the Holy Spirit and fire. At Jesus' baptism the Spirit descends like a dove and a voice from heaven calls him the beloved Son; his genealogy runs through Joseph, David, and Abraham to Adam. Luke also reports Herod's imprisonment of John after John rebukes his conduct.

Direct textual basis

Luke 3:19-20 closes the section on John with his imprisonment before returning to Jesus' baptism in 3:21-22. Mark 1:9-14 narrates Jesus' baptism before John is imprisoned.

 Mark 1:9-14: Jesus' baptism precedes John's arrest in Mark's sequence.
John 3:22-24: John is still baptizing before his imprisonment.
All implemented reference changes

Mark 1:9-14: Jesus' baptism precedes John's arrest in Mark's sequence.
John 3:22-24: John is still baptizing before his imprisonment.

Luke closes his account of John's public ministry with the imprisonment report (3:19-20), then returns to Jesus' baptism (3:21-22). The placement of the report is not a statement that John was imprisoned before that baptism; Mark 1:9-14 gives the order explicitly.

All implemented note changes

Context and interpretation: Luke closes his account of John's public ministry with the imprisonment report (3:19-20), then returns to Jesus' baptism (3:21-22). The placement of the report is not a statement that John was imprisoned before that baptism; Mark 1:9-14 gives the order explicitly.

Editorial rationale

Distinguish Luke's arrangement of the material from the chronology inferred by reading adjacent summary clauses as successive events.

The plow saying is Jesus' illustration of the commitment required to follow him (9:57-62). The picture is of someone beginning to plow and looking back; Jesus applies it to fitness for the kingdom.

All implemented note changes

Context and interpretation: The plow saying is Jesus' illustration of the commitment required to follow him (9:57-62). The picture is of someone beginning to plow and looking back; Jesus applies it to fitness for the kingdom.

Editorial rationale

Make the figurative statement a warning, rather than an invented physical action.

S098
Luke 10Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

Answered by the Samaritan can make the Samaritan sound like a participant answering the law expert rather than a figure in Jesus' story.

Original app summary

The seventy-two go out two by two and return rejoicing that demons submit, though Jesus points them to names written in heaven and blesses what they see; a law expert's test draws out love of God and neighbor, answered by the Samaritan who showed mercy to the robbers' victim, and busy Martha hears that Mary, listening at the Lord's feet, has chosen the good portion.

Original translation notes

Seventy-two (or seventy): Manuscripts read either 72 or 70 (10:1-17).

Peace to this house: Greeting and blessing to a household (10:5).

I saw Satan fall like lightning: Jesus’ statement as the messengers report back (10:18).

denarius / denarii: A Roman silver coin equal to one day's wage for a laborer (Matthew 20:2). 100 denarii were 3 to 4 months of wages. Bore the image of the emperor (Mark 12:16), making it the focus of the 'render to Caesar' tax question.

sackcloth: A coarse, dark goat-hair fabric worn against bare skin as a universal sign of mourning, repentance, or distress (1 Kings 21:27; Jonah 3:5; Matthew 11:21). Wearing it publicly broadcast grief or contrition; the discomfort itself was part of the symbolism.

Levite: A male descendant of Levi (excluding the Aaronic priestly line). Assisted the priests in temple service: music, gatekeeping, instructing the people, preparing sacrifices. The priest and Levite who pass by the wounded man in the Good Samaritan parable (Luke 10:31-32) make Jesus' point cut sharper; both were professionally obligated to help.

Samaria: The central highland region between Judea and Galilee; capital of the northern kingdom of Israel from 880 BC until Assyrian deportation in 722 BC. The mixed-population descendants who remained ('Samaritans') were despised by Judeans as ethnically and religiously impure. Jesus' choice to travel through Samaria (John 4) and to make a Samaritan the hero of His parable (Luke 10) deliberately upends that prejudice.

Hades: The Greek-language counterpart to Hebrew Sheol used in the Septuagint and New Testament; the general realm of the dead, not specifically a place of punishment. Revelation 1:18 has Christ holding 'the keys of Death and Hades.' Distinct from Gehenna.

The seventy-two go out two by two and return rejoicing that demons submit, though Jesus points them to names written in heaven and blesses what they see; a law expert's test draws out love of God and neighbor, and Jesus answers his question with the story of a Samaritan showing mercy to a robbers' victim, and busy Martha hears that Mary, listening at the Lord's feet, has chosen the good portion.

Direct textual basis

Luke 10:25-29 records the law expert's questions; Jesus tells the story in 10:30-35 and asks which person acted as a neighbor in 10:36-37.

 Deuteronomy 6:5: The command to love the LORD with all one's heart, soul, and strength is one of the Torah texts the law expert quotes before Jesus tells the Samaritan story.
Leviticus 19:18: The command to love one's neighbor accompanies a ban on revenge and grudges; the law expert quotes it before asking Jesus to define neighbor.
All implemented reference changes

Isaiah 52:7: A messenger proclaims peace, good news, and salvation to Zion and announces that God reigns, a prophetic comparison for the messengers' proclamation in Luke.
Revelation 20:12: Books are opened before the judgment throne, including the Book of Life, and the dead are judged according to their recorded deeds. This supplies book imagery to compare with the names written in heaven in Luke 10:20.
Deuteronomy 6:5: The command to love the LORD with all one's heart, soul, and strength is one of the Torah texts the law expert quotes before Jesus tells the Samaritan story.
Leviticus 19:18: The command to love one's neighbor accompanies a ban on revenge and grudges; the law expert quotes it before asking Jesus to define neighbor.

Jesus tells the Samaritan story in response to the law expert's question, 'Who is my neighbor?' (10:29-30). At its end Jesus asks which person became a neighbor to the injured man, and the expert answers, 'The one who showed him mercy' (10:36-37).

All implemented note changes

Context and interpretation: Jesus tells the Samaritan story in response to the law expert's question, 'Who is my neighbor?' (10:29-30). At its end Jesus asks which person became a neighbor to the injured man, and the expert answers, 'The one who showed him mercy' (10:36-37).

Editorial rationale

The existing Levite note already calls this a parable; align the summary and add the two Torah commandments explicitly quoted in the scene.

S099
Luke 12Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The rich fool is not marked as a parable, and full barns end that very night assigns the fate to the barns when the story says the man's life is required.

Original app summary

Warning against the leaven of hypocrisy, Jesus commands fear of the One with authority over hell and promises the Spirit's words under trial; a rich fool's full barns end that very night, so he tells his little flock not to worry but to seek the kingdom, to keep lamps burning like servants awaiting their master, and to be faithful managers, then declares he brings fire and division rather than peace and chides hypocrites who read the sky but not the present time.

Original translation notes

Leaven: Used figuratively for influential teaching/practice; here, hypocrisy (12:1).

Blaspheme: Speaking impiously against; here of the Holy Spirit (12:10).

assarion: A small Roman copper coin, one sixteenth of a denarius. Two sparrows cost an assarion (Matthew 10:29); Jesus uses the price to argue God's care for even the smallest creature.

watches of the night: Old Testament Hebrew time-keeping used three night watches (Lamentations 2:19). Roman-era New Testament texts use four watches, evening, midnight, cockcrow, and dawn (Mark 13:35), each about three hours long. The 'fourth watch' is roughly 3 a.m. to 6 a.m.

Pharisee: A lay reform movement emphasizing strict observance of the written Torah PLUS an oral tradition (the 'tradition of the elders'). Believed in bodily resurrection, angels, and a coming Messiah. Paul was a Pharisee (Philippians 3:5); Jesus' sharpest disputes were typically with this group, though He shared many of their core beliefs.

lepton (mite): The smallest copper coin in circulation in Jesus' time; one hundred twenty-eighth of a denarius. The widow's offering of two leptons (Mark 12:42; Luke 21:2) was pennies in modern terms, yet praised as more than the wealthy gave.

Gehenna: From the Valley of Hinnom outside Jerusalem, where child sacrifice to Molech was once practiced (2 Kings 23:10) and where the city refuse was later burned. Used by Jesus as imagery for final judgment (Matthew 5:22; 10:28; 23:33). Often translated 'hell' in English Bibles. Distinct from hades (the realm of the dead).

Warning against the leaven of hypocrisy, Jesus commands fear of the One with authority over hell and promises the Spirit's words under trial; in his parable a rich man's plans for larger barns are cut short when his life is required that night, so he tells his little flock not to worry but to seek the kingdom, to keep lamps burning like servants awaiting their master, and to be faithful managers, then declares he brings fire and division rather than peace and chides hypocrites who read the sky but not the present time.

Direct textual basis

Luke 12:16 introduces a parable. The man plans larger barns in 12:18; in 12:20 God says the man's life will be required and asks who will receive his goods.

 Ecclesiastes 2:18-21: The worker leaves accumulated goods to another.
Psalm 49:16-20: Wealth cannot be carried away at death.
All implemented reference changes

Psalm 147:9: God feeds the animals and the young ravens that call, providing a parallel to Jesus' teaching about God's care for creatures.
Ecclesiastes 2:18-21: The worker leaves accumulated goods to another.
Psalm 49:16-20: Wealth cannot be carried away at death.

Luke introduces the rich man's account as a parable (12:16). His plans concern bigger barns, but God's warning concerns his life being required that night and the possessions passing to someone else (12:18-20). The point is stated in 12:21: storing treasure without being rich toward God.

All implemented note changes

Context and interpretation: Luke introduces the rich man's account as a parable (12:16). His plans concern bigger barns, but God's warning concerns his life being required that night and the possessions passing to someone else (12:18-20). The point is stated in 12:21: storing treasure without being rich toward God.

Editorial rationale

Distinguish the teaching story from an event and correct which thing ends.

S100
Luke 13Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

Jesus tells a parable in which the vineyard keeper asks for another year; he does not personally grant a tree another year.

Original app summary

Unless you repent you will all likewise perish, Jesus warns after Pilate's slaughter and Siloam's tower, granting the barren fig tree one more tended year; he frees a daughter of Abraham bound eighteen years on the Sabbath, likens the kingdom to mustard seed and leaven, commands striving through the narrow door before the master shuts it, answers Herod the fox with his third-day goal, and laments over Jerusalem, whose house is left desolate.

Original translation notes

Repent: To change mind/turn; repeated warning (13:3-5).

Parable: Story/figure laid alongside to teach (13:6-20).

Loosed/freed: Release from a bond/ailment (13:12-16).

Sabbath day's journey: About two thirds of a mile (1 km); the rabbinic limit on travel from one's dwelling on the Sabbath, derived from the camp boundaries in Exodus 16:29 and Numbers 35:5. Acts 1:12 places the Mount of Olives this distance from Jerusalem.

sabbath: The seventh day, set apart by God's command (Exodus 20:8-11). All work ceased from sunset Friday to sunset Saturday. By Jesus' day, rabbinical tradition had elaborated 39 categories of forbidden labor; many of Jesus' healings deliberately challenged those additional rules without violating the Torah command itself.

mustard seed: The seed of the black mustard plant (Sinapis nigra); proverbially the smallest seed a Galilean farmer planted (about 1 mm across), growing into a 10-foot shrub. Jesus uses it as His image for faith (Matthew 17:20) and for the kingdom of heaven (Matthew 13:31-32).

Pharisee: A lay reform movement emphasizing strict observance of the written Torah PLUS an oral tradition (the 'tradition of the elders'). Believed in bodily resurrection, angels, and a coming Messiah. Paul was a Pharisee (Philippians 3:5); Jesus' sharpest disputes were typically with this group, though He shared many of their core beliefs.

seah: About 7 quarts (7 liters); one third of an ephah. The 'three measures of flour' in the parable of the leaven (Matthew 13:33) are three seahs / roughly 30-40 lbs of flour, enough to feed a sizeable household for days.

Unless you repent you will all likewise perish, Jesus warns after Pilate's slaughter and Siloam's tower, then tells a parable in which a vineyard keeper asks for one more year to tend a barren fig tree; he frees a daughter of Abraham bound eighteen years on the Sabbath, likens the kingdom to mustard seed and leaven, commands striving through the narrow door before the master shuts it, answers Herod the fox with his third-day goal, and laments over Jerusalem, whose house is left desolate.

Direct textual basis

Luke 13:6 explicitly introduces a parable. The vineyard keeper asks the owner to leave the barren fig tree for another year of care (13:8-9); the story gives no reply from the owner.

 Isaiah 5:1-7: The LORD's vineyard is Israel: he expects justice but finds bloodshed and announces judgment, providing a prophetic comparison for Luke's fruitless-tree warning.
All implemented reference changes

Isaiah 5:1-7: The LORD's vineyard is Israel: he expects justice but finds bloodshed and announces judgment, providing a prophetic comparison for Luke's fruitless-tree warning.

The fig tree appears in a parable introduced in 13:6. Its keeper asks the owner for another year to dig around it and fertilize it (13:8-9). The owner gives no recorded answer, leaving the warning about fruit and repentance open.

All implemented note changes

Parable: The fig tree appears in a parable introduced in 13:6. Its keeper asks the owner for another year to dig around it and fertilize it (13:8-9). The owner gives no recorded answer, leaving the warning about fruit and repentance open.

Editorial rationale

The existing generic Parable note is correct, but the summary turns the keeper's request into Jesus personally granting a year.

S101
Luke 14Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

Distinguish instructions about seating and the banquet parable from reported events. Explicitly identify the great banquet as a parable.

Original app summary

At a leading Pharisee's Sabbath table Jesus heals a man with dropsy, seats guests in the last place since the self-exalting will be humbled, and tells hosts to invite the poor who cannot repay; a great banquet fills with the poor and the compelled after the invited excuse themselves, and he turns to the crowds with the cost of discipleship, carrying one's cross, counting like the tower builder and the warring king, and giving up everything.

Original translation notes

Dropsy: A condition involving fluid retention/swelling (14:2).

Place of honor: Preferred seat at a feast (14:7).

Compel: “Urge strongly/press to come in” (14:23).

Sabbath day's journey: About two thirds of a mile (1 km); the rabbinic limit on travel from one's dwelling on the Sabbath, derived from the camp boundaries in Exodus 16:29 and Numbers 35:5. Acts 1:12 places the Mount of Olives this distance from Jerusalem.

sabbath: The seventh day, set apart by God's command (Exodus 20:8-11). All work ceased from sunset Friday to sunset Saturday. By Jesus' day, rabbinical tradition had elaborated 39 categories of forbidden labor; many of Jesus' healings deliberately challenged those additional rules without violating the Torah command itself.

Pharisee: A lay reform movement emphasizing strict observance of the written Torah PLUS an oral tradition (the 'tradition of the elders'). Believed in bodily resurrection, angels, and a coming Messiah. Paul was a Pharisee (Philippians 3:5); Jesus' sharpest disputes were typically with this group, though He shared many of their core beliefs.

myriad: Ten thousand; also used idiomatically for 'countless.' Revelation 5:11's 'ten thousands of ten thousands' (myriades myriadon) of angels could be 100 million as a literal floor, though the intent is more naturally read as 'innumerable.'

Hate his father and mother: Matthew reports the same saying, cross and all, as 'Anyone who loves his father or mother more than Me is not worthy of Me' (Matthew 10:37). Both the Hebrew and the Greek word are used in Scripture for the lesser-loved of two: Genesis 29:30 says Jacob loved Rachel more than Leah, and 29:31 says the LORD saw that Leah was hated, which English versions render 'unloved'. Deuteronomy 21:15-17 builds an inheritance law on that same pair, 'one beloved and the other unloved', and Malachi 1:2-3 sets the two words against each other in one line: 'Jacob I have loved, but Esau I have hated.'

At a leading Pharisee's Sabbath table Jesus heals a man with dropsy, teaches guests to choose the last place since the self-exalting will be humbled, and tells hosts to invite the poor who cannot repay; in his parable, a great banquet fills with the poor and the compelled after the invited excuse themselves, and he turns to the crowds with the cost of discipleship, carrying one's cross, counting like the tower builder and the warring king, and giving up everything.

Direct textual basis

Luke 14:7-11 records teaching about choosing a seat, not Jesus seating the guests. Verses 16-24 are his story of a great banquet.

 Proverbs 25:6-7: The seating instruction echoes the counsel to wait to be invited higher.
Isaiah 25:6: The LORD prepares a feast for all peoples.

All implemented note changes

Context and interpretation: At the meal Jesus instructs guests to choose the lower place (14:7-11). He then tells the story of a great banquet whose original invitees refuse to attend (14:16-24). The seating counsel and banquet story are teachings within the account of the meal.

Editorial rationale

Retain the already useful seating and banquet cross-references; distinguish teaching from narrated action.

S103

The widow and tax collector are summarized as unmarked incidents rather than the two parables Luke introduces.

Original app summary

A persistent widow before an unjust judge teaches praying always, and a tax collector's plea for mercy goes home justified over a Pharisee's self-trust; Jesus receives the little children as those to whom the kingdom belongs, watches a wealthy ruler balk at selling all, says what is impossible with man is possible with God, foretells his mocking, death, and third-day rising, and gives sight to a blind beggar near Jericho whose faith has healed him.

Original translation notes

Beat me down / wear me out: Idiomatic in 18:5 for the judge’s concern over the widow’s continual coming (figurative ‘give me a black eye’ / exhaust).

Be merciful to me: Verb related to ‘propitiation/atone’; plea for God’s mercy (18:13).

Justified: Declared righteous/acquitted in 18:14.

Pharisee: A lay reform movement emphasizing strict observance of the written Torah PLUS an oral tradition (the 'tradition of the elders'). Believed in bodily resurrection, angels, and a coming Messiah. Paul was a Pharisee (Philippians 3:5); Jesus' sharpest disputes were typically with this group, though He shared many of their core beliefs.

publican (tax collector): Local Jews contracted to collect Roman taxes; and to skim extra for themselves. Universally despised as collaborators and as ritually unclean from constant contact with Gentiles and money. Matthew the apostle and Zacchaeus are the prominent New Testament examples; Jesus' table fellowship with them was deliberately scandalous.

Jesus tells two parables: a persistent widow and an unjust judge teach perseverance in prayer, while a tax collector asking for mercy goes home justified rather than a self-confident Pharisee; Jesus receives the little children as those to whom the kingdom belongs, watches a wealthy ruler balk at selling all, says what is impossible with man is possible with God, foretells his mocking, death, and third-day rising, and gives sight to a blind beggar near Jericho whose faith has healed him.

Luke 18:1 introduces the widow-and-judge parable; 18:9 introduces the Pharisee-and-tax-collector parable and identifies its audience as people confident in their own righteousness.

Luke 11:5-13: Jesus uses a midnight request for bread to teach persistence, then describes the Father's giving of the Holy Spirit to those who ask, complementing the widow parable's call to prayer.

All implemented note changes

Editorial rationale

Mark the stories as Jesus' teaching rather than implying that these were additional encounters on his journey.

S106
John 8Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The included adultery account is summarized without the manuscript-context notice that the translation itself publishes for John 7:53-8:11.

Original app summary

When the woman's accusers withdraw one by one before 'Let him who is without sin cast the first stone,' Jesus does not condemn her and tells her to sin no more; declaring himself the light of the world, he warns that those who disbelieve die in their sins, promises that the truth will set free those who continue in his word, tells his opponents that their father the devil is a liar and murderer, and declares 'before Abraham was born, I am,' so they pick up stones as he leaves the temple.

Original translation notes

Without sin… first to throw: Jesus’ statement recalling Deut 17:7 about witnesses initiating punishment (8:7).

Pharisee: A lay reform movement emphasizing strict observance of the written Torah PLUS an oral tradition (the 'tradition of the elders'). Believed in bodily resurrection, angels, and a coming Messiah. Paul was a Pharisee (Philippians 3:5); Jesus' sharpest disputes were typically with this group, though He shared many of their core beliefs.

Samaria: The central highland region between Judea and Galilee; capital of the northern kingdom of Israel from 880 BC until Assyrian deportation in 722 BC. The mixed-population descendants who remained ('Samaritans') were despised by Judeans as ethnically and religiously impure. Jesus' choice to travel through Samaria (John 4) and to make a Samaritan the hero of His parable (Luke 10) deliberately upends that prejudice.

Who is being spoken to: The exchange that runs from 8:31 to the end of the chapter is introduced as words spoken 'to the Jews who had believed Him', straight after 8:30 says many believed in Him. Inside that exchange Jesus grants their descent outright, 'I know you are Abraham's descendants' (8:37), and then puts the question on conduct: 'If you were children of Abraham, you would do the works of Abraham' (8:39). Everyone in the room is Jewish, the speaker included, and John has already recorded Jesus saying 'salvation is from the Jews' (4:22).

Ioudaioi: John uses this one word more than sixty times, and it does not always cover the same people. At 4:9 it marks a Judean over against a Samaritan; at 6:41 it is a grumbling crowd in Galilee; at 11:19 and 11:31 it is the neighbours who came to console Martha and Mary, many of whom then believe (11:45); at 19:3 it is the title Roman soldiers use to mock Him, 'King of the Jews'. Inside this chapter alone it covers a group that had just believed Him (8:31) and, by 8:59, a group picking up stones.

Before Abraham was born, I am: The Greek changes tense mid-sentence: Abraham 'came into being', a past event, and then 'I am', present. That is the wording God gives Moses at the bush, 'I AM WHO I AM' (Exodus 3:14), which the Greek Old Testament renders ego eimi ho on. The reaction in 8:59 is immediate; they pick up stones, the penalty Leviticus 24:16 sets for blaspheming the Name.

When the woman's accusers withdraw one by one before 'Let him who is without sin cast the first stone,' Jesus does not condemn her and tells her to sin no more; declaring himself the light of the world, he warns that those who disbelieve die in their sins, promises that the truth will set free those who continue in his word, tells his opponents that their father the devil is a liar and murderer, and declares 'before Abraham was born, I am,' so they pick up stones as he leaves the temple.

Direct textual basis

The account appears in the app's John 8:1-11. The Berean Standard Bible note at John 7:52 records that some early manuscripts do not include John 7:53-8:11.

 Deuteronomy 17:6-7: Witnesses are required and initiate the execution.
Leviticus 20:10: The law prescribes death for both the adulterer and the adulteress, while John's scene describes the accusers bringing only the woman.
All implemented reference changes

Deuteronomy 17:6-7: Witnesses are required and initiate the execution.
Leviticus 20:10: The law prescribes death for both the adulterer and the adulteress, while John's scene describes the accusers bringing only the woman.

John 7:53-8:11 is included in this edition. Some early manuscripts do not contain this passage, as the Berean Standard Bible footnote at 7:52 records. Within the account, Jesus both refuses to condemn the woman and tells her to sin no more (8:10-11).

All implemented note changes

Context and interpretation: John 7:53-8:11 is included in this edition. Some early manuscripts do not contain this passage, as the Berean Standard Bible footnote at 7:52 records. Within the account, Jesus both refuses to condemn the woman and tells her to sin no more (8:10-11).

Translation source notes

Editorial rationale

The summary can remain; the proposal is the missing textual-context note, alongside the already strong legal cross-references.

S107
John 12Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The summary asserts that Jesus answers the Greeks directly, while the scene records Philip and Andrew relaying their request. The nard note additionally imports Mark's broken flask into Mary's anointing of the feet.

Original app summary

Mary anoints Jesus' feet with costly nard for his burial over Judas's protest, and crowds come to see Lazarus too; riding a young donkey into Jerusalem amid palm branches and Hosannas, Jesus answers the Greeks that the hour has come for the Son of Man to be glorified, a grain of wheat must die to bear much fruit, and when lifted up he will draw everyone to himself, while Isaiah's words explain the unbelief and Jesus cries out that he came as light, speaking exactly what the Father commanded.

Original translation notes

Nard: Aromatic oil from spikenard; called “pure”/“genuine” (12:3).

Pound: Roman pound measure; about a third of a kilogram (12:3).

Money box: Container for funds carried by the group (12:6).

denarius / denarii: A Roman silver coin equal to one day's wage for a laborer (Matthew 20:2). 100 denarii were 3 to 4 months of wages. Bore the image of the emperor (Mark 12:16), making it the focus of the 'render to Caesar' tax question.

nard (spikenard): A costly perfume oil extracted from the Himalayan Nardostachys plant; imported across the Silk Road. The alabaster flask Mary breaks over Jesus' feet (John 12:3; Mark 14:3) was worth about 300 denarii, almost a year's wages, and was likely a family heirloom.

Pharisee: A lay reform movement emphasizing strict observance of the written Torah PLUS an oral tradition (the 'tradition of the elders'). Believed in bodily resurrection, angels, and a coming Messiah. Paul was a Pharisee (Philippians 3:5); Jesus' sharpest disputes were typically with this group, though He shared many of their core beliefs.

pound (litra): About 12 oz (340 g). Mary anoints Jesus with 'a pound of pure nard' (John 12:3); a quantity worth roughly 300 denarii, almost a year's wages.

Mary anoints Jesus' feet with costly nard for his burial over Judas's protest, and crowds come to see Lazarus too; riding a young donkey into Jerusalem amid palm branches and Hosannas, after Greeks ask to see Jesus and Philip and Andrew relay the request, Jesus declares that the hour has come for the Son of Man to be glorified, a grain of wheat must die to bear much fruit, and when lifted up he will draw everyone to himself, while Isaiah's words explain the unbelief and Jesus cries out that he came as light, speaking exactly what the Father commanded.

Direct textual basis

 Mark 14:3-9: Mark records an unnamed woman breaking a flask and anointing Jesus' head.
Matthew 26:6-13: Matthew likewise describes an unnamed woman and anointing of the head.
All implemented reference changes

Mark 14:3-9: Mark records an unnamed woman breaking a flask and anointing Jesus' head.
Matthew 26:6-13: Matthew likewise describes an unnamed woman and anointing of the head.

Philip and Andrew bring the Greeks' request to Jesus; John records his response without describing the requested meeting (12:20-23). John names Mary and describes her anointing his feet and wiping them with her hair (12:3). Mark 14:3 describes an unnamed woman breaking a flask and pouring perfume on his head. Combining every detail as one event is a harmonization beyond John's narration.

All implemented note changes

nard (spikenard): A costly perfume oil extracted from the Himalayan Nardostachys plant; imported across the Silk Road. S109
Acts 3Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The crowd kills Jesus; God raises him. The original assigns both verbs to the crowd.

Original app summary

Original translation notes

Beautiful Gate: A temple gate known to the audience; exact identification among the temple entrances is not specified in the text (3:2-10).

Alms: Gifts to the poor; the man asks those entering the temple for alms (3:2-3).

Ninth hour: About 3 p.m., a stated time of prayer (3:1).

In the name of Jesus: Formula of authority/appeal tied to Jesus; the action is done invoking his name (3:6).

third / sixth / ninth hour: The Jewish day was divided into twelve daylight hours starting at sunrise (~6 a.m.). The third hour is about 9 a.m., the sixth is noon, the ninth is 3 p.m. Jesus is crucified at the third hour (Mark 15:25); darkness comes at the sixth, He dies at the ninth.

Peter heals a man lame from birth at the Beautiful Gate in the name of Jesus, and the man walks and leaps praising God; to the amazed crowd Peter declares that God glorified His servant Jesus, whom they killed but whom God raised from the dead, and calls them to repent so that times of refreshing may come.

All implemented note changes

S110
Acts 15Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

For fellowship makes one interpretation of the four requirements' purpose sound like the council's explicit statement and leaves the actual four requirements unnamed.

Original app summary

The Jerusalem council decides not to require circumcision of Gentile believers and sends a letter with four abstentions for fellowship.

Original translation notes

Circumcision: Covenant sign from Genesis 17; the question is whether Gentiles must receive it to be saved (15:1-5).

Polluted by idols (idol food): Food connected with idolatrous sacrifice; listed among the abstentions (15:20-29).

Strangled / blood: Animals not bled (strangled) and consuming blood are prohibited in the letter (15:20-29).

It seemed good to the Holy Spirit and to us: Letter’s phrasing about the decision’s source (15:28).

sabbath: The seventh day, set apart by God's command (Exodus 20:8-11). All work ceased from sunset Friday to sunset Saturday. By Jesus' day, rabbinical tradition had elaborated 39 categories of forbidden labor; many of Jesus' healings deliberately challenged those additional rules without violating the Torah command itself.

Pharisee: A lay reform movement emphasizing strict observance of the written Torah PLUS an oral tradition (the 'tradition of the elders'). Believed in bodily resurrection, angels, and a coming Messiah. Paul was a Pharisee (Philippians 3:5); Jesus' sharpest disputes were typically with this group, though He shared many of their core beliefs.

Samaria: The central highland region between Judea and Galilee; capital of the northern kingdom of Israel from 880 BC until Assyrian deportation in 722 BC. The mixed-population descendants who remained ('Samaritans') were despised by Judeans as ethnically and religiously impure. Jesus' choice to travel through Samaria (John 4) and to make a Samaritan the hero of His parable (Luke 10) deliberately upends that prejudice.

The Jerusalem council rejects requiring circumcision for Gentile salvation. Its letter asks Gentile believers to abstain from food sacrificed to idols, blood, meat from strangled animals, and sexual immorality; Judas and Silas deliver the decision with Paul and Barnabas.

Direct textual basis

Acts 15:1-11 addresses whether Gentiles need circumcision for salvation; 15:20,28-29 names idol food, blood, strangled meat, and sexual immorality as the requirements in the letter.

 Leviticus 17:10-14: The law forbids Israelites and resident foreigners to eat blood and requires hunted animals to be drained, supplying Torah background for the council's restrictions on blood and strangled meat.
Leviticus 18:6-23: The law prohibits incest, adultery, and other sexual acts, providing Torah context for the council's prohibition of sexual immorality.
Acts 21:25: Jerusalem's leaders repeat that Gentile believers should abstain from idol food, blood, strangled meat, and sexual immorality, confirming the contents of the earlier council letter.
All implemented reference changes

Leviticus 17:10-14: The law forbids Israelites and resident foreigners to eat blood and requires hunted animals to be drained, supplying Torah background for the council's restrictions on blood and strangled meat.
Leviticus 18:6-23: The law prohibits incest, adultery, and other sexual acts, providing Torah context for the council's prohibition of sexual immorality.
Acts 21:25: Jerusalem's leaders repeat that Gentile believers should abstain from idol food, blood, strangled meat, and sexual immorality, confirming the contents of the earlier council letter.

The dispute begins with a demand that Gentiles be circumcised to be saved (15:1). The council's letter rejects that burden and names four requirements (15:28-29). Verse 21 mentions Moses being read in the synagogues; the text does not reduce the whole decision to a single stated purpose such as table fellowship.

S112
2 Corinthians 5Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

Paul describes mortal life and longing to be clothed with life, not imprisonment in an intrinsically bad body.

Original app summary

Paul teaches that believers groan while trapped in their mortal bodies but possess the Holy Spirit as a guarantee of their eternal heavenly home; he urges them to live for Christ and serve as ambassadors of reconciliation, since Christ died for all and rose again.

Original translation notes

Judgment seat: Raised platform for judicial decisions; image for Christ’s tribunal.

Ambassadors: Official envoys representing authority; Paul applies it to gospel ministry.

Tent: Paul worked at the tent trade in Corinth with Aquila and Priscilla, cutting and stitching cloth or leather panels (Acts 18:1-3). In 5:1-4 he uses the trade for the body: a tent that gets struck and folded away, set against a building from God not made with hands. The same word group names the tabernacle, the tent Israel carried through the wilderness.

Reconciliation: The verb first meant to exchange one thing for another, including changing money into other coin, and from that to change enmity into friendship. It was ordinary language for ending a quarrel between two men or two cities. The word group appears five times in 5:18-20, and the accounting sense stays live: 5:19 says God was not reckoning their trespasses to their account.

Paul describes believers groaning in their mortal bodies and longing to be clothed with life, with the Holy Spirit as God's pledge of what is to come; he urges them to live for Christ and serve as ambassadors of reconciliation, since Christ died for all and rose again.

Direct textual basis

In 2 Corinthians 5:1-5 Paul describes groaning in the earthly tent, longing to be clothed so mortality is swallowed up by life; verse 4 expressly says the desire is not to be unclothed.

1 Corinthians 15:53-54: The mortal puts on immortality.
All implemented reference changes

1 Corinthians 15:53-54: The mortal puts on immortality.

Paul describes the body as an earthly tent and longs to be clothed with the heavenly dwelling. Verse 4 says he does not wish to be unclothed, but clothed so that mortality is swallowed up by life. His hope includes the transformation of mortal life.

All implemented note changes

S113
Galatians 5Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The warning concerns seeking justification through circumcision; circumcision itself is not a universal prohibition.

Original app summary

It is for freedom that Christ has set us free, so believers must stand firm and refuse circumcision, since what matters is faith expressing itself through love; called to serve one another in love, they are to walk by the Spirit, whose fruit of love, joy, peace, patience, kindness, goodness, faithfulness, gentleness, and self-control stands against the obvious works of the flesh.

Original translation notes

Cut themselves off: Strong hyperbole directed at agitators pressing circumcision; the verb means to cut off/mutilate. Paul uses deliberate shock language.

Circumcision: For an Israelite this was done on the eighth day of life as the sign of the covenant with Abraham (Genesis 17:9-14, Leviticus 12:3). What the agitators were asking of grown gentile men in Galatia was surgery without anaesthetic, weeks of recovery, and real risk of infection, and Paul adds in 5:3 that it obligated the man to keep the whole law. It also marked him permanently in a world where men exercised and bathed naked: 1 Maccabees 1:14-15 records Jewish men in Jerusalem building a gymnasium and having the mark surgically undone to pass as Greeks.

A little leaven: Leaven was not a packet of yeast. It was a lump of dough held back from the last baking and left to sour, then worked into the new batch, so one handful carried through everything it touched. At Passover the law required every trace of it out of the house and out of Israel's whole territory for seven days, and eating it cut a man off from the people (Exodus 12:15, 19, 13:7). The Mishnah describes searching the house for it by lamplight the night before (Pesachim 1:1). That is the picture behind 5:9.

Sorcery: The word in the list at 5:20 is the drug word, the root behind English pharmacy. It covers the mixing of potions, spells, and poisons together, since the same practitioner supplied all three. The Greek Old Testament uses the related word for the magicians who matched Moses in Pharaoh's court (Exodus 7:11, 22) and for the sorceries Babylon trusted in (Isaiah 47:9, 12). Revelation puts it in the same kind of list at 9:21, 18:23, and 21:8.

It is for freedom that Christ has set us free, so believers must stand firm and not seek justification through circumcision, since what matters is faith expressing itself through love; called to serve one another in love, they are to walk by the Spirit, whose fruit of love, joy, peace, patience, kindness, goodness, faithfulness, gentleness, and self-control stands against the obvious works of the flesh.

Direct textual basis

Galatians 5:2-4 addresses people seeking justification through circumcision and law; 5:6 says neither circumcision nor uncircumcision counts in Christ.

 Acts 16:3: Paul has Timothy circumcised because of the local Jews and Timothy's Greek father, a case to read alongside Galatians' warning about relying on circumcision for justification.
1 Corinthians 7:18-19: Paul counsels people not to change circumcision status.
All implemented reference changes

Acts 16:3: Paul has Timothy circumcised because of the local Jews and Timothy's Greek father, a case to read alongside Galatians' warning about relying on circumcision for justification.
1 Corinthians 7:18-19: Paul counsels people not to change circumcision status.

Paul's warning addresses seeking justification through circumcision and the law (5:2-4). He immediately says that neither circumcision nor uncircumcision has value in Christ; what counts is faith expressing itself through love (5:6). Acts 16:3 records him circumcising Timothy in a different setting.

All implemented note changes

Context and interpretation: Paul's warning addresses seeking justification through circumcision and the law (5:2-4). He immediately says that neither circumcision nor uncircumcision has value in Christ; what counts is faith expressing itself through love (5:6). Acts 16:3 records him circumcising Timothy in a different setting.

Editorial rationale

Refuse circumcision makes a contextual warning sound like a universal ban. Preserve the existing 1 Corinthians 7:19 connection and add Acts 16:3.

S114
Philippians 2Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

Paul sends Timothy turns a hoped-for future visit into an accomplished dispatch and merges Timothy's plan with the decision about Epaphroditus.

Original app summary

United in one spirit, believers are to do nothing from selfish ambition but in humility take the mind of Christ Jesus, who, existing in the form of God, emptied Himself, humbled Himself to death on a cross, and was exalted with the name above all names, every knee to bow and every tongue confess that Jesus Christ is Lord; they are to work out their salvation with fear and trembling as God works in them, shining as lights in the world, and Paul sends Timothy of proven worth and Epaphroditus, who nearly died for the work of Christ.

Original translation notes

Participation in the Spirit: Means fellowship or shared life produced by the Holy Spirit.

The form of a servant: The word in 2:7 is doulos, slave, and the ancient reader pictured a sale. Slaves were displayed on a raised platform in the market, often stripped so buyers could examine them, with a placard hung round the neck stating origin, skills, and defects; the aediles' edict required a seller to declare a slave's illnesses and faults, and imported slaves had their feet chalked white. A slave had no legal standing, no claim on his own body, and no right to refuse. That is the position 2:7 says was taken up deliberately, and 2:8 carries it to a death Rome reserved for slaves and rebels.

Every knee will bow: Philippians 2:10-11 quotes Isaiah 45:23, where the LORD swears by Himself that every knee will bow to Him and every tongue swear allegiance; Paul quotes the same verse again at Romans 14:11. The Greek Old Testament renders the divine name of the Hebrew text, the four letters YHWH, with kyrios, Lord, and kyrios is the word standing at the end of 2:11. The reader of an English Bible sees Lord in both places without seeing that the quotation and the confession use the same word.

Epaphroditus: Philippi to Rome was something over seven hundred miles: the Egnatian Way west across Macedonia, a sea crossing of the Adriatic, then the Appian Way up to Rome, six weeks or more each way in good conditions and not attempted in winter. Epaphroditus made that trip carrying the congregation's gift, fell ill on the work and nearly died, and the news of his illness had time to reach Philippi and the news of their worry to get back to him (2:25-30). The word Paul uses of him in 2:30 is a gambler's word: he staked his life on the throw.

United in one spirit, believers are to do nothing from selfish ambition but in humility take the mind of Christ Jesus, who, existing in the form of God, emptied Himself, humbled Himself to death on a cross, and was exalted with the name above all names, every knee to bow and every tongue confess that Jesus Christ is Lord; they are to work out their salvation with fear and trembling as God works in them, shining as lights in the world, and Paul hopes to send Timothy of proven worth and is sending back Epaphroditus, who nearly died for the work of Christ.

Direct textual basis

Philippians 2:19,23 says Paul hopes to send Timothy when he knows the outcome of his situation. Verse 25 says he considers it necessary to send Epaphroditus back.

 Philippians 4:18: Epaphroditus is also named as the bearer of the Philippians' gift.
All implemented reference changes

Philippians 4:18: Epaphroditus is also named as the bearer of the Philippians' gift.

Paul distinguishes the two travel plans. He hopes to send Timothy after learning how his own situation will turn out (2:19,23), while he considers it necessary to send Epaphroditus back to the congregation (2:25-28).

All implemented note changes

Context and interpretation: Paul distinguishes the two travel plans. He hopes to send Timothy after learning how his own situation will turn out (2:19,23), while he considers it necessary to send Epaphroditus back to the congregation (2:25-28).

Editorial rationale

Correct the status of Timothy's visit; do not turn a planned action into an event already reported.

Day of the Lord: Biblical phrase for God’s decisive intervention in judgment and salvation.

All implemented reference changes

Isaiah 11:4: The ruler slays the wicked with the breath of his lips, the image echoed when 2 Thessalonians describes Jesus destroying the lawless one.
Daniel 7:25-26: The one who opposes the Most High and oppresses the saints loses his dominion when the court convenes, a parallel to the lawless one's eventual defeat.

Paul rejects the claim that the day has already come and names rebellion and the revealing of the lawless one (2:2-3). The Lord destroys that figure at the appearing of his coming (2:8). Paul refers to a restrainer but does not name the restrainer in this letter.

All implemented note changes

Editorial rationale

The present list wrongly makes destruction precede the day. Keep the existing restraint about identifying the restrainer; do not add a detailed end-times timetable.

S116
1 Timothy 6Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The chapter instructs slaves about conduct toward masters; it does not give a second set of instructions to masters.

Original app summary

Paul instructs Timothy on proper conduct for slaves and masters, warns against false teachers who promote controversy and greed, and charges him to pursue righteousness while commanding the wealthy to be generous rather than arrogant. He emphasizes that godliness with contentment is gain, the love of money leads to ruin, and urges Timothy to guard the faith against false knowledge.

Original translation notes

Under the yoke of slavery: The yoke is the wooden beam laid across the necks of a plough team, so the picture is of a man harnessed like an animal. Slavery in the Roman world was not based on race: people entered it through capture in war, piracy, debt, being exposed as infants, or being born to a slave mother, and slaves worked as miners, cooks, doctors, tutors, and estate managers. What Paul addresses in 6:2 is the situation the assemblies actually created, where a slave and the man who legally owned him sat in the same room as brothers.

The love of money: Literally 'love of silver'. The sentence is commonly quoted wrong. Paul does not write that money is the root of all evil; the Greek has no article, so it reads that love of money is a root of all kinds of evils. Six verses later he tells the rich to keep their wealth and use it, being generous and ready to share (6:17-19), which would be nonsense if the money itself were the problem.

Pontius Pilate: The Roman prefect of Judea from about AD 26 to 36, under Tiberius. He was based at Caesarea on the coast and came up to Jerusalem with troops for the feasts, when crowds made trouble likely. In 1961 a limestone block was found reused in the theatre at Caesarea carrying his name and his title, prefect of Judea, which puts the man and the office in stone. The confession Paul points to is the exchange in John 18:33-37.

King of kings and Lord of lords: This was a real imperial title before it was a hymn line. Great kings who ruled over lesser kings used it: Nebuchadnezzar is called king of kings in Daniel 2:37 and Ezekiel 26:7, and Artaxerxes signs himself that way in Ezra 7:12. Deuteronomy 10:17 had already applied the pattern to God, 'God of gods and Lord of lords'. Paul is putting the highest title a man could claim where it belongs.

Paul instructs Timothy on how slaves should treat their masters, warns against false teachers who promote controversy and greed, and charges him to pursue righteousness while commanding the wealthy to be generous rather than arrogant. He emphasizes that godliness with contentment is gain, the love of money leads to ruin, and urges Timothy to guard the faith against false knowledge.

Direct textual basis

1 Timothy 6:1-2 addresses how slaves should regard and serve their masters. It does not give a second set of instructions addressed to masters.

 Ephesians 6:5-9: Paul instructs slaves to serve sincerely and masters to stop threatening, since both answer to the same heavenly Master; this passage directly addresses both groups.
Colossians 3:22-4:1: After telling slaves to serve Christ in their work, Paul tells masters to provide what is right and fair because they too have a heavenly Master; the instructions include both groups.
All implemented reference changes

Ephesians 6:5-9: Paul instructs slaves to serve sincerely and masters to stop threatening, since both answer to the same heavenly Master; this passage directly addresses both groups.
Colossians 3:22-4:1: After telling slaves to serve Christ in their work, Paul tells masters to provide what is right and fair because they too have a heavenly Master; the instructions include both groups.

The instructions in 6:1-2 address enslaved believers and their conduct toward masters, including masters who are believers. Instructions addressed directly to masters appear in Ephesians 6:9 and Colossians 4:1.

All implemented note changes

Context and interpretation: The instructions in 6:1-2 address enslaved believers and their conduct toward masters, including masters who are believers. Instructions addressed directly to masters appear in Ephesians 6:9 and Colossians 4:1.

Editorial rationale

Correct the recipients; the current Ephesians cross-reference already supplies the useful contrast.

S117
Philemon 1Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

Paul asks Philemon to receive Onesimus as a brother; do not turn that appeal into a statement of completed legal emancipation.

Original app summary

Paul thanks God for Philemon's love and faith, then appeals for Onesimus, whose father he became in chains; he sends him back no longer as a slave but as a beloved brother, asks Philemon to receive him as he would receive Paul himself, and pledges in his own hand to repay whatever Onesimus owes.

Original translation notes

Prisoner of Christ: Paul describes himself as imprisoned on account of his service to Christ.

Onesimus: The name means Useful, and it was a common slave name; owners named slaves for what they wanted out of them. Paul plays on it three times and English can only show one. In verse 11 he is achrestos, useless, and now euchrestos, useful. In verse 20, 'let me have some benefit from you', the verb is onaimen, built on the same root as the man's name. Paul is punning on a slave's name while asking his owner to take him back as a brother.

A runaway slave: Under Roman law a slave who left remained his owner's property wherever he went, and the law was built to get him back. Owners advertised, and professional slave-catchers, fugitivarii, worked for a fee. A recovered runaway could be flogged, branded on the forehead, or fitted with an iron collar; collars survive with the message cast into them telling the finder to return the wearer to his master. Anyone who sheltered another man's slave was liable to the owner for the loss. Sending Onesimus back was the legally safe act; the letter he carried was not.

No longer as a slave, but a beloved brother: Rome had a formal route out of slavery: an owner could free a slave before a magistrate, by entering him on the census, or by will, and a slave freed that way by a Roman citizen became a citizen himself, still owing his former owner deference and set days of work as a patron. Paul does not name that procedure. He asks Philemon to receive Onesimus as he would receive Paul (verse 17), says he will do more than is asked (verse 21), and leaves it there. The letter is also addressed to Apphia, Archippus, and the assembly meeting in the house (verses 1-2), so the request was read out in front of everyone who would see the answer.

I write this with my own hand: Letters were normally dictated to a trained secretary, and the sender then wrote the last lines himself as a signature. The secretary of Romans names himself in the text: 'I, Tertius, who wrote down this letter' (Romans 16:22). Paul flags his own hand in 1 Corinthians 16:21, Galatians 6:11, Colossians 4:18, and calls it the mark in every letter in 2 Thessalonians 3:17. Here that hand signs a debt: charge whatever he owes to my account, I will repay it. It is written in the shape of a promissory note.

Prepare a guest room: Xenia is both the guest quarters and the bond itself. Guest-friendship in that world was a formal, binding, reciprocal obligation, not a courtesy: the host owed a guest food, lodging, and protection, and the tie passed down to the children of both houses. In the Iliad two warriors find mid-fight that their grandfathers were guest-friends, and they refuse to fight each other and swap armour instead (Iliad 6.212-231). Paul asks for a room in the house of the man he is asking to take back a runaway.

Paul thanks God for Philemon's love and faith, then appeals for Onesimus, whose father he became in chains; he sends him back and asks Philemon to welcome him as a beloved brother, receiving him as he would receive Paul himself, and pledges in his own hand to repay whatever Onesimus owes.

Direct textual basis

Philemon 12,16-17 records Paul sending Onesimus and asking Philemon to receive him as a beloved brother. Verses 15 and 18 refer to separation and a conditional debt; the letter does not narrate an escape or theft, or state that a legal manumission has occurred.

 Colossians 4:9: Onesimus is called a faithful and beloved brother and one of the Colossians.
All implemented reference changes

Colossians 4:9: Onesimus is called a faithful and beloved brother and one of the Colossians.

Paul asks Philemon to receive Onesimus as a beloved brother and as he would receive Paul himself (16-17). The letter refers to a period of separation and says, conditionally, 'if he has wronged you or owes you anything' (15,18). An escape, theft, and completed legal emancipation are not narrated. The familiar runaway-slave reconstruction is an inference.

All implemented note changes

The runaway-slave interpretation: Paul asks Philemon to receive Onesimus as a beloved brother and as he would receive Paul himself (16-17). The letter refers to a period of separation and says, conditionally, 'if he has wronged you or owes you anything' (15,18). An escape, theft, and completed legal emancipation are not narrated. The familiar runaway-slave reconstruction is an inference.

Prepare a guest room: Xenia is both the guest quarters and the bond itself. Guest-friendship in that world was a formal, binding, reciprocal obligation, not a courtesy: the host owed a guest food, lodging, and protection, and the tie passed down to the children of both houses. In the Iliad two warriors find mid-fight that their grandfathers were guest-friends, and they refuse to fight each other and swap armour instead (Iliad 6.212-231). Paul asks for a room in the house of the man he is asking to receive Onesimus as a brother.

Editorial rationale

The existing A runaway slave term and final guest-room sentence assert a reconstruction as fact. The existing manumission note already correctly says Paul does not name the legal procedure.

S118
Hebrews 2Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

To perfect salvation changes the object of perfecting: the verse says God perfects the author or pioneer of salvation through suffering.

Original app summary

Believers must heed the salvation announced by Jesus and confirmed through miracles and the Holy Spirit, lest they drift away; Jesus was made lower than angels and suffered death to perfect salvation and free humanity from fear of death, becoming a merciful high priest who helps those being tempted.

Radiance of God’s glory: Phrase describes the Son as the shining forth or exact expression of God’s divine glory.

High priest: Title for chief priest of Israel; here applied to Jesus as mediator between God and humans.

A little lower than the angels: Hebrews 2:6-8 quotes Psalm 8:4-6 from the Greek Old Testament, which reads lower than the angels where the Hebrew of Psalm 8:5 reads lower than elohim. The Greek phrase brachy ti can mean a little in rank or a little while in time, and 2:9 uses it both ways: Jesus was made lower, and is now crowned with glory and honour because He suffered death.

The message spoken by angels: The word translated angel means messenger. The message in 2:2 is the law given at Sinai, which the New Testament says repeatedly was delivered through angels: Stephen charges Israel with receiving the law ordained by angels (Acts 7:53), and Paul says it was administered through angels by a mediator (Galatians 3:19). Deuteronomy 33:2 describes the LORD coming from Sinai with ten thousands of holy ones.

Believers must heed the salvation announced by Jesus and confirmed by witnesses, signs, and the Holy Spirit. All implemented reference changes

Hebrews 5:8-10: Jesus learns obedience through suffering and, made perfect, becomes the source of eternal salvation to those who obey him; God designates him high priest.

Editorial rationale

Restore the grammatical object and connect the perfection language with the letter's own discussion of sinlessness and priesthood.

S119
Hebrews 5Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

Separate divine appointment from the account of Jesus praying and learning obedience through suffering; prayer is not stated as the means of appointment.

Original app summary

A high priest is chosen from among people to represent them before God; Christ did not claim this honor himself but was appointed by God and designated priest in the order of Melchizedek through his prayers, suffering, and obedience. The author notes his readers have become dull and need basic instruction again rather than the deeper teaching they should be ready to receive.

Original translation notes

Melchizedek: Name means 'king of righteousness'; king of Salem, priest of God Most High, type for Christ’s eternal priesthood.

Called by God, as Aaron was: Priests were installed by a seven-day ceremony (Exodus 29, Leviticus 8) in which blood was put on the right ear, right thumb, and right big toe of each man and oil was poured over his head. The Hebrew idiom for ordaining is to fill the hand, and the ram used is called the ram of filling. Taking the office uninvited was fatal: Korah and his company were destroyed for it (Numbers 16:1-40) and King Uzziah was struck with leprosy for burning incense in the temple (2 Chronicles 26:16-21).

Trained by constant use: The verb in 5:14 is the one behind the English word gymnasium; it is what an athlete does in training, not what a student does at a desk. It runs with the milk and solid food image of 5:12-13: an infant is fed, an adult chews. The letter comes back to athletic language at 12:1, where the Christian life is a race run in a stadium.

Learned obedience from what He suffered: The two Greek verbs differ by one letter: emathen, he learned, and epathen, he suffered. It is a fixed pairing in Greek and the English cannot carry it; Aeschylus uses the same play in the Agamemnon, pathei mathos, learning by suffering. Hebrews 2:10 states the matching idea, the author of their salvation made perfect through sufferings.

A high priest is chosen from among people to represent them before God; Christ did not claim this honor himself but was appointed by God and designated priest in the order of Melchizedek. In his earthly life he offered prayers and learned obedience through suffering. The author notes his readers have become dull and need basic instruction again rather than the deeper teaching they should be ready to receive.

Direct textual basis

Hebrews 5:4-6 grounds appointment in God's call; 5:7-10 then describes Jesus' prayers, suffering, and learned obedience. Prayer is not stated as the means by which he won the appointment.

 Psalm 110:4: The LORD swears an unchanging oath appointing a priest forever in Melchizedek's order, the declaration Hebrews uses for Christ's priesthood.
Hebrews 2:10: The pioneer of salvation is perfected through suffering.
All implemented reference changes

Numbers 16:1-40: Korah's company challenges the priestly order; the ground opens at the rebels' tents and swallows people and households, while fire consumes the 250 men offering incense.
Psalm 110:4: The LORD swears an unchanging oath appointing a priest forever in Melchizedek's order, the declaration Hebrews uses for Christ's priesthood.
Hebrews 2:10: The pioneer of salvation is perfected through suffering.

God's call is the basis of Christ's priestly appointment (5:4-6). Verses 7-10 describe his prayers and the obedience learned through suffering. Hebrews 4:15 also says he was without sin; learned obedience does not mean he first had to stop disobeying.

All implemented note changes

Context and interpretation: God's call is the basis of Christ's priestly appointment (5:4-6). Verses 7-10 describe his prayers and the obedience learned through suffering. Hebrews 4:15 also says he was without sin; learned obedience does not mean he first had to stop disobeying.

Editorial rationale

Separate the divine appointment from the subsequent account of prayer and suffering; the present through clause merges them causally.

S120
Hebrews 6Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The compressed warning can sound like an exhaustive rule that any backsliding is permanently unforgivable, while omitting the passage's specified experience, apostasy description, and immediate reassurance.

Original app summary

The author urges readers to move beyond basic Christian teaching toward maturity and warns that those who have tasted God's grace and then fallen away cannot be restored to repentance. God's promise to Abraham, confirmed by oath, serves as an anchor of hope; Jesus has entered the heavenly sanctuary as a high priest forever in the order of Melchizedek.

Original translation notes

Melchizedek: Name means 'king of righteousness'; king of Salem, priest of God Most High, type for Christ’s eternal priesthood.

Washings: The plural word in 6:2 is the general term for ritual washings, not the usual word for Christian baptism. Washing was ordinary life under the law: after contact with a corpse, after childbirth, after skin disease, and before priestly service (Leviticus 14-15, Numbers 19). Stepped immersion pools cut into bedrock have been excavated by the southern steps of the Temple Mount and across Jerusalem, Qumran, and Galilee, so a reader in the first century pictured a flight of steps down into water, not a font.

An anchor behind the curtain: Ancient anchors were wooden hooks weighted with lead or pierced stone, and a ship in trouble dropped several at once: the crew on Paul's ship let out four from the stern and prayed for daylight (Acts 27:29). Hebrews 6:19 then puts the anchor somewhere no anchor goes, inside the veil of the sanctuary, the room the high priest entered once a year and no one else entered at all (Leviticus 16:2).

He swore by Himself: An oath named something greater than the swearer as its guarantee, which is the point of 6:16, that men swear by someone greater. Hebrews 6:13-14 quotes Genesis 22:16-17, spoken at the binding of Isaac: by Myself I have sworn. There was nothing greater to name.

The author urges maturity and gives a solemn warning about falling away after the experiences described in verses 4-6, then expresses confidence in better things for the readers and urges persevering hope. God's promise and oath to Abraham ground that hope, with Jesus entering the heavenly sanctuary as high priest forever.

Direct textual basis

Hebrews 6:4-6 describes enlightened people sharing the Spirit, tasting the word and coming age, then falling away and openly shaming the Son. Verse 9 immediately expresses confidence in better things connected with salvation.

 Hebrews 3:12-14: A related warning concerns unbelief and holding confidence to the end.
Hebrews 10:26-29: This warning names trampling the Son, profaning covenant blood, and insulting the Spirit of grace, giving the rejection language alongside the warning in Hebrews 6.
All implemented reference changes

Hebrews 3:12-14: A related warning concerns unbelief and holding confidence to the end.
Hebrews 10:26-29: This warning names trampling the Son, profaning covenant blood, and insulting the Spirit of grace, giving the rejection language alongside the warning in Hebrews 6.

The warning names people who have been enlightened, tasted the heavenly gift, shared in the Holy Spirit, and tasted God's good word and the powers of the coming age, then fallen away (6:4-6). It describes their rejection in terms of crucifying the Son of God again and exposing him to public disgrace. Verse 9 nevertheless expresses confidence in better things for the readers. Interpreters differ over the spiritual status of those described and the force of the warning; the paragraph combines severe warning with a call to persevering hope.

All implemented note changes
