S121
Hebrews 11Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

Some received promised blessings (v33), while the complete promised fulfillment remained ahead (vv39-40). Avoid denying every receipt of a promise.

Original app summary

The author defines faith as confidence in unseen things and catalogs the faithful acts of Abel, Enoch, Noah, Abraham, Sarah, Isaac, Jacob, Joseph, Moses, and others who obeyed God without receiving the promises in their lifetimes. He notes that some faithful were tortured and killed while wandering destitute, yet all were commended for their faith.

Original translation notes

Faith: Carries sense of trust, confidence, or faithfulness; Hebrews 11 defines it as assurance of hope and conviction of unseen realities.

Unshakable kingdom: Describes the eternal reign of God, contrasted with things of creation that can be shaken and removed.

Tortured: The word in 11:35 names a particular execution: the victim was stretched over a frame or wheel, drum-tight, and beaten to death. The account behind it is 2 Maccabees 6-7, from the persecution under Antiochus IV in the 160s BC, where an old scribe named Eleazar and then a mother with seven sons were tortured to death after refusing release in exchange for eating swine's flesh, saying they looked to be raised again.

Sawed in two: Jewish tradition attached this line to Isaiah. The Martyrdom of Isaiah, a Second Temple Jewish work, says King Manasseh had him sawn in half with a wooden saw, and the Babylonian Talmud records the same execution at Yevamot 49b. Manasseh's reign is the one 2 Kings 21:16 describes as filling Jerusalem with innocent blood from end to end.

Rahab: Rahab kept a house built into the wall of Jericho, which is how she could let the spies down on a rope through her window after the city gate was shut behind the search party (Joshua 2:15). The scarlet cord she left hanging there marked the house to be spared, and Joshua 6:22-25 says she and her whole family were brought out alive and settled among Israel. Matthew 1:5 lists her in the line of David.

The author defines faith as confidence in unseen things and catalogs the faithful acts of Abel, Enoch, Noah, Abraham, Sarah, Isaac, Jacob, Joseph, Moses, and others who obeyed God without yet receiving all that God had promised. He notes that some faithful were tortured and killed while wandering destitute, yet all were commended for their faith.

Direct textual basis

Hebrews 11:33 says some gained what was promised, while 11:39-40 says the complete promised outcome awaited fulfillment together with us. Verse 5 also distinguishes Enoch from those who died.

 Genesis 21:1-3: Abraham and Sarah receive the promised Isaac.

Genesis 21:1-3: Abraham and Sarah receive the promised Isaac.

S122
1 John 3Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

Children of the devil, who sin can imply that any sinful act identifies someone as a child of the devil, dropping the chapter's practice/continuance language and the letter's provision for sinning believers.

Original app summary

John contrasts children of God, who love and practice righteousness, with children of the devil, who sin and hate; believers know love by Christ laying down his life.

Original translation notes

Lawlessness: Means deliberate violation of God’s law; used here to equate sin with rebellion against God’s standard.

Cain: The firstborn son of Adam and Eve, who killed his brother Abel in a field after God accepted Abel's offering and not his own (Genesis 4:1-16). John does not use the ordinary word for murder here. The Greek verb is sphazo, to cut the throat, the butcher's word for slaughtering a sacrificial animal, used again of the Lamb in Revelation 5:6.

Lay down his life: The wording is lifted straight out of the Good Shepherd passage, where Jesus says it of Himself in John 10:11, 10:15, and 10:17-18. John then applies the same phrase, word for word, to what believers owe one another.

Withholds his compassion: Splanchna are the inward organs, the heart and liver and lungs. Greek and Hebrew both located strong feeling in the gut where English puts it in the heart, so the literal sense of 3:17 is 'shuts his inward parts against him'. The same word stands behind 'tender mercy' in Luke 1:78 and 'affection' in Philippians 1:8. The 'earthly possessions' of that verse is bios, a man's means of living.

John contrasts children of God, who love and practice righteousness, with children of the devil, who practice sin and hate their brothers; believers know love by Christ laying down his life.

Direct textual basis

The app's 1 John 3:6-10 speaks of continuing in sin and practicing sin or righteousness; 1:8-2:2 denies sinlessness and gives believers an advocate when they sin.

 1 John 1:8-2:2: Believers confess sin and have Christ as their advocate.
All implemented reference changes

1 John 1:8-2:2: Believers confess sin and have Christ as their advocate.

This passage contrasts practiced righteousness and love with practiced sin and hatred (3:6-10,15). The same letter says that claiming to have no sin is self-deception and that believers who sin have an advocate in Jesus Christ (1:8-2:2). The contrast is not an instruction to deny believers' need for confession and forgiveness.

All implemented note changes

Context and interpretation: This passage contrasts practiced righteousness and love with practiced sin and hatred (3:6-10,15). The same letter says that claiming to have no sin is self-deception and that believers who sin have an advocate in Jesus Christ (1:8-2:2). The contrast is not an instruction to deny believers' need for confession and forgiveness.

Editorial rationale

Use the practice language already present in the canonical translation; do not claim that Greek tense alone settles every doctrinal question.

S123
2 John 1Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The final clause can sound like a ban on ordinary greeting of every non-Christian, rather than a warning framed around deceivers arriving without the teaching about Christ.

Original app summary

John rejoices that the chosen lady's children walk in the truth and urges the commandment held from the beginning, to love one another by walking in His commandments; he warns that many deceivers who refuse to confess Jesus Christ coming in the flesh have gone out, the deceiver and the antichrist, and forbids receiving or even greeting anyone who does not bring the teaching of Christ.

Original translation notes

Elect lady: Likely refers to a specific woman believer and her household, though some interpret it figuratively of a church; literally means 'chosen lady.'

The elder: The Greek has the definite article: not an elder, but the elder. He gives no name, which tells you the people receiving this letter needed none. The same word names an office held in every assembly, with elders appointed city by city (Acts 14:23; Titus 1:5), and Peter uses it of himself in 1 Peter 5:1. Third John opens with the identical two words.

Do not receive him into your home: Travelling teachers had nowhere else to go. Inns were few, rough, and had a bad name, so a man arriving in a town went to a believer's house, ate at that table, slept under that roof, and was sent on with supplies for the next stage; 3 John 1:5-8 shows the same arrangement working properly. Taking a teacher in was therefore a public endorsement of him, and the household's own standing went behind whatever he then taught. Shutting the door was the only lever an ordinary family had.

Paper and ink: A chartes is a sheet of papyrus, made by laying strips cut from the pith of the papyrus reed crosswise, then pressing and drying them into a single page. Melan is literally 'the black', ink mixed from soot or lampblack with gum and water, laid on with a cut reed. A letter this short is one sheet. In 3 John 1:13 he names the pen as well, the kalamos, a reed cut to a point and split at the tip.

John rejoices that the chosen lady's children walk in the truth and urges the commandment held from the beginning, to love one another by walking in His commandments; he warns that many deceivers who refuse to confess Jesus Christ coming in the flesh have gone out, the deceiver and the antichrist, and warns against welcoming or greeting the deceiving teachers who arrive without this teaching, since doing so shares in their work.

Direct textual basis

2 John 7-11 concerns deceivers rejecting Jesus Christ coming in the flesh, people arriving without this teaching, reception into a home, and sharing in their evil work.

 3 John 1:5-8: Supporting faithful traveling teachers makes the hosts fellow workers in the truth.
All implemented reference changes

3 John 1:5-8: Supporting faithful traveling teachers makes the hosts fellow workers in the truth.

The warning follows the description of deceivers who refuse the confession of Jesus Christ coming in the flesh (7-10). Receiving a visiting teacher into the household supplied lodging and support for his work; verse 11 warns that the welcome shares in that work. 3 John 1:5-8 gives the corresponding positive instruction about supporting faithful teachers.

All implemented note changes

Do not receive him into your home: The warning follows the description of deceivers who refuse the confession of Jesus Christ coming in the flesh (7-10). Receiving a visiting teacher into the household supplied lodging and support for his work; verse 11 warns that the welcome shares in that work. 3 John 1:5-8 gives the corresponding positive instruction about supporting faithful teachers.

Editorial rationale

The existing hospitality note supplies the setting correctly; bring that setting into the abbreviated summary instead of leaving anyone unqualified.

S124
Revelation 3Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

Will be protected from coming trials expands a specific promise about the hour of testing into a general promise of protection from future trials.

Original app summary

Christ addresses three churches: Sardis is spiritually dead despite its reputation and must repent or face sudden judgment, though a few remain faithful; Philadelphia is weak but obedient and will be protected from coming trials; Laodicea is lukewarm and self-deceived about its poverty, and Christ calls it to buy spiritual refinement and repent.

Original translation notes

Sardis: The old capital of Lydia and of Croesus, set on a spur of Mount Tmolus so steep the garrison thought one side needed no watch. It was taken that way twice. Cyrus captured it about 546 BC after a soldier watched a Lydian climb down the cliff for a dropped helmet and led a party up the same route (Herodotus 1.84), and Antiochus III took it the same way in 214 BC. Verse 3 tells the church that if it does not wake, He will come like a thief and it will not know what hour.

Philadelphia: Founded by Attalus II Philadelphus and named for his loyalty to his brother. It sat on a fault line: the earthquake of AD 17 flattened twelve cities of the province in one night (Tacitus, Annals 2.47), and Strabo writes that its walls were constantly cracking and much of the population lived outside in the fields. Verse 12 promises the one who conquers that he will be made a pillar in the temple of God and will never go out.

Laodicea's water: Laodicea had no spring of its own. Water was piped in by aqueduct from springs about five miles off, and the stone pipe blocks recovered on the site are thick with mineral scale from it. Hierapolis, whose hot mineral springs are visible across the valley about six miles north, and Colossae with its cold water ten miles east, both had what Laodicea did not. The word in 3:16 for what will be done with the lukewarm mouthful is emeo, to vomit.

Gold, white garments and eye salve: The letter offers the city three things it was famous for producing. Laodicea was a banking centre so rich that after the earthquake of about AD 60 it rebuilt itself and refused imperial help (Tacitus, Annals 14.27). Its district was known for glossy black wool. It lay in Phrygia, the source of the powder used in ancient eye ointments, and had a medical school. Verse 18 tells it to buy gold, white clothes and eye salve from Christ instead.

The key of David: Verse 7 takes the wording from Isaiah 22:22, where the LORD strips the palace steward Shebna of his office and gives the key of the house of David to Eliakim, who opens and none shuts. A royal steward's key was a physical badge of office, carried on the shoulder, controlling access to the king. The image is authority over admission.

A pillar in the temple: Solomon's temple had two free-standing bronze pillars at its porch, each with a name of its own, Jachin and Boaz (1 Kings 7:15-22). Greek temples carried the names of benefactors and priests carved on their columns. In an earthquake city, a pillar that never goes out is a promise with weight.

The book of life: Cities kept a citizen register, and a name struck off it ended a man's legal standing. The image is older than that: Moses asks to be blotted out of the book God has written (Exodus 32:32-33), and the psalmist speaks of the book of the living (Psalm 69:28). Verse 5 promises the opposite, that the name will not be erased and will be acknowledged before the Father.

Christ addresses three churches: Sardis is spiritually dead despite its reputation and must repent or face sudden judgment, though a few remain faithful; Philadelphia is weak but obedient and is promised keeping from the coming hour of testing; Laodicea is lukewarm and self-deceived about its poverty, and Christ calls it to buy spiritual refinement and repent.

Direct textual basis

Revelation 3:10 promises Philadelphia keeping from the hour of testing coming on the whole world, alongside a call to perseverance and holding fast in 3:8,10-11.

 John 17:15: Jesus prays for his followers' protection without asking for their removal from the world.
Revelation 2:10: Christ warns Smyrna of imprisonment and ten days of tribulation, and calls the church to remain faithful even to death.
All implemented reference changes

John 17:15: Jesus prays for his followers' protection without asking for their removal from the world.
Revelation 2:10: Christ warns Smyrna of imprisonment and ten days of tribulation, and calls the church to remain faithful even to death.

The promise to Philadelphia concerns the coming 'hour of testing' (3:10), and the letter also calls for perseverance and holding fast (3:10-11). The verse does not name how this keeping will occur. It should not be expanded into a promise that this church will experience no further suffering.

All implemented note changes

Context and interpretation: The promise to Philadelphia concerns the coming 'hour of testing' (3:10), and the letter also calls for perseverance and holding fast (3:10-11). The verse does not name how this keeping will occur. It should not be expanded into a promise that this church will experience no further suffering.

Editorial rationale

Narrow the promise to its stated object and avoid deciding a disputed mechanism or end-times scheme through the summary.

S125
Revelation 6Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

Before judgment imposes a blanket future placement, although the fourth seal already depicts killing and the closing cry says the day of wrath has come.

Original app summary

Opening of six seals reveals horsemen bringing conquest, war, famine, death, martyrs seeking justice, and cosmic upheaval before judgment.

Original translation notes

A quart of wheat for a denarius: A choinix is about a quart, and it was reckoned as one man's grain ration for one day; Herodotus (7.187) uses exactly that figure to calculate the supply of Xerxes' army. So the cry in 6:6 prices a single day's bread for a single man at a full day's wage, with nothing left for a family or for anything else. In ordinary times a denarius bought something like eight to sixteen quarts of wheat. This is famine pricing, roughly ten times normal, announced as a market rate.

Denarius: A Roman silver coin, the standard day's wage for a labourer, which is the going rate Jesus names in the parable of the vineyard (Matthew 20:2). It carried the emperor's head, which is why it was the coin produced in the tax question (Mark 12:16). Two denarii covered the injured man's care at the inn (Luke 10:35).

A pair of scales: Grain was normally sold by the sack and eaten without counting. Weighing it out on a balance is itself a sign of scarcity, and the Law uses that image for judgment: they shall deliver your bread again by weight, and you shall eat and not be satisfied (Leviticus 26:26; Ezekiel 4:16). The rider holds the balance, and the price is called out from the middle of the living creatures.

Do not harm the oil and the wine: Olives and vines are deep-rooted perennials that survive a dry year which destroys a grain crop, and both were the region's cash exports rather than its daily food. The limit set in 6:6 spares the two things a poor man cannot eat and leaves the grain ruined. Grain, oil and wine are the standard triad for a land's produce (Deuteronomy 7:13; Joel 2:19).

The pale horse: Chloros is the ordinary word for the green of growing things; Mark 6:39 uses it of green grass, and it is the root of English chlorophyll. Applied to a face or a body it means the greenish grey of sickness and death. The colour is the point of the rider's name.

Sword, famine, pestilence and wild beasts: The four instruments listed in 6:8 are the same four the LORD names in Ezekiel 14:21 as His four disastrous acts of judgment against Jerusalem: sword, famine, wild beasts and pestilence. The word rendered pestilence here is thanatos, death, which is how the Septuagint often translates the Hebrew for plague. The list is a quotation, not a new inventory.

Black as sackcloth of hair: Sackcloth was woven from black goat hair, coarse and dark, and worn against the skin as a sign of mourning. Verse 12 does not just say the sun went dark; it says it went the colour of mourning cloth, and the moon the colour of blood. Joel 2:31 and Acts 2:20 use the same pair of images.

Hades: Hades is not hell. It is the term the Septuagint uses to render Hebrew Sheol, the common realm of the dead. In 6:8 Hades follows the fourth rider as a named figure, the way a burial detail follows an army. It is distinct from Gehenna and from the lake of fire, into which 20:14 says Death and Hades are themselves thrown.

The first six seals reveal horsemen bringing conquest, war, famine, and death, martyrs crying for vindication, and cosmic upheaval as earth's rulers and peoples cry that the great day of wrath has come.

Direct textual basis

Revelation 6:8 gives the fourth rider authority to kill; 6:10 has martyrs still awaiting vindication; 6:17 announces that the great day of wrath has come.

 Ezekiel 14:21: The LORD names sword, famine, wild beasts, and plague as judgments against Jerusalem, the same four agents used by Revelation's fourth rider.
Joel 2:31: Joel describes the sun darkening and the moon becoming blood before the great day of the LORD, imagery also used when Revelation's sixth seal opens.
All implemented reference changes

Zechariah 6:1-8: Four chariots with differently colored horses go out to patrol the earth, providing a prophetic comparison for Revelation's mounted visions.
Joel 2:31: Joel describes the sun darkening and the moon becoming blood before the great day of the LORD, imagery also used when Revelation's sixth seal opens.
Ezekiel 14:21: The LORD names sword, famine, wild beasts, and plague as judgments against Jerusalem, the same four agents used by Revelation's fourth rider.

The martyrs still ask for vindication at the fifth seal (6:10), while those confronted by the sixth seal say the day of wrath has come (6:17). The fourth seal's sword, famine, plague, and beasts echo the judgments of Ezekiel 14:21. The visions should be summarized without placing every judgment after this chapter.

All implemented note changes

Context and interpretation: The martyrs still ask for vindication at the fifth seal (6:10), while those confronted by the sixth seal say the day of wrath has come (6:17). The fourth seal's sword, famine, plague, and beasts echo the judgments of Ezekiel 14:21. The visions should be summarized without placing every judgment after this chapter.

Editorial rationale

The existing judgment-instruments note already identifies the Ezekiel background; remove the summary's added temporal claim.

S126
Revelation 8Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The mountain-like object turns a third of the sea to blood; Wormwood makes freshwater bitter. Do not conflate the two trumpet judgments.

Original app summary

The seventh seal opens to silence; seven angels receive trumpets and an angel offers incense with the prayers of God's people at the altar. As the first four angels sound their trumpets, devastating plagues strike the earth - hail and fire burn vegetation, a burning mountain poisons the sea, the star Wormwood contaminates water supplies, and darkness covers a third of the sky; an eagle warns of three more woes to come.

Original translation notes

Silence for about half an hour: The only measured stretch of silence in Scripture, and it follows six seals of noise. In the temple the moment of incense was the quiet one, with the crowd outside praying and no sound from within (Luke 1:10). Habakkuk 2:20 and Zephaniah 1:7 both call for silence before the LORD when judgment is at hand.

The golden censer: A fire pan for carrying live coals from the altar and burning incense on them. The high priest carried one behind the veil on the Day of Atonement (Leviticus 16:12-13), and the daily incense went up on the golden altar morning and evening. In 8:5 the angel fills the same censer with fire from the altar and throws it to the earth, so the vessel that carried up the prayers brings down the answer.

Trumpets: Not a musical instrument but a signal. Numbers 10:1-10 gives the priests two silver trumpets for summoning the assembly, breaking camp, going to war and sounding over the offerings. Seven trumpets circled Jericho for seven days (Joshua 6). A trumpet in this book is an announcement everyone hears at once.

Wormwood: A shrub of the Artemisia family, grey-leaved and intensely bitter, common in dry country. Scripture uses it as the standard image for what is bitter and poisonous to drink: a root bearing poisonous fruit and wormwood (Deuteronomy 29:18), justice turned to wormwood (Amos 5:7), and the LORD feeding a rebellious people wormwood and poisoned water (Jeremiah 9:15). In 8:11 the star is given the name.

A third of everything: The fraction runs through the whole chapter: a third of the earth, the trees, the sea, the creatures, the ships, the rivers, the sun, the moon, the stars, the day and the night. It is a limited stroke, larger than the fourth given to the horsemen in 6:8 and short of the whole. Ezekiel 5:1-4 divides the prophet's shorn hair into thirds for three different judgments.

The seventh seal opens to silence; seven angels receive trumpets and an angel offers incense with the prayers of God's people at the altar. As the first four angels sound their trumpets, devastating plagues strike the earth - hail and fire burn vegetation, something like a burning mountain is thrown into the sea, turning a third of it to blood, the star Wormwood contaminates water supplies, and darkness covers a third of the sky; an eagle warns of three more woes to come.

Direct textual basis

Revelation 8:8-9 describes something like a burning mountain and a third of the sea becoming blood. In 8:10-11 Wormwood affects freshwater, making it bitter.

 Exodus 7:20-21: When Aaron strikes the Nile as commanded, its water turns to blood and its fish die, the Exodus plague that parallels Revelation's sea-to-blood vision.
Jeremiah 9:15: The LORD threatens to give his people wormwood and poisoned water, a prophetic use of bitter, deadly drink echoed by the Wormwood star's effect on rivers and springs.
All implemented reference changes

Jeremiah 9:15: The LORD threatens to give his people wormwood and poisoned water, a prophetic use of bitter, deadly drink echoed by the Wormwood star's effect on rivers and springs.
Exodus 7:20-21: When Aaron strikes the Nile as commanded, its water turns to blood and its fish die, the Exodus plague that parallels Revelation's sea-to-blood vision.

The second trumpet brings something like a burning mountain into the sea, and a third of the sea becomes blood (8:8-9). The third trumpet brings the star Wormwood onto rivers and springs, making their waters bitter (8:10-11). These are distinct visions.

All implemented note changes

Context and interpretation: The second trumpet brings something like a burning mountain into the sea, and a third of the sea becomes blood (8:8-9). The third trumpet brings the star Wormwood onto rivers and springs, making their waters bitter (8:10-11). These are distinct visions.

Editorial rationale

Separate sea-to-blood imagery from bitter freshwater; poisoned sea conflates the two trumpets.

S127
Revelation 13Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

Distinguish the two beasts and the coerced mark; the mark is the name or number of the name, not exclusively the printed numeral 666.

Original app summary

John sees a beast from sea and another from earth empowered by dragon, demanding worship, persecuting saints, and marking followers with 666.

Original translation notes

The mark: Charagma is not a vague sign. It is the technical word for something stamped or engraved: the imprint of a die on a coin, the official stamp certifying a document, a brand burned into a slave or an animal. It is placed on the right hand or the forehead (13:16), the two places nobody can hide, and 13:17 ties it to the right to buy or sell. Acts 17:29 uses the same word for the engraved work of an idol.

The number written in letters: Greek had no separate digits. The alphabet did double duty, so the number in 13:18 stands in the manuscripts as three letters, chi xi stigma. Because every name was therefore also a sum, people played with it: a wall at Pompeii carries the line 'I love her whose number is 545'. That letter arithmetic is what 'let the one who has understanding calculate' refers to, and the text supplies the total rather than the name.

The image that was given breath: An eikon is a statue or portrait, and compulsory reverence toward one was a real civic instrument in the province these letters go to. Pergamum built the first temple in Asia to Rome and Augustus in 29 BC, Smyrna won one for Tiberius in AD 26, and refusing the required gesture at such a temple was treated as disloyalty rather than as a private belief. Daniel 3:5-6 is the older version of the same demand: bow at the sound of the music or go into the furnace.

Two beasts, and what the second is called later: Verse 1 has a beast come up out of the sea; verse 11 has a second come up out of the earth, with two horns like a lamb and the voice of a dragon. This second one is the one that makes the image, works signs, and enforces the mark. Later in the book it is called by a different name, the false prophet (16:13; 19:20; 20:10), which is the text's own identification.

Forty-two months: Three and a half years, expressed three different ways across these chapters: forty-two months here and at 11:2, 1,260 days at 11:3 and 12:6, and a time, times and half a time at 12:14. Daniel uses the same span (Daniel 7:25; 12:7). It is a period with a stated limit, counted from its start.

John sees a beast from the sea empowered by the dragon, blaspheming God and waging war against the saints. A second beast from the earth promotes worship of the first and requires a mark for buying and selling: the beast's name or the number of its name, given as 666.

Direct textual basis

Revelation 13:2 gives the sea beast authority from the dragon. The earth beast enforces worship and the mark in 13:12-17; the mark is the beast's name or number of its name, not exclusively a printed 666.

 Daniel 3:4-6: An image is worshiped under threat of death.
Revelation 14:9-12: Beast worship and its mark are contrasted with the saints' endurance.
All implemented reference changes

Daniel 3:4-6: An image is worshiped under threat of death.
Revelation 14:9-12: Beast worship and its mark are contrasted with the saints' endurance.

The second beast makes people worship the first and enforces the mark needed for buying and selling (13:12,16-17). The mark is described as the beast's name or the number of its name; verse 18 gives the number as 666. Some manuscripts read 616, as the Berean Standard Bible footnote records. The passage does not name a modern person or technology.

All implemented note changes

The number written in letters: Greek letters could serve as numerals, so 666 could be written as chi xi stigma. Some manuscripts instead read 616, as the Berean Standard Bible footnote records. Because every name was therefore also a sum, people played with it: a wall at Pompeii carries the line 'I love her whose number is 545'. That letter arithmetic is what 'let the one who has understanding calculate' refers to, and the text supplies the total rather than the name.

Context and interpretation: The second beast makes people worship the first and enforces the mark needed for buying and selling (13:12,16-17). The mark is described as the beast's name or the number of its name; verse 18 gives the number as 666. Some manuscripts read 616, as the Berean Standard Bible footnote records. The passage does not name a modern person or technology.

Editorial rationale

Differentiate the beasts' actions and coerced economic allegiance. The existing number note also needs the attested 616 variant rather than implying every manuscript simply has the same three letters.

S128
Revelation 19Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The beast and kings lose their armies; the rider does not defeat his own heavenly army.

Original app summary

Heaven erupts in praise as God judges the great prostitute, and a wedding supper for the Lamb is announced; then a rider on a white horse called Faithful and True wages war against the beast and kings of the earth, capturing the beast and false prophet while his armies are defeated by the sword from his mouth.

Original translation notes

Hallelujah: A Hebrew imperative, praise, joined to the shortened form of the divine name. It opens and closes many psalms, especially the block of Psalms 146 to 150 where it stands at both ends of every one. Revelation 19:1, 3, 4 and 6 are the only four occurrences of the word in the New Testament, and they all fall in these six verses.

Fine linen, bright and pure: Byssos is the expensive bleached linen of Egypt, the cloth used for priestly garments and for the rich man's clothing in Luke 16:19. It appears twice in this chapter, on the bride (19:8) and on the armies following the rider (19:14). Verse 8 states what it stands for and leaves nothing to guess: the righteous deeds of the saints.

A robe dipped in blood: The robe is already stained when the rider appears, before any battle in the chapter is fought. Isaiah 63:1-3 is the passage in view: someone comes from Edom with crimson garments because he has trodden the winepress alone and their lifeblood spattered his clothes. Verse 15 names the same winepress.

The sword from His mouth: Two Greek words are translated sword. The machaira is the short blade a soldier or a butcher carried; the rhomphaia is a long broad Thracian sword, the one used in this book (1:16; 2:12; 19:15, 21). It comes out of His mouth, not His hand, which matches Isaiah 11:4, where He strikes the earth with the rod of His mouth.

King of kings and Lord of lords: King of kings was the formal title of the great kings of the East, claimed by Babylonian and Persian monarchs; Ezra 7:12 has Artaxerxes use it of himself and Daniel 2:37 applies it to Nebuchadnezzar. Deuteronomy 10:17 and Psalm 136:2-3 give the corresponding title to God. Verse 16 has it written on the robe and on the thigh, the place a rider's name would be visible to anyone standing beside the horse.

The marriage supper of the Lamb: A Jewish wedding ran in two stages: the betrothal, legally binding from the day it was made, and later the procession that brought the bride to the groom's house for a feast lasting days. Verse 9 pronounces a blessing on those invited to that supper. Verses 17-18 immediately set a second supper against it, the one the birds are called to.

Heaven erupts in praise as God judges the great prostitute, and a wedding supper for the Lamb is announced; then a rider on a white horse called Faithful and True wages war against the beast and kings of the earth, capturing the beast and false prophet while the beast's and kings' armies are defeated by the sword from the rider's mouth.

Direct textual basis

Revelation 19:14 places heaven's armies with the rider. Verses 19-21 place the beast and earthly kings' armies against him, with those opposing forces defeated.

 Isaiah 11:4: The ruler strikes the earth with the rod of his mouth and slays the wicked with his breath, a prophetic comparison for the sword from the rider's mouth.
Ezekiel 39:17-20: The LORD summons birds and beasts to eat fallen warriors and drink their blood, the feast image echoed by Revelation's summons to birds and defeat of the rider's enemies.
All implemented reference changes

Isaiah 11:4: The ruler strikes the earth with the rod of his mouth and slays the wicked with his breath, a prophetic comparison for the sword from the rider's mouth.
Ezekiel 39:17-20: The LORD summons birds and beasts to eat fallen warriors and drink their blood, the feast image echoed by Revelation's summons to birds and defeat of the rider's enemies.

The armies of heaven follow the rider (19:14). The beast and the kings of the earth gather their armies to oppose him (19:19). Those opposing forces are defeated by the sword from the rider's mouth (19:20-21).

All implemented note changes

Context and interpretation: The armies of heaven follow the rider (19:14). The beast and the kings of the earth gather their armies to oppose him (19:19). Those opposing forces are defeated by the sword from the rider's mouth (19:20-21).

Editorial rationale

Replace his armies with the beast's and kings' armies, so the rider is not depicted defeating his own followers.

S129
Revelation 22Implemented and audited in all 13 languages

The summary assigns the adding-and-taking-away warning directly to Jesus, although verses 18-19 do not explicitly reidentify their first-person speaker.

Original app summary

The angel shows John the river of life, tree of life, and throne of God in the holy city where God's servants will serve him and see his face; Jesus declares he is coming soon and warns against adding to or removing words from this prophecy.

Original translation notes

The river and the tree of life: A river flows from the throne down the middle of the street, with the tree on both sides bearing twelve crops, one each month, its leaves for the healing of the nations (22:1-2). Ezekiel 47:1-12 describes water from under the temple threshold with trees on both banks fruiting monthly and leaves for healing. The tree first appears in Genesis 2:9 and was closed off in Genesis 3:22-24; here the guard is gone.

Do not seal up the words: Daniel was told the opposite: shut up the words and seal the book until the time of the end (Daniel 12:4, 9). Sealing a document kept it closed and unread until the right moment. The instruction in 22:10 is that this scroll is not to be sealed, because the time is near.

Adding and taking away: The warning in 22:18-19 is the same formula Moses uses twice, do not add to the word and do not take from it (Deuteronomy 4:2; 12:32). Ancient treaties and public documents commonly carried a clause of this kind to stop copyists and officials from altering the text. Here it closes the book it belongs to.

Come, Lord Jesus: The prayer in 22:20 is preserved in its Aramaic form by Paul at the end of 1 Corinthians, marana tha (1 Corinthians 16:22), and the Didache, an early church manual, uses the same Aramaic word in a communion prayer. Aramaic was the everyday language of Judea, so this is one of the few places where the actual sound of the earliest believers' prayer survives in the text.

The bright morning star: The morning star is the planet Venus, the last light still visible as the sky goes grey, the sign that night is ending. Balaam's oracle says a star shall come out of Jacob and a sceptre rise out of Israel (Numbers 24:17), and 2 Peter 1:19 speaks of the morning star rising in your hearts. In 22:16 Jesus applies the title to Himself, along with the root and the offspring of David.

Serve: Latreuo means to serve or minister, especially in priestly duty, and it is the word behind English liturgy's companion term latria. Luke 1:74-75 uses it of serving God in holiness all our days, and Paul applies it in Romans 12:1 to presenting your body as a living sacrifice. Revelation 22:3 says His servants will latreuo Him and see His face, which is temple language for work, not for a posture.

The angel shows John the river of life, tree of life, and throne of God in the holy city where God's servants will serve him and see his face; Jesus declares he is coming soon, and the book closes with a warning against adding to or removing words from this prophecy.

Direct textual basis

Revelation 22:16 explicitly names Jesus, 22:17 names Spirit and bride, 22:18 begins I testify without naming the speaker again, and 22:20 distinguishes the testimony and the response Come, Lord Jesus.

 Deuteronomy 4:2: A parallel warning prohibits adding to or subtracting from the commanded word.
Deuteronomy 12:32: Israel is told to obey the commands without adding to or subtracting from them, a further parallel to Revelation's warning against changing the words of its prophecy.
All implemented reference changes

Zechariah 14:8-11: Living waters flow from Jerusalem in summer and winter, the LORD reigns over all the earth, and Jerusalem is inhabited securely without further utter destruction.
Deuteronomy 4:2: A parallel warning prohibits adding to or subtracting from the commanded word.
Deuteronomy 12:32: Israel is told to obey the commands without adding to or subtracting from them, a further parallel to Revelation's warning against changing the words of its prophecy.

The conclusion contains successive voices: Jesus speaks in 22:16, the Spirit and bride in 22:17, a first-person testimony in 22:18-19, and the response to Jesus' promise in 22:20. The warning belongs explicitly to the words of prophecy in this book; its speaker is not named again in verses 18-19.

All implemented note changes

Context and interpretation: The conclusion contains successive voices: Jesus speaks in 22:16, the Spirit and bride in 22:17, a first-person testimony in 22:18-19, and the response to Jesus' promise in 22:20. The warning belongs explicitly to the words of prophecy in this book; its speaker is not named again in verses 18-19.

Editorial rationale

Use a neutral attribution rather than silently deciding the speaker at a transition. The existing adding-and-taking-away note already limits the warning to this prophecy.

All rows were approved for implementation across 13 languages. Reference descriptions supply passage-specific context; translation notes identify interpretive uncertainty and edition differences. This review does not claim that unflagged passages admit only one interpretation.
